Summary

“Look, I don’t have time for this! If you lost my eggs, or let them defrost or something it’s fine. I don’t need ‘em anymore. So you can—“ 

“They were implanted in another woman.” Clifton speaks quickly, rushing so fast to make his confession that the words almost blend together.

 

Carol finds out her eggs were wrongly implanted in to a stranger, and her life is turned upside down overnight when she's drawn in to the woman's world.

Something Unexpected Between Us

by Trufreak89

https://archiveofourown.org/works/79940346

    Chapter 1


    “With that, Raban—proud, haughty Raban—mounted the plank and leaped from sight. The amaranthine slip-sand closed over him, leaving nary a ripple.”

    Carol Sturka closes the book in her lap to raucous applause. She smiles, her eyes scanning the crowded bookstore for Helen, her manager—and wife.

    It takes her a painfully long time to remember that Helen isn’t there. That she isn’t anywhere. Not anymore.

    Her eyes land on her publicist, Margaux. An over-eager, fresh-faced young woman all of twenty-two. Recently out of college and assigned by her publishing company, she’s been with Carol for the full six weeks of the tour promoting Carol’s latest book: Bloodsong of Wycaro.

    Margaux smiles too much and talks too loud for Carol’s liking. She also makes Carol feel old. She’s fast approaching the wrong side of fifty, and Carol swears the young woman speaks an entirely different language sometimes. She doesn’t know what mewing is or what cheugy means, and she doesn’t care to learn.

    Carol is too old for that shit. Too old and too tired. Besides, this is the last night of the book tour, and the end of the Wycaro series altogether. Carol is done with all of it. After tonight, she won’t have to put up with the likes of Margaux and her fake platitudes, or rabid fangirls who think Carol owes them another book.

    Carol glances at the clock as Margaux attempts to corral the attendees into a line for the book-signing portion of the evening. Forty minutes. Carol only has forty more minutes of this shit and then she’s free.

    For good.

    She brushes her hair back behind her ears and smooths down her emotions, shelving thoughts of Helen for the rest of the evening. Putting on a show in public has been Carol’s bread and butter for the past thirty years. She can push herself through for the next forty minutes—keep up the facade for one more night.

    Time drags.

    Carol swears she’s signed a hundred books, but another furtive glance at the clock reveals it’s only been fifteen minutes. Maybe this is hell—or it’s purgatory and Carol’s penance will be to live out her final night on public display over and over until the end of time itself.

    Carol’s suspicions are confirmed when the next person in line is Maureen, a longtime fan and the president of the New Mexico chapter of Wycarians. Carol forces a smile she doesn’t feel as Maureen gushes at her like it’s the first time they’ve met.

    It’s only after Carol signs her book that Maureen cradles it to her chest and asks, in a small voice, “Raban is alive, right?”

    Carol swallows her initial response. She wants to tell Maureen that people die. That’s life. You can love someone with your whole heart and it won’t matter. They’ll still up and die on you.

    She offers Maureen a conspiratorial smile, drops her voice to a stage whisper, and says, “Page 218. You won’t be disappointed.”

    Maureen beams at her like Carol’s just handed her the whole world.

    She asks about the next book, and Carol makes some noncommittal noise. Carol doesn’t have the heart to tell Maureen there won’t be another book. She hasn’t made the announcement yet, and her publisher still thinks they can talk her into signing a new three-book deal.

    They can’t. Carol’s made up her mind.

    Between the revenue from her four Wycaro books and the money from Helen’s life insurance, Carol is financially stable.

    Another ten excruciating minutes drag by when Carol’s phone vibrates in her pocket. She ignores it, letting it go to voicemail, and focuses on getting through the last dozen people in the queue still waiting for her to sign their books.

    The ordeal is finally over after Carol signs the last book and poses for a few group photographs with Maureen and the rest of the New Mexico Wycarians. Carol’s grateful the publisher scheduled the last stop of the tour in her hometown of Albuquerque so she can head home to her own bed after six weeks on the road.

    Carol dodges Margaux’s invitation to join her and some friends for a drink at a trendy wine bar downtown. The younger woman’s hand lingers on Carol’s arm for a beat too long as she bats her eyelashes at her.

    Carol isn’t quite sure how she keeps her jaw from dropping to the floor. Despite not even realising Margaux was into women, she’s more than twice the girl’s age. She and Helen were already decorating their first shitty apartment together when Margaux was in utero.

    She can’t lie, a small part of her is flattered by the attention; but Carol hasn’t been with anyone other than Helen since she was younger than Margaux. She begs off, making an excuse about being tired. There’s a little extra swagger in her hips as she walks to her car.

    She fishes in the pockets of her yellow leather jacket—a favourite of hers and a gift from Helen for her forty-fifth birthday—and stumbles upon her cell phone and the waiting voicemail. She plays it, pressing the phone to her ear with her shoulder as she unlocks her car and climbs behind the wheel.

    “Hi, Ms. Sturka. This is Maxwell Clifton. I am the director of the Clifton Fertility Institute…”

    Carol frowns at the unfamiliar voice. She recognises the name of the clinic, though. It’s the place she and Helen used to freeze Carol’s eggs fifteen years ago, back when they’d talked about having kids—before Wycaro exploded overnight, throwing Carol into the public eye.

    Helen had always regretted not having children. It had never seemed the right time. They’d kept saying “next year,” until suddenly they’d been too old for kids, and then there was no “next year” for Helen.

    Carol closes her eyes, fighting off a wave of melancholy that threatens to submerge her as she dwells on thoughts of Helen—of the life her premature death robbed them of.

    Carol hadn’t been sure she’d make it a month without Helen. Nearly two years on and here she is, hanging on by a thread.

    She listens to the rest of the message, the director of the clinic requesting she call him back at her earliest convenience. Carol sighs. It’s probably some bullshit about retaining her eggs. She contemplates not calling back. She can’t even bring herself to clear out Helen’s wardrobe, never mind contemplate getting rid of her eggs.

    “Fuck it.” Carol calls the clinic back. A quick glance at the digital display on the dashboard tells her it’s already almost eight o’clock. She doubts anyone will answer, but then it was already after seven when the director called. It seems like odd hours for a fertility clinic to keep—and even stranger for the director to be calling her directly.

    “Ms. Sturka, thank you so much for calling back.”

    Carol jumps as the director actually picks up. She’s even more surprised when the director recognises her number.

    “Uh, hi. No problem… What is this about?”

    “My name is Maxwell Clifton. I’m the director here at Clifton Fertility Clinic. You may remember, some time ago you and your husband—”

    “Wife.” Carol practically growls the word, jaw clenched tight. She’s already on edge from the unexpected phone call. The fact that this Clifton guy seems to have no idea what he’s talking about doesn’t help.

    “I beg your pardon?”

    “Wife,” Carol repeats. “My wife and I chose your clinic to freeze my eggs—” She stops, considers whether she should be supplying the stranger with any information. What if it’s some kind of scam? Carol holds her breath, waiting for the expected request for money.

    “Oh.” On the other end of the line, Clifton sounds flustered. He recovers quickly. “My apologies, Ms. Sturka. The Clifton Fertility Clinic actually sponsored a float in last year’s Pride—”

    “What is this about?” Carol demands, tone short as she loses what little patience she still had with him. She pinches the bridge of her nose, feeling a headache coming on. She doesn’t need this shit right now.

    All Carol wants to do is go home, crash into bed, and stay there for a solid month while her social battery recovers from six weeks of touring.

    There’s a silence on the other end that stretches so long Carol thinks he might have hung up on her. She frowns. “Are you still there?”

    “Oh, yes. Yes. Sorry.” Clifton clears his throat and the noise is like nails on a chalkboard for Carol. She’s seconds away from hanging up on him when he finally answers. “Um, Ms. Sturka, I’m calling about an unfortunate misunderstanding—”

    Carol covers her face with a hand and stifles a groan. What the fuck did these people do?

    “Look, I don’t have time for this! If you lost my eggs, or let them defrost or something, it’s fine. I don’t need ’em anymore. So you can—”

    “They were implanted in another woman.”

    Clifton speaks quickly, rushing so fast to make his confession that the words almost blend together. The phone drops from Carol’s hand, hitting the car seat with a thump before falling to the floor. She sits in stunned silence, struggling to process what the hell she just heard.

    “Ms. Sturka? Hello? Ms. Sturka? Carol?”

    Clifton’s distant voice comes from the phone on the floor. Carol stares down at the lump of metal and plastic like it’s a venomous snake. She reaches warily for it.

    “Ms. Sturka, are you still there?”

    “Uh… yeah.” Carol’s voice trembles as she brings the phone back to her ear. “I’m sorry, what did you just say?”

    “Ms. Sturka, I understand how distressing this—”

    “You understand?” Carol scoffs, her voice going two octaves higher as panic floods her chest. “You understand? I’m sorry, were your eggs stolen and put in some random lady?”

    “Uh, well… no,” Clifton admits, his discomfort at the question clear. “But—”

    “But nothing!” Carol snaps, shock giving way to anger. “When the fuck did this happen? How did it happen?”

    “There was an unfortunate mix-up by a technician. I can promise you the employee in question has been fired and a full investigation is being—”

    “Who the fuck got my eggs?” Carol wants answers, not excuses. She feels her blood boiling in her veins, heart hammering in her chest, and she wants nothing more than the ground to open up beneath her and swallow her whole.

    Carol hasn’t felt like this since Helen died.

    “Unfortunately, I can’t give out that information under HIPAA—”

    “HIPAA?” Carol squawks, her voice shrill as anger and panic compete for dominance. “I’m pretty fucking sure putting my eggs in someone else counts as a fucking HIPAA violation!”

    “Ms. Sturka, I understand you’re upset—”

    “Stop saying that! Stop saying you understand!”

    “I un—” Clifton stops himself this time. With a sigh, he finally sounds human as he says, “The eggs were implanted eight weeks ago. We only picked up on the error a few days ago during our quarterly inventory review.”

    Eight weeks. Carol feels a flood of relief. They’re talking about something the size of a grape, not a living, breathing child that could show up on her doorstep.

    “Okay.” Carol takes another couple of deep breaths, wrestling her temper back under control. “Okay, so there’s still time to fix this, right? I mean, they can terminate the pregnancy, right?”

    “Uh…” Clifton doesn’t offer a response right away. Carol can hear him shifting in his seat, the leather creaking in protest under him. “The mother actually wants to continue with the pregnancy.”

    “What? What kind of crazy person wants another woman’s baby? That’s insane!” Carol rambles, forgetting she’s talking to a man whose business it is to help desperate women who can’t conceive naturally.

    She doesn’t stop to consider how long the woman who received her eggs may have been trying to get pregnant, or what heartbreak she might have endured. Carol doesn’t let herself think about that stuff.

    She can’t.

    Her anger is the only thing keeping her going right now. “I want to talk to her. The lady who got my eggs. I want to meet her.”

    She forces herself to take a deep breath as the dashboard swims in and out of focus in front of her. Her chest tightens and she’s pretty sure she’s thirty seconds away from a full-blown panic attack.

    “I can certainly pass along the request, but she’s under no obligation to meet with you.”

    Carol can’t believe what she’s hearing. “No obligation? No ob—There’s a fucking part of me growing inside of her as we speak! My eggs! My DNA! So yes, there’s an obligation!”

    Carol can hear the discomfort in Clifton’s voice as he says, “Unfortunately, under New Mexico law, you have no legal say in the matter, regardless of your genetic material being used.”

    “Oh yeah? Well fuck you and fuck her too!” Carol snaps. “I am going to sue both your asses off!” She hangs up, her temper finally reaching breaking point.

    Carol tosses her phone aside, glaring at it like it’s personally responsible for impregnating a stranger with her eggs. “FUCK!” she screams at the top of her lungs, the sound reverberating around the car as she slams her balled fists against the steering wheel. “Fuck, fuck, fuck!”

    Carol retreats to the safety of the nearest bar. It’s something of a dive, with dated decor, faded leather booths, and a floor her feet stick to as she walks across it toward the bar. Carol feels perfectly at home.

    She slides onto a stool and signals the bartender over. She’s a plump woman pushing sixty at best. Faded tattoos line her forearms, her skin wrinkled like aged leather.

    “What can I get you, doll?”

    Carol doesn’t hesitate. “Whisky. Top shelf.” She hasn’t drunk the hard stuff in over a year, not since she got blind drunk on the anniversary of Helen’s death and ended up having to get her stomach pumped. “Double.”

    “That kind of day, huh?” The older woman grins as she reaches back for a bottle of Colkegan single malt and—thankfully—a clean glass.

    “That kind of life,” Carol grumbles. She takes the glass and swallows it in one go. The liquor burns its way down her throat, making her wince. She’s out of practice.

    Well, there’s only one way to resolve that. “Can I get another?”

    “Sure, doll.” The bartender refills her glass with an overly generous pour and a wink. “For what it’s worth, whoever he is, he’s a dumbass.”

    Carol barks out a laugh before clamping her hand over her mouth. She wishes it were that simple.

    The bartender offers her a sympathetic smile before moving off to serve another patron, calling over her shoulder for Carol to give her a shout when she needs a top-up.

    Carol picks up her glass and retreats to the anonymity of one of the booths in the corner. The table has deep gouge marks along with about ten coats of lacquer, and whatever cushion the seats once had went out with the first Bush administration.

    It’s the kind of run-down place she and Helen used to love visiting while on tour. They’d made a competition of finding the seediest dive bars across the country.

    Carol leans forward, pressing the cold glass to her forehead with a sigh. She still can’t quite process the phone call she just had. Someone is out there right now, pregnant with Carol’s child.

    The child Helen had wanted so badly.

    Children had never really been on Carol’s list. She’d agreed to try mostly to please Helen. The first round of IVF hadn’t taken, though, and Carol had taken that as a sign. Helen had agreed to take some time before they tried again, but then Carol’s first Wycaro novel had taken off and their lives got too busy to think about children. Eventually Carol had hit an age where it wasn’t really an option anymore, and Helen had stopped bringing IVF up.

    Helen would have made a great mom. God knows she mothered Carol’s ass enough. Carol regrets not having been able to give Helen what she really wanted; but things turned out for the best. Helen is gone and no kid deserves to be stuck with Carol for a mother.

    Carol takes a long swig before setting the glass down and pulling her phone out of her jacket. She scrolls through her contacts until she lands on Davis Taffler.

    The man is an insufferable bore, and a Republican to boot, but he’s also the lawyer her publisher assigned to handle her last two DUIs. He got a mandatory jail sentence reduced to probation and an ignition interlock installed on her car. Helen had done most of the driving for the six months it was in place.

    “Carol?” Taffler greets her with surprise. It’s been three years since her last DUI. “Did you get in some trouble? Where are you? I’ll get you bailed out—”

    “No, I’m not in trouble, Davis. At least not that kind.” Carol sighs. She doesn’t know where to start. “Have you got five minutes?”

    “Of course.”

    Carol fills him in on the whole sorry situation—the clinic, her eggs, the other woman going ahead with the pregnancy. Taffler listens carefully. He doesn’t interrupt, just makes small affirming noises to let her know he’s still listening. Carol’s thankful he doesn’t comment when she mentions wanting the woman to terminate the pregnancy.

    “So we can sue, right?” Carol asks, hopeful.

    There’s a beat before Taffler answers. “We can certainly sue the clinic for negligence.”

    “And what about ending the pregnancy?”

    “Eh, that’s a little trickier. Under New Mexico law, you technically don’t have a leg to stand on.”

    “So I’ve been told,” Carol mutters. Then, louder: “Look, I need to meet this woman. She has to understand… She can’t have my kid. She just can’t. Fuck knows one of me is enough for the world.”

    Chapter 2

    Chapter Notes

     

    Zosia unlocks the front door of the restaurant before the New Mexico sun is even up, the key sticking for half a beat before the tumblers give way with a metallic click. The street outside is still cloaked in darkness, the kind that makes the world look washed out, like a painting left unfinished.

    Inside, the restaurant smells faintly of last night’s dinner service—warm and inviting.
    Zosia flicks on the lights one bank at a time. The dining room hums awake in stages. Thirty-two tables. Eight booths. The bar top she resurfaced herself last year. She’s the first one in, and was last out the night before.

    It’s how it’s been for six months now.

    The kitchen greets her with stainless steel and silence. Zosia ties her apron tight around her waist and pulls her long brown hair into a loose ponytail at the nape of her neck.

    The fridge compressors drone steadily in the background; the walk-in exhales cold air when she opens it for a quick check of the inventory. She scans crates of heirloom tomatoes, bundled herbs wrapped in damp towels, sides of salmon resting on crushed ice.

    “Low on shallots,” she mutters to herself, already calculating the order in her head. She’ll write it down later.

    Zosia throws herself into her work because it’s easier than dwelling on her life right now. Work is measurable. You reduce a sauce, it thickens. You salt a soup, it blooms. You overwork a dough, it punishes you back. The rules are clear.

    Unlike everything else in Zosia’s life.

    She boots up the office computer long enough to send a produce order, adjust the weekend schedule, and confirm the fish delivery for Friday. By the time the next person shuffles in at six fifteen, Kusimayu yawning into her sleeve, Zosia has two stocks simmering and pastry dough resting under plastic wrap.

    “Morning, Chef,” Kusimayu greets her.

    “Morning.” Zosia greets the young waitress who doubles as the morning cleaner. Her two aunts work there too, one as a line cook and the other as a waitress. Zosia takes pride in paying them, and all of her staff, as much as she can afford while still making a living for herself. The restaurant feels more like a family to her than a business.

    She’s lucky in that it’s flourished in the last couple of years. The Albuquerque Journal, just last summer, described it as the “best kept secret in the city.” Bookings had exploded practically overnight.

    “Hey,” Zosia calls over her shoulder as the young woman heads toward the cleaning cupboard. “Have you had breakfast?”

    Kusimayu ducks her head with a bashful smile, which is an answer in and of itself. Zosia clucks her tongue and gives a shake of her head. “Come. Stir this stock for me and I’ll make you something. We can’t have you working on an empty stomach.”

    “Zosia, you don’t have to,” Kusimayu protests, but she comes over to take care of the stock like Zosia asked. Zosia grins at her. They have this argument almost daily. She knows the young woman’s family doesn’t have much, and Kusimayu chooses to skip breakfast because of it.

    Zosia insists on feeding her at every opportunity.

    Kusimayu offers her boss a warm smile as she watches Zosia flit around the kitchen, cracking eggs into a bowl, chopping up peppers and mushrooms, and adding it all to a skillet.

    Within five minutes, a fresh omelette sits in front of Kusimayu. “Thank you. You’ll make a great mother someday, with all your fussing.”

    Zosia touches a self-conscious hand to her stomach. She hasn’t told anyone about the pregnancy yet. At only eight weeks, she’s still a long way from showing. Zosia also knows, from the dozens of pregnancy books she’s read, that the first trimester is also the most dangerous. Saying it out loud right now seems too much of a jinx. “Perhaps,” she agrees, fighting back a smile.

    Zosia turns back to her prep table while Kusimayu eats, but the warmth that had flared in her chest a moment ago dims as quickly as it came.

    Three rounds of IVF.

    The first failed quietly. Zosia and her husband hadn’t even told anyone they’d tried. The second failure hit harder. At thirty-three, Zosia hadn’t been sure she had another attempt in her. Then the restaurant had taken off, and she and Malick had stopped talking about kids altogether.

    Now, at thirty-nine, Zosia is finally pregnant. She’d cried when the two lines appeared on the pregnancy test in her bathroom at home. She’d pressed her forehead to the cool tile and laughed through tears because finally, finally her body had cooperated. Her husband was gone, but Zosia wouldn’t be alone.

    Joy had lived in her for exactly a month. A month of panicking every time she felt a twinge in her stomach or a careless busboy knocked into her front.

    Then came the call from the clinic. There had been a mistake. They’d used someone else’s eggs. The embryo growing inside her carried her husband’s sperm, but nothing of hers.

    Zosia slices carrots into uniform batons with mechanical precision. The knife moving cleanly through flesh, blade whispering against wood.

    Not hers.

    And yet—hers. Growing inside her body.

    In time, more bodies file in—Michael on the grill, Tara on garde manger. The dishwasher clocks in with a clatter. The kitchen fills with the percussion of knives against boards, the hiss of gas burners catching flame, and dishes clinking.

    At nine, Craig—her sous chef—pushes through the back door with a gust of cold air and the scent of cigarette smoke clinging faintly to his coat. He starts apologising for being over an hour late.

    Zosia waves his excuses off. “Yeah, yeah. Taste the veal stock before you touch anything,” she replies, already slicing onions into precise translucent crescents. “I think it’s missing something, but I can’t figure it out.”

    Halfway through the morning, satisfied the kitchen won’t fall apart without her for five minutes, Zosia wipes her hands on her apron and retreats to the office under the pretense of checking invoices.

    The email notification sits at the top of her inbox. Clifton Fertility Institute. It had been there, waiting for her, after work last night. Zosia had put off clicking on it until now.

    Her pulse stutters as she clicks. Her eyes quickly scan over the contents of the email, picking out the most important parts. ‘We have informed the biological egg donor of the error… She has requested a meeting.’

    Zosia leans back in her chair, the vinyl creaking beneath her. She has requested a meeting. The woman. The one whose DNA is knitting itself cell by cell inside her uterus. The one who is, by blood at least, her child’s mother.

    Zosia presses her palm to her stomach again. The gesture is firmer this time. This is her child. No matter what a lab report says. No matter whose chromosomes the baby has. She’ll raise it and love it with her whole heart.

    But Zosia cannot stop the questions that come to her.

    Is the other woman pregnant too? Did they implant one of Zosia’s embryos in her? Could there be another baby out there—genetically hers—growing in someone else?

    Zosia is an only child.

    She thinks of her home country. Poland—gray apartment blocks and the sharp smell of vodka on her father’s breath. His voice filling rooms whether he was shouting or not. Her mother’s silence, heavier than his fists.

    She had left at nineteen with a single suitcase and never looked back. She’d been utterly alone from that moment on, making her own way in the world— until she’d met her husband. Malick. He’d been the first person who stayed. The first person who made “home” feel like something comforting instead of a jail cell.

    Malick is gone now, and Zosia is the only adult left standing between the child growing inside of her and the world. She’s already made a silent vow to herself to be better than her own parents were—though, Zosia’s parents set that bar admittedly low.

    Zosia’s thoughts drift to her future child’s potential medical history. The inadvertent egg donor is a mystery to her. She wonders about allergies. Heart conditions. Cancer markers. The only responsible thing to do is to meet the woman. Sooner rather than later.

    The office door swings open without a knock and Koumba Diabaté saunters in like he owns the place. He’s an immigrant to the US, just like Zosia. He, and his younger brother Malick, moved to the states from their native Mauritania more than twenty years ago.

    Zosia had first met Malick eight years ago, when she’d been a sous chef in another restaurant. He’d been the floor manager, and the chemistry between the two had been instant. Eventually, he’d introduced Zosia to his older brother—the charming Koumba. He’d recognised Zosia’s talents in the kitchen and had helped the couple fund their dream of opening their own restaurant. As such, he was a thirty percent stakeholder in the business.

    Both men were tall and lean, blessed with thick, curly black hair and rich brown skin. The two were polar opposites beside that, though. Malick quiet, reserved, while Koumba the life and soul of any room he walked in to. Malick wore his hair short, cropped tight at the back and sides, while Koumba’s tight curls fall down past his ears. Koumba’s fashion sense borders on the eccentric, with flashy coloured suits and bow-ties, while Malick had been a jeans and a t-shirt kind of guy.

    “Why do you look like someone just told you the health inspector’s on his way?” Koumba Diabaté teases, earning an eye roll from Zosia.

    “I don’t,” Zosia says too quickly, eyes still scanning over the accounts.

    He studies her closely, dark eyes narrowing on her. “You do.”

    “I’m fine.”

    “Zosia, you can’t lie to me.”

    She exhales slowly. He will not let this go.

    “I’m pregnant,” she says. The word feels fragile as they hang in the air between them.

    Koumba blinks. “You… you went through with it?”

    “Yes.”

    “I thought the last round—”

    “It worked this time.”

    Relief flashes across his face. Then confusion. “So why do you look like someone died?” He winces at his poor choice of words. “Zosia, I—”

    Zosia folds her hands together on the desk. “Because they didn’t use my eggs.”

    Koumba pulls a face. “What does that even mean?”

    “It means,” she says carefully, “the baby inside me is half Malick’s and half another woman’s. They implanted someone else’s egg.”

    He stares at her like she’s speaking in another language. “How is that even possible?”

    “Negligence,” Zosia answers honestly. She hasn’t put much thought into the how. Her thoughts have been on just getting through the day. One day at a time, much the same as it has been for the last six months.

    “And this woman?” Koumba presses.

    “She wants to meet.”

    Her brother-in-law runs a hand over his mouth. “And what is it you want, Ukhti?” He addresses her with the term of endearment—meaning ‘my sister’ in his native Arabic. Koumba has called her sister since long before she and Malick were ever married. That hasn’t changed now he’s gone.

    Zosia hesitates. “I want this baby,” she says finally. “I fought for it. I am carrying it.” Her hand rests on her stomach again. “But what if she wants to be involved? What if she tries to take it? She froze her eggs for a reason, the same as me. What if she and her husband can’t have children and she wants this one?”

    Koumba steps closer, his hand finding her shoulder. “We’ll deal with whatever comes. One day at a time. You’ve been alone too long, Zosia. You don’t have to fight everything by yourself.”

    She almost laughs at that.

    People leave. Mothers check out. Husbands disappear. If the world has taught Zosia anything, it’s that the only person she can rely on is herself.

    “I’m not going anywhere.” Koumba promises. Zosia nods, but she does not let herself lean into that reassurance. Koumba excuses himself to go check on the front of house. As a shareholder of the business—as well as being the current manager—the success of the restaurant is just as important to him as it is to Zosia.

    Zosia turns her attention back to the email from the clinic. Better to face a storm than pretend the sky is clear. She replies before she can reconsider.

    I am willing to meet.

    Back in the kitchen, the rhythm has shifted toward lunch prep. Oil crackles in a sauté pan. Garlic hits heat and blooms sharp and fragrant, joining a chorus of other heavenly scents wafting through the kitchen.

    Zosia picks up her knife, but her thoughts are somewhere else—on a faceless woman who shares something intimate and invisible with her.

    Her grip slips. The blade kisses the side of her index finger. A bright, immediate sting. “Shit.” She drops the knife. Blood wells red against pale skin.

    Craig looks up. “Chef?”

    “I’m fine.” She wraps the finger in a towel, more annoyed at herself than anything else. Her phone vibrates in her apron pocket.

    “Chef,” Tara calls out, half-teasing, “no phones on the line.” Zosia shoots her a look that would send lesser mortals running. “Policy applies to everyone,” Tara adds with a grin, throwing Zosia’s own words back at her.

    Zosia offers the younger woman a begrudging smile as she steps away, pressing the phone to her ear with her shoulder and cradling her finger in the towel while she listens to the voicemail that was just left for her.

    Mr. Clifton, the clinic director, waffles on in his usual manner before he gets to the meat of the conversation. A meeting has been arranged for two o’clock tomorrow. Right in the middle of dinner prep.

    Zosia closes her eyes briefly. She hasn’t missed a single dinner prep in six months. She can’t afford to be away from the restaurant during that crucial part of the day, but she needs the answers to her questions—before she loses her mind.

    Zosia shoots off a quick email to the director in response, confirming she can make the appointment. No getting out of it now.

    She unwraps her finger and inspects the cut. It’s already stopped bleeding. Shallow. Manageable.

    “Back to work,” she sighs to herself, heading back into the chaos of the kitchen. It roars around her, alive and demanding, distracting her from her thoughts.

    Tomorrow she will meet the woman whose blood runs through the child growing in her womb.

    Today, she has forty covers for lunch and a stock that needs fixing. Zosia throws herself headfirst into her work—the same thing she’s been doing for the last six months.

     

    —

     

    The Clifton Fertility Institute sits in a low, beige building that looks like it was designed by someone afraid of offending anyone. Neutral brick. Neutral glass. Neutral landscaping. Even the sign out front is tasteful to the point of sterility.

    Carol hates it on sight.

    She steps out of the car in heels she hasn’t worn in months, smoothing down the skirt of a navy wrap dress that signals composed, affluent, entirely in control. The total opposite of how Carol feels right now. Her hair is styled, makeup understated but deliberate. She is the version of herself that does book readings in Barnes & Noble and makes small talk with people who dress up as pirates.

    Davis Taffler adjusts his tie beside her, briefcase tucked under one arm. “Let me do most of the talking,” he murmurs. He’s represented Carol enough to know just how easily her mouth can get her into trouble.

    “Yeah. That’s never been my strong suit,” Carol replies flippantly, earning a glare from her counsel.

    Inside, the air smells faintly of antiseptic and air freshener. The receptionist smiles with too many teeth and ushers them toward a glass-walled conference room.

    The other woman is already there. Alone. No lawyer. No husband. No entourage of righteous indignation like Carol. She rises when they enter, and Carol’s first, unhelpful thought is: Oh.

    Tall. Dark hair pulled back in a low ponytail. Sharp jaw, high cheekbones, the kind of bone structure a Renaissance painter would weep over. A light touch of makeup. She’s strikingly beautiful, and not at all what Carol was expecting.

    She’s wearing black trousers and a cream blouse with the sleeves rolled to her elbows. If Carol didn’t know any better, she might have mistaken her for the director of the clinic, not a client.

    Maxwell Clifton clears his throat from the head of the table and he is everything Carol expected. Fat and balding, stuffed in to a suit too tight across the chest for him. He indicates for Carol and Taffler to take a seat. “Ms. Sturka. Ms. Diabaté. Thank you both for coming.”

    Zosia nods once. “Of course.” Her voice is low. Calm. There’s a faint accent Carol can’t quite place. Something distinctly European.

    Clifton folds his hands, attempting to show contrition. “I would like to begin, again, by apologising for the distress this unfortunate error has caused both of you.”

    Carol smiles. It’s the tight kind of smile she directs at her editor when they suggest she remove an entire chapter from one of her books. “An apology,” she says evenly, “frankly, is bullshit.” The smile cracks on the last word.

    Taffler leans forward before she can continue. “We intend to pursue litigation for negligence and emotional damages,” he says smoothly. “My client entrusted this clinic with her genetic material, and that trust was violated.”

    Clifton nods as if he expected nothing less. “I understand your position. However, as outlined in Section 12B of the consent agreement signed at the time of cryopreservation—”

    He slides a copy of the contract across the table for Carol and Taffler to inspect. Carol recognises Helen’s looping signature—as witness—beside her own. She reaches out, traces her fingers along the familiar lines.

    “—the clinic is absolved of liability in the event of accidental embryo misallocation, provided no gross misconduct occurred.”

    Carol stares up at Clifton in disbelief. “Accidental embryo misallocation,” she repeats flatly. It’s not a sentence that exactly rolls off the tongue. “How often does that happen for you to have a fucking clause about it?”

    Clifton stiffens. “It is exceedingly rare.”

    “Obviously not rare enough!” Carol snaps, cheeks flaring with anger.

    Beside her, Taffler’s hand finds her arm as he murmurs sternly, “Carol.”

    Across the table, Zosia has barely moved. She’s watching. Listening. Her hands folded lightly over her midsection. Carol’s gaze drops there.

    Eight weeks. A cluster of cells the size of a grape. Carol’s cells.

    And this woman is sitting there like she’s above it all, watching with careful eyes. “Why aren’t you mad, too?” Carol blurts out, unable to help herself. “You’re just sitting there! Like, like they mixed up your order at the drive-through!”

    Zosia’s brow furrows slightly. “I assure you, Ms. Sturka, I am just as upset as you are.”

    “Really? Because you look pretty damn calm to me!”

    “I’m trying to be rational about all of this.”

    “Rational?” Carol laughs, sharp and humorless. She hates that the other woman looks so poised. So put together. “There’s a part of me growing inside of you! What the fuck is rational about that?”

    Zosia’s hand tightens protectively over her stomach. “This baby is also a part of my husband.”

    Her words land with weight. Carol leans back, crossing her arms over her chest like a child sulking. “The egg was mine. That should count for something. I should get a say in what happens next!”

    Zosia’s eyes flash. “And what say would that be?”

    Carol hesitates only a second, despite the weight of the woman’s glare on her. “Whether… whether this pregnancy continues.”

    Silence drops in the room like a guillotine. Clifton shifts uncomfortably. Taffler mutters, “Let’s tread carefully here, Carol.”

    Carol doesn’t. “Are you even here legally?” she demands, the words out before she can filter them.

    Zosia goes very still. “I beg your pardon?”

    “I’m just saying,” Carol pushes, heat rising in her chest, “if we’re talking about rights—”

    “Carol,” Taffler hisses.

    “I’m not being an asshole!” she insists, even as she hears how she sounds. “But my rights should count for something. I should get a say in this too. Right?”

    Zosia says something under her breath. It’s quick. Sharp. Not in English. It gets Carol’s back up even further.

    “What was that?” Carol snaps.

    Zosia meets her eyes without blinking. “I said you are a bitch.”

    Clifton inhales sharply. Taffler closes his eyes, expecting the worse.

    Carol feels the word like a slap to the face. “Listen, lady!” she says, voice climbing. “The best thing here is for you to get rid! You and your husband can try again. You’ve done it before. Where is he, anyway? Does he even know you’re planning to raise someone else’s kid? I mean, where is he? He’s too much of a flake to even show up today, never mind raise a kid—”

    Carol cuts herself off. She knows she’s crossed a line. Zosia’s face changes. Not anger. Not outrage. Something quieter. More devastating.

    “My husband,” Zosia says carefully, “died six months ago.”

    The room tilts. Carol’s pulse stutters in her ears.

    “We tried for three years,” Zosia continues, her voice steady in a way that feels earned. She stares Carol down. “Round after round. Nothing worked… and then Malick died. There was enough of his sperm frozen for one more attempt.” Her hand rests firmly over her stomach. “When it worked, it felt like a miracle.”

    Carol can’t look at her. She looks down at her hands on the table, wishing the ground would open and swallow her whole. She knows the grief the other woman feels all too well.

    “I thought,” Zosia goes on, “that I would always have a part of Malick with me in this child. And then they told me the egg wasn’t mine.” Zosia finishes, “And I was angry, God was I angry, but now—now my only concern is making sure this child is healthy and happy.”

    Carol’s throat feels tight. Heat floods her face, not anger this time.

    Shame.

    “I…” She swallows. “I’m sorry.” The words feel inadequate and entirely too late.

    Zosia exhales slowly. Carol realises she was wrong. Zosia isn’t calm, not really. They’re just in the eye of the storm right now, and the wind and the rain will be back any moment. “I came today because it was the responsible thing. I wanted information. I wanted to know your medical history, your background. And I felt”—she hesitates—“an obligation to let you decide if you wanted to be part of this child’s—my child’s— life.”

    Carol looks up at that, just in time to see Zosia’s face scrunch up. “That was a mistake. Obviously.” Zosia says as she stands, intending to leave.

    Clifton half-rises. “Ms. Diabaté—” he tries to call to her, but Zosia is already walking toward the door.

    Carol watches her go, lets the door shut behind her for all of three seconds before she’s pushing back her chair to follow her. She leaves her lawyer to deal with Clifton.

    The parking lot is sun-bleached and unforgiving as Carol steps out of the temperature-controlled climate of the clinic. She misses the air conditioning immediately as the heat shimmers off the asphalt. Zosia is halfway to a blue Toyota Corolla when Carol calls her name.

    “Ms. Diabaté!”

    Zosia turns, expression guarded. Carol slows as she approaches, heels clicking too loudly on concrete, and raises her hands in mock surrender.

    “I was an ass in there,” she says without preamble. “I’m sorry. It’s… kind of my default setting.”

    Zosia’s mouth twitches despite herself.

    “It—this is all just out of left field. Trust me, I never expected to have a mini me running around the place. It’s kind of a shock.”

    “You froze your eggs,” Zosia points out evenly. Carol has to remind herself it’s not an attack.

    She exhales slowly. “My wife wanted kids,” she says, pointedly looking anywhere but at Zosia.

    Zosia’s gaze softens a fraction, picking up on Carol’s use of the past tense. “She… passed?”

    Carol nods stiffly. “Two years ago. So I get it. I do… wanting a piece of them to stay behind. To feel like you’re not alone.”

    Zosia looks down at her stomach, then back at Carol. “I am sorry about your wife.”

    “Thank you. I’m sorry about your husband.”

    They stand there, the space between them still charged but no longer quite as hostile.

    “Can we…” Carol gestures vaguely. “Talk? Somewhere neutral? No pricks in suits and their lame apologies?”

    Zosia studies her for a long beat. “Sure. As long as it’s nowhere within five miles of a Planned Parenthood.”

    Carol blinks, unsure how to take that. Zosia’s lips curve—just slightly—and Carol lets out a nervous laugh. “Jesus. I thought you were serious.”

    “I was. A little.” Zosia grins, a protective hand still over her relatively flat stomach.

    “There’s a bar around the corner,” Carol says. “I promise not to order you a gin with a side of metal coat hanger.”

    Zosia arches a brow. She considers it, then nods once. “Sure.”

    Carol gestures toward the sidewalk. “After you, Ms. Diabaté.”

    “You can call me Zosia.”

    “Oh. Uh, I’m Carol.” Carol holds her hand out and Zosia awkwardly shakes it before they fall into step beside each other, keeping a polite distance wide enough to drive a truck through. Ahead of them is a bar, and a conversation neither of them quite knows how to have.

    Zosia is adamant about carrying the pregnancy to term, and Carol needs to find a way to make peace with that. Zosia’s earlier offer—of Carol being in the child’s life in some capacity or other—keeps ringing in her ears with every step they take toward the bar and the inevitable conversation.

    Carol glances sideways at Zosia, quietly taking the measure of the other woman. She’s striking, but there’s much more to her than that. Upon first encountering Zosia, Carol had dismissed her as diminutive; but there’s a core of steel to Zosia Diabaté.

    Not to mention she’s carrying something that belongs to both of them now—whether Carol wants it or not.

    Chapter End Notes

    Thanks for reading, and for all the comments and kudos. The support is always appreciated when I start a new fic and it definitely helps encourage me to write more. I'm not really a 'write by a schedule' person but I'm hoping to update at least once a week, and a lot more if I get the time.

    - Tan

    Chapter 3

    The bar they find themselves in is quiet. It’s the kind of upscale place that serves fancy cocktails and only offers beer in bottles—nothing on tap. It’s the middle of the afternoon, but the windows are shuttered and covered over with heavy drapes, casting the bar in the dim, cosy light of the artificial candles set out on the tables.

    The bar is practically empty, with only a scattering of customers, most of whom sit at the bar. Carol doesn’t exactly want her and Zosia’s conversation overheard, so she chooses a booth at the back.

    The server pounces on them the moment they’re seated. She’s a young thing, barely old enough to drink herself, and has the eager enthusiasm of someone on drugs. She smiles from ear to ear as she addresses both women at once.

    “Hi there, what can I get for you folks?” she asks, a thick southern twang to her accent. Texan, if Carol had to guess.

    Carol’s first instinct is to tell the girl to give them a damn minute, but Zosia cuts in before she can get a single word out. Her eyes spot the girl’s name tag.

    “Hi Brittany, we’d just like a minute with the menus, is that okay?” Zosia’s tone is light and breezy, her smile sincere. She’s good with people—unlike Carol. She’s spent the last decade faking it on book tours, making small talk with fans, but that was with Helen’s support. She’s no good at it on her own.

    Zosia doesn’t need any help, though. She’s a people person. She manages Brittany with ease, buying them some time to actually pick up the damn menus.

    “Sure thing, you just holler when y’all are ready.” Brittany beams, touching Zosia’s arm before she goes. Carol quirks an eyebrow at the exchange.

    “Well, she’s pretty friendly.”

    Zosia laughs it off. “She’s probably making minimum wage. Smile. Make eye contact. Physical touch. All of it helps increase the size of the tip.”

    Carol is impressed. “You sound like you know what you’re talking about.”

    Zosia shrugs. “I waited tables in college. Twenty years in the service industry, you pick up a thing or two.”

    “What do you do now?” Carol asks, making small talk as she scans the drinks menu. In truth, she’s putting off the inevitable—trying to avoid discussing their current situation.

    “I’m a chef. I work at—well, I own Per Se—”

    “That restaurant down in Old Town?” Carol interrupts, her face lighting up with awe as Zosia nods. “You own that? Wow, small world. Helen loved it there. We were at the grand opening, what… four years ago?”

    Zosia nods again, her smile now a permanent fixture. It’s clear she takes pride in her work. “That’s right. I’m sorry I don’t remember you. It was quite a hectic night and I barely got out of the kitchen. What is it that you do, Carol?”

    “Uh, well, I was a writer.”

    “Was?”

    “Yeah. I just published my fourth book, but, uh… I’m done with that now.”

    “What is it you write? Anything I’d know?” Zosia leans in, genuinely curious. The battery-operated candle in the middle of the table between them casts her face in a soft, flattering light.

    Carol laughs, the sound awkward and strangled as she shakes her head. “No. Probably not.” There’s no way she’s admitting to the beautiful, successful, serious woman in front of her that she writes pirate smut for a living. She changes the topic. “Do you know what you’re having?”

    “Oh.” Zosia’s hand finds her stomach. “It’s still too early to tell—”

    “No. Sorry. I meant what are you having to drink?”

    “Right. Of course. Sorry.” Zosia chuckles at the misunderstanding, the sound light and melodic. Carol finds she likes it. “I’ll go for a virgin Shirley Temple.”

    Since Zosia’s going with a cocktail—albeit a non-alcoholic one—Carol decides to push the boat out and settles on an Old Fashioned. Zosia gets the server’s attention and Brittany comes bouncing over, eager as a puppy to take their order.

    Her eyes linger on Zosia’s face too long while she listens to what they want, whereas she barely bats an eyelid at Carol. “She’s totally into you.” Carol scoffs.

    “What?” Zosia looks taken aback, and Carol’s suddenly reminded that she isn’t Helen. She’s too used to mostly having her wife’s company. Sometimes she forgets not everyone is gay.

    “Uh, sorry, I—”

    “Carol, she looks like she’s half my age!” Zosia laughs again, her eyes twinkling with amusement. Carol laughs along with her, some of the tension easing from her shoulders.

    Both women just about hold in their laughter as Brittany comes back with their drinks, her eyes still all over Zosia. “There you go, darlin’.” she says, placing Carol’s Old Fashioned down in front of Zosia by mistake.

    “Whoa, that one’s mine.” Carol reaches across the table, ignoring Brittany’s glare, and swaps the drinks around so the non-alcoholic one sits in front of Zosia. “No alcohol for her. She’s pregnant… It’s mine.” Carol shoots a wink at Brittany, who suddenly looks flustered as she stutters an apology and can’t get away from their table fast enough, leaving the two older women to break out in another fit of laughter.

    “Carol! That poor girl,” Zosia chastises once they’ve settled down and she’s gotten her breath back, but she’s still grinning nonetheless. Carol finds herself smirking back at her as she picks up her drink. Things feel a lot easier between them compared to an hour ago in the stuffy clinic. Carol wishes they could stay like this, but she knows they have to discuss the embryo in the room.

    “So… you definitely want to have the baby?” Carol asks, careful to keep her tone level. The last thing she wants is for them to descend into another shouting match, like in the clinic.

    “I do.” Zosia’s answer comes without a moment’s hesitation. “I know the situation is… unorthodox. But Malick and I tried for years to get pregnant. This baby… it feels like a miracle.”

    “Okay.” Carol nods, her hands clasped together on the table. It’s easier to keep her cool now she knows Zosia’s story—that she’s not some fruit loop having someone else’s baby on a whim.

    Encouraged, Zosia continues. “I looked into the law, and in New Mexico you have no legal claim to the child—and no obligation either. I won’t be coming at you for child support or anything like that. All I’d like from you is some family background, medical history, things like that.”

    “Okay.” Carol repeats. “Okay, I can do that.”

    “Great.” Zosia beams, and Carol can see the relief on her face. “And, just for the record, if you want more—if you want to be in this child’s life—I can work with that too.”

    “Yeah, I’m not sure about that.” Carol gives a nervous laugh. She picks up her drink and takes a long swig. Her cocktail is supposed to be sipped. The bitters hit the back of her throat and she winces, puckering her lips. “I’m not great with kids.”

    Zosia shifts, her chin coming to rest against her palm as she studies Carol carefully. “But you froze your eggs? You must have wanted them at some point, right?”

    Carol shrugs, idly twirling the straw around in her drink. “Helen wanted kids… She would have been an amazing mom. God knows she used to pick up after me enough.” She lets out a hollow laugh, the ache in her chest growing stronger.

    “You didn’t want children, yet you still went ahead and froze your eggs for her?” Zosia doesn’t mean it as an accusation, but it still stings like one. Carol takes a moment to answer, working up to it. She’s not in the habit of airing her dirty laundry to strangers, but Zosia is surprisingly easy to talk to.

    She clears her throat. “Uh, Helen had this thing… a condition. Primary Ovarian Insufficiency. Her, uh, her ovaries basically stopped producing eggs in her late twenties. We didn’t know about it until we first started looking in to IVF.” Carol’s head is bowed, her attention fixed on swirling the straw, but she can feel Zosia’s eyes burning into her. “We uh… we had to use my eggs instead.”

    “Did you ever try to have them implanted?” Zosia asks, voice soft.

    “Yeah.” Carol sighs, finally looking up again. She almost regrets it. Zosia’s big brown eyes seem to stare right through her. “It didn’t take. We were going to try again, but then my book got picked up, and the next, and it just never seemed like the right time.”

    Carol had never felt like it was the right time. Helen had pressed, year after year, until finally she’d stopped asking. Stopped bringing it up. Slowly resigned to the fact that they would never be parents.

    Carol blinks back the tears she feels welling up in her eyes. She absolutely refuses to cry in front of a stranger. “It was probably for the best. Any kid of mine would be fucked up for sure.”

    Carol shoots a look at Zosia’s stomach as reality hits her. The life blossoming in the other woman’s stomach is her child. Her mouth suddenly feels dry. She’s lightheaded but takes another drink anyway. “Fuck, sorry.”

    Zosia waves her apology off. “I’m sure you have your reasons for feeling like that,” she says, her voice soft and gentle, like she’s handling Carol with kid gloves. “Personally, I believe in nurture over nature. My father… he was a piece of work. That never stopped me wanting children of my own, though. It never made me feel like I wasn’t worthy of having them.”

    “Good for you.” Carol can’t help the bitter edge to her voice.

    Picking up on the shifting tone, Zosia changes the conversation, asking Carol how her drink is as she sips at her own. “It’s not bad.” Carol shrugs. She’s had better.

    The conversation trails off after that, some of the earlier awkwardness returning. Carol is actually grateful when she empties her glass and Zosia pushes her own—still half full—aside before catching Brittany’s attention to ask for the check.

    The younger woman isn’t nearly as bubbly this time around as she comes over, silently sliding a small plastic tray with the check on it between the pair with an air of impatience before stalking back off.

    “I think Brittany’s pissed you’re off the menu.” Carol teases, breaking the tense silence between them as she reaches for the check. Zosia reaches for it at the same time, their fingers brushing.

    “Sorry,” Zosia apologises, pulling her hand back with a bashful smile. “Please, let me.”

    “No, no, I got it,” Carol insists. She pulls her wallet out of her jacket pocket, fishes out two twenty-dollar bills, and tosses them onto the tray. She hopes Brittany chokes on the tip.

    The two of them walk back to the clinic and their respective cars, and Carol finds herself disappointed the afternoon is over. She walks Zosia right to her car, a quirky little sedan that stands out against Carol’s bulky Chevy truck.

    “Oh, before you take off. Here.” Carol fishes her business card out of her wallet and hands it to Zosia. “It’s got my email address on it. You can message me with the questions you have about medical history and stuff.”

    “Thank you.” Zosia takes the card from her. She runs her fingers over the embossed lettering of Carol’s name before she slips it into her trouser pocket. “Well…” she says, her smile strained. They stand in an awkward silence, and it reminds Carol of the end of her first—and only—date since losing Helen.

    She’d been set up on a blind date by her neighbour, and eighteen months of loneliness had left her desperate enough to agree. The woman, her neighbour’s cousin and an investment banker in her late forties, had been pleasant enough. There just hadn’t been any spark between them. The woman hadn’t even made her smile, never mind laugh, and the conversation hadn’t felt easy—not like it had with Zosia.

    Eventually they’d finished the meal and made their way out to the parking lot. The goodbye had been awkward. Carol, believing they were on the same page about the lack of chemistry, had been completely floored when her date invited her back to her place. Carol had declined—and then stood waiting twenty minutes for her Uber to show up while the other woman angrily shouted about Carol wasting her time.

    Zosia isn’t shouting at her, but the tension between them feels thick and palpable as Carol rocks on the balls of her feet. She points over her shoulder at her truck. “I should get going.”

    “Of course,” says Zosia, her mouth tight. Carol can tell she wants to say something, but whatever it is doesn’t make it past her lips.

    “Okay then. Nice meeting you.” Carol walks the short distance to her truck and unlocks it with her key fob. She’s gripping the door handle but doesn’t pull the door open. Instead, she lets go and turns back, stalking back over to Zosia like a woman on a mission.

    “Don’t tell the kid about me. Okay?” Carol demands, catching Zosia off guard. Her lips part to respond, but Carol doesn’t let her get a word in. “As far as he or she needs to know, you’re their mom. They don’t need to know about me.”

    “If that’s what you want,” Zosia agrees.

    “It is.” Carol nods, lips pursed. “It’s for the best. For everyone involved.”

    “Carol, I—” Zosia starts to say, but her words are cut off by a sudden wave of pain in her abdomen. Her eyes widen, her purse falling off her shoulder as her hands immediately move to her stomach. She gasps, and another spasm of pain has her bent over.

    “Zosia?” Carol is at her side in an instant, expression one of concern. Her voice trembling, she asks, “What is it? What’s happening?”

    Zosia can’t get a word out. Her lips move, but the only sound that comes from her throat is an anguished groan. Eventually, she fights through the pain to be able to choke out, “Something’s wrong… I—”

    It feels like the worst cramp Zosia’s ever had. Hot, angry tears well up in the corners of her eyes as she refuses to let herself acknowledge what that might mean.

    Carol has no such illusions. Her first thought is to get out her cell phone and call an ambulance, but then she realises they’re only a few blocks from the hospital on Coors Boulevard. Carol knows they have an ER—it’s the one Helen died in.

    She pushes that morbid thought from her mind and focuses all of her attention on Zosia instead. “There’s a hospital nearby. Can you—do you think you could get into my truck?”

    Zosia shakes her head, teeth clenched in pain. “Fuck.” Carol curses. “Okay. New plan. Where are your keys?”

    Zosia points at her purse on the ground, still bent over in agony. Carol doesn’t hesitate. She rifles through the other woman’s purse until she comes across her car key. Seconds later she has the rear passenger door open and is helping Zosia onto the back seat of the little blue Corolla.

    Carol slams the door shut with a little too much force before scrambling to the other side of the car and getting into the driver’s side. Considerably shorter than Zosia, she has to pull the seat forward to comfortably reach the gas pedal.

    Carol has her foot down the whole way to the hospital. It’s a miracle they don’t get into any kind of accident. The car screeches to a stop in an ambulance bay.

    There’s a nurse on her break, smoking just beyond the front entrance. Carol snaps her fingers at her as she jumps out of the car. “Hey! You! We need help here!”

    The nurse just gawps as Carol grabs an empty wheelchair from out front and pushes it over to the car. “Hey!” Carol shouts at the nurse again as she wrenches the car door open. The nurse kicks into gear as Zosia stumbles into the chair, still bent over in pain and holding her stomach.

    Everything that happens next will later be a blur to Carol as the nurse helps her rush Zosia inside the hospital. The pregnant woman is whisked away from her and through a set of double doors with a sign that says ‘no unauthorised entry,’ leaving Carol standing alone in the crowded reception.

    Thoughts racing. Heart pounding. She’s terrified for a woman she’s only known for a few hours—and a baby she never wanted in the first place. Carol doesn’t know what she’ll do if anything happens to either of them.

    Chapter 4

    The waiting room smells like disinfectant and burnt coffee, and Carol has counted the ceiling tiles—twice. She’s been sitting under the same flickering fluorescent panel for three hours. Three hours of watching the automatic doors open and close. Three hours of listening to the television mounted in the corner mutter about weather fronts no one in this room cares about.

    Every fifteen minutes she stands, walks to the reception desk, and asks the same question.

    “Any update on Zosia Diabaté?”

    The receptionist gives her the same tight, apologetic smile every time. “I’m sorry, ma’am. I’m not authorized to share patient information.”

    Carol presses her lips together. Nods. Sits back down. Her knee won’t stop bouncing. Carol closes her eyes.

    This hospital.

    Of all the hospitals in the city.

    Two years ago, she sat in this same building while machines breathed for Helen. She learned the particular pitch of a cardiac monitor flatlining; and she walked out alone.

    The anniversary is next month.

    She hasn’t let herself think about it. She tries not to now, but the memory presses at the edges anyway—white sheets, the waxy pallor of Helen’s skin, Carol’s fingers gripping a hand that was already cooling.

    She swallows hard.

    Not now.

    A door opens somewhere down the corridor. A nurse steps into the waiting room and calls out, “Carol Sturka?” Carol is on her feet before the surname finishes echoing.

    “That’s me.”

    “Ms. Diabaté is stable,.”

    Carol’s chest loosens slightly. “Can I see her?” she asks.

    The nurse offers a reassuring smile. “She’s been asking for you. Come, this way.”

    Carol follows, heels clicking sharply against the polished linoleum. The corridor smells stronger of antiseptic. They pass curtained bays, glimpses of IV poles and pale faces.

    The nurse stops at one of the bays and pulls back the curtain. Zosia is propped up on a narrow bed, a hospital bracelet circling her wrist. She looks exhausted—dark shadows under her eyes—but the tightness that had contorted her face earlier is gone.

    Relief hits Carol so fast it almost knocks her sideways. The feeling is too strong. Too disorienting. Carol focuses on something tangible instead.

    “Why is she in a bay?” Carol demands. “Shouldn’t she be in a private room?”

    The nurse blinks, taken aback. “Your partner is being monitored, Ms. Sturka. This is appropriate for her condition.”

    Partner.

    The word rings in Carol’s ears, but she’s got more important things on her mind than correcting the nurse. Besides, what would she even say?

    We’re not dating, but I am the reason she’s pregnant.

    Before Carol can reply, Zosia reaches out, fingers brushing her wrist. “Carol.”

    Carol looks down. The other woman’s touch is foreign, yet familiar at the same time.

    “I’m fine,” Zosia says softly, her voice weary.

    The nurse gives them both a measured look, then pulls the curtain closed around the bay. “I’ll give you some privacy.” The thin fabric swishes shut. It does little to muffle the sounds of the emergency department beyond it.

    Carol’s free hand covers Zosia’s without much thought.

    “I was so worried,” Carol says, her voice coming out thinner than she intended. “You were white as a sheet. And I thought—” Carol stops. She doesn’t let herself give voice to some of the thoughts that have been running through her head over the last few hours.

    “I’m sorry,” Zosia murmurs.

    Carol scoffs. “You don’t have to apologize. I’m just glad you’re okay… both of you.” She pulls her hand back and takes a seat in the chair at Zosia’s bedside. “What happened?”

    Zosia shifts against the thin pillow. “I have PCOS. Polycystic Ovary Syndrome. It’s why I struggled to conceive naturally in the first place.”

    Carol frowns. It sounds serious. She still thinks Zosia should be in a private room, not in a side bay.

    Zosia carries on. “They’ve taken blood to check my hormone levels. And I’m waiting for an ultrasound.”

    “For what?” Carol feels a surge of panic.

    “They need to check the pregnancy isn’t ectopic.”

    Carol’s stomach drops. “Ectopic. That sounds bad.”

    “It can be,” Zosia admits evenly. “But the doctor thinks it’s unlikely. Cramping and light spotting are normal in early pregnancy.”

    “Normal?” Carol stares at her. “You looked like you were dying. That is not normal!”

    Zosia’s lips twitch faintly. She’s touched that Carol seems to care—not just about her, but also her unborn child.

    The curtain rustles before Carol can argue further. A different nurse steps in, wheeling a portable ultrasound machine.

    “Ms. Diabaté? We’re going to do that scan now.” She glances at Carol. “Would you like some privacy?”

    Zosia doesn’t hesitate. “It’s fine. She can stay. That is, if you want to? I just—I don’t really want to be alone.” Carol can see the worry in Zosia’s face. She answers by taking Zosia’s hand in her own and giving it a small squeeze.

    “Of course,” says Carol.

    Zosia squeezes back in thanks.

    The nurse pulls up Zosia’s shirt to just below her bra, exposing the faint swell of her stomach.

    Carol’s eyes betray her.

    The newly exposed skin is pale and smooth, and she catches a glimpse of the white lace of the cups of Zosia’s bra. Heat climbs Carol’s neck. She looks at the wall. The ceiling. Anywhere but at the other woman.

    Carol releases a breath she hadn’t realized she was holding.

    Guilt pricks at her chest.

    Earlier this afternoon, she’d told this woman to terminate the pregnancy. And for the last few hours, sitting under fluorescent lights, she’d been certain she’d somehow willed this scare into existence.

    The nurse adjusts the controls again. “Would you like to hear the heartbeat?”

    Zosia doesn’t hesitate. “Yes.”

    Carol blinks. “You can hear it already?”

    “Oh yes,” the nurse replies. “Fetal cardiac activity usually starts around week five or six.”

    She presses a button and the room fills with a rapid, rhythmic whooshing. Strong. Insistent. Fast.

    Carol’s brow furrows. “Should it be that quick?”

    “It’s perfect,” the nurse assures them. “One hundred and fifty beats per minute. Exactly where we want it.”

    One hundred and fifty.

    Carol listens to it, something primal and ancient stirring in her chest. It sounds like a galloping horse. Like life refusing to be quiet.

    The nurse hands Zosia a bunch of paper towels to wipe the gel from her stomach. “I’d recommend scheduling a transvaginal ultrasound with your OB-GYN. That’ll give you more precise measurements and a due date.”

    “I’ll call first thing,” Zosia says, her eyes still glued to the image on the screen.

    The nurse nods. She switches off the machine with an apologetic smile and slips out of the bay with it, the curtain swaying closed behind her.

    The sudden quiet feels intimate.

    Carol takes the damp paper towels from Zosia once she’s finished and tosses them into the trash.

    “Thank you,” Zosia says.

    “No problem.”

    Zosia’s gaze holds hers. “I wasn’t talking about the paper towels.”

    Carol stills.

    “For getting me here,” Zosia continues. “And for staying.”

    Carol shrugs, biting her lip. “Despite your first impression of me, I’m not a totally heartless asshole.”

    Zosia’s smile is soft. “I am learning that.”

    Carol exhales slowly. “I’m sorry. For what I said. About ending the pregnancy.” Her throat tightens. “When you were in the parking lot—I thought…” She swallows, finally giving voice to the thoughts that have been swirling around her mind. “I thought something had happened. To you. To the baby.”

    Her voice drops. “I haven’t exactly had the best experience in this hospital, either.”

    Zosia’s expression shifts. “What does that mean?”

    Carol hesitates. Zosia is still practically a complete stranger. Looking away, she says quietly, “My wife died here.”

    Understanding settles over Zosia’s features. “Carol, I’m so sorry.”

    “Yeah.” Carol nods once, stuffing her hands into her pockets. She hunches her shoulders, trying to make herself smaller. “Me too.”

    “What happened?” Zosia asks gently. She’s genuinely curious. She can see the grief Carol carries like an invisible weight pressing down on her shoulders.

    Carol opens her mouth, but the curtain parts before she can answer, and a doctor steps in, tablet in hand.

    “Ms. Diabaté,” he greets, offering a professional smile. “I have your blood results.”

    Zosia straightens slightly. Carol remains standing beside her.

    “Your progesterone levels are low,” he explains. “That can cause cramping in early pregnancy, especially in women with your condition.”

    “We’re going to prescribe a supplement. It should bring the levels back up quickly.” He taps the tablet. “I’d recommend rest and plenty of fluids. Otherwise, everything looks good. We’ll get you discharged shortly.”

    “That’s it?” Carol demands. “What if it happens again?”

    The doctor meets her gaze calmly. “If Ms. Diabaté takes the progesterone and follows my advice, it’s unlikely to recur.”

    “Unlikely isn’t exactly comforting,” Carol mutters.

    “Thank you, Doctor,” Zosia says, and the doctor gives a measured nod before leaving. The curtain falls closed again.

    Carol exhales sharply, running a hand through her hair. “Maybe we should get a second opinion? The amount of pain you were in—that can’t be normal. And what does that guy know about being pregnant?”

    “Carol, it’s fine. Really,” Zosia reassures her. “I’ve lived with PCOS my whole life. I’ve had worse flare-ups. I’m just grateful the baby is okay.” Her hand rubs at her stomach. “Thank you again for staying. I really do appreciate it.”

    The discharge papers are thin and crinkled when the nurse brings them in, smelling faintly of toner and antiseptic. Carol insists on reading every line before Zosia signs anything, as if the fine print might conceal some hidden catastrophe. Zosia watches her with a mixture of fondness and impatience.

    Carol shoots her a look. “I’ve learned my lesson about reading contracts.”

    Zosia’s mouth curves.

    The late evening air hits them as they step through the sliding hospital doors—cooler now, the desert heat finally retreating. The sky over Albuquerque is indigo, the Sandia Mountains a dark silhouette in the distance.

    Zosia inhales deeply, like someone surfacing from underwater. “Freedom,” she murmurs.

    “Let’s not get dramatic,” Carol replies, though her voice is softer than usual. “You were only in there three hours.”

    “Three hours too long.” Zosia sighs. A glance at her watch tells her she’s already missed the start of dinner service at the restaurant. She left Koumba a message earlier, telling him something had come up and she needed him to ask Craig to step in as head chef for the evening.

    She hadn’t told him she was in the hospital. He would only worry and insist on coming out to be with her. Zosia couldn’t have that. She needed at least one of them at the restaurant. It was her livelihood. Now that she was pregnant, she was going to need every penny.

    Carol had gone out to the ambulance bay earlier and moved Zosia’s car before a tow truck could even think about circling.

    Zosia unlocks her car and turns to Carol. “Can I drop you back at the clinic? To get your truck.”

    Carol blinks at her. “You think you’re driving?”

    Zosia lifts a brow. “Yes?” she answers, but it sounds more like a question.

    “You were curled up in the parking lot not three hours ago, looking like you’d been stabbed!”

    “I think you’re being a little bit dramatic.”

    “Really?” Carol stares her down.

    Zosia sighs. “Carol. I can drive.”

    Carol folds her arms, still not backing down. “Do you have someone who can pick you up?”

    Zosia hesitates.

    “Family?” Carol presses. “Friends?”

    “My family is in Poland,” Zosia says evenly. “And we don’t… speak.” A beat. “Most of my friends are colleagues from the restaurant. It’s the middle of dinner service. I can’t pull them away.”

    “What about your brother-in-law?”

    “Koumba is managing tonight. We’re fully booked.” There’s a flicker of pride there, even now. “I need him there.”

    Carol studies her. Zosia looks pale, yes—but steady. Too steady. The kind of steady that pushes through pain because stopping isn’t an option.

    Carol doesn’t like it.

    “I’m driving you home,” she says. Holding out her hand, she adds, “Keys. Hand ’em over.”

    “That’s not necessary.”

    “I’m driving you home,” Carol repeats. “Then I’ll Uber back to get my truck.”

    Zosia frowns. “You’ve already done enough.”

    Carol shrugs. “Add it to my growing list of redeeming qualities.”

    A pause.

    Zosia relents with a small nod and hands over the keys.

    The interior of Zosia’s car smells faintly of rosemary and something citrusy—clean, understated. Carol hadn’t noticed it on the drive to the hospital. She’d been too preoccupied with Zosia’s condition. She adjusts the seat and starts the engine.

    “Where are we headed?” she asks.

    “Old Town,” Zosia replies, giving the street name.

    Carol’s fingers still on the steering wheel, she says, “That’s where your restaurant is.”

    Zosia doesn’t answer immediately.

    “You live above it?” Carol asks, though she already suspects the answer.

    “No.”

    “Then why are we going there?”

    Zosia stares out the window, refusing to meet Carol’s eye. “I just want to check in. Make sure everything is running smoothly.”

    Carol’s head snaps toward her, her suspicions confirmed. “Absolutely not!”

    Zosia blinks. “Excuse me?”

    “The doctor said rest. Fluids. Not ‘pop by and inspect the risotto.’”

    “I would not inspect the risotto,” Zosia mutters indignantly.

    “You would absolutely inspect the risotto,” Carol fires back. She hasn’t known Zosia very long, but she gets the feeling she’s something of a workaholic.

    Silence stretches between them. Finally, Zosia exhales. “Fine.” She gives Carol a different address. Still Old Town, but a residential street this time.

    The drive is quiet, but not strained. Streetlights streak gold across the windshield. The radio remains off. The hum of the engine and the rhythmic click of the turn signal fill the space instead.

    Carol glances at Zosia once at a red light. She’s leaning back in the passenger seat, one hand resting over her abdomen. Protective. Unconscious.

    This afternoon, Carol had told her to end the pregnancy. Now, the thought makes her stomach twist. She isn’t sure what she wants anymore. She only knows that the sound of that heartbeat—fast and insistent—has lodged itself somewhere under her ribs.

    —

    Carol pulls up in front of a duplex painted adobe cream with turquoise trim. A small porch. Clay pots with succulents lining the steps. The air smells faintly of dust and night-blooming flowers.

    “It’s nice,” Carol says, cutting the engine.

    “It’s small,” Zosia replies. “But it’s enough for now.”

    They step out into the cool night. It’s after eight now, properly dark. Porch lights cast soft halos onto the sidewalk.

    Carol hands Zosia her keys. There’s an awkward pause as Carol pulls out her phone, her thumb hovering over the Uber app.

    “Do you have to rush off?” Zosia asks.

    Carol looks up.

    “Would you like to come in?” the other woman asks. “I could make us some tea.”

    Carol hesitates only a second, reluctant to leave Zosia alone just yet. “Sure.”

    Zosia unlocks the left-hand door of the duplex and flicks on the lights. Warm lamplight spills across hardwood floors. The space smells faintly of cumin and something slow-cooked—like the memory of dinner still clinging to the walls.

    It’s intimate. Lived-in.

    A soft gray sofa with woven throw blankets. Books stacked neatly on a low table. A patterned rug in deep reds and golds. The air feels warmer than outside, wrapping around Carol like a quiet exhale.

    “Make yourself at home,” Zosia says, slipping off her shoes. Carol does the same.

    “Do you want something stronger than tea?” Zosia adds. “There’s wine in the fridge. I won’t be drinking it, obviously.”

    Carol shakes her head. “Tea’s fine. I can make it, if you want.”

    “I can make it,” Zosia counters.

    “You’re supposed to be resting.”

    Zosia turns toward her, one brow lifting. “Carol. I am pregnant. Not incapacitated.”

    Carol opens her mouth, then closes it.

    “Sit,” Zosia says gently. “I’ve been making tea longer than you’ve been yelling at nurses.”

    There’s the faintest tease in it.

    Carol exhales and lowers herself onto the sofa while Zosia disappears into the kitchen. The soft clink of ceramic mugs and the hiss of a kettle drift into the living room.

    Carol’s gaze wanders.

    There are framed photographs along the wall. A few landscape shots—desert sunsets, mountains. And one larger frame on the side table.

    She stands before she can talk herself out of it.

    The photo shows Zosia between two men. All three are smiling at the camera. The men share dark, tightly curled hair and rich brown skin. The same proud nose. Clearly brothers.

    One stands slightly closer to Zosia, his arm wrapped around her waist.

    Carol studies the man on the left—the one holding Zosia. His smile is wide, his eyes crinkled at the corners. There’s an ease to him. She wonders if he’s Zosia’s husband.

    “You found them.”

    Carol jumps slightly, heat flooding her face as she turns. She replaces the frame carefully on the table, as though she’s been caught rifling through a drawer.

    “Sorry. I was just—”

    “Snooping?” Zosia supplies, not unkindly.

    “Observing,” Carol corrects.

    Zosia smiles, carrying two steaming mugs into the room. “Malick is on the left,” she says softly. “Koumba’s on the right.”

    Carol looks back at the photo. So she was right.

    She nods once. “He looks…” She searches for the right word. “Kind.”

    “He was,” Zosia replies.

    There’s no dramatic break in her voice. Just fact.

    Zosia hands Carol a mug. Their fingers brush briefly again—warm porcelain between them. Carol wraps both hands around the cup, letting the heat seep into her palms.

    The room feels smaller as they both take a seat on the sofa.

    Carol looks at the photo once more, then at Zosia. The baby her womb carries has Malick’s DNA—and Carol’s.

    The reality of that settles differently here, in this warm, lamplit room, than it did under fluorescent hospital lights. Not abstract. Not theoretical. Real.

    Carol takes a slow sip of tea. She isn’t sure what this is becoming. She only knows she doesn’t want to leave just yet.

    She clears her throat. “I, uh, I was thinking about Helen—back when we were in the hospital.”

    “I’m sorry. It couldn’t have been easy for you, being there.”

    Carol shrugs. She’s never been big on sharing her feelings. Helen used to say it was like pulling teeth getting Carol to open up. “Yeah… Actually, I was thinking more about what she would do. If—if it had been her eggs you got.”
