    “Oh.” Surprise plays across Zosia’s face. She shifts, turning to face Carol head-on with her legs tucked underneath her. “And what would Helen do?”

    “Honestly? Probably try to adopt you.” Carol chuckles, and Zosia grins. “I’m being serious. She would have wanted to be involved… with the baby. Have it call her Aunty Helen or some shit.”

    Zosia listens closely. She isn’t sure where this is heading, but she’s picked up on the shift in Carol since the incident in the parking lot. She’s softer. More invested.

    Carol goes on. “I’m not—I don’t know if I want to be in the kid’s life, but I do know that I was terrified when I thought something bad had happened.”

    Zosia has Koumba, but she’s not sure that counts for much in Carol’s eyes. Not when Carol had to drive her home from the hospital.

    “What are you saying?” Zosia asks.

    “I’m saying…” Carol swallows hard, not entirely sure she hasn’t lost her mind. “I don’t want you to go through this alone. I want to help.”

    Zosia looks down, tucks her hair back behind her ear with a bashful smile before looking back up at Carol. “I’d like that… I could definitely use a friend right now.”

    Carol surprises herself when she says, “Me too.”

    Chapter 5

    Chapter Notes

    As ever, thanks for reading and for all of the lovely comments! This chapter was supposed to go up yesterday, but Ao3 was down. On the bright side, the next chapter is halfway done since Ao3 was down last night 😅

    Carol lingers on the sidewalk outside Zosia’s duplex, the porch light casting a pale circle at her feet. The night has cooled properly now, the desert heat finally bled out of the pavement. Somewhere down the block, a dog barks once and then gives up.

    She pulls out her phone.

    Carol: You awake? Need a lift. Old Town.

    The response is almost immediate.

    Manousos: I’m awake. You okay?

    She hesitates.

    Carol: Fine. Just stranded. Need a ride to my truck.

    The reply takes longer this time.

    Manousos: On my way.

    No emojis. No speculation. That’s Manousos.

    She slips her phone back into her pocket and glances once at the duplex window. The lamplight still glows inside. A shadow passes across the curtain—Zosia moving slowly. Deliberately.

    Only until it’s born, Carol reminds herself.

    Headlights turn the corner five minutes later. Manousos’s dark pickup rolls to a stop in front of her. It’s older than it should be and immaculately maintained, like everything else in his life.

    She climbs in without ceremony and he gives her a brief once-over. “You don’t look stranded.” Carol replies with the finger.

    He pulls away from the curb. “You want to tell me why I’m picking you up in Old Town at this time of night?”

    Carol stares out the windshield. “I left my truck downtown. I need you to drop me off.”

    “Why?”

    She exhales. “Because I drove someone to the hospital in her car.”

    He nods once. “Okay.” He doesn’t fill the silence. He never does. He waits.

    Manousos moved in next door eighteen months ago, hauling furniture in by himself, speaking little, observing everything. Paraguayan. Former civil engineer. Divorced. The first month they’d argued over a shared fence line and irrigation runoff like two territorial cats. He’d called her inflexible. She’d called him sanctimonious—among other things.

    Now he fixes the loose hinge on her gate without asking and leaves guava pastries on her doorstep when he comes back from the market.

    He didn’t know Helen and only met Carol at her worst. He knows her as she is now—all scowls and sharp edges. It’s a miracle he stuck around.

    “She’s pregnant,” Carol says finally.

    He glances at her, then back at the road. “The woman you drove?”

    “Yes.”

    “And?”

    “With my child.”

    A beat. He doesn’t swerve. Doesn’t ask questions. Just processes.

    “Explain,” he eventually says.

    “Helen and I froze my eggs. The clinic implanted them in the wrong woman.” The words still sound absurd, even to her. “Her husband’s sperm. My egg. Voilà.”

    He nods slowly. “So the baby is biologically yours.”

    “Half.”

    “And she intends to continue the pregnancy.”

    “Yes.”

    Silence settles between them again, but it isn’t uncomfortable. It’s evaluative. Maybe that’s why Carol enjoys Manousos’s company so much. He doesn’t waste his words, and he usually lets Carol rant as much as she wants. Tonight is no exception.

    “She almost lost it today,” Carol adds, her voice lower. “She collapsed in the parking lot outside the fertility clinic.”

    His jaw tightens slightly. “But she didn’t?”

    “No. She’s okay… They’re both okay.” She rubs her thumb against her palm, remembering the sound of the heartbeat. “For a few hours, I thought…” She stops.

    “You thought you were about to lose your child.” The bluntness makes her flinch.

    “It’s not mine!” she snaps automatically.

    He flicks her a look. “Biologically, it is.”

    They drive past a row of dark storefronts. Streetlights flash across the dashboard, lighting his profile in brief intervals—strong nose, steady mouth, eyes that miss very little.

    “I told her I’d help,” Carol says after a while, when the silence becomes too much for her. “Doctor’s appointments. Logistics. Whatever she needs.” She crosses her arms. “Until it’s born.”

    “And after?”

    “I’m not planning that far ahead right now.”

    “That’s not what I asked.”

    She exhales sharply. “After, it’s her baby. She wanted this. I didn’t.”

    “That’s not true,” he says evenly.

    Carol turns to him, indignation in her eyes. “Excuse me?”

    “You froze your eggs, didn’t you?”

    “Yeah, because Helen wanted kids!”

    “But you agreed to it. This child is not here by accident, Carol. Maybe it is the world’s way of giving you something you need.”

    Carol looks away. “Maybe the world needs to fuck off and mind its own business.”

    He doesn’t press. He rarely does. He simply lays facts on the table and lets her sit with them.

    “I don’t want to mess the kid up,” she says after a moment. “I don’t want to hover in and out. Show up when it’s convenient. Or resent it. Or—” She breaks off. “I’m not built for that.”

    “You don’t know that.”

    “I do.”

    “You’re afraid,” he corrects.

    She glares at him. “Don’t psychoanalyze me.”

    “I’m not. I’m observing.” They pull up at a red light.

    “She invited me in for tea,” Carol says abruptly, as if that explains something.

    “And?”

    “And nothing.” She scowls. “Don’t make it into something.”

    “I didn’t. You brought it up.” They sit in the red glow of the traffic signal.

    “Do you like her?” he asks.

    Carol hesitates.

    “She’s… nice,” she says finally. “Stubborn as all hell, though. She runs a restaurant. Refused to call the only person in her life to pick her up because he’s the manager and they were mid-service.” A pause. “She lost her husband six months ago.”

    Manousos nods once. “That’s recent.”

    “Yes.” The light turns green.

    “You’re already involved,” he says.

    “Only until the baby’s born.”

    “That’s still involvement.”

    They turn into the clinic parking lot. Her truck sits under a lamppost, alone in the empty lot. He parks beside it and cuts the engine, turning to address her with the full scrutiny of his gaze.

    “You don’t have to decide the rest tonight,” he says. “But don’t lie to yourself. If you care, you care.”

    “I care that she doesn’t miscarry in a parking lot,” Carol mutters.

    He almost smiles. “That’s a start.”

    She reaches for the door handle, then pauses. “You don’t think I’m insane?”

    He considers that seriously. “This situation is insane. You are responding to it the best you can.”

    “That’s not an answer.”

    “It is the only one I have for you, Carol Sturka.”

    She studies him for a moment. In the dim light, he looks less like the man she once fought over fence posts and more like what he’s become—something solid at her flank. Not sentimental. Not effusive. Just there. Keeping her from going over the edge into oblivion.

    “You’re not going to try to set me up with your cousin again, are you?” she asks with a smirk.

    He raises a brow. “No. She said you were a waste of her time.”

    Carol snorts. “Rude.”

    “Accurate.”

    She opens the door, then leans back in slightly. “I’m not into her or anything.”

    “I didn’t say you were.”

    “And I’m not becoming some tragic co-parenting cautionary tale.”

    “Then don’t.” Simple as that.

    She nods once and steps out, shutting the door with a solid thud. As she walks to her truck, she feels his gaze on her—measuring, protective in a way he’d never admit out loud. She climbs into the driver’s seat and starts the engine. The dashboard glows to life, and she pulls out after Manousos, tailing him all the way home.

    Only until it’s born, she tells herself again—but the certainty doesn’t land the way it did before.

    -

    The restaurant smells like lemons and extinguished flame. Zosia stands alone in the kitchen, sleeves rolled to her elbows, hair tied back in a messy bun with strands falling out of it. The stainless-steel counters gleam under the fluorescent lights. Everything is clean—her kitchen staff always leaves it clean—but that doesn’t stop her from wiping down the counter again.

    She hadn’t meant to come in. She’d promised Carol she would stay home and rest after the other woman left. Drink more tea. Put her feet up. But Zosia hadn’t expected to lose the entire afternoon. The restaurant runs on precision. On preparation. On her.

    The dining room beyond is dark, chairs flipped upside down on tables, their legs like thin silhouettes against the dim wall sconces. Albuquerque hums faintly outside, Old Town never completely asleep.

    Zosia presses a palm briefly to her abdomen. The cramping has dulled to a whisper. The progesterone prescription sits on her counter at home, a white paper bag folded neatly at the top.

    The back door creaks. She doesn’t jump—she recognizes the rhythm of the hinge—but she does straighten. Koumba steps inside, keys jangling softly in his hand. He’s still in his dark button-down from service, sleeves rolled, collar open. He pauses when he sees her.

    “I was driving past,” he says. His voice is low, controlled. “The light was on.”

    Zosia sets the cloth down. “I couldn’t sleep.”

    “You were missed at dinner service.”

    “I know.”

    He walks further into the kitchen, his gaze scanning her face with quiet intensity. The resemblance to Malick is sharpest when he frowns—the same dark eyes, the same set of his jaw.

    “Your message was vague,” he says. “Something came up?”

    She exhales slowly. She had hoped to avoid this conversation tonight. It all still feels far too raw to talk about.

    “I had a scare,” she says. “After the meeting at the clinic. Some cramping… and bleeding.”

    The words shift the air between them. Koumba goes still.

    “And?” he asks carefully.

    “And everything is fine,” she answers quickly. “Just low progesterone. The hospital prescribed me some supplements—”

    His jaw tightens. “You went to the hospital?”

    “Yes.”

    “And you didn’t call me?”

    Zosia lifts her chin slightly. “You were in the middle of service.”

    “I would have left.”

    “I know you would have. That’s why I didn’t tell you.”

    Silence expands, heavy but not hostile. The refrigerator hums behind them.

    “You dealt with that alone?” he asks, his heart heavy at the thought of Zosia on her own in some sterile, soulless hospital.

    “No,” she says quietly. “I wasn’t alone.”

    His eyes narrow slightly, not understanding. Most of the people Zosia knew—at least the ones she would consider calling—had been in the restaurant with him all night. “Who was with you?”

    “Carol.” The name lands between them, but Koumba doesn’t immediately recognize it. “The woman I was meeting with at the clinic.”

    Koumba absorbs that. His expression doesn’t change much, but something shifts beneath it.

    “You hardly know this woman,” he says.

    “I know enough,” Zosia argues.

    “She’s a stranger.”

    “She’s half the reason I’m pregnant in the first place, Koumba,” Zosia replies evenly. Her hand finds her stomach again.

    “That doesn’t make it right. She’s not your family, Zosia.”

    Zosia studies him. “Neither were you, once.”

    He exhales through his nose, conceding the point but not liking it.

    “I am your family now,” he says. “If something happens, you call me. Not a woman you only just met today.”

    Zosia rests her hands on the cool metal edge of the prep table. “Normally I’d agree with you, but there is something growing inside of me that is half hers,” she says. Koumba’s gaze flickers briefly to her abdomen.

    “She said she doesn’t want anything to do with the child,” Zosia adds. “After it’s born.”

    A beat.

    “That’s good,” he says.

    She doesn’t answer immediately. When she’d first met Carol in that conference room at the clinic, Zosia would have agreed. Having her refuse to be any part of the child’s life had felt practical. Contained. Now it feels… wrong.

    “She seems lonely,” Zosia says instead, softer. “Her wife died.”

    Koumba’s expression shifts, just slightly. He hadn’t known that; but then they knew next to nothing about this woman who had just crashed into their lives without invitation.

    “She was there for me today,” Zosia continues. “She argued with the nurses. She drove me home. She stayed.”

    “And that obligates you?”

    “No.” Zosia shakes her head.

    “Then what?”

    Zosia looks down at her hands. At the faint crescent marks where her nails press into her skin.

    “I don’t know,” she admits.

    She does not say what she has been thinking since the sound of that heartbeat filled the curtained hospital bay and Carol’s face came alive. That maybe Carol, sharp and frightened and pretending otherwise, might soften with something to hope for. Those thoughts feel too fragile to share.

    Instead, she says, “I’m calling my gynaecologist first thing in the morning. I want a proper appointment. A full scan.”

    Koumba nods immediately. “I’ll come.”

    “You have to be here, Koumba.”

    “I don’t care.”

    “We’re fully booked through this month,” she reminds him gently. “I need you here when I can’t be. You know how important this place is to me. You’re the only one I can trust to run it.”

    He opens his mouth to argue that she doesn’t have to be alone when Zosia adds, “Besides, Carol offered to take me.”

    There it is again—that small tightening around his eyes.

    “I thought she wanted nothing to do with the pregnancy,” he says.

    “She doesn’t want to be in the baby’s life,” Zosia replies. “That’s different.”

    He studies her carefully. “And you believe that.”

    “For now.”

    The fluorescent lights buzz faintly overhead. Somewhere outside, a car passes, bass thudding through closed windows.

    “She said she would help me through the pregnancy,” Zosia continues. “Appointments. Logistics. She knows this place matters to me. That if I’m not here, you have to be.”

    Koumba’s shoulders ease slightly despite himself. He understands sacrifice for the restaurant. He understands obligation.

    “She has seven months to reconsider,” he says quietly. “What if she changes her mind and decides she wants a part in this child’s life?”

    Zosia’s fingers curl instinctively over her stomach. “She might,” she says.

    She doesn’t add that she’s hopeful she will. That she suspects Carol doesn’t yet understand how far in she already is.

    Koumba moves closer, resting his palms on the counter opposite her. “Fine. But next time,” he says firmly, “you at least call me.”

    “I will.”

    “I mean it, Zosia.”

    “So do I.”

    They hold each other’s gaze for a long moment. Brother and sister not by blood, but by loss and choice.

    Finally, he nods once. “Go home.”

    “I will.”

    “Now. You need your rest.”

    She offers a faint smile. “I promise.”

    He reaches out and squeezes her shoulder, solid and grounding, before heading toward the back door again. He pauses there.

    “And Zosia?”

    “Yes?”

    “If this Carol woman hurts you, or that child, I will not be held accountable for my actions.”

    Despite everything, she laughs softly. “Noted. Thank you, Koumba. ’Night.”

    “Goodnight.”

    When he’s gone, the restaurant feels larger again. Colder. Zosia turns off the kitchen lights one by one. The room dims in stages, stainless steel fading to shadow.

    Before she leaves, she stands alone in the darkened dining room and presses her palm once more to her abdomen. Seven months is a long time—long enough, perhaps, for Carol to change her mind.

    -

    Carol pulls up outside Zosia’s house just after one o’clock the following day. The afternoon sun washes the adobe walls of the duplex in pale gold. A cottonwood tree shifts lazily in the dry breeze, its leaves flashing silver-green. Carol keeps the engine running, fingers tap-tap-tapping against the steering wheel before she stills them.

    Zosia steps out a moment later, locking the door behind her. She’s wearing a loose linen dress the color of the sky and flat sandals, hair braided over one shoulder. She’s practically glowing.

    Carol exhales without realizing she’d been holding her breath. Zosia slides into the passenger seat and smiles. “Hi, Carol.”

    “Hey. How are you feeling?” Carol asks immediately.

    “Good,” Zosia says, fastening her seatbelt. “The cramping is all gone.”

    “Any bleeding?”

    “No.”

    Carol nods once. Clinical. Efficient. Relief kept carefully contained.

    “Thank you,” Zosia adds softly, her hand reaching across the centre console and finding Carol’s arm. “For yesterday. And for today.”

    Carol almost slips a gear as she pulls away from the curb, distracted by Zosia’s touch. “Don’t mention it.”

    Zosia smiles faintly and looks out the window as they drive toward her gynaecologist’s office. The sky is an unbroken blue, sharp and cloudless. Albuquerque hums around them—traffic lights blinking, heat rising from the asphalt.

    They fall into easy conversation.

    Zosia tells her about the dinner service she missed. About Koumba’s overreaction. About a supplier who delivered the wrong cut of lamb last week and tried to blame it on “regional interpretation.”

    Carol snorts. “What’s regional about a rack of lamb versus a shoulder?”

    “Exactly!” Zosia laughs.

    The interaction is light. Uncomplicated.

    Carol is surprised by just how uncomplicated it feels. Zosia is easy to be around. There’s no performance. No sharp edges. She doesn’t have to calibrate herself around Zosia the way she does with most people.

    Carol finds herself talking more than she intended—about how her publisher is up her ass for another book. How she’s been avoiding her publicist’s calls after the much younger woman hit on her the other night.

    By the time they pull into the medical plaza parking lot, the air between them feels settled.

    Inside, the gynaecology office smells faintly of disinfectant and lavender plug-in air freshener. The waiting room is painted a muted sage green. Posters line the walls—diagrams of uterine anatomy, smiling couples cradling rounded bellies, bullet points about folic acid and iron intake.

    Carol’s eyes land on a poster about fetal development. Zosia checks in at the desk, then lowers herself carefully into the empty chair beside Carol.

    Carol leans closer to the poster, reading.

    “Size of a raspberry,” she murmurs.

    Zosia glances at her, amused.

    “About 1.6 centimeters,” Carol continues, slipping unconsciously into the same cadence she uses during her book readings. “Heartbeat detectable. Limb buds developing into arms and legs. Neural tube closed. Facial features forming. Cool. The bean has a face.”

    Zosia’s smile widens. “Is that so?”

    “Yup, and the fingers and toes are beginning to separate. Cartilage turning into bone.” She tilts her head slightly. “It says here the tail is nearly gone.”

    “The tail?” Zosia laughs softly.

    “Embryonic tail. Perfectly normal.”

    “I’m relieved.”

    Carol glances at her and catches the warmth in Zosia’s expression—not mockery, not indulgence. Something softer. Something dangerously close to the patient way Helen used to look at her.

    “What?” Carol asks.

    “Nothing,” Zosia says. “You’re just so excited. It’s cute.”

    Carol looks back at the poster, throat tightening inexplicably. She’s almost relieved when Zosia’s name is called.

    The other woman rises, smoothing her dress. “I won’t be long.”

    Carol nods. “I’ll be here,” she says, though she’s secretly fighting the urge to run right out the door and never look back.

    The exam room is cool and brightly lit when Zosia steps into it. The gynaecologist—a woman in her mid-forties with silver threaded through dark hair—reviews Zosia’s chart.

    “Zosia, hi. It’s been a while since we’ve seen you. I see here you’re pregnant. I take it the last round of IVF worked? Congratulations.”

    “Thank you, Doctor Beeken,” Zosia replies, her face cracking open with a bright smile. “I had an appointment scheduled with you for next week, but I had some bleeding and cramping yesterday. I wanted to get checked out sooner.”

    “That’s very sensible. How far along are you?”

    “Eight weeks.”

    The doctor nods, setting Zosia’s chart aside. “Okay, let’s take a look.”

    The paper sheet crinkles beneath Zosia as she settles back. The gel is cold. The transvaginal ultrasound probe is clinical and invasive, but Zosia focuses on the ceiling tile above her. She’s experienced worse in her quest to get pregnant.

    A grainy image flickers onto the monitor.

    “There,” the doctor says, adjusting the angle. “Cardiac activity is strong.”

    The sound fills the room—rapid, insistent. Alive. Zosia’s breath catches in her throat. Even after only hearing it yesterday, the sound stirs something intrinsically maternal within her.

    “Everything looks appropriate for gestational age,” the doctor continues. “Actually, measuring closer to eight weeks and six days. So nearly nine weeks.”

    “Nearly nine,” Zosia repeats faintly.

    “Your due date will be approximately seven months from now. I’ll work it out and write it down for you.” The doctor clicks through measurements. “No sign of active bleeding. Cervix is closed. Progesterone supplementation was appropriate.”

    Relief moves through Zosia like warmth.

    “Would you like a picture?” the doctor asks.

    “Yes,” Zosia says immediately. Then hesitates. “Could I get two?”

    “One for your husband?” Doctor Beeken smiles. The question is well-meant, automatic. She’d met Malick once or twice when they’d first started their IVF journey.

    Zosia swallows the lump in her throat. “My husband died last year.”

    The doctor’s expression softens, guilt flashing across her face. “Oh, Zosia. I’m truly sorry.”

    “I went ahead with IVF,” Zosia continues steadily. “But there was a mix-up and they used someone else’s egg. I wanted to ask if that affects the pregnancy in any way.”

    “It does not,” the doctor says firmly, but not unkindly. “Genetics influence the baby’s traits, but your body supports the pregnancy the same way. Uterine environment, placental development—those are yours.”

    Zosia nods. “The donor is involved,” she adds after a moment. “She’s in the waiting room.”

    The doctor raises her brows slightly but doesn’t comment on the strangeness of the situation. “Well, that’s good. If she’s willing to, I’d like a full personal and family medical history. Given your age and your PCOS, your pregnancy is already classed as high risk, and I’d like to avoid any surprises.”

    “Of course.” Zosia tries not to react to the words “high risk.”

    “We’ll schedule prenatal visits monthly for now. From weeks twenty-eight to thirty-six, every two weeks. Weekly from thirty-six until delivery. We’ll do a detailed anatomy scan at twenty weeks.”

    She prints the ultrasound images.

    “I also recommend antenatal classes two to three months before your due date,” she adds. “I’d book now if you can. They fill quickly.”

    Zosia carefully accepts the two glossy printouts the doctor hands her, as though they might dissolve. “Thank you.”

    When she steps back into the waiting room, Carol is on her feet immediately. “Is everything okay?” she asks, the composure from earlier gone. “Any complications?”

    Zosia’s smile is radiant as she reaches her. “Everything is fine.”

    Carol’s shoulders drop slightly. “You’re sure?”

    “Nearly nine weeks,” Zosia says. “Strong heartbeat.”

    Carol exhales, a quiet sound she doesn’t try to disguise.

    Zosia holds out one of the images, and Carol takes it automatically. The black-and-white blur resolves slowly in her mind—a small curved shape within a dark oval. A flicker of brightness at its center.

    The same-shaped bean from yesterday’s scan. She stares intently at it. “Oh,” she breathes. Her fingers tighten around the edge of the printout before she catches herself and tries to hand it back.

    “It’s for you,” Zosia says, pushing Carol’s hand back toward her.

    Carol looks up sharply. “Zosia—”

    “You came with me,” Zosia says simply. “I want you to have it.”

    Carol’s cheeks burn unexpectedly. She isn’t sure how to feel about keeping the scan; about what it might mean. “I don’t—”

    “It’s yours too,” Zosia says, not forceful. Just factual.

    Carol lowers her gaze to the image again. The little curve. The suggestion of a head. She nods once, unable to find the correct words for what presses against her ribs.

    “Thank you,” she says quietly.

    The drive to the restaurant is quieter, but not strained. Carol keeps glancing at the ultrasound resting on the dashboard, as though it might disappear when she isn’t looking.

    Zosia notices.

    At the restaurant, Carol pulls into the narrow lot behind the building. She’d tried to drive Zosia home, but the other woman insisted on going in to work.

    “I need to check everything’s ready for tonight,” Zosia says. “And I should start looking for a head chef.”

    “For now?” Carol asks.

    “In the next few months. I won’t be able to run the kitchen when I’m nine months pregnant.”

    “You should find someone sooner rather than later,” Carol says. “You need to take it easy.”

    “I am taking it easy.”

    “Working a seventy-hour week is not taking it easy, Zosia.”

    Zosia smiles, her hand finding Carol’s arm again. She’s a tactile person, always touching. Carol feels her touch everywhere all at once. “I appreciate the concern.”

    Carol hesitates. “I mean it.”

    “I know.”

    Zosia gathers her purse from the backseat, then pauses. “Would you like to have dinner tonight? It would be after service. Around nine?”

    Carol blinks. “You don’t have to—”

    “I want to,” Zosia says. “As a thank you.”

    Carol considers for half a second before nodding. “Nine.”

    “I’ll see you then.” Zosia beams at her. She steps out of the car, sunlight catching in her braid. Before closing the door, she leans back in slightly.

    “And Carol?”

    “Yeah?”

    “I’m glad you were there yesterday.”

    Carol swallows. “Me too,” she says.

    Zosia closes the door and heads toward the back entrance of the restaurant, waving as she goes. Carol sits for a moment longer, takes the ultrasound image from the dashboard, and holds it in her hand. Nearly nine weeks. Seven months to go.

    She traces the edge of the glossy paper once with her thumb before carefully sliding it into her shirt pocket, as though storing something far more fragile than ink on paper.

    Chapter 6

    The kitchen is loud in the way only a kitchen at the end of service can be—the clang of metal on metal, the hiss of a final deglaze, the low murmur of staff already thinking about home. The last tickets hang limp from the rail.

    “Table twelve cleared,” someone calls.

    “Eighty-six lamb,” another voice answers.

    Zosia stands at the pass, sleeves rolled high, heat blooming across her cheeks from the ovens. The dining room closes at nine; the kitchen at eight-thirty. It’s eight twenty-two, and Carol just texted to say she’s leaving her house now.

    Zosia ties her apron tighter and turns back to the stove. She isn’t cooking from necessity now, fulfilling some faceless stranger’s order. She’s cooking with intention now, cooking for Carol.

    Duck confit—Carol had paused on that dish when she’d scanned the menu on Zosia’s phone earlier in the day, when she’d offered to cook dinner for her. Roasted fingerling potatoes with rosemary and smoked salt. Charred broccolini with lemon zest and shaved pecorino. And for dessert, a flourless chocolate torte.

    Zosia had approved all of her choices. Carol had good taste—though in truth, it was entirely Zosia’s menu, so she would have approved of anything Carol picked.

    Zosia seasons carefully, tasting and adjusting, layering fat and acid and heat. She presses the duck skin down into the pan until it crackles properly. The smell of rendered fat rises, rich and indulgent, filling the kitchen.

    She wants it perfect. Not because Carol expects perfection—but because Zosia does. Because gratitude, to her, is something plated. Because the last two days have left her shaken in ways she hasn’t admitted aloud.

    When she’d been in that hospital bed, staring intently at one specific ceiling tile and listening for a heartbeat, Carol had been the only steady thing in the room.

    T’ika appears at her elbow without warning, as she often does. “You need help, Chef?” she asks, voice warm and accented, wiping her hands on a towel tucked into her waistband.

    Sisa is tall and sturdy, long dark hair shot through with silver and braided down her back. She smells faintly of cumin and eucalyptus hand cream. She’s the aunt of the young server, Kusamayu. Village-born. Sharp-eyed. Impossible to fool.

    “I thought we were finished?” she adds, glancing at the now-empty ticket rail. “No more orders.”

    “We are finished,” Zosia says lightly, flipping the duck. “This isn’t for a table.”

    T’ika leans slightly to look at the plate components assembling under Zosia’s hands. Her brows lift. “Ah.”

    Zosia keeps her gaze on the pan.

    “For you?” T’ika asks. Her eyes flick, quick and knowing, to Zosia’s midsection. “You are eating for two now?” The question lands soft but direct. Heat climbs Zosia’s neck.

    “No,” she says too quickly. “No, I— It’s just dinner. With a friend.”

    T’ika studies her for a long second. Not intrusive. Just assessing. “A good friend?” she asks.

    Zosia plates the duck with deliberate care, spooning jus in a careful arc. “A friend,” she repeats, focus completely on plating up.

    T’ika’s mouth curves faintly. “Good. You work too much. Always last to leave. Even when there is nothing left to do, you find something.”

    Zosia smiles despite herself. “Not tonight.”

    “Good.” T’iks pats her arm, flour-dusted fingers gentle. “Go home. Sit down. Let someone take care of you for once.”

    The words tug somewhere tender.

    “I will,” Zosia says.

    T’ika nods once, satisfied, and drifts back toward the sinks where the last of the pans are being scrubbed.

    Zosia moves quickly now, assembling everything into compostable takeout containers—lined neatly, lids snapped tight. She adds small ramekins of extra sauce, an extra slice of lemon.

    The kitchen lights feel harsher now that the burners are off. The stainless steel reflects her own image back at her—flushed, slightly tired, but eyes brighter than they have been in a long time.

    She presses a hand briefly to her abdomen. A quiet, instinctive gesture. Everything is fine, she reminds herself. Nearly nine weeks. The first trimester is the most dangerous time. If she can get to the second, to thirteen weeks, she’ll let people know she’s pregnant; starting with T’ika.

    She gathers everything up in her arms and steps out into the dimmed dining room. Chairs are already flipped onto tables. The scent of lemons and extinguished flame lingers in the air.

    Koumba stands behind the bar counting the till, sleeves rolled, expression focused. “You’re really leaving me with this mess?” he says without looking up.

    “It’s hardly a mess.”

    He glances toward the containers bundled in her hands. “What’s that?”

    “Dinner.”

    “For yourself?”

    “No, I’m having dinner with a friend.”

    He watches her more closely now. “With Carol?”

    Zosia stills for half a second. She knows how Koumba feels about bringing the stranger into their lives. “Yes,” she finally admits.

    He sets the drawer closed with a soft click. “Do I get to meet her?” There’s curiosity in his tone. And something else. Something protective.

    “Maybe next time,” Zosia says lightly.

    He arches a brow. “I promise not to interrogate her.”

    “We both know that’s not a promise you can keep, Koumba,” she says, but there’s no malice in her tone. His mouth twitches. Zosia steps closer and presses a quick kiss to his cheek before he can protest. “Be good,” she murmurs.

    “I’m always good.”

    Zosia shoots him a pointed smile. “That’s categorically untrue.”

    He huffs quietly but nods toward the door. “Go. I’ve got everything here.”

    “You’re sure?”

    “Yes.”

    She searches his face for a moment, then nods, reassured. “Call me if anything goes wrong.”

    “Nothing will go wrong.”

    “Things always go wrong.”

    “Go, Zosia.”

    She laughs softly and pushes through the front door into the parking lot. The air has cooled, desert heat finally releasing its grip. A faint breeze carries the scent of dust and distant mesquite. The sky above Old Town is a deepening violet.

    Carol’s truck idles near the edge of the lot, headlights cutting pale beams across the asphalt. She leans back in the driver’s seat, one arm draped over the steering wheel, the other bent and resting out of the window. When she sees Zosia, she straightens. Something in her expression shifts—guard lowering, just a fraction.

    Zosia approaches and lifts the containers she’s holding slightly. “I come bearing gifts.”

    Carol’s mouth curves. “You didn’t have to make all that.”

    “I wanted to,” Zosia says.

    Carol steps out of the truck to meet her around the hood. The parking lot is mostly empty now, the restaurant dark behind them. “You look tired,” she says, scanning her face.

    “I’m fine.”

    “Are you?”

    Zosia holds her gaze. “Yes.”

    A beat passes between them—quiet, steady. The kind that hums rather than strains. Carol reaches for the containers automatically. Their fingers brush as she takes them. The contact lingers a second longer than strictly necessary.

    “Smells incredible,” Carol says, breaking the charged moment.

    Zosia smiles, a proud warmth spreading through her chest. “Wait until you taste it.”

    Once the containers are safely stored on the back seat, Carol rounds the truck and opens the passenger door for her without thinking about it. Zosia notices. Says nothing.

    The engine hum deepens as they pull out of the lot and head deeper into Old Town, streetlights blinking on one by one as the evening settles fully around them.

    Carol turns onto Zosia’s street slowly, headlights washing over low adobe walls and neat gravel yards. The duplex sits halfway down the block, warm porch light glowing against the deepening indigo of the sky. Zosia’s small blue hatchback rests in the driveway.

    Carol eases the truck to the curb.

    “You can pull in on the other drive if you want?” Zosia says, already reaching for her bag.

    Carol glances at the drive, then at the adjoining unit. “Your neighbour won’t mind?”

    “Not at all. That’s Koumba’s place,” Zosia replies, unbuckling. “He usually doesn’t come home after work. And even if he does, he won’t mind.”

    Carol lifts a brow. “You live next door to your brother-in-law?”

    “Yes.” Zosia smiles faintly. “He and Malick were very close. Family is… was important to Malick.” A small shrug. “It was nice. Especially since I’m not close to mine.”

    Carol shifts the truck into park after she pulls in to Koumba’s drive. “When did you last see your folks?”

    Zosia pushes her door open, cool night air slipping in. “When I left Poland.” There’s no drama in the way she says it. Just simple fact. Zosia pauses on the pavement, looking at Carol across the hood. “You probably think that’s bad, right?”

    “I don’t,” Carol answers immediately. Zosia studies her, as if weighing whether she believes that.

    “My mom’s a piece of work,” Carol adds, shutting off the engine. “I walked out at eighteen and never looked back.”

    Zosia’s mouth softens. “That’s something we have in common then.”

    “Looks like it.”

    They step out into the quiet street. Gravel crunches underfoot. Carol reaches into the back seat for the containers while Zosia digs through her purse for her keys, muttering softly in Polish when she can’t find them at once.

    “Got them,” she says finally, triumph bright in her voice.

    The front door opens into warmth. Not just temperature—something else. The air smells faintly of citrus cleaner, rosemary, and the deeper, comforting trace of browned butter that seems permanently woven into the walls. It smells like Zosia. Like the kitchen. Like something alive and lived in.

    Carol steps inside and closes the door behind them. Zosia leads her straight through to the kitchen.

    It’s exactly what Carol expected and somehow more intimate for it. Fresh herbs crowd the windowsill—thyme, basil, parsley—leaves brushing the glass. A heavy cream-colored stand mixer sits on the counter in pride of place, flour dust caught in its seams. A long rack of spices lines one wall in careful order—smoked paprika, coriander, cumin, jars all labelled in tidy handwriting.

    Carol sets the containers down and turns slowly, taking it in.

    “Judging my kitchen?” Zosia teases, pulling plates from a cabinet.

    “I’m appreciating it.”

    Zosia hums softly, pleased.

    She works with quiet efficiency, snapping open lids, transferring duck to ceramic plates, spooning jus in smooth, controlled arcs as though she were still standing at the pass in the restaurant. The sauce glistens dark and glossy under the kitchen light, and it smells even more amazing out of the container. She arranges potatoes with deliberate care, tucks broccolini just so.

    “You don’t need to do all that,” Carol says, leaning back against the counter. “I would’ve eaten it straight from the container. Save on the dishes.”

    Zosia pauses, looks at Carol over her shoulder, wearing an expression like the other woman just suggested they should stamp on the food before eating it. “Food is an experience of the senses, Carol,” she says, mock stern. “It should be savoured. That includes the presentation.”

    Carol snorts. “Wow, that’s a little pretentious.”

    Zosia gasps softly, hand to chest. “Are you calling me pretentious?”

    “If the shoe fits,” Carol shrugs, feigning innocence. “Though, I should probably expect that from ‘Albuquerque’s Top Chef 2024.’”

    Zosia narrows her eyes, genuinely taken aback. “How do you know about that?”

    Carol hesitates half a second, like she’s been caught with her hand in the cookie jar before dinner, then she shrugs. “I looked you up.”

    Zosia’s brows lift, and Carol suddenly feels like she might have overstepped. She tries to deflect it with humour. “I thought I ought to know more about who my baby momma is,” Carol says lightly.

    Zosia doesn’t bristle. If anything, her expression warms. “You could always ask. I guess I can’t really judge, though… I looked you up too.”

    “Oh?”

    “Four books,” Zosia says, placing a knife and fork carefully beside the plate. “All on the New York bestseller list and over a million copies sold worldwide. That’s impressive.”

    Carol waves a hand dismissively, feeling her ears burning. Even after all this time, after four books, she still feels like an impostor calling herself a writer. “It’s not a big deal.”

    “It really is,” Zosia insists as she pours Carol a glass of wine from an open bottle in the fridge. For herself, she grabs a can of Diet Coke.

    “It’s basically mommy porn,” Carol says dryly. “Hey, you might like it.” She jokes, hoping to earn a laugh. She likes it when Zosia laughs.

    A flicker of something—mischief?—passes through Zosia’s eyes. “I ordered the first one,” she admits in a quiet voice.

    Carol’s stomach drops. “You did?”

    “Yes.”

    Carol forces a grin, tries not to show her internal panic at the thought of a woman like Zosia critiquing her work. “Well, good luck.” She reaches for the wine glass Zosia placed on the counter in front of her, busying her hands. “It’s just… you know. Smut with plot.”

    Zosia tilts her head, seeing right through Carol’s bluster. “You sound worried.”

    “I’m not.” Too quick. Carol clears her throat. “I just don’t want you thinking I’m secretly some kind of degenerate.”

    Zosia’s smile curves slow and knowing. “I would never think that of you, Carol.”

    “Yeah, we’ll see if you still think that after you’ve read it.” Carol laughs despite herself, tension bleeding off a fraction.

    Zosia hands her a plate—duck crisped to deep bronze, potatoes glistening, broccolini bright against white ceramic. Their fingers brush again, brief but warm. It sends a thrill up Carol’s spine, which she chooses to ignore.

    They move into the dining room next door. It’s small but layered with history. A woven kente cloth runner lies across the table. Blue-and-white Polish pottery lines a sideboard. A carved wooden mask hangs beside a framed photograph of a seaside town that must be somewhere colder, greener. The space feels curated, but not staged. Lived in.

    Carol sits, absorbing it all in.

    They eat. The duck is tender, skin shattering under the knife with a delicate crackle. The jus is rich and balanced, rosemary rising from the potatoes in fragrant bursts. Carol closes her eyes for a second after the first bite and barely suppresses a moan.

    “God, this is incredible. I mean, it’s better than sex.” Carol speaks without thinking and only realises the connotation once the words are out there.

    Zosia laughs, amusement playing on her lips. “I’m not sure whether to be flattered, or to wonder what kind of sex you’re having.”

    Carol swallows. She doesn’t want to go down that path. To admit she hasn’t had sex with anyone in a long time. Not since Helen.

    “It was Helen’s favourite,” she says. The name falls into the space between them and lingers there. Carol’s fork slows. A flicker of guilt moves through her chest—sharp and familiar. She is sitting in a warm dining room, eating with a beautiful woman, while her wife lies cold and dead in the ground.

    Carol’s been on dates since Helen’s passing. A handful. Never seeing the same woman twice. She always finds a way to ruin it—too much wine, too much silence, too much Helen in every conversation.

    Zosia is different. She doesn’t shut down when Carol brings up her wife. She doesn’t grow quiet and look for an excuse to leave.

    This isn’t a date, though, Carol tells herself firmly. Zosia needs a friend. Stability. Support. And Carol needs something to focus on now that she’s decided to stop writing.

    That’s all this is.

    Zosia wipes her mouth gently with a napkin. “How did you meet?” she asks. “You and Helen.”

    Carol stiffens almost imperceptibly—she’s not used to people asking her directly about Helen—then nods. “College. We were both taking this literature class.” A small huff of amusement as Carol is drawn into her own memories. “We spent this whole class arguing about Chaucer. I said he was overrated. She said I was insufferable… I invited her out for a drink so we could keep on arguing.”

    Zosia smiles.

    “She was naked in my bed by that night,” Carol adds before she can stop herself. Heat rises up her neck. She takes a gulp of wine before apologising. “Sorry. You probably don’t want to hear that.”

    Zosia looks genuinely puzzled. “Why wouldn’t I? It’s sweet.”

    Carol shrugs. “Straight women can be weird about lesbians talking about… that.”

    Zosia chuckles, a soft, private sound. “Yes,” she says lightly. “They can.”

    Carol catches it—the word. They. Not we. She files it away without commenting. She’s afraid to ask Zosia to elaborate—not sure she’s ready for that particular can of worms.

    “And you?” Carol asks, then clarifies, “You and Malick?”

    Zosia’s expression shifts, gentler now. “We worked at the same restaurant downtown. He was the floor manager and I was the assistant chef.”

    “Sounds like a good plot for a book,” Carol teases. “I assume it was love at first sight?”

    Zosia laughs outright, shaking her head. “No. Not in the slightest. He was so quiet. Shy. Nothing like Koumba—who hit on me the first day I walked in. I was there for months before Malick gathered the courage to speak to me.”

    Carol smiles around a bite of potato. “What changed?”

    “He brought me coffee one morning,” Zosia says softly. “No words. Just set it down next to me and nodded. Very serious. Like it was a formal offering.” Her lips curve at the memory. “He was very sweet. Kind. Patient.”

    Her fingers drift unconsciously to the ring on her left hand, thumb turning it in slow circles. Carol remembers how she was six months after Helen’s death. She’d barely been able to get out of bed most days, never mind juggling running a restaurant and planning a pregnancy.

    “He would have made a great father,” Zosia adds, voice thinning at the edges. Silence settles over the dining room, heavier this time.

    Carol watches the movement of Zosia’s fingers on the wedding band. She never wore hers. Not consistently. Not publicly. Even married, she’d kept it subtle. Tucked away. Now it lies on a silver chain under her shirt, resting over her heart. She fights the urge to reach up and touch it.

    Her mother’s voice still echoes sometimes—sharp, condemning. The conversion camp. The prayers. The shame. Even with Helen, who had been open and radiant and unafraid, Carol had stayed smaller than she should have. Helen had understood. Had been patient with her—far more patient than Carol thinks she deserved.

    Across the table, Zosia’s ring catches the light as she picks up her fork again. The two of them carry on eating in silence. Two women shaped by loss and united by new life.

    Carol takes another bite of duck, grounding herself in the sensory experience of the food; the salt and heat and fat. In the present. This is just dinner, she reminds herself.

    Just dinner.

    Chapter 7


    The plates sit abandoned on the dining table, streaked with dark jus and the faint sheen of duck fat. Carol had been particularly appreciative of the chocolate tart. The house is quiet in that dense, post-meal way—full of warmth, pleasing scents, and the low hum of the refrigerator in the kitchen.

    “I’ll get these,” Zosia says, already stacking the plates with efficient precision.

    Carol rises at the same time. “Let me.”

    “You don’t have to.”

    “I know.” Carol steps in anyway, reaching for the cutlery before Zosia can protest further. “But I’m going to.”

    Zosia gives her a look—half fond, half assessing. “You’re the guest.”

    “And you’re pregnant,” Carol replies, not unkindly.

    Zosia lifts her chin slightly. “I’m not incapable, Carol.”

    “I didn’t say you were.” Carol moves toward the kitchen with the dishes stacked in her hands. “But you gave me a scare yesterday. You worked a full shift tonight, and you cooked for us. You can let someone else clear up.”

    Zosia follows her into the kitchen despite herself. The overhead light is bright and steady, illuminating the clean lines of the counters and the neat row of herbs on the windowsill. “I don’t need to rest. I can at least help,” she says, turning on the tap. Warm water rushes into the sink.

    Carol reaches past her and turns it off again.

    Zosia blinks at her.

    “I’ve got it,” Carol says, softer now but immovable. “Sit.”

    “I can stand and supervise,” Zosia counters.

    Carol arches a brow. “You don’t trust me with dishes?”

    Zosia’s mouth twitches. “I’m sure you’re an expert. I just—like things done a certain way.”

    “Oh my God, you’re one of those.” Carol chuckles.

    “One of what?” Zosia demands.

    Carol sets the plates down on the counter and crosses her arms with a smirk. “A control freak.”

    “I am not!” Zosia insists, even as she picks the plates back up.

    “So prove it. Let me clean up, and you can go sit your ass down over there.” Carol gestures at the breakfast counter with two stools tucked neatly underneath.

    Zosia doesn’t budge. Carol gently takes the plates from her hands, and their fingers brush—brief, warm, grounding. Zosia stills for half a second but doesn’t pull away.

    “Go,” Carol repeats, nudging her lightly with her hip. “Doctor’s orders.”

    “Are you my doctor now?”

    “No, but I was there when she said stress isn’t good for you.” Carol turns the tap back on and slides the first plate under the stream. “And I’m told arguing with me can be pretty stressful. So, humour me.”

    Zosia hesitates. Control has always been easier than surrender, especially in the kitchen—her kitchen. But her shoulders are heavier tonight, and her feet ache. The day lingers in her bones.

    She exhales. “Fine. But I reserve the right to inspect them when you’re done.”

    “Yes, Chef.” Carol grins at her, amused at the way Zosia rolls her eyes.

    Zosia doesn’t go sit down like Carol told her too, though. She leans against the counter instead, arms folded loosely, watching as Carol fumbles through the choreography of someone not used to another person’s kitchen.

    “You’re using too much soap,” Zosia comments.

    Carol glances back at her. “You’re supervising now?”

    “I said I might.” A quiet smile flickers between them.

    Carol adjusts, rinsing more thoroughly this time. Steam curls upward, softening the sharp light overhead. The scent of lemon dish soap replaces rosemary in the air.

    “You really scared me yesterday,” Carol says after a moment, her voice low over the running water.

    Zosia’s gaze shifts. “I didn’t mean to.”

    “I know.” Carol scrubs carefully, jaw tight. “I just—” She stops, reconsiders. “I didn’t know what was happening, and I was so worried about you. Which is crazy, because I only just met you—”

    “I appreciated you being there,” Zosia says gently. Carol nods.

    When the last plate is rinsed and on the drying rack, Carol dries her hands and turns, leaning back against the counter opposite Zosia. The space between them feels smaller than it should.

    “I keep thinking about that hospital room,” Zosia admits quietly. Her fingers drift to her abdomen again, unconsciously. Carol watches the gesture closely. “How quickly everything could have changed. I thought… I thought if I prepared myself for the worst, it wouldn’t hurt as much if it happened.”

    “Yeah, that’s not how that works,” Carol says, not unkindly. She’s had the worst happen—Had days to prepare herself for the doctors switching off the ventilator that kept Helen’s brain dead body alive.

    “I know.” Zosia gives a small, humourless smile.

    The kitchen hums around them.

    “I’m afraid,” Zosia adds, softer now. “That I’ll do something wrong. That I’ll get attached and it will disappear… just like Malick did.”

    Carol pushes off the counter without thinking. She closes the gap until there are only inches between them.

    “You won’t go through anything alone,” Carol says, a certainty to her tone.

    Zosia searches her face. “You can’t promise that.”

    “I can.” Carol holds her gaze. “I’m promising I’ll be there… until the baby comes.” The words settle into the room.

    Zosia’s breath shifts almost imperceptibly. “You sound very sure.”

    “I am.” Carol’s known this woman for all of two days, but there’s a connection between them she can’t deny—even if she’s not ready to admit it goes beyond the child growing in Zosia’s stomach.

    Silence stretches. Not awkward, but charged in a different way. Zosia’s back is still against the counter. Carol is close enough now to feel the warmth radiating from her skin. Close enough to notice the faint citrus scent of her shampoo.

    “You should sit down,” Carol says after a moment, her tone gentler. “You look tired.”

    “I am not—”

    “You are.” Carol’s mouth curves faintly. “And Dr. Sturka is insisting this time.”

    Zosia studies her, then relents with a quiet sigh. “You are very bossy, Dr. Sturka.”

    “You don’t know the half of it.” Carol grins.

    Zosia laughs softly and guides Carol toward the living room. They settle onto the couch. This time there’s less deliberate space between them. Their knees touch immediately. Carol stiffens and crosses her legs, trying to create some distance.

    Crickets chirp outside, and the house feels wrapped in the kind of silence that only comes late at night. Zosia switches on the TV for some background noise. An episode of The Golden Girls plays.

    Carol leans back, exhaling. “You can’t carry everything alone,” she says after a while.

    “I’ve been doing it for a while now.”

    “You shouldn’t have to.”

    Zosia turns her head slightly. The air shifts. Carol’s hand rests on the cushion between them. Zosia’s fingers hover close, not quite touching—close enough that the warmth is undeniable. Neither moves first.

    Instead, Zosia lets her shoulder tip sideways until it rests lightly against Carol’s. “Well then, it’s a good thing I met you.”

    “Yeah.” Carol lets out a shaky breath, her heart hammering in her chest. She reaches up, feeling hers and Helen’s wedding rings on the chain around her neck under her shirt. It’s getting late, and she should go—let Zosia get her rest.

    Carol knows she should go. Still, when Zosia asks if she’d like another glass of wine, she finds herself saying, “Sure.”


    -


    A hand at her shoulder wakes Carol, along with some gentle shaking. “Carol?”

    “Five more minutes, Helen…” Carol sighs, burying her face deeper in the couch cushion. The shaking comes again, more insistent this time.

    “Carol. You’ve got to wake up.” The voice comes again, but Carol is awake enough now to realise it doesn’t belong to Helen. Helen is gone. The realisation hits her every morning upon waking; and it never gets any easier.

    Carol’s eyes fly open. The unfamiliar living room is washed in the pale blue of pre-dawn light. She blinks, eyes slowly focusing on Zosia bent over her. She’s dressed differently than Carol remembers. “What time is it?” Carol murmurs.

    “Four thirty.”

    Carol groans softly. “That’s not a real time—”

    “Yes, it is.” Zosia’s voice holds amusement. Unlike Carol, she’s wide awake, fresh-faced and vibrant. “We fell asleep.”

    Zosia had woken only thirty minutes earlier, curled up with Carol in a tangle of limbs. She’d extracted herself and gone to wash and dress, leaving the older woman to sleep a little longer.

    “I have to go to work,” Zosia says softly, already holding her purse and ready to leave.

    Carol pushes herself upright immediately, her post-sleep haze clearing. “I’ll drive you.”

    “You absolutely will not,” Zosia says sternly.

    “Why not?” Carol frowns, thinking she’s missed something. Has she pissed Zosia off by falling asleep on her couch?

    “Because you’re still half-asleep.”

    Carol rolls her shoulders, wincing slightly at the pain in her neck from sleeping crooked. She’d slept on her own couch for months after Helen died, not wanting to face sleeping in their bed alone. “I’m fine,” she argues.

    “You need to rest,” Zosia says, echoing Carol’s sentiment from last night. Carol sits up, facing her. The warmth from the couch still lingers between them.

    “I meant it,” Carol says quietly. “About being here.”

    Zosia holds her gaze. “I know.”

    “Are you sure you don’t want me to drive you?”

    Zosia shakes her head, but her mouth curves at Carol’s insistence. “I’m fine. You should stay and get some more sleep. You can let yourself out later.”

    “Bossy,” Carol murmurs, though she lies back down without much preamble.

    Zosia chuckles and reaches for the crochet blanket lying on the back of the couch. She drapes it over Carol’s frame, lingering for a second as she watches the other woman—eyes already closed, breathing steady.

    Zosia glances at the picture from her wedding day sitting on the mantle: her and Malick standing by their wedding cake, hand in hand holding the knife to cut the first slice. Malick in his tuxedo, gazing lovingly at Zosia in her dress. It’s Zosia’s favourite picture of them.

    She rubs absently at her still-flat stomach. Soon it will be rounded and full. She wonders what pictures will grace her mantle once the baby is born—and who will be in them. She’s not even sure why, but she wants to imagine Carol in them. Carol smiling and proud of the child she helped create.

    Zosia closes her eyes with a heavy sigh. She barely knows this woman. Maybe it’s just the hormones coursing through her system. Carol has already made it crystal clear she doesn’t want to be in the child’s life. Which means—despite her promises to be there for Zosia now—Carol will be gone in seven months.

    Gone like Malick.

    Zosia closes the front door gingerly behind her, careful not to wake the relative stranger she leaves sleeping on her couch.


    -


    Traffic on Central is unmoving. Carol drums her fingers against the steering wheel of the Chevy, jaw tight, eyes flicking between the red brake lights ahead and the clock glowing on the dashboard.

    11:52.

    She said noon to Zosia. She had messaged—confidently, firmly—I’ll be there at noon, be ready to go. The irony is not lost on her. She grabs her phone at the next standstill and thumbs out a message.

    Carol: Running late. Traffic’s a nightmare. Five, ten mins tops. Sorry.

    The reply comes almost immediately.

    Zosia: It’s fine. The appointment isn’t until one anyway.

    Carol frowns at her phone. She types with one hand, the other on the wheel as the traffic inches forward.

    Carol: Then why did you tell me twelve?

    There’s a pause. Then:

    Zosia: So you wouldn’t be late.

    Carol huffs a laugh despite herself.

    Carol: Control freak.

    The typing dots appear instantly.

    Zosia: I had chocolate tart saved for you. But now you don’t get it.

    Carol smiles, tension loosening just slightly.

    Carol: I apologise. Deeply. Profoundly. I’ll be there soon. Don’t eat it out of spite.

    A beat.

    Zosia: I make no promises.

    The traffic finally lurches forward and starts to clear. Carol exhales and merges into the next lane with more determination than strictly necessary.

    When she pulls into the restaurant parking lot at 12:07, she expects to see Zosia waiting—arms folded, mock-annoyed, probably pretending not to check her phone.

    The space out front is empty. Carol scans the lot once more.

    Nothing. No sign of Zosia. She sends another message.

    Carol: I’m here. Where’s the chocolate tart?

    No reply.

    She waits a minute. Two. The quiet inside the truck presses in around her. Finally, she climbs out, locking the door with a sharp click, and heads toward the entrance. The restaurant is closed for lunch service, but the door isn’t locked. She steps inside.

    The space is warm and shadowed, all earth tones and handwoven textiles. Exposed wooden beams cross the ceiling; turquoise tiles frame the bar. It’s done in that classic New Mexico style—intimate, deliberate, textured. The air still holds traces of cumin and roasted chilli.

    Carol hasn’t stood in this room since the last anniversary dinner she celebrated with Helen. The memory arrives uninvited—laughter over wine, a shared dessert, fingers brushing beneath the table. She pushes it down.

    “Sorry, we don’t open for lunch.”

    The voice comes from behind the bar.

    Carol startles. A stylishly dressed man in a sharply tailored orange suit leans casually against the counter, polishing a glass. His posture is easy, but his eyes are assessing.

    “Oh—no, I’m not—” Carol clears her throat. “I’m here to pick up Zosia.”

    His gaze sweeps over her—measured, not unfriendly, but thorough. Then he sets the glass down and comes around the bar. “You must be Carol,” he says, extending his hand. “Koumba.” His grip is firm. Confident.

    “I’ve heard a lot about you,” Carol replies, offering a polite smile.

    “All good things, I hope?” He lays the charm on thick, voice warm as honey.

    Carol laughs lightly. “All good,” she assures him.

    Before he can respond, the kitchen door swings open. Zosia steps out, wiping her hands on a towel, laughing at something said by the man following her.

    She’s glowing—cheeks flushed from the heat of the kitchen, hair pulled back, eyes bright. She wears a loose-fitting men’s shirt, hiding the small bump of her stomach. She looks animated, alive.

    Carol feels it before she can stop herself. A surge. Sharp and unexpected. Envy. It’s ugly and irrational and immediate.

    She tries to smooth her expression, but when her eyes flick back toward Koumba, she catches something mirrored there—a similar green-eyed monster lurking within his expression.

    “Carol! Hey. I’m sorry for keeping you,” Zosia says, approaching. “I was just finishing up.”

    The man beside her steps forward. “Ray,” he says, offering his hand to Carol.

    She takes it reluctantly. His palm is dry, grip limp. He is the most aggressively generic straight white man she has ever seen—thinning hair combed carefully over at the crown, a neatly trimmed goatee, department-store cologne that arrives a second too late.

    “Carol,” she replies, without enthusiasm.

    “I was just interviewing Ray,” Zosia explains, “for the interim head chef position.” Carol vaguely remembers Zosia telling her she had a few candidates lined up to interview for the role last week, when she’d had Carol over for dinner again.

    Ray smiles in a way that suggests he assumes the job is already his. “Well,” he says, nodding at both Carol and Koumba—who Carol assumes was introduced to him earlier—“nice to meet you both.”

    “Mm.” Carol manages a weak smile. Koumba doesn’t say anything.

    Zosia turns back to him, smile polite and professional. “Thank you for coming in, Ray. I’ll be in touch.”

    Ray gives her one last lingering look—his eyes raking over her in a way that is anything but professional—before heading for the door.

    Carol waits until it shuts. “Are you going to hire him?” she asks, aiming for casual and landing somewhere just north of brittle.

    Zosia studies her for half a second. “Of course not,” she says simply, as if that should have been obvious. “He’s a line cook. No head chef experience. I’m not even sure why he applied.”

    Relief blooms hot and fast in Carol’s chest. She doesn’t want to address why and keeps her face neutral. “Oh.”

    Across the room, Koumba exhales subtly. He looks relieved too. Zosia glances between them. “Have you two been introduced?”

    “Yes,” Koumba and Carol say at the same time.

    Carol gestures toward the door. “We should take off. Wouldn’t want to be late.”

    Zosia nods, grabbing her bag from behind the bar. “Ready.”

    Koumba steps forward as they move toward the exit, briefly stepping in front of Carol. “It was nice to meet you properly, Carol. Nice to put a face to the name—even if it was brief.”

    “Likewise,” Carol replies.

    “We should do dinner sometime,” he continues smoothly. “The three of us. Get to know one another.”

    Carol opens her mouth—instinctively prepared to deflect—but Zosia lights up before she can.

    “That’s a great idea.”

    Carol forces a smile that she hopes reads as agreeable and not cornered. “Sure. Yeah. That would be… nice.” Koumba’s eyes flick between them again, perceptive.

    “Excellent.”

    The two women say their goodbyes and step out into the bright afternoon sun. The air is crisp, the sky painfully blue. As they walk toward the truck, Zosia bumps her shoulder lightly against Carol’s.

    “You’re late,” she says, teasing.

    “Actually, I’m super early. Someone told me to come pick her up at twelve, even though her appointment isn’t until one…” Carol counters.

    Zosia smiles, sliding into the passenger seat. “Effective leadership.”

    Carol starts the engine, glancing sideways at her. “More like control freak.” Zosia only smiles wider.

    They pull out of the lot, heading toward the gynaecologist’s office and the twelve-week scan. Carol quietly wonders if she’ll get another picture to go along with the one stuck to her fridge with a magnet.

    The waiting room smells faintly of antiseptic and overbrewed coffee when they step into it. Carol stands in front of the laminated fetal development poster again. She read it at nine weeks and is reading it now like there might suddenly be new information on it.

    “At twelve weeks,” she says, tapping the corner with her finger, “the baby is about two inches. Reflexes are developing. Fingers and toes aren’t webbed anymore. And the kidneys are starting to function.”

    Zosia, seated beside her with her hands folded loosely over her abdomen, watches her with poorly concealed amusement. “Have you memorised it?” she asks.

    “I have not.”

    “You absolutely have.”

    Carol squints at the diagram as if verifying her own expertise. “It also says the vocal cords are forming.”

    Zosia smiles softly. “Oh good. I wonder if it will come out arguing?”

    “Maybe. It is genetic,” Carol replies with a smirk, without missing a beat.

    The last few weeks have fallen into an easy rhythm. Appointments. Text messages. Carol checking in after shifts. Dinners that stretch longer than they mean to. She has become a constant presence—steady, attentive, almost startlingly reliable.

    Zosia hadn’t realised how much she needed that until it was there.

    The nurse appears at the doorway, calls Zosia’s name, and scans the waiting room. Zosia stands, offering Carol a shy smile as she asks, “Would you like to come in? It’s only an external scan this time.”

    “Uh, sure.”

    Inside the exam room, it’s brighter. Cooler. Clinical. The paper on the examination table crinkles as Zosia climbs up. There’s a knock at the door, then Dr. Beeken steps in, efficient and warm in equal measure.

    “Good afternoon, Zosia,” she says, addressing the mother-to-be first, before glancing at Carol. “And you must be Carol.”

    “Yup. That’s me.”

    “Thank you for emailing the medical history form back so promptly.”

    Carol waves a hand. “No problem. I’ve got to say, it was pretty thorough. By the end of it, I was surprised you all didn’t ask for a DNA sample.”

    Zosia, already lifting her shirt and settling back against the paper-covered pillow, gestures to her stomach. “I think they already have one.”

    Carol snorts before she can stop herself. “Oh. Right. Fair.”

    Dr. Beeken smiles at both of them, then wheels the ultrasound machine closer. “Shall we?” She squeezes gel onto Zosia’s abdomen, and she inhales sharply at the cold.

    Carol instinctively moves closer, pulling the chair beside the exam table nearer without thinking. The monitor flickers to life. Static. Grain. Then—there. A small, unmistakable shape. Carol leans forward, breath catching.

    “Shit…” she whispers. The fetus is no longer an abstract blur. The head is distinct now, rounded and proportioned. Limbs extend outward in miniature symmetry.

    “That’s your baby,” Dr. Beeken says gently. Carol isn’t sure if she’s addressing Zosia, her, or both of them.

    The heartbeat fills the room a moment later—rapid, rhythmic, strong. A steady gallop that vibrates in the air.

    Carol presses her lips together. “It looked like a kidney bean three weeks ago.”

    Dr. Beeken chuckles. “They grow quickly at this stage.”

    “About the size of a plum,” Carol adds automatically—remembering from the poster out in the waiting room. Zosia turns her head on the pillow, eyes carefully taking in the image on the screen.

    Dr. Beeken adjusts the wand slightly. “Have you had any more cramping or spotting?”

    Zosia bites her lip. “Some cramping,” she admits. “The week after my last appointment.”

    Carol’s head snaps away from the screen, eyes burning into Zosia’s. “You didn’t tell me that.”

    Zosia avoids her gaze. “It passed.”

    “You should have told me,” Carol repeats, quieter now.

    “I didn’t want to worry you.”

    Dr. Beeken interjects gently. “Mild cramping can be normal as the uterus expands. As long as there’s no heavy bleeding, it’s typically not concerning. You’ll be entering your second trimester next week, which also significantly reduces the risk factor.”

    Carol exhales through her nose but doesn’t look entirely satisfied. Zosia gets the feeling Carol is going to be insufferable about her resting after this—and she’s already threatened to duct-tape her to a chair more than once when she thought Zosia was overdoing it at the restaurant.

    “How’s the morning sickness?” the doctor continues.

    “It’s easing,” Zosia replies.

    “That’s good. The placenta is taking over hormone production now. That often helps.”

    On the screen, the tiny figure shifts. An arm—no bigger than a thumbnail—jerks upward.

    Carol gasps like she’s just seen footage of Bigfoot in the woods. “It moved!”

    “Yes,” Dr. Beeken says, amused.

    “Is it supposed to do that?” Carol asks, eyes wide, as if the baby might suddenly somersault off-screen. “That wasn’t on the poster.”

    Zosia squints at the monitor. “I didn’t feel anything.”

    “The fetus is still very small,” Dr. Beeken explains. “Give it a few more weeks. You’ll start feeling movement soon enough. Just don’t complain when the baby starts kicking your bladder.”

    Zosia laughs softly, while Carol stares at the screen like she’s watching live theatre. Another twitch. A flicker of motion.

    “That’s—” She breaks off, laughing under her breath. “That’s incredible.”

    Dr. Beeken captures a still image. “Would you like another picture?”

    “Yes,” Zosia says immediately.

    The printer hums softly. Two glossy black-and-white images slide out. Dr. Beeken hands them over without comment. Zosia takes both, glances at them briefly, then holds one out to Carol.

    Carol accepts it carefully, with the same reverence she took the first one. She studies the grainy silhouette. The curve of the head. The suggestion of a spine. Zosia watches Carol’s face—the awe, the fierce protectiveness already forming there, the unguarded wonder.

    “We made that,” she says quietly, without realising she’s said it out loud.

    Chapter 8

    Chapter Notes

    As ever, thanks for reading and for all of the lovely comments! It's great to know people are enjoying reading this as much as I'm enjoying writing it.

    The diner is already half full when Carol pushes through the glass door, the bell above it jangling sharply. She checks her watch as she steps inside. “Shit,” she mutters under her breath, already ten minutes later than she told Zosia she would be.

    The place hasn’t changed in years. Blue and yellow vinyl booths line the long wall of windows facing the street outside, the early afternoon sun pouring through the glass and painting bright rectangles across the checkerboard floor. The air smells like fried onions, coffee that’s been sitting on a burner too long, and the faint sweetness of homemade apple pie.

    Carol pauses just inside the doorway, scanning the booths. She spots Zosia immediately. She’s sitting halfway down the row of windows, one leg tucked beneath her in the booth. A paperback rests open in her hands. She’s completely absorbed, brow faintly furrowed in concentration.

    Carol starts toward her—and then stops mid-step. Because she recognises that cover. A red-headed heroine and a serious looking dark haired pirate—who looks a lot like Zosia in a way Carol doesn’t want to begin to think about—stare up at Carol from across the room.

    Wycaro.

    Carol closes her eyes briefly and drags a hand down her face. “Of course,” she murmurs. Zosia had told her she’d ordered the book. Carol just didn’t expect to see her reading it in public.

    Zosia notices her a moment later. She glances up from the page, expression brightening immediately.

    “You made it.” She greets the other woman cheerily.

    “Barely,” Carol says, sliding into the booth opposite her. “Traffic was a nightmare. Sorry for being late. Again.”

    “You’re here now.” Zosia gives a little shrugs as she tucks a bookmark neatly between the pages and closes the book, setting it on the table between them. Carol stares at it like it might explode.

    “You know,” she says slowly, “there are whole libraries full of far more respectable literature you could be reading.”

    Zosia tilts her head, amused. “You don’t think your book is respectable?”

    “I think it involves pirates, sword fights, and at least three morally questionable romantic decisions.”

    “That doesn’t make it bad.”

    Carol groans softly and drops her forehead against her hands. “Please don’t tell me you unironically like it,” she mutters. “I’m begging you.”

    Zosia laughs under her breath. “It’s actually quite good.”

    “Zosia…” Carol sighs, and shifts slightly in the booth. Compliments from strangers are easy enough to deflect, but from Zosia they land hard.

    “I’m serious.” Zosia taps the cover lightly. “It’s not the kind of thing I usually read, but I like your prose.”

    Carol makes a strangled noise somewhere between embarrassment and despair. Zosia watches her with growing amusement.

    “How do you even survive book readings?” she asks. “All those people gushing over your work.”

    Carol lifts her head again. “That’s different.”

    “How?”

    “Those are strangers.” Carol gestures vaguely toward the book. “Having people I know read my work has always felt a bit like… exposing myself.”

    Zosia arches a brow. “That’s a little dramatic.”

    “How would you feel me watching you create a new recipe?” Carol shoots back, but doesn’t get the response she expects.

    “I wouldn’t mind. I miss having a test subject… Malick was always my guinea pig when it came to new recipes.” Zosia smiles, but there’s a tinge of sadness to it.

    Carol decides to change the subject to something that feels less like a minefield. She clears her throat, then nods toward the paperback. “Where are you up to, anyway?”

    “The scene in the ship’s galley.” Zosia answers without needing to check.

    Carol’s eyes light up with recognition. It’s one of the more…spicy scenes of the first book.

    “Oh no.” Carol groans, covering her face with her hands as she feels a searing heat spreading across her cheeks.

    “Oh yes,” Zosia continues, eyes alight with mischief. “Where Raban and Lucasia are doing things that would absolutely get a kitchen shut down by any health inspector in their right mind.”

    Carol laughs, leaning back in the booth and trying to take the teasing in her stride. “No health inspectors in Wycaro I’m afraid.”

    “That seems like a serious oversight.”

    “Pirates,” Carol shrugs. “Standards are lower.”

    Before Zosia can reply, a woman approaches their table with an open notebook in her hand. “Look who finally decided to show up.”

    Carol glances up and grins. “Hey, Bri.”

    The waitress, Bri, is a middle-aged Asian American woman with short dark hair and the calm authority of someone who has spent decades wrangling impatient diners. She sets two menus on the table out of habit but doesn’t bother opening them.

    “You want your usual?” she asks Carol.

    “Please,” Carol says.

    She’d written most of her first Wycaro novel right there in the diner, with Bri helpfully keeping her caffeinated throughout.

    Bri nods knowingly. “Burger, fries, strawberry milkshake.”

    Carol gives a small salute.

    Bri turns to Zosia. “And for you?”

    Zosia glances at Carol, then back at Bri. “I’ll have the same.”

    Bri raises an approving eyebrow. “Good choice. I like the easy customers.” She shoots a wink at Zosia as she scribbles something on her pad. She let’s them know it won’t be too long, and then disappears back behind the counter.

    Carol watches her go, then leans back in the booth, turning her attention back to Zosia. “So,” she says. “How’s things at the restaurant?”

    Zosia brightens immediately. “I hired the new head chef.”

    Carol perks up. “Please tell me it’s not Ray.”

    Zosia snorts. “It is absolutely not Ray.”

    “Thank God.”

    “His name is Bob,” Zosia continues. “He has actual experience running a kitchen. I asked him to cook for me during the interview and he was excellent.”

    Carol nods approvingly.

    “Also,” Zosia adds, “he’s married and, unlike Ray, didn’t spend the entire interview trying to look down my shirt.”

    Carol visibly relaxes. “Oh good. So probably not a sleaze.”

    Zosia smiles faintly. “He and his wife just moved to the city.”

    “That’s promising.”

    “I also told my staff about the baby.”

    Carol pauses. “You did?”

    Zosia nods. “Sixteen weeks now. I thought it was time.”

    Carol’s gaze flicks briefly downward. Even through the loose shirt Zosia’s wearing today, the small curve beneath the fabric is more noticeable than it was a few weeks ago.

    Carol nods slowly. “When does Bob take over the kitchen?”

    “Soon,” Zosia says. “I’m going to work alongside him for a couple of weeks. After that I’ll step back and let him run things.”

    “And you?”

    “I’ll still run the restaurant,” Zosia says. “Mostly the administrative side. Koumba can run the floor.”

    Carol nods slowly. Zosia speaks off Koumba often. It sounds like she has all the faith in the world in him. Carol studies her for a moment. “So no more twelve-hour shifts on your feet?”

    Zosia smiles reassuringly. “No more twelve-hour shifts.”

    Carol exhales. “Good.”

    “I’m fine, by the way” Zosia adds. “No cramping. I just get tired a little easier.”

    Carol nods, satisfied enough. There’s a small pause before Zosia speaks again. “Are you still okay for dinner this weekend?” Carol looks up. “With Koumba.”

    Something tightens briefly in her chest. The date had been sitting quietly in the back of her mind all week. Sunday will be two years to the day since Helen passed. But Zosia is watching her expectantly, and Carol forces an easy shrug.

    “Saturday, right? Sure. Wouldn’t miss it.”

    Zosia brightens immediately. “Great! I’m excited for you two to meet properly.”

    Carol hums noncommittally just as Bri reappears balancing two plates on one arm and a tray with two tall milkshakes in the other hand.

    “Alright,” she says, sliding the food onto the table. “Two burgers, fries, and two strawberry shakes.”

    Once the food is set in front of them, Bri moves off to take the next table’s order. For a few minutes the conversation fades into comfortable quiet as they start eating.

    Carol reaches automatically for a fry, dips it into her milkshake, and takes a bite. Across the table, Zosia freezes. She stares at Carol with abject horror.

    Carol notices eventually. “What?”
