    Zosia slowly shakes her head. “Americans,” she mutters.

    Carol grins, “Snob.”

    “Oh, come on. That is a crime!” Zosia objects.

    “It’s delicious!”

    “It’s disgusting.” Zosia grimaces as Carol deliberately dips another fry into the milkshake. “I thought I was supposed to be the one with weird pregnancy cravings, not you.” She scoffs.

    “You should try it. You’ll love it.”

    “Absolutely not!”

    “You’re being totally judgy right now.”

    “I am a chef,” Zosia says firmly. “I have standards.”

    “Just one. You can even dip it in mine so you don’t contaminate yours.” Carol slides the strawberry milkshake across the Formica table and toward Zosia.

    She hesitates, eyeing the milkshake like it’s a hand grenade. Carol raises an eyebrow, grinning “C’mon, Zos, live a little.”

    With visible reluctance, Zosia picks up a fry. She dips it carefully into the pink foam of Carol’s milkshake. Carol watches with poorly concealed delight.

    Zosia takes a cautious bite, then pauses. Her brow furrows slightly. Carol leans forward. “Well?” She asks.

    Zosia chews slowly, then looks up, clearly surprised. “…That’s actually good.”

    Carol beams in triumph. “See?” she says smugly. “Don’t knock it until you’ve tried it.”

    “Hmm.” Zosia hums. Reaching over, she immediately tries to dip another fry in to Carol’s milkshake.

    “Hey!” The other woman protests, covering the rim of her glass with a protective hand. “You only get the first dip free.”

    Zosia pouts, her hand still hovering with the fry mid-air as she flashes those big doe eyes of hers at Carol. “How about I trade you? Unrestricted access to your milkshake in exchange for me making you another chocolate tart?”

    Carol considers the deal for all of two seconds before she removes her hand and flops back against the booth with a dejected sigh. “You know, if you keep feeding me those things I’m going to look nine months pregnant way before you do.”

    Zosia scoffs, happily dipping her fry into Carol’s milkshake. “Don’t be silly. You look great.”

    Carol ducks her head, heat rising up her cheeks again. When she glances up, Zosia is still looking at her. Zosia smiles, drops her head and looks away as she pops the milkshake covered fry in her mouth.

    Across from her, Carol shifts her gaze to the Wycaro book— and tries to push thoughts of the galley scene far from her mind.


    -


    Saturday evening arrives faster than Carol would like.

    Her bedroom looks like a minor clothing explosion has taken place. Two pairs of shoes sit abandoned on the floor, a tan blazer is draped over the back of the chair, and three different shirts have been rejected and tossed across the bed in varying states of inside-out defeat.

    Carol stands in front of the mirror with her arms folded, staring critically at her own reflection. “This is ridiculous,” she mutters.

    On the bed behind her lies the outfit she’d originally planned to wear: dark jeans, boots, and a soft charcoal button-down. Comfortable. Uncomplicated. The sort of thing she wears most days when she isn’t pretending in front of a bookstore full of straight women with a thing for mommy porn.

    Next to it hangs the alternative she’s just pulled from the closet. The maroon wrap dress. Carol eyes it suspiciously.

    It’s the kind of thing she usually reserves for book signings or public readings—events where her publisher gently encourages her to appear a little more… approachable. The fabric is soft and drapes easily, the neckline modest but unmistakably feminine.

    She sighs and drags a hand through her hair.

    The problem isn’t Zosia. Carol has never felt like she has to perform around her. Over the last month they’ve settled into an easy rhythm—prenatal appointments, dinners at Zosia’s apartment, long evenings spent talking about everything and nothing while Carol pretends not to notice how much she enjoys being there.

    With Zosia she can wear whatever she likes and it doesn’t seem to matter. But tonight isn’t just Zosia. Tonight includes Koumba.

    Carol doesn’t really know Koumba. She only knows what Zosia’s told her—that Koumba runs the front of house at the restaurant and has been helping pick up the slack while Zosia slows down a little.

    Carol stares at the dress again with the charcoal shirt in her hands. She wonders if Zosia will be dressed up tonight. Koumba has probably seen her dressed up hundreds of times before. The thought irritates her more than it should.

    “Screw it,” she mutters.

    A few minutes later she’s stepping into the maroon fabric, pulling the wrap tight around her waist and tying the knot at her hip. The material falls just above her knees. She adds a pair of black heels she rarely bothers wearing and studies the result in the mirror.

    She looks… different. More polished. It’s not entirely unpleasant, the colour complements her skin tone and the fabric hugs her modest curves in all the right ways.

    Carol tilts her head, examining the effect with mild suspicion, like someone who’s accidentally wandered into the wrong version of themselves. She thought she’d put this shit behind her when she insisted her last book tour really would be her last.

    Her phone buzzes on the bedside table. Carol jumps and her hand shoots for it automatically.

    Zosia.

    Her stomach does that small, annoying flip it’s started doing whenever Zosia’s name appears on the screen. Her first instinct is to check the time, just in case she’s lost an hour disassociating in front of the mirror.

    Six o’clock. Dinner is at seven. She exhales slowly. Not late. Not even close. She leaves the bomb site that is her bedroom and heads downstairs to the living room. Only then does she open the message.

    Zosia: I reached the captain’s quarters scene…

    Carol snorts softly. Zosia has been sending her updates on her Wycaro progress all week. She’s spent every spare moment—between prepping for dinner service and taking care of the office admin—devouring Carol’s words. A second message appears almost immediately.

    Zosia: I have questions.

    Carol smiles despite herself and types back.

    Carol: What do you want to know?

    The typing bubble appears right away.

    Zosia: How is that physically possible?

    Carol stares at the screen. She laughs under her breath.

    Carol: It is if you’re determined enough.

    The message sends before she can second-guess it. Immediately Carol feels a small spike of panic. Was it too flirty? Definitely too flirty she decides.

    “Smooth, Sturka” she mutters. She quickly types again, trying to distract from the last message.

    Carol: Are you actually reading the book or just skimming the sex scenes?

    The reply comes a few seconds later.

    Zosia: It’s not my fault there’s a lot of smut in this book.

    Carol leans against the dresser, already smiling. Another message follows.

    Zosia: I think the writer might be some kind of deviant.

    A month ago that might have stung. Carol has always been careful about curating her reputation. The majority of her fans don’t even know she’s gay, never mind that she was married to her manager.

    She’s comfortable enough with Zosia now to recognise her words are meant to be teasing, not a direct attack. Zosia has no idea Carol’s own mother wielded the word ‘deviant’ against her like a weapon.

    Carol pushes thoughts of her mother back down in to the locked box in the pit of her soul and concentrates instead on replying to Zosia.

    Carol: You didn’t hear it from me, but I think the writer might be gay.

    The typing bubble appears instantly and Carol bites her lip. She’s not known for this kind of whimsy. Zosia just brings it out in her. Carol doesn’t stop to think about why.

    Zosia: That makes sense. The writing comes across very sapphic…

    Her phone buzzes again.

    Zosia: A lot of focus on breasts.

    Carol stares at the message. “Oh, you’re baiting me,” she murmurs to herself, yet she types her reply anyway.

    Carol: Are you an expert on sapphic writing?

    The reply arrives almost immediately.

    Zosia: Not an expert.

    Another message follows before Carol can respond.

    Zosia: I’d say 50/50.

    Carol freezes. Her thumb hovers over the screen. She reads the message again.

    50/50.

    The words sit there, deceptively casual. Carol’s mind immediately begins trying to interpret them. Fifty-fifty. Was that—

    She frowns slightly.

    It wouldn’t be the first time Zosia has hinted at being something other than straight. There have been little comments here and there over the past month. Offhand things that Carol never quite knows how seriously to take.

    “Don’t overthink it,” she tells herself quietly. It wouldn’t be the first time she’d misinterpreted an overly friendly straight woman’s attention towards her. Carol’s gaze drifts away from the phone and lands across the living room.

    On the liquor cabinet.

    The bottles sit exactly where they always have—whiskey, rum, gin, tequila. Most of them have gathered dust over the last month. Carol hasn’t touched them.

    Driving Zosia to appointments. Picking her up after work when they have dinner plans. Making sure she gets home safely. Alcohol and driving don’t mix well for Carol.

    Helen had spent the better part of a year cleaning up the mess from Carol’s last DUI. Lawyers. Court appearances. Conversations Carol still doesn’t particularly enjoy remembering.

    She isn’t proud of her record— or what it took to make it quietly disappear.

    Carol can be reckless, particularly when it comes to her own life, but she’s not stupid enough to risk Zosia and the baby by getting behind the wheel after drinking.

    Still. Tonight feels… different. Carol glances back at the clock. Forty-five minutes before she’s expected at Per Se, the fancy Old Town restaurant Zosia owns with her brother in-law.

    Her stomach twists slightly. Dinner with Koumba tonight. And then tomorrow—

    Carol presses her lips together.

    Two years.

    The thought sits heavy in her chest. She pushes herself away from the liquor cabinet and walks into the kitchen. The vodka has been sitting untouched in the freezer for a month. Carol pulls it out, the glass fogging faintly in the warm air.

    She grabs a short tumbler from the cupboard and pours without measuring. The liquid splashes halfway up the glass. A double. At least.

    Carol lifts it, hesitates for half a second, then knocks it back in one swallow. The burn hits immediately—sharp and clean, sliding down her throat and settling warm in her stomach.

    “For courage,” she mutters to herself, the house around her empty and hollow. Carol tells herself the nerves she feels are about meeting Koumba properly. Not about the way Zosia lights up whenever she talks about him.

    Her phone buzzes again on the counter where she left it. She picks it up, stomach fluttering at the sight of Zosia’s name again.

    Zosia: Don’t be late.

    Carol: I’m never late.

    Zosia: You’re never not late…

    Carol smiles despite herself.

    Carol: See you at seven. On the dot.

    She sets the phone down and glances once more at her reflection in the darkened microwave door.

    Maroon dress. Black heels. A faint flush still warming her cheeks from the vodka. Carol just needs to curl her hair, add a little makeup, and she’ll be ready to go. She’s determined to be on time.

    She eyes the bottle on the counter warily. Then pours another shot. For courage.


    -


    The streets of Old Town are just as busy as Carol expected for a Saturday evening, the pavements crowded with couples and small groups drifting between bars and restaurants.

    Carol’s taxi pulls up out front of Per Se. The restaurant’s windows glow amber against the dark street, the same warm, understated elegance she remembers from the last time she came here. Soft lighting, polished wood, the suggestion of quiet luxury rather than anything ostentatious.

    She pays the driver, mindful to give him a healthy tip, before climbing out of the back seat. Her heels click against the concrete as she approaches the front entrance. She regrets them immediately. They look good with the dress, but the click-clack of them draws unwanted attention from a man lingering by the doorway with a cigarette in hand.

    Inside, the familiar scent of garlic, wine, and seared meat greets her. The low murmur of conversation fills the room, punctuated by the soft clink of cutlery and glasses. The dining room is nearly full. Candles flicker on each table, reflecting in the dark glass of the windows.

    A young waitress recognises her from previous visits and offers a friendly smile, approaching her while the host is off seating a couple.

    “Hello, Carol. Are you here to see Zosia?”

    “Hi, Kusimayu. Yes, I am,” Carol says, smoothing her dress instinctively.

    The waitress nods toward the back of the room. “She’s in the kitchen.”

    Of course she is. Carol tries not to roll her eyes. It’s supposed to be Zosia’s night off.

    “Let me take you through,” Kusimayu offers, and Carol follows, weaving through the tables toward the rear corridor. The noise of the dining room fades as the heavy swing door to the kitchen closes behind them.

    Heat rushes at her immediately. The kitchen is bright and loud—metal pans clattering, burners hissing, chefs moving quickly between stations. The smell is intoxicating: butter, roasted garlic, something citrusy cutting through the richness.

    Zosia is standing near the stainless-steel prep counter, wearing a flowing blue and green dress that gathers over her slight baby bump before the fabric spills into a long, airy skirt. She’s laughing at something someone has said, head tipped slightly back, one hand resting against the curve of her stomach.

    For a second, Carol just watches her.

    A month ago, seeing Zosia here—so comfortable, so entirely part of this world—would have felt strange. Now it feels almost… normal. Zosia notices her, and her face lights up.

    “Carol!”

    She wipes her hands quickly on a towel and walks over, the slit at the front of her dress showing a flash of leg.

    “You made it.”

    Carol glances at the clock above the service window.

    “On time too.” Carol grins, feeling proud of herself when she sees she’s actually a few minutes early.

    “Just,” Zosia teases. She looks Carol up and down, a small smile forming.

    “You look nice.”

    Carol immediately feels the vodka warmth creep up her neck.

    “It’s not too much?”

    “No, you look wonderful,” Zosia says, trying to dispel Carol’s discomfort. The corners of her eyes crinkle with her smile. Her gaze lingers on the dress a second longer than necessary before she looks back up. “You clean up well.”

    Before Carol can respond, a deep voice cuts in behind them. “Carol, lovely to see you again.”

    She turns and finds Koumba walking through the swing door she just came through. A server passes behind him carrying a tray of glasses. Koumba shifts slightly to let them through without even looking, the movement instinctive. Unlike Carol, he belongs there.

    “Koumba.” She fixes him with the same fake smile she usually reserves for over-eager fans at her book signings. Beside her, Zosia hovers, watching the exchange closely. Carol knows she wants this night to be a success, for her new friend and her brother-in-law to get along. With this in mind, she adds, “Nice to see you.”

    “Well,” he says, straightening slightly as he does up the single button of his flamboyant red suit jacket. “We should go claim our table before someone gives it away. We’re very busy tonight.”

    He gestures toward the dining room doors, and they step out of the heat and noise of the kitchen and back into the softer atmosphere of the restaurant.

    The lighting feels warmer out here, candlelight reflecting against polished glass and dark wood. Conversations murmur around them, the steady rhythm of a busy Saturday evening.

    A corner table near the window has been set aside for them. Koumba pulls out Zosia’s chair automatically. Carol moves at the same time, the two gestures overlapping slightly before Zosia sits in the chair Carol pulled out for her.

    Koumba notices. Carol sees it in the brief flicker of his expression, something observant passing behind his eyes. He chuckles and extends his hand for Carol to take the offered seat.

    “Thanks,” she says, taking the seat while Koumba walks around to the other side of the table. Carol feels the nervous tension she carried begin to settle. The warmth from the vodka earlier lingers faintly in her chest, softening the sharp edges of the evening.

    Koumba reaches for the wine bottle waiting in the centre of the table and pours two glasses with careful precision. When he reaches Zosia’s place, he swaps the bottle for a chilled carafe of water, filling her glass from that instead. Carol notices the automatic nature of it.

    For a moment, the three of them sit in the warm glow of the candlelight while the restaurant hums around them. Cutlery clinks softly against plates from nearby tables, a low ripple of conversation rising and falling beneath the gentle music playing somewhere overhead.

    Koumba leans back slightly in his chair, studying Carol with open curiosity. “So,” he says, “you’re the pirate novelist.”

    Carol sighs, instantly feeling on edge once more. “That’s one way of putting it,” she says coolly.

    “Zosia has been very enthusiastic about them lately.”

    Carol shoots Zosia a sideways look, while Koumba smiles faintly into his wine. “Apparently they’re rather good. A lot of spicy scenes, from what I hear from Zosia.”

    “It’s not that much,” Carol protests weakly.

    Zosia laughs. It’s light and melodic and Carol never gets tired of hearing it. “It’s a lot.”

    Carol rubs a hand across the back of her neck, half embarrassed and half amused. “Well,” she mutters. “My readers seem to enjoy it.”

    “Zosia certainly does,” Koumba says mildly.

    Zosia swats his arm. “Stop.”

    He chuckles, clearly pleased with himself.

    A server arrives with a small plate of amuse-bouches—delicate bites arranged like tiny works of art. Zosia thanks them quietly before nudging the plate toward Carol. “Try these,” she insists.

    Carol picks one up cautiously. “What is it?”

    “Scallop tartare,” Koumba answers before Zosia can. “Citrus, fennel, a little chilli.”

    Carol takes a bite. Her eyebrows lift immediately. “Okay,” she admits. “That’s so good.”

    Zosia beams, clearly pleased. “Thank you.”

    “You made these? I thought you were taking the night off?” Carol chides.

    To her left, Koumba scoffs. “Have you tried to keep this woman out of the kitchen? She’s like a homing pigeon. Anywhere there’s an oven within twenty feet, she’ll find it.”

    Carol chuckles softly and takes a sip of her wine. She notices something as the conversation continues—something subtle.

    Every time Zosia speaks, Koumba watches her carefully. The way people who have been through something together learn to read the smallest shifts in mood. It shouldn’t bother Carol. It really shouldn’t. And yet a faint, uncomfortable tension begins to coil in her chest.

    Because Koumba belongs here. In this restaurant. In Zosia’s life. In a way Carol doesn’t. He’s been by her side through some of the best—and worst—times of her life, and probably always will be. She lifts her wine glass again, taking a slightly longer drink this time.

    Across the table, Zosia talks animatedly about the kitchen renovations she and Koumba want to start later this year. Her hands move when she talks, expressive, alive. Carol stares at her without meaning to.

    She tries not to think about the way her stomach churns when Zosia laughs at something Koumba says, how Zosia reaches across the table and touches his arm with a sense of familiarity—about how easily she’s slipped into the habit of wanting Zosia’s attention entirely to herself.

    It’s ridiculous, but she wishes it were just her and Zosia at the table; like it was at the dinner. Easy and intimate.

    The server returns to clear the amuse-bouche plates and replace them with the main course.

    Koumba leans forward slightly as dishes are set down—duck confit with a glossy reduction, roasted vegetables arranged with deliberate precision. He glances at each plate like a manager assessing execution rather than a guest anticipating dinner.

    “Our new hire, Bob, cooked the duck. Tell me what you think,” he says to Carol.

    There’s challenge in it. Not hostility—but scrutiny. Carol picks up her fork, cutting into the duck. The skin cracks softly under pressure, steam rising in a thin ribbon.

    She tastes it. The meat is tender. The fat rendered clean. The sauce rich without being heavy. She swallows slowly. “It’s excellent,” she says honestly.

    Koumba studies her face for a beat too long, as if verifying whether she’s being polite. “You’re not just saying that? Zosia still has her doubts about our new hire.”

    Carol lifts an eyebrow. “I’m not in the habit of lying. Bob did a good job. He’s not a patch on Zosia, but it’s nice.”

    Zosia smiles beside her, clearly pleased. She reaches for her water glass, and Koumba instinctively shifts forward to refill it before she even lifts it fully.

    The movement is automatic. Efficient. Familiar. Carol watches the gesture—and something sharp twists in her chest. He knows the rhythm of her restaurant. Knows her needs and how to anticipate them before she even has to ask. To Carol, it goes far beyond the attentiveness of a brother-in-law.

    Carol swallows another bite of duck to push the thought down. “How long have you guys had this place?” she asks, aiming for neutral as she picks up her wine glass again.

    Koumba glances at Zosia. “It feels like a lifetime since the three of us bought it.” The words land lightly—but carry a weight behind them. There’s an empty chair at their table, and it might as well have Malick’s name on it.

    The memory of Zosia’s husband lingers over them like an uninvited guest. Carol feels it like a quiet reminder that she is the outsider at this table. She takes another slow sip of wine.

    Zosia isn’t the only one carrying the weight of an absent spouse. Helen has been on Carol’s mind a lot of late, particularly as the anniversary of her passing grows nearer. She can’t help but consider what Helen might think of Zosia and the baby.

    She’d want Carol to step up, to be in the child’s life. But Carol’s already built her life around another person once before. She failed Helen. The last thing she needs is something precious and helpless depending on her.

    Zosia turns toward her suddenly. “Are you okay?” she asks, hand reaching across the table for Carol’s arm. Carol does her best to resist the urge to pull away.

    She blinks. “Yeah. Why?”

    “You went quiet on us.”

    Koumba’s gaze shifts to Carol again, silently observing the interaction between the two women.

    Carol forces a small smile. “Sorry. It’s all good… just enjoying the food so much.”

    Zosia doesn’t look convinced—but she lets it go. The main courses are cleared, replaced by a second lighter course, and conversation resumes on safer topics this time.

    Koumba asks about Carol’s next book project. Carol answers tactfully, telling him she’s taking a break from Wycaro for a while. It feels controlled again. Manageable. Until—

    A laugh erupts from the table behind them, loud and unabashed. Carol glances instinctively over her shoulder. It sounds just like Helen’s. A couple sits close together, leaning in toward each other. The woman rests her hand on her partner’s wrist in an unconscious gesture of intimacy.

    Carol’s jaw tightens. She turns back, and Zosia is watching her. Noticing. “Are you sure you’re okay?” Zosia asks again, quieter now.

    “I said I’m fine.” The words come out sharper than she intends as she reaches for her glass again. Koumba’s eyes narrow slightly—not at her tone, but at the shift in energy around the table.

    Zosia’s smile falters. “Okay,” she says gently.

    Carol immediately regrets snapping at her. She exhales through her nose and forces herself to soften.

    “I just…uh…”

    Zosia reaches across the table without hesitation and rests her fingers briefly over Carol’s hand. It’s a grounding touch. Warm and real. The contact lingers longer than necessary as Zosia stares at her with genuine concern in those big brown eyes of hers.

    Koumba watches closely. Carol sees the micro-expression that crosses his face but can’t quite identify it. He withdraws slightly into his chair, giving them space—but his gaze remains attentive.

    It feels like a silent recalibration. Like he’s just measured something important.

    “I’m fine,” Carol finally splutters out, and Zosia pulls her hand back. Carol feels both a sense of loss and relief at the same time. The absence of warmth makes Carol aware of how much she’d been holding onto it.

    Koumba clears his throat. “Is everyone ready for dessert?” he asks briskly, shifting the tone of the table back to neutral ground.

    Zosia nods quickly. “Could you ask the kitchen to send it?”

    “Of course.” He nods at Zosia, the two of them sharing a look as he pushes his chair back and rises to his feet.

    “Ladies, I’ll be right back,” Koumba announces before he takes off for the kitchen, moving through the crowded dining room with the confidence of someone who controls the space.

    Carol watches him walk away. Then she turns to Zosia, eyes narrowing. “Does he always do that?” she asks.

    “Do what?” Zosia frowns, not quite following.

    “Watch.”

    Zosia tilts her head. “He’s… protective.”

    “Of you?”

    “Of his family.”

    Carol forces herself to nod, and for the first time that evening, she feels something sharper than her own insecurity. It’s clarity. Koumba isn’t trying to compete with her for Zosia’s attention—he doesn’t need to. Koumba is already positioned at the centre of Zosia’s world.

    And Carol is balanced precariously on the edge of it, one foot hovering over the edge as she refuses to allow herself to fall.

    She picks up her wine again, realising she’s already finished the glass. She refills it from the bottle in the middle of the table and takes another large gulp. It doesn’t erase the tightening in her chest. Carol wishes she could order something stronger.

    Koumba returns moments later with dessert plates balanced expertly in his hands. He sets them down one at a time with practised ease.

    The chocolate tart glistens under the dim light, dark and glossy beneath a thin dusting of cocoa. Zosia’s fork sinks easily into the delicate crust.

    Carol watches the movement without really tasting the bite she’s just taken. The chocolate melts on her tongue, rich and smooth. It’s good. It’s always good—but tonight, with the spectre of Helen hanging over her thoughts and Koumba’s all-encompassing presence at the table, even dessert tastes a little bitter.

    Chapter 9

    Carol swallows and takes another sip of wine. The glass is emptier than she remembers. Across the table, Koumba takes a bite of his own dessert. “Still the best thing on the menu,” he says casually.

    Zosia smiles faintly. “Flattery will not improve your portion size.”

    Koumba chuckles under his breath. “I’m being quite serious. Malick used to say the same thing.” The name lands softly on the table, but the air shifts all the same.

    Zosia’s expression changes, her eyes a little less bright. She lowers her fork and lets out a short breath through her nose. “He always ordered two,” she says, a fondness creeping into her voice. “Said one was never enough.”

    Koumba nods. “That man had no self-control when it came to chocolate.”

    For a second, the two of them share the same look—something private, something warm and familiar. The kind of look that comes from years of sharing the same person.

    Carol stares down at her plate, feeling like an outsider looking in. Her fork drags slowly through the tart, cutting a neat line through the glossy surface. Malick. Helen. Two ghosts at the table.

    She lifts her wine glass again and drains what’s left before she even realises she’s doing it. The warmth spreads through her chest, loosening something she’s been holding tight all evening.

    Across from her, Zosia laughs softly at something Koumba murmurs. She reaches across the table without thinking and nudges his arm with the back of her fingers.

    Carol watches the movement. The easy familiarity of it. The way Koumba glances at Zosia the moment she speaks. The way he automatically picks up the water carafe to refill Zosia’s glass.

    Carol watches Zosia take a sip without looking, trusting it will be there. Something twists unpleasantly in Carol’s chest.

    Carol sets her empty glass down a little harder than she intends. The sound cuts briefly through the quiet murmur of the restaurant.

    Zosia looks over immediately, eyes full of concern as they land on her. Carol can see her physically biting on her lip to keep from asking if she’s okay.

    Carol knows she isn’t acting right. She’d love to be able to blame it on the second glass of wine—and the vodka she knocked back earlier—but she knows it runs deeper than that. She forces a tight smile, but it does little to reassure the other woman.

    Koumba reaches for the wine bottle in the centre of the table. “Another?” he offers.

    Carol’s hand moves before she can stop herself, sliding her glass toward him. “Please.”

    He pours with the same careful precision as before, the deep red wine curling against the glass.

    “Careful,” Koumba says mildly as he sets the bottle down. “That one sneaks up on you.”

    Carol lets out a short laugh that doesn’t sound entirely like humour. “Oh, I’m sure I’ll manage.”

    Zosia frowns slightly at the tone but doesn’t comment. She takes another small bite of her dessert.

    “This really is good,” Carol mutters after a moment, gesturing vaguely with her fork. “I see why Malick liked it so much.”

    Zosia’s smile returns, softer now.

    “He had terrible self-control around sweets,” Koumba offers.

    “So you said.” Carol’s tight smile grows even more strained.

    Koumba leans back in his chair, unaware Carol is spiralling before his very eyes. “Zosia, do you remember all the times he would sneak into the kitchen at the end of service to hide the leftovers whenever you made a batch?”

    Zosia rolls her eyes fondly. “That happened once.”

    “One a week, actually.” Koumba chuckles.

    Carol watches the exchange unfold in front of her like a scene from a play she wasn’t cast in.

    The inside jokes. The shared corrections. The easy rhythm of people who have known each other long enough to finish each other’s sentences.

    The knot in Carol’s chest tightens. She takes another swallow of wine. Her third glass. Carol feels light-headed.

    “You two have a lot of history,” she says suddenly, interrupting the pair’s trip through memory lane. The words slip out before she really thinks them through.

    Zosia glances up. “I suppose we do.”

    “We built this place together. All three of us,” Koumba adds simply.

    Carol nods slowly.

    “Right,” she says. “With Malick.”

    Her fork rests untouched beside her plate now, her appetite gone. She clutches the wine glass in her hand, reluctant to put it down.

    Zosia tilts her head slightly. “Carol?”

    Carol looks up again. The warmth of the alcohol hums just beneath her skin now, dulling the careful restraint she usually keeps wrapped tightly around her thoughts.

    “It’s just…” she starts, then stops.

    Zosia’s brow furrows; she leans closer to Carol, eyes wide as she asks, “What is it?”

    Carol exhales through her nose and gives a small, careless shrug.

    “Nothing.” She swallows what she really wants to say, along with another mouthful of tart red wine.

    Koumba’s gaze sharpens slightly, watching the interaction between them without interrupting. Carol lowers the glass slowly, the dam breaking thanks to the deluge of wine.

    “You know, you don’t have to keep checking on her like that,” she says suddenly, the words coming out sharper than she intended.

    Both Zosia and Koumba stare quizzically at her, and Carol gestures vaguely between them with her hand. “The hovering,” she adds. “The water. The staring.”

    Zosia blinks, caught off guard. Koumba’s expression doesn’t change, but his posture stiffens slightly. Carol presses on before she can stop herself.

    “She’s pregnant, Koumba. Not made of glass.”

    A heavy silence settles over the table.

    Zosia’s smile fades a fraction. “Carol—”

    “I’m just saying,” Carol cuts in quickly, suddenly aware of the tightness in her own voice. “You don’t have to supervise her every move.”

    For a long moment, no one speaks. Koumba studies Carol across the table, his expression calm in a way that makes Carol want to slap it right off his face. She hates that he’s making her look like the crazy one.

    Zosia glances between them, confusion flickering across her face. “Carol, that’s not fair. Koumba just worries about me… like you do,” she says gently.

    Carol lets out a soft, humourless laugh. “Right. He worries,” she scoffs, shaking her head. Almost immediately, she feels the shift in the atmosphere around them. The warmth from earlier in the evening has cooled, replaced by something brittle and uncertain.

    Carol clears her throat and reaches for her glass again, suddenly wishing she hadn’t said anything at all. She’s fucked everything up in the way only she can.

    Across the table, Zosia watches her with quiet concern. Carol stares down at the dark smear of chocolate left on her plate and realises she’s made a fool of herself. “I’m, uh… I’m sorry. I think I should go.”

    She stands before Zosia can ask her to stay.

    The night air hits Carol the moment she steps outside, sharp and unexpectedly cold. She shivers, the thin fabric of her dress doing nothing to keep out the chill. The street is quieter here, with only the low hum of traffic and distant laughter spilling faintly from the restaurant behind her.

    She leans back against the brick wall beside the door, closing her eyes for a moment and wishing she had a cigarette.

    Her head feels light, and not just from the wine. The memory of the table—the fork dragging through the tart, the sharpness in her own voice, Zosia’s concerned eyes, Koumba’s calm scrutiny—presses at her chest like a hand she can’t shake.

    Carol digs her phone out of her clutch, thumb hovering over the screen. Maybe a message would fix it. Maybe she could just apologise, explain herself.

    Except she can’t think of the words to say. She’s not sure how to fix things between her and Zosia when she doesn’t know why she had to go ahead and fuck them up in the first place. She tucks her phone away in her purse. A coward’s move, yes—but easier than facing what she’s done.

    The choice is taken from her, though, as the door behind her opens. “Carol.” She doesn’t need to turn to know whose voice it is. Soft, breathless, gentle. Concerned.

    Carol turns anyway, forcing herself to meet Zosia’s eyes.. Zosia steps closer, her hand brushing lightly against Carol’s arm—not quite contact, but enough to make Carol’s stomach do that small, familiar flip.

    “You didn’t have to leave,” Zosia says quietly, her gaze steady.

    “I… uh, thought I should,” Carol mutters, looking down at the sidewalk. Her voice is uneven, the wine still loosening her tongue. “I kind of made a scene in there.”

    Carol swallows, heat rising. “…Because that’s what I do. Make everything awkward. Make everyone… uncomfortable. Push them away.” Her laugh is short, brittle. She feels on the verge of hysteria.

    Zosia doesn’t flinch. She doesn’t lecture. She just stands there, letting Carol spill the words she usually swallows; letting her crash into the walls of her own insecurities.

    “And the wine,” Carol adds, gesturing wildly as her voice grows louder. “That didn’t help.”

    A faint smile tugs at Zosia’s lips. “Probably not. Do you want to talk about it?” She asks, then leans a little closer, close enough that Carol can smell the faint sweetness of her perfume, the subtle warmth of her skin. “I’m a good listener.”

    Carol’s throat narrows. Her hands curl into fists at her sides. She wants to apologise, to laugh at herself and how stupid she’s being. And at the same time, every instinct in her body is screaming for her to run away before she can do any more damage.

    “I… I don’t—” she starts, faltering, then cuts herself off. There’s too much to say, too many emotions tangled up inside her, and she knows whatever comes out will sound messy. Wrong.

    Zosia tilts her head again, patient, unafraid of the chaos radiating off Carol in waves. “You don’t have to talk about it. Not yet. Not if you’re not ready, but I know something is bothering you. You weren’t yourself tonight.”

    Carol swallows hard, the warmth of the wine mingling with the heat creeping into her cheeks. She wants to tell Zosia she was exactly herself—and that’s the problem.

    She wants to say she doesn’t belong here. That she’s an outsider, a passing visitor in Zosia’s life, and that Koumba—everything about him—reminds her of that. But the words stick in her throat. Instead, she nods. Just a small, almost imperceptible nod.

    It’s all the confirmation Zosia needs to step forward, closing the distance between them. She wraps her arms around Carol and holds her tight as the other woman sags into her arms, choking back a sob.

    Carol buries her face in Zosia’s shoulder, her sweet, floral perfume and the warmth of her body instantly engulfing her.

    Just for tonight, Carol tells herself.

    She’ll let herself fall apart in Zosia’s arms just for tonight.


    -

    Zosia turns into the quiet curve of Cielo Rosado Court, easing her car down the smooth asphalt as the cul-de-sac opens out in front of her. Seven houses sit arranged in a careful arc, each one large, immaculate, and painted in variations of sand and clay that blend almost seamlessly into the desert landscape beyond.

    Carol’s house sits at the very end. Zosia pulls into the driveway and cuts the engine. For a moment, she stays in the driver’s seat, just looking at it.

    The house is exactly as she remembers—beige stucco walls glowing softly under the late afternoon sun, flat rooflines, and a second-floor balcony overlooking the sparse garden. High-end, understated. Expensive without shouting about it.

    Very Carol.

    She’s only been here once before, weeks ago, when Carol had insisted on swinging by to pick something up before driving her to a prenatal appointment. Zosia had waited in the car while Carol disappeared inside for five minutes and returned with a jacket and her phone charger.

    Zosia has never actually been inside.

    The thought presses faintly at the back of her mind as she climbs out of the car. Carol has eaten at her house dozens of times now—sat at her table, lingered over coffee in her kitchen, fallen asleep on her sofa more than once after a late dinner and too much wine.

    But Zosia has never crossed the threshold of Carol’s home.
    It leaves a strange feeling in her chest as she walks up the driveway, the hot pavement radiating heat through the soles of her sandals.

    Like she’s been welcomed into Carol’s life—but only to a point. She shakes the thought away and presses the doorbell. The chime echoes faintly somewhere inside the house.

    Zosia waits, shifting her weight slightly as the sun beats down on the back of her neck. The air smells faintly of dry earth and hot sage from somewhere nearby.

    There’s no sign of Carol inside. No footsteps approaching the door, no movement from behind the drawn curtains. Her truck sits patiently in the drive, though.

    Zosia presses the bell again and waits. Still nothing. She frowns and glances at her phone. Her message thread with Carol sits open—several texts sent over the past few days, all unanswered since Saturday night.

    Zosia’s stomach tightens. She opens Snapchat instead. Carol isn’t exactly a heavy user of the app—she’d only downloaded it after Zosia insisted it would save her sanity.

    “Then I can see how far away you are when you’re late,” Zosia had explained cheerfully.

    Carol had rolled her eyes but installed it anyway. Now the little map loads slowly on the screen. A tiny blonde avatar in a brown blazer sits directly over Carol’s house.

    Zosia exhales softly.

    “Well, your phone’s here,” she murmurs to herself. She presses the doorbell one more time. Still there’s no answer.

    After another minute of standing in the relentless sun, she turns back toward the driveway, her unease growing heavier with each quiet second.

    That’s when she notices movement across the cul-de-sac.
    A man in the yard opposite is dragging a large blue recycling bin toward the curb. He’s dark-haired, maybe in his late forties, wearing a faded football shirt and work boots.

    Zosia raises a hand. “Excuse me!” she calls across the road. The man pauses, eyeing her cautiously. “Are you Manousos?”

    His expression sharpens with recognition. “…Yes?”

    Zosia walks a little closer, offering a friendly smile. “Hi. I’m a friend of Carol Sturka’s.”

    The man studies her for a second longer before nodding slowly.

    “Zosia.”

    Zosia blinks in surprise. “How did you know that?”

    Manousos’ mouth quirks with amusement as he wipes his hands on the back of his jeans. “Carol does not have many beautiful pregnant friends… or friends.” He adds with a shrug.

    The compliment catches Zosia completely off guard. She feels heat rise instantly to her cheeks. “Oh,” she says, suddenly self-conscious. She wonders—briefly—if that’s how Carol described her.

    The thought sends a strange flutter through her chest. She clears her throat. “Have you seen Carol lately?” Zosia asks.

    Manousos nods toward the house behind her. “Saturday night.”

    “When?”

    “Late.” His brow furrows as he thinks. “Maybe one in the morning.”

    Zosia’s stomach drops slightly. “One?” Carol had left the restaurant at eight-thirty in a taxi. Zosia had put her in it herself.

    Which means she’d gone somewhere else before coming home. A bar, probably.

    Zosia folds her arms loosely over her stomach, suddenly uneasy.

    “I haven’t seen her since Saturday night either,” she says. “She’s not answering her phone. Her location says she’s home, but she’s not answering the door.”

    Manousos’ expression darkens a little. “That’s not good.”

    “No,” Zosia agrees. “Do you think she’s okay?” she asks quietly.

    The man exhales slowly, glancing toward Carol’s house again.

    “Maybe.” The hesitation in his voice does nothing to calm her. He scratches the back of his neck. “…This time of year is hard for her.”

    Zosia tilts her head, not following. “What do you mean?”

    Manousos studies her face for a moment, like he’s weighing whether it’s his place to say or not. “Two years today,” he says finally. “Since her wife died.”

    The words land like a small stone dropped into still water. Zosia goes very still. “Two years… today?”

    Manousos shakes his head. “Sunday, I think. She gets… difficult around the anniversary.”

    Zosia stares at him, the realization spreading slowly through her chest. Carol never told her. Didn’t mention the anniversary coming up. Not once.

    A dull ache forms somewhere behind her ribs.

    Zosia knows very little about Helen. Only fragments. Small things that slipped out occasionally before Carol shut the conversation down again.

    Carol doesn’t like talking about her. Now she understands why the past few days have felt so wrong. Why Carol seemed so off during dinner Saturday night.

    Her worry deepens. “What do you mean ‘difficult’?” she asks.

    Manousos shrugs, his expression carefully neutral. “She can be… self-destructive.” He doesn’t elaborate. Some things are Carol’s business alone.

    Zosia looks back at the silent house. The drawn curtains. The unmoved truck in the drive. Her chest tightens.

    “Should we call the police?” she asks after a moment. “Maybe ask for a welfare check?”

    Manousos immediately shakes his head. “No.”

    The answer is firm, instinctive. Zosia looks back at him, surprised.

    He hesitates before adding, “Where I come from, calling the police is… not always the best option.”

    Zosia nods slowly, understanding more than he probably realizes. “Me too.”

    Manousos rubs his chin thoughtfully. “I have a key,” he says finally. “For emergencies.”

    He glances toward the house again. “This feels like one.”

    Relief flickers faintly across Zosia’s face. “Would you mind?”

    “Wait here.” He disappears back toward his own house, leaving Zosia standing in the hot stillness of the cul-de-sac.

    The sun feels harsher now. The quiet heavier. Her concern for Carol having grown tenfold in the space of a few minutes.
    Moments later, Manousos returns, a small brass key held between his fingers. “Come,” he says, marching with purpose toward his neighbor’s house.

    They cross the road together. Zosia’s pulse beats faster with every step up the driveway. She feels a quickening flutter low down in her abdomen and places a hand over the growing bump of her stomach.

    All of this stress can’t be good for the baby.

    Manousos unlocks the front door and the latch clicks. He pushes it open slowly. Cool air spills out from the darkened house.

    “Carol Sturka?” he calls inside, voice loud and booming in the otherwise silent house. No answer comes. Zosia steps forward behind him as Manousos enters.

    “Carol!” He shouts again as he walks down the hallway. Manousos stops in his tracks at the archway leading into the living room. Zosia almost walks right into him.

    She follows his gaze and spots a mop of blonde hair—still lightly curled from Saturday night—spilling over a cushion tucked into the corner of the couch. There’s a patchwork quilt that’s been kicked off, lying at the other end.

    Carol lies facing the back of the couch, her small frame curled up in much the same position as the baby in Zosia’s belly. Her chest rises and falls in a slow, steady rhythm, and the relief Zosia feels is instant.

    Manousos looks unsure how to proceed. He goes to take a step toward the couch, but Zosia places a hand on his shoulder. “We should let her rest,” Zosia says softly.

    Manousos nods gruffly. “Are you coming?” he asks, turning back toward the door. Zosia takes in the carnage that is Carol’s living room.

    The curtains are drawn, plunging the room into darkness apart from the glow of the television. The air is thick and stale. It smells like the bar Zosia worked in during college.

    There are empty bottles and glasses littered all over. Golden Girls DVDs scattered around the floor in front of the television, which is still on, the DVD menu replaying over and over on mute.

    There’s a photo album open on the coffee table. Pictures of Carol and a smiling brunette stare back at Zosia in quiet accusation from a variety of locations; one has the aurora borealis lingering behind them.

    “I’m going to stay a while. Tidy up a little. Maybe make some dinner,” Zosia answers, confident it’s the right thing to do.

    Manousos doesn’t seem to question it. “Okay.” He nods at her. “Nice to meet you, Zosia.”

    “You too. Thank you, Manousos.” Zosia offers him a grateful smile. It slips off her lips as he closes the front door behind him.

    “Carol…” She mutters softly, her heart breaking at the sight of the other woman’s pain made manifest in empty liquor bottles.

    She leaves Carol sleeping and works quietly, picking up bottles and glasses and carrying them into the kitchen. The other room is surprisingly clean. There’s no evidence the whirlwind that is Carol’s grief has touched this room, no empty plates or containers littering the counter-tops.

    Zosia wonders when Carol last ate. She has a sneaking suspicion it might have been Saturday night. Once the trash is all cleared from the living room, and she’s mopped up the spills on the coffee table—moving the cherished photo album to a side table for safekeeping—she turns her attention to making dinner.

    The fridge is as barren as the desert stretching out behind Carol’s house. The freezer isn’t a whole lot better, but there are at least some staples Zosia can work with in the cupboards. She finds some soup, which she sets to simmer in a pan on the hob.

    There are a few slices left of a loaf of sourdough bread. Zosia fetches the block of what passes for cheese to Americans from the fridge and cuts a couple of slices off to top the bread for a grilled cheese, to go along with the soup.

    She rummages through Carol’s pantry and finds a bottle of Worcestershire sauce—her secret weapon for elevating the otherwise bland cheese—and adds a dash before putting the two slices that make up the sandwich under the broil to grill.

    Zosia leaves it to bubble and melt, putting the scattered DVDs away in their respective boxes while she waits. Once the cheese is golden, she takes it back out and sandwiches the two slices of bread together before plating it up.

    She pours the hot soup into a bowl and sets the pan down on one of the unused burners to cool. The soup bowl sits in the middle of the plate, the grilled cheese off to one side, as Zosia carries it into the living room.

    She sets it down on the coffee table before turning to kneel in front of the couch Carol is currently passed out on. She’s turned over while Zosia’s been busy, now facing the television again but still sound asleep.

    Her hair falls over her face, obscuring it. Zosia tucks it back behind her ear, fingers lightly caressing Carol’s cheek in their wake. The other woman stirs, brilliant blue eyes slowly blinking the world into focus.

    A lazy smile tugs at her lips as she takes Zosia in. “Hey,” she says, voice gravelly with sleep.

    “Hi.” Zosia greets her, matching Carol’s smile. She’s relieved to see Carol is okay—at least physically. Her hand lingers by Carol’s cheek, and she cups it, the pad of her thumb tracing Carol’s jaw.

    Carol’s eyes darken as the touch seems to rouse her properly, and she realises Zosia is actually there—in her living room—and not some kind of apparition.

    She frowns as Zosia pulls her hand back, the quiet moment between them shattered. “Zosia… What… What are you doing here?” Carol’s head snaps around as she looks frantically around the room, trying to assess the damage.

    Her frown deepens when she finds the room clean and tidy. Her gaze lingers on the photo album on the side table. She swallows the lump in her throat before she turns her attention back to Zosia.

    Zosia sits back, resting on the coffee table to give Carol her space before answering. The last thing she wants is Carol feeling trapped. “I was worried about you. I hadn’t heard from you since Saturday.”

    “Okay.” Carol rubs at the back of her neck. Her eyes self-consciously travel to the stains down the front of her pale green tank top, and she crosses her arms over her chest. “That doesn’t explain what you’re doing here, though. In my house.”

    “Manousos let me in,” Zosia answers sheepishly. “I was really worried.”

    “Sorry.” Carol sighs. Sitting up properly, she places her feet down on the floor, either side of Zosia’s knees. She only realizes how intimate this position feels when the inside of her thigh brushes against Zosia’s leg.

    She clears her throat—wishes the floor would open up and swallow her. “I, uh, got your messages Sunday morning. I meant to text back, but… yesterday just got away from me,” Carol admits in a quiet voice.

    It’s Zosia’s turn to frown. “Carol, yesterday was Monday. It’s Tuesday.”

    Carol’s eyes widen. “Fuck…” She curses, rubbing at her head. No wonder she feels like death warmed up. It’s been a long time since she’s gone on a three-day bender… about a year, in fact.

    Helen’s anniversary had crept up slowly, thanks to being distracted by Zosia and the baby stuff, but it had hit harder than ever as a result.

    Carol had gone to a bar after dinner Saturday night. She’d knocked back shot after shot of tequila, and then carried the party on back at home.

    Sunday had consisted of much of the same, drinking herself into oblivion, moping over photos of her and Helen during happier times—and steadfastly avoiding Zosia’s calls.

    Monday was a black hole.

    “Sorry…” Carol mutters again, at a loss for what else to say. “I didn’t mean to worry you.”

    “It’s okay. I’m just glad you’re okay,” Zosia says, handling Carol softly. She reaches beside her and picks up the plate with the bowl of soup and the grilled cheese sandwich. She offers it to Carol, who looks at it like it might be radioactive.

    “I didn’t think you’d eaten,” Zosia explains.

    “I haven’t.” Carol admits. She takes the offered food with a soft, “Thanks.”

    Zosia quietly watches her take the first bite of the grilled cheese. There’s something comforting to her in providing for the people she cares about.

    “This is so good,” Carol says, her mouth full from a second bite, and Zosia feels a flutter of pride.

    Carol looks around the living room again, taking it all in. “You cook and you clean. You’re like the world’s most awesome burglar.”

    Zosia purses her lips. “I’m not sure I’m comfortable being labelled a burglar. It suggests breaking and entering. I just… entered.” She chuckles, drawing a genuine smile from Carol.

    “Well, either way, you’re pretty awesome… and I’m an insensitive asshole who ignores your calls and makes you worry so much you came all the way out here.”

    “I was in the neighbourhood.” Zosia shrugs, playing down the fear she felt when Carol stopped responding.

    Carol raises a brow. “Really?” She already knows the answer. It’s a thirty-minute drive from Old Town. Zosia didn’t end up here by accident.

    “No.” Zosia shakes her head softly. She reaches out, her left hand finding Carol’s right knee. Carol almost chokes on a bite of grilled cheese but hides it with a cough, bringing a hand to cover her mouth.

    “Sorry, my throat’s a little dry.”

    “Let me get you some water,” Zosia offers and stands, her hand falling from Carol’s leg. She misses the contact almost immediately.

    “You don’t have to—“

    “I don’t mind.”

    Zosia disappears into the kitchen, which gives Carol time to pull herself together. She puts the plate with the bowl of soup down on the coffee table before running her fingers through her hair.

    Carol can only imagine what kind of sight she looks like after three days of endless drinking and sleeping on the couch. She sniffs at her armpit and curses, “Fuck.”

    There’s not much she can do about it as Zosia walks back in, carrying two glasses of water. She hands one to Carol and keeps the other for herself as she takes a seat in the chair facing the couch.

    Carol drinks the water greedily. She can’t remember the last non-alcoholic thing she had to drink. It feels like there isn’t enough water in the world to parch her first, and she finishes the glass with the same fervour as a frat boy chucking a beer.

    Zosia notices and holds out her own glass. “Would you like mine?”

    “Oh. No. Thank you.” Carol shakes her head, politely declining—even though her throat still feels like it’s coated in sandpaper.

    Zosia sees right through her. Leaning forward, she sets her glass on the coffee table in front of Carol. Carol eyes it for all of five seconds before she picks it up and knocks it back like the first one; gasping for breath when she’s done.

    “Thanks… God, I’m a fucking mess.” Carol gives a mirthless chuckle. Presses the cool glass to her forehead. It’s a small relief against her raging headache.

    Zosia doesn’t correct her. Doesn’t tell Carol she’s not a mess. They both know she is—at least right now.

    Zosia doesn’t press, either. She doesn’t confront Carol with what she knows. She doesn’t demand that Carol share her grief with her.

    Zosia knows how it feels to lose the most important person in your life and still—somehow—have to carry on. So, no. She doesn’t try to take from Carol. Zosia gives to her instead.

    She sits with her hands on her knees, tries to keep her voice steady as she shares the most painful memory she has. “Malick died in a hit-and-run.” Zosia looks up and finds she has Carol’s attention.

    “The driver didn’t stop,” she continues. “He never should have been behind the wheel. He’d lost his license the year before. Driving under the influence.”

    Carol looks pale. Swallows hard. “Alcohol?” she asks, her own guilty conscience gnawing away at her.

    Zosia shakes her head. “Meth.”

    Carol rolls the glass in her hands, looking anywhere but at Zosia. “Why are you telling me this? Why now?”

    “Because, it’s what friends do, Carol.”

    Carol’s jaw tightens. Her shoulders hunch. She suddenly looks like she’d rather be anywhere else than in her own living room with Zosia. “Manousos let you in, right?”

    Zosia nods.

    “And he told you, didn’t he?” Carol doesn’t elaborate. She doesn’t need to. Zosia nods again, slower this time. Carol sits forward, her elbows resting on her knees as her hands cover her face. She can’t do this right now. She can’t talk about Helen—least of all with Zosia.

    Zosia doesn’t ask her to.

    “Malick and I were on a date night. A rare night off from the restaurant,” Zosia ducks her head, tucks her hair behind her ear. “We were at a junction, had the green light… the other vehicle came out of nowhere. This big SUV. It smashed right into the driver’s side. Malick died on impact.”

    Zosia stops, wrings her hands together. Carol can see how painful this is for her to talk about, even after seven months.

    “I’m sorry,” Carol says, and hates how hollow the words ring to her own ears. She knows they’re meaningless.

    Nonetheless, Zosia nods her gratitude anyway. “Thank you.”

    Across from her, Carol wrestles with more than just a hangover. “Helen, I… I can’t talk about it. Not now. Not today.”

    “Okay.” Zosia doesn’t push. She never does. “You can tell me when you’re ready. Whenever that might be… For now, why don’t you go take a shower and I’ll make you some strong coffee.”

    Carol sighs, runs a hand through her hair, and pushes it back from her face. “Yeah. Yeah, that sounds good. Thank you.”

    Zosia offers her a patient smile. “Any time.”

    Chapter 10

    The hot water comes almost painfully at first. Carol stands under it anyway. She braces one hand against the cool tile of the shower wall and lets the spray pound down over the back of her neck, over her shoulders, and the tight knots that have taken hold along her spine.

    Steam fills the small bathroom quickly, fogging the mirror, blurring the edges of the room until the only thing that exists is the steady rush of water and the slow loosening of muscles that have been clenched for days.

    Her head still feels thick, the dull echo of three night’s drinking pulsing faintly behind her eyes. She tips her face up into the stream, closes them, and breathes.

    Downstairs, Zosia is in her house. In her space. The thought settles uneasily somewhere in Carol’s chest. Until now it has always been the other way around.

    Carol showing up at Zosia’s apartment, eating at her table, lingering in her kitchen long after dinner is finished. Zosia inviting her in without hesitation. Without reservation.

    Having her here feels… different.

    Carol rolls one shoulder slowly, working at a stubborn knot beneath the skin.

    Zosia had been sitting in her living room when Carol woke up. Calm. Steady. Like she belonged there.

    Like she wasn’t surrounded by the evidence of Carol’s worst habits. The empty bottles. The stale smell of old alcohol. The mess.

    Carol exhales slowly through her nose.

    And then there’s what Zosia had told her about Malick. The words replay in her mind whether she wants them to or not.

    A hit and run.

    Carol presses her palm against the tile again, harder this time.

    Zosia had said it so quietly. So simply. As if the horror of it had been worn down over the months into something smaller. Something manageable.

    Carol knows that’s a lie people tell themselves. Grief never really gets smaller. It just settles deeper. Guilt pricks uncomfortably under her ribs.

    Zosia had opened the door for her, offered her something raw and painful and personal. And Carol had done what she always does.

    Shut down.

    Her jaw tightens. It’s not that she doesn’t want to tell Zosia. Not that she doesn’t trust her. It’s just—

    Too raw.

    The anniversary still sits heavy in her chest like a stone weighing her down as she does her best to keep her head above water. Memories of Helen’s death are still sharp enough to cut if she looks at them too closely.

    Carol drags her fingers back through her wet hair, pushing it off her face. The water starts to cool slightly, but she stays where she is, letting it run over her skin while the fog of steam thins around her.

    Another thought creeps in, unwelcome.

    What must Zosia think of her?

    Drunk. Passed out on the couch. The house looking like a bar after closing time. Carol scrubs a hand roughly across her face. It’s a hell of a first impression of the place.

    She stays under the water longer than she probably should, reluctant to step back into the real world downstairs. Reluctant to face the quiet understanding she knows she’ll find in Zosia’s eyes.

    It would almost be easier if Zosia were mad at her.

    Eventually the hot water begins to run thin. Carol sighs and reaches for the tap, shutting it off. The sudden quiet of the bathroom feels all too loud.

    She towels off slowly, drying her hair just enough that it won’t drip down her back before stepping into the bedroom.

    Late afternoon sunlight filters weakly through the blinds, laying pale stripes across the bed. Her closet door stands half open. Carol pulls on a clean t-shirt and a pair of loose jeans, then reaches automatically toward the back of the closet.

    Her hand finds the soft green cardigan before she even really thinks about it. The fabric is worn thin in places, the wool softened by years of wear. Carol pauses for a second with it in her hands.

    She lifts the cardigan and presses the sleeve briefly against her face. It hasn’t smelled like Helen for a long time. Not really. Whatever trace once lingered has long since been replaced by laundry detergent and the passage of time.

    Still.

    She breathes in once, then pulls it on, the familiar softness settling over her shoulders like Helen’s arms themselves.

    Downstairs, the house is quieter than she expects. Carol hesitates halfway down the stairs before continuing into the living room.

    Zosia is sitting on the couch exactly where Carol left her. One hand rests lightly over the curve of her stomach, while the the other is wrapped around a mug of tea. When she looks up, her expression softens slightly in relief; like maybe she’d contemplated the idea of Carol climbing out of her own bathroom window to avoid her.

    On the coffee table in front of the couch sits another mug. This one of strong smelling coffee. Steam curls lazily from the top of it.

    Carol crosses the room slowly and picks it up, taking a cautious sip. The warmth spreads through her chest almost immediately. She pauses, realising Zosia knows how she takes her coffee without being asked. “One sugar… and oat milk.”

    Zosia watches her over the rim of her own cup, a small smile tugging at the corner of her mouth. Carol takes another sip, more thoughtfully this time. She scrunches her face up.

    “Is that…” She tilts the mug slightly, tasting again. “Cinnamon?”

    “And a dash of cayenne,” Zosia confirms.

    Carol lets out a quiet huff of amusement, shaking her head faintly. “God, you’re amazing.”

    Zosia’s smile widens just a touch.

    Carol lowers herself to perch on the arm of the couch, cradling the mug between both hands. The heat feels good against her palms.

    For a moment neither of them says anything. Carol studies the dark swirl of coffee inside the mug.

    Somehow, somewhere along the line, Zosia has become the first real friend Carol has had in a long time—And she’d very nearly ruined that Saturday night.

    Carol takes a deep breath and swallows her pride. “I need to ask you something,” she says finally.

    Zosia looks up, curious.

    “Will you apologise to Koumba for me?” Carol asks, her mouth tightening slightly around the words.

    “I wasn’t… myself.” A small pause, and then a wry chuckle, “Or maybe I was too much myself.”

    Zosia considers her for a moment before nodding. “I’ll pass it on.”

    Carol exhales quietly, relieved.

    “But you don’t need to worry,” Zosia adds. “He wasn’t mad. He was worried about you too.”

    The words land heavier than Carol expects. She leans back slightly, rubbing a hand over the back of her neck. “Great,” she mutters under her breath. Now she feels like even more of an ass.

    Carol shifts slightly, hugging her coffee mug a little tighter. “Do you…have to leave soon?” she asks, her voice quieter than she intended. The words sound awkward, hesitant, like she’s testing the air between them. Dinner service at the restaurant starts in a couple of hours. She expects Zosia will need to go.

    “No,” Zosia replies, tilting her head and letting a small smile tug at her lips. “I took the night off. Bob’s doing great—really stepping up. I’m starting to trust him to run the kitchen without me… hovering.”

    Carol frowns slightly, not knowing whether to be relieved or embarrassed. “Good. That’s great… So you can stay? I mean…if you want to?” she stammers, unsure of herself.

    Zosia blinks, a soft warmth in her eyes. “Do you…want me to stay?”

    Carol flushes, caught off guard. She nods. “…I…yeah,” she admits finally. “…If you want to.”

    Zosia’s smile widens, gentle, teasing. “I’d like that.”

    A quiet moment stretches between them, filled only by the soft hum of the air conditioning.

    “We could…watch a movie, or something?” Carol ventures, as if testing her own courage.

    “That would be wonderful.”

    Later, long after the sun has set, the living room glows softly with the flicker of the television screen. Zosia lies curled up on the other end of the couch from Carol, her legs tucked under her, and a hand resting lightly on the curve of her stomach.

    Carol watches her for a long moment, hesitating before taking the blanket lying over the back of the couch and draping it over the sleeping pregnant woman.

    Carol picks up her second mug off coffee from the table in front of her. It’s almost empty. Cinnamon and a whisper of cayenne still cling to the sides. The scent hangs in the air, warm and comforting—just like Zosia’s presence.

    The a benign romantic comedy drones softly in the background—Zosia’s choice—dialogue and music blending into the quiet. The rest of the house is still, untouched. The emptiness doesn’t press down on Carol the way it usually does.

    She leans back, eyes tracing the steady rise and fall of Zosia’s chest. The weight she’s carried for so long, the tightness in her own chest, loosens, if only just a little.

    Carol takes a long, slow breath. For the first time since Helen died, the silence in the house doesn’t feel heavy. Not tonight—Not with Zosia.

    -

    Carol arrives at Per Se mid-morning. Sunlight spills through the tall front windows, warming the polished wood floors and the dark grain of the bar. Inside, the restaurant smells faintly of roasted coffee beans and lemon polish.

    Kusimayu wipes down one of the tables near the window, a cloth draped over her shoulder. She looks up when the door opens and her face brightens immediately.

    “Carol,” she says warmly, straightening up. “Good morning.”

    Carol offers a small smile as she steps inside, letting the door swing shut behind her. The quiet of the dining room always feels strange compared to the usual busy evening hum she’s used to.

    “Morning.”

    Kusimayu sets the cloth aside and nods toward the back hallway. “Zosia’s just finishing up in the office. She shouldn’t be long.”

    “Oh, no problem.” Carol slips her hands into the pockets of her jacket, nodding once. “Thanks.” That’s what she gets for turning up early for a change.

    Before Kusimayu can say anything else, the swing door from the kitchen pushes open and Koumba steps through carrying a crate of bottled beer.

    Carol stiffens before she can stop herself. It’s the first time she’s seen him since the disastrous dinner a few weeks ago.

    Koumba pauses when he notices her, then his face breaks easily into a friendly smile. “Carol,” he says, setting the crate down behind the bar. “Good to see you.”

    There’s no edge to his voice. No lingering irritation. Just the same easy warmth he always seems to carry with him.

    “Hey,” Carol replies, trying to sound normal.

    “Zosia won’t be long,” he says, already reaching for the bottles to restock the fridge beneath the bar. “Can I get you a coffee while you wait?”

    “I’m good,” Carol says quickly. It would be easier to justify her dislike of the man if he weren’t so damned nice. “Thanks.”

    She slides onto one of the stools at the bar, the leather seat cool beneath her. Kusimayu disappears through the kitchen door a moment later, leaving the two of them alone in the quiet dining room.

    Koumba lines up bottles with quiet efficiency, the soft clink of glass the only sound between them for a moment. Then he glances up.

    “Big day today,” he says casually.

    Carol exhales slowly. “Yeah.”

    It’s the day of Zosia’s twenty-week scan, a detailed procedure where the doctor will check the baby’s physical development from head to toe. If anything isn’t as it should be, they’ll find out today.

    Koumba shuts the fridge door and leans lightly against the bar. “Are you hoping for a boy or a girl?”

    Carol hesitates just long enough to feel it. “I…haven’t really thought about it,” she says, the lie coming out smoother than she expected.

    In reality, she’s been thinking about little else but the scan.

    She barely slept last night.

    The last scan had been at twelve weeks, when the baby still looked more like a vague shape than a person. This one is different. The technician will check everything—the spine, the organs, the heart.

    The baby should look… human now. Have little tiny arms and fingers, even fingernails. The thought makes something strange and tight twist in her chest.

    They’re halfway through the pregnancy now. Twenty weeks down. Twenty to go.

    In another five months there will be an actual baby in the world. A tiny, living, breathing thing that Carol—albeit inadvertently—helped create.

    Carol stares down at the polished wood of the bar.

    When she’d first been told about the error with her eggs, the whole thing had felt abstract. Clinical. Even knowing that—someday soon—she’d have a child out there in the world, it hadn’t felt real.

    But that had been before Zosia somehow slipped into her life like she’d always belonged there. Now the idea of the baby feels different. Real in a way she hadn’t anticipated.

    And utterly terrifying.

    She had been very clear in the beginning. She wanted nothing to do with the child once it was born. That had seemed simple enough at the time. Practical, even.

    But things aren’t that simple anymore. Not being in the baby’s life also means not being in Zosia’s. The other woman has become almost like a best friend—Carol refuses to acknowledge her feelings for Zosia have long since passed the friend zone.

    Carol swallows. What happens when the baby is actually here? What if it looks like her? What if it has her nose, or her eyes?

    What if it inherits her temper? Her sharp edges? Her innate ability to destroy everything she touches.

    Her fingers tighten slightly around the edge of the bar as she tries to banish the worst of the doubts swirling around her mind.

    Being part of Zosia’s life doesn’t just mean the baby will always be there between them, but Koumba too. The quiet clink of bottles draws her attention back to him.

    Carol clears her throat.

    “Koumba.”

    He glances over his shoulder at her.

    “I wanted to…say I’m sorry. For how I behaved at dinner the other week.”

    Koumba’s brows lift slightly in surprise, but he doesn’t interrupt.

    “I was nervous about it,” she continues, rubbing the back of her neck. “And I drank too much.”

    Koumba nods once.

    “Zosia passed on your apology,” he says. “But it wasn’t necessary. She said you were… going through something.”

    Carol freezes. A flash of defensiveness sparks instinctively in her chest. It must show on her face, because Koumba raises a hand slightly.

    “She didn’t tell me the details,” he says calmly. “Just that it was a difficult time for you.”

    The tension in Carol’s shoulders loosens a fraction.

    “I hope you’re dealing with it,” he adds gently. “Whatever it was.” The sincerity in his voice catches her off guard.

    Carol hesitates. Her first instinct is to shut down, to refuse to acknowledge the grief gnawing away at her chest. Then she sighs softly.

    “It was the anniversary of my wife’s death,” she says.

    Koumba goes still. For a moment he looks genuinely stunned, and Carol knows he was being genuine when he said Zosia didn’t go into any details with him.

    “You were married?” he asks quietly.

    Carol nods once. “She died. Two years ago.”

    “I’m sorry,” he says.

    “Thank you.”

    An awkward silence settles between them as neither quite knows what to say to the other. Koumba is the first to break, finally clearing his throat.

    “I appreciate you being there for Zosia,” he says, catching Carol off guard. She looks up from her hands, which are clasped together on the bar.

    “I’ll admit,” he continues, “I was sceptical of you at first.”

    Carol raises an eyebrow at that.

    “She had just lost Malick. She was pregnant. Vulnerable.” He shrugs lightly. “I worried about her. I always worry about her.”

    Carol can’t blame him for that. She worries more for Zosia than she does for herself. There’s something about the other woman that just makes Carol want to take care of her—even though Zosia is one of the strongest people she knows.

    “But you’ve been good for her,” Koumba says simply. “She’s happier than I’ve seen her in a long time.”

    There’s a brief pause before he adds, “Since before Malick died.”

    Carol opens her mouth, unsure what she’s supposed to say to that. As luck would have it, she doesn’t have to. The kitchen door opens again and Zosia steps into the dining room.

    She’s wearing a maternity dress, something light and flowing that falls over the pronounced bump of her stomach. Her face lights up the moment she sees Carol.

    “There you are,” she says, crossing the room quickly. Before Carol can react, Zosia wraps her arms around her in a warm hug that Carol melts into, even with Koumba watching.

    “Ready to go?” Zosia asks as she pulls away, much too soon for Carol’s liking.

    Carol slides off the stool almost immediately. “Absolutely.”

    Both women say their goodbyes to Koumba. Carol doesn’t look back as she follows Zosia toward the door, suddenly very eager to get out of the restaurant and away from Koumba’s discerning eye.

    Out in the parking lot, Carol reaches her truck first and pulls open the front passenger door of the old Chevy. Without thinking about it, she holds out her hand.

    Zosia smiles gratefully and places her palm in Carol’s. “Thanks.”

    Carol braces lightly as Zosia steps up into the seat, steadying her as the pregnant woman settles in. Her grip is warm and soft. The contact lasts only a second, but it sends a small, unwelcome flutter through Carol’s stomach.

    She quickly lets go and shuts the door.

    By the time she rounds the front of the truck and climbs into the driver’s seat, she’s vibrating with a nervous kind of energy she can’t quite explain.

    The engine rumbles to life with the familiar low growl of an old pickup.

    It’s about twenty minutes to Zosia’s gynaecologist’s office. Carol pulls out of the parking lot and merges into traffic while Zosia fills the silence easily, launching into the latest gossip from the restaurant.

    “Apparently Bob tried to reinvent the béchamel yesterday,” Zosia says, rolling her eyes slightly. “Without telling anyone.”

    Carol glances over. “That sounds dangerous.”

    “It was tragic,” Zosia replies solemnly. “I found nutmeg in it.”

    Carol snorts quietly. She doesn’t know enough about the culinary arts to know whether nutmeg in béchamel sauce is enough to warrant the death penalty, but if Zosia’s tone is anything to go by, it certainly is.

    The conversation flows easily after that—small stories about the kitchen staff, a supplier who delivered the wrong produce again, a dishwasher who accidentally sprayed an entire rack of clean plates with sanitizer.

    Eventually the topic shifts.

    “I saw the most beautiful crib online last night,” Zosia says, scrolling through something on her phone. “Handmade. Solid wood. It’s carved with these little vines along the rails...”

    Carol glances briefly at the screen when Zosia tilts it toward her. The crib looks like something out of a storybook—smooth pale wood, delicate craftsmanship.

    “It’s ridiculously expensive,” Zosia admits. “But I can’t stop thinking about it, and I really need to make a start on the nursery soon.”

    Carol nods as she signals and turns down the road that leads toward the clinic. For a moment the cab of the truck fills with quiet again.

    “Are you going to find out the sex today?” Carol asks casually as she pulls into the parking lot.

    Zosia bites her lip, clearly torn.

    “I’d like to know before the baby is born,” she says slowly. “But I’ve always wanted to have one of those gender reveal parties.”

    She glances at Carol quickly. “Before you start—I know they’re dumb.” Zosia gives a small, self-deprecating laugh, like she’s bracing herself for Carol to agree.

    Carol pulls into the nearest parking space she can find close to the entrance. The engine idles for a moment before she shuts it off.

    “I don’t think they’re dumb,” she says.

    Thirty seconds ago she absolutely would have said anyone participating in such an asinine custom should have their heads examined.

    But Zosia lit up when she talked about it, and that… changes things.

    “Really?” Zosia asks, looking faintly sheepish. “I didn’t think they’d be your thing.”

    Carol shrugs. “Maybe I’m just trying to be open to new things.”

    Zosia’s smile is radiant.

    “I was thinking of asking them to write the sex down and put it in an envelope for Koumba,” she says. “He really wants to know. And he could do some kind of reveal.”

    Carol feels something in her chest tighten. She bristles before she can stop herself. “I could do it,” she says quickly.

    Zosia blinks.
