    “I could organise the gender reveal party for you.” The words leave Carol’s mouth before she has time to think them through.

    Zosia gives her a doubtful look. “I… I wouldn’t want to put that on you.” Carol knows where that hesitation comes from.

    Ever since Zosia saw the tail end of Carol’s drunken binge a few weeks ago, she’s been treating her with kid gloves. Like she might shatter if Zosia even looks at her too hard.

    “Zos, I can do it,” Carol insists. “Trust me.”

    “I do,” Zosia says immediately.

    She reaches across the centre console and places her hand over Carol’s where it rests on the gear stick. The warmth of her palm spreads through Carol’s fingers.

    “I just don’t want to add any stress onto you right now.”

    “Zosia, I’m fine,” Carol says, a little more firmly than she intended. “And I want to do this. For you.”

    She can feel the tips of her ears burning with the admission. Zosia studies her for a long moment, then breaks into one of her prize-winning smiles. “Okay.”

    Relief floods Carol’s chest.

    “Thank you,” Zosia adds softly.

    She squeezes Carol’s hand once before pulling hers away. Carol misses the contact instantly.

    “Do you have a preference?” Carol asks after a moment. “Whether it’s a girl or a boy?”

    Zosia shakes her head. Her hand drifts down to rest over the curve of her stomach, fingers spread protectively across the pronounced bump. “I just want them to be healthy.”

    Carol nods slowly. She knows today’s scan checks for more than a dozen different potential complications. She’d made the mistake of googling them last night. Every single one.

    It’s a miracle she slept at all.

    “What about Malick?” Carol asks.

    Zosia looks momentarily surprised.

    Carol rarely asks about him. Conversations about their dead spouses tend to stay carefully avoided—at least from Carol’s side.

    “When you guys were first trying,” Carol continues quietly, “what did he want?”

    “A boy,” Zosia answers. Some of the tension leaves her shoulders as she speaks.

    “He wanted a little mini Malick to toss a football around with in the backyard. Coach his little league games. Take him hunting.” She shrugs lightly. “Typical guy stuff.”

    A small pause settles between them. Then Zosia asks carefully, “What did Helen want?”

    Carol doesn’t look away. She doesn’t shut down or change the subject like she usually would. She’s trying to be open to new things.

    She swallows, glances out of the windshield. “Uh… a girl,” Carol admits. “She wanted a little girl. Mostly just to dress up.” A faint smile tugs at the corner of her mouth.

    Zosia laughs quietly.

    “I told her buying one of those Build-A-Bear things would be cheaper,” Carol adds.

    Zosia’s big brown eyes are fixed on Carol as they brighten with amusement. “How did that work out for you?”

    Carol huffs a small laugh. “Long night on the couch,” she says. “Boy, could that woman hold a grudge.”

    But the memory is warm rather than painful. It feels good, somehow, to talk about Helen as she used to be. Not just about the fact that she’s gone.

    For two years Carol could barely say her wife’s name out loud without feeling like she might fall apart.

    Zosia grins. “Malick had his fair share of those too,” she says. “But usually I’d feel sorry for him and let him back into bed.”

    “Lucky guy,” Carol mutters. The words slip out before she can stop them. Heat floods her cheeks when Zosia looks at her curiously.

    “Uh—” Carol clears her throat quickly. “Not having to sleep on the couch, I mean.”

    She makes a show of glancing at her watch, steadfastly avoiding looking Zosia in the eye. “We should get moving,” she adds, hot desert air rushing in as she opens her door. “Don’t want to be late for the doc—“

    “Wait.” Zosia’s hand closes suddenly around her wrist.

    Carol startles a little at the unexpected contact. Before she can ask what’s wrong, Zosia gently presses Carol’s hand against the soft curve of her stomach, keeping it in place with her own.

    For a moment Carol’s brain simply… stalls.

    Her palm rests awkwardly over the stretched fabric of Zosia’s dress, warmth seeping through the thin material. She’s acutely aware of everything at once—the quiet cab of the truck, the faint tick of the cooling engine, the soft citrus scent of Zosia’s shampoo drifting through the small space.

    “Just—hold it there,” Zosia murmurs.

    Carol opens her mouth to ask why, and then she feels it. A sudden, unmistakable flutter beneath her palm. Not quite a kick—more like a small, deliberate thump from somewhere deep inside.

    Carol freezes. Her eyes snap to Zosia’s. “Did you—?” Another movement bumps lightly against her hand.

    Carol’s breath catches in her throat. “Holy—” The word dies halfway out.

    She stares down at where her hand rests against Zosia’s stomach, like the fabric itself might suddenly turn transparent and reveal what’s happening underneath.

    The baby moves again. A tiny push, stronger this time. Something strange and electric shoots up Carol’s arm and settles somewhere behind her ribs.

    “That’s…” She huffs out a disbelieving breath. “…that’s the baby?”

    Zosia smiles, warm and a little proud. “They’ve been pretty active this morning. I was hoping to get to show you.”

    Carol lets out a quiet laugh under her breath, shaking her head faintly as she keeps her hand exactly where it is, afraid the moment will vanish if she moves.

    “I mean—I knew there was a baby in there,” she says slowly. “But that’s…”

    Another small kick taps against her palm. Carol lets out a giddy laugh, her fingers shifting instinctively over the curve of Zosia’s stomach a she follows the movement

    “That’s real,” she finishes softly.

    The realisation settles over her in a way none of the ultrasounds or posters on development ever quite managed.

    For a moment neither of them speaks.

    Carol becomes aware that she’s still touching Zosia—her hand resting possessively over the bump like it belongs there. Heat creeps up the back of her neck.

    Carol clears her throat and finally pulls her hand back, rubbing it awkwardly against her jeans like she’s not quite sure what to do with it now.

    She can still feel the ghost of that tiny kick lingering in the centre of her palm when she walks around the truck to help Zosia out, offering her that same hand—all the more excited for the upcoming scan.

    Chapter 11

    The waiting room of the clinic smells faintly of disinfectant and stale coffee. A television mounted in the corner plays a muted daytime talk show while a few other patients sit scattered across the rows of plastic chairs, flipping through magazines or staring down at their phones.

    Zosia steps up to the reception desk, resting a hand instinctively over the curve of her stomach as she waits for her turn to give the receptionist her name. Carol, meanwhile, drifts off almost immediately.

    There’s a large poster on the far wall—one she’s already studied at least half a dozen times over the past two appointments. It charts fetal development week by week, each tiny illustration showing how the baby grows from something vaguely alien into something unmistakably human.

    Zosia glances over as Carol crosses the room toward it. A small smile tugs at the corner of her mouth. Carol always does this. Every appointment she studies the poster like it’s a classified briefing she needs to memorise before the doctor calls them in.

    They’ve fallen into these small routines without ever really talking about them. Carol checking the poster. Zosia pretending not to notice how invested she is.

    “Good morning—?”

    The receptionist’s voice pulls Zosia back abruptly and she realises the woman in line ahead of her has already been seen to and sat down.

    “Oh—sorry,” Zosia says quickly, a little flustered. “Zosia Diabaté.”

    She gives the rest of her details automatically, though her attention keeps drifting back toward Carol across the room.

    Carol is already reading the twenty-week section, one hand braced on the wall beside the poster. Her brow is furrowed in concentration like she’s preparing for an exam.

    Zosia finishes checking in and makes her way over to the row of chairs beside her. The moment she sits down, Carol turns toward her, eyes bright.

    “Twenty weeks,” she announces immediately.

    Zosia laughs softly. “Yes, Carol. That is why we’re here.”

    “No, listen,” Carol insists, leaning forward slightly. “It’s the size of a banana now.”

    Zosia raises an eyebrow.

    “A banana?”

    “According to the poster.” Carol gestures vaguely over her shoulder. “About ten inches from head to heel now. And the digestive system’s starting to produce meconium. That’s its first poop.”

    Zosia blinks. “…I’m not sure I needed to know that part.”

    Carol shrugs, unbothered. “Seems like important information.”

    The animation in her voice, the way her hands move when she talks, makes Zosia’s chest feel warm in a way she’s starting to recognise.

    She can’t help but smile. Zosia only ever sees Carol get like this when it comes to these appointments. Before she can respond, a door opens down the hallway.

    “Zosia Diabaté?”

    Both women look up. Carol stands automatically, like it’s the most natural thing in the world for her to go with her. Zosia doesn’t even think to ask. They simply walk down the hall together.

    Doctor Beeken greets them in the exam room with her usual easy smile. “Good morning, Zosia. Carol.”

    “Morning, Doc,” Carol says, hovering near the wall while Zosia settles herself on the exam table. The doctor flips through the chart briefly before looking up.

    “Alright,” she says. “First question, Zosia—did you manage the full bladder we talked about?”

    Zosia lets out a quiet groan. “Yes. And I feel like I’m about to pee myself.”

    Doctor Beeken chuckles. “Perfect.”

    Zosia gives her a slightly incredulous look.

    “It helps lift the uterus a little so we can get clearer images,” the doctor explains. “We’ll get this done as quickly as we can. I promise.”

    She wheels the ultrasound machine closer and continues speaking as she prepares the equipment.

    “So today is the twenty-week anatomy scan. It’s our most detailed look at the baby during pregnancy. We’ll be measuring the head, spine, limbs, organs—basically making sure everything is developing the way it should.”

    Carol shifts slightly closer to the monitor.

    “It usually takes around thirty minutes,” Doctor Beeken continues, snapping on a pair of gloves. “Assuming our little friend here cooperates and isn’t lying in a difficult position.”

    Zosia pushes her dress up over her stomach, revealing the soft curve of her bump. She tugs the waistband of her shorts down slightly to give the doctor room to work.

    The gel is cold when it touches her skin and Zosia inhales sharply.

    “Sorry,” the doctor says lightly.

    The transducer glides slowly across her stomach, and within seconds the grainy black-and-white image appears on the screen.

    Carol leans forward immediately.

    Doctor Beeken begins narrating quietly as she moves the wand. “Alright… head measurements look good. Right on track for twenty weeks.”

    She clicks a few buttons, capturing images.

    “Spine looks beautiful.”

    Another click.

    “We’ve got ten fingers and toes.”

    The room grows quieter as she continues scanning, the only sounds the soft hum of the machine and the occasional tap of keys.

    Doctor Beeken’s expression becomes focused as she angles the transducer carefully, studying the screen. Zosia’s fingers curl slightly against the paper sheet beneath her as the silence stretches.

    Carol notices the tension creeping into Zosia’s shoulders. Without saying anything, she reaches over and takes her hand. Zosia startles slightly at the contact, then squeezes back.

    Doctor Beeken glances over the monitor and seems to pick up on the shift. “Sorry,” she says gently. “I tend to get quiet during these scans. I’m concentrating on capturing the images we need. Silence doesn’t mean anything is wrong.”

    Zosia exhales slowly. “Okay.”

    The doctor nods and continues moving the wand. “There’s the heartbeat,” she says a moment later, turning the sound on briefly.

    A fast, steady rhythm fills the room and Carol feels something tighten in her own chest. Her thumb traces the back of Zosia’s hand idly as she stares intently at the screen.

    “That’s strong,” Doctor Beeken adds. “Exactly where we want it.”

    The baby shifts on the screen suddenly, a small, unmistakable wiggle. “And they’re really moving today,” the doctor says with a smile.

    Zosia laughs softly. “I’ve felt it a lot more this week. Especially in the evenings. And after I eat.”

    “That’s completely normal,” the doctor replies. “Babies tend to get more active after meals—just watch your sugar intake.”

    She adjusts the transducer again, capturing several more images. After a moment she glances up. “Would you like to know the sex today?”

    Zosia shakes her head quickly. “Actually—we’d like to do a gender reveal.”

    Doctor Beeken nods.

    “So if it’s okay,” Zosia adds, glancing toward Carol, “maybe you could tell Carol at the end when I step out?”

    “Of course,” the doctor says easily. “We can absolutely do that.”

    A few minutes later, as she continues scanning, she asks casually, “Have you looked into prenatal classes yet?”

    Zosia frowns slightly. “Not yet. It’s still early, isn’t it?”

    “They usually start somewhere between twenty-six and thirty-two weeks,” the doctor explains. “They fill up fast, though. UNM Health runs a great group-based prenatal program for older mothers.”

    Carol shifts slightly. “Older mothers? Zosia’s only thirty-nine. It’s not like she’s geriatric.”

    Doctor Beeken laughs at that. “Zosia is in her prime right now, but in the medical field we refer to anyone over thirty-five and pregnant as being advanced maternal age.”

    Carol’s brow furrows immediately. “Why does she need a specialist prenatal class?” she asks quickly. “Is something wrong?”

    The doctor looks mildly surprised. “No. Nothing at all is wrong,” she reassures. “Everything we’re seeing today looks excellent.”

    Carol relaxes slightly.

    “The class is less about medical risk,” Doctor Beeken continues, “and more about building community and support. It’s five to eight mothers and their birthing partners meeting regularly through the remainder of pregnancy to help prepare them for the birth and parenthood in general.”

    Zosia tilts her head. “That actually sounds lovely.”

    “It really is,” the doctor says. “Many of the women stay friends long after their babies are born.”

    “I’d love the details,” Zosia replies.

    “Of course. I really think you and Carol would get a lot out of it.”

    Zosia shifts awkwardly on the table. “Oh—Carol isn’t actually my birthing partner.”

    Doctor Beeken pauses. “Oh? I’m sorry, I just assumed.”

    “What?” Carol frowns beside her, visibly confused. They’ve never even discussed Carol being her birthing partner or not, whatever that entails. “Wait, so who is?”

    “Koumba,” Zosia says softly, glancing down and away from the other woman as she answers.

    Carol blinks slowly. “Your brother-in-law?” she asks before she can stop herself.

    Zosia shrugs slightly. “Well… I didn’t think you’d want to do it.”

    Carol stiffens at that.

    “You were pretty clear you didn’t want to be involved in the baby’s life,” Zosia adds, her voice carrying the faintest edge now. “I wasn’t sure when that line started exactly. If you’d walk out of the delivery room the moment the baby popped out.”

    The air in the room goes still. Carol feels the words land like a punch to the chest.

    Doctor Beeken clears her throat gently. “Perhaps that’s a conversation for after the appointment,” she suggests diplomatically.

    Carol nods once, jaw tight. The worst of it is Zosia is right. Carol did say all that stuff.

    It seems like a lifetime ago now.

    The scan ends a few minutes later. Doctor Beeken wipes the gel away and prints two small sonogram images. Zosia studies the tiny figure on the glossy paper with a soft, almost disbelieving smile.

    “Alright,” the doctor says. “You can run to the restroom now before that bladder situation becomes urgent.”

    Zosia laughs weakly as she slides off the table. She holds her back. “I’m not sure about the running part.”

    “Grab a sample pot from reception on the way,” the doctor adds. “We’ll run the usual tests.”

    Zosia nods and disappears down the hall. Carol lingers behind to find out the gender of the baby. Doctor Beeken surprises her when, instead of gleefully announcing girl or boy, she asks, “How are you doing?”

    Carol looks mildly startled. “I’m fine. I’m not pregnant for starters.”

    The doctor folds her arms lightly. “Carol… you’re in a fairly unusual situation.”

    Carol raises an eyebrow. That’s the understatement of the century.

    “Watching your egg grow inside of another woman, seeing that baby develop,” Doctor Beeken says gently.

    Carol shrugs, wishing she was anywhere else but having this conversation. “It’s really not that big a deal.”

    The doctor studies her for a moment. Then she sighs lightly, relenting. “If you’re sure,” she says. “But talking to someone wouldn’t hurt. Someone professional.”

    Carol nods stiffly. “I’ll keep that in mind.” She clears her throat. “So… the gender?”

    Doctor Beeken smiles and reaches for a piece of paper from her desk. “Of course.”

    She writes something down, folds the paper neatly in half, and hands it to Carol. She takes it slowly, like the doctor is offering her a loaded handgun.

    “If you’d like to be surprised too,” the doctor adds, “you can pass this along to whoever you go with for the reveal without looking.”

    Carol blinks. “That’s… actually a great idea. Thanks, Doc.” She slips the folded paper carefully into the front pocket of her shirt.

    Zosia is waiting near the reception desk when Carol steps out. Her eyes light up immediately, like it’s Christmas morning and Carol’s dressed as Santa Claus.

    “Well?” she asks, bouncing slightly on her toes. “Did you find out?”

    “But don’t tell me!” she adds quickly.

    Carol chuckles, glad the earlier uneasiness between them has passed. “The doctor wrote it down,” she explains, tapping her pocket. “So technically I don’t know either.”

    Zosia’s shoulders drop in relief. “Oh thank God.”

    “Were you worried I’d forget?” Carol teases.

    “More… worried I might’ve forced it out of you,” Zosia admits.

    Carol scoffs, raising an eyebrow. “How were you planning on doing that, exactly?”

    Zosia grins, knocking her shoulder casually into Carol’s as they head for the door. “Bribery, of course,” she answers like it’s obvious.

    Carol feels her throat tighten. She swallows hard. “Uh, with what?”

    “My chocolate tart.”

    Carol snorts. “You think I’m that easy?”

    “I know you are.” Zosia grins, but she tilts her head thoughtfully as Carol gets the door for her. “Hmm, but if that didn’t do it, then I’d have to break out the really big guns.”

    Carol impulsively leans closer, intrigued. “And what would that be?”

    Zosia lingers in the doorway, lowers her voice to a teasing whisper as she leans in to meet her.

    “My chocolate soufflé. You’d never be able to say no to that.”

    Carol laughs under her breath. She isn’t entirely sure she could ever say no to Zosia, chocolate soufflé or not; she keeps that thought firmly to herself.

    There are still things they need to talk about—prenatal classes, birthing partners, everything that hangs uncomfortably between them.

    But that’s a conversation for another day.

     

    -

     

    Carol sits at the small kitchen table, elbows braced on the worn wood, staring down at the folded piece of paper in her hands. It’s already soft along the creases from how many times she’s handled it.

    Three days.

    Three days since Doctor Beeken pressed it into her palm. Carol rolls the paper slowly between her fingers, feeling the faint ridge where it’s been folded in half.

    Inside is a single word. Boy, or girl. It would take less than a second to find out.

    Her thumb slips under the corner of the fold before she catches herself. She exhales through her nose and flips the paper over instead, studying the reverse side like it might suddenly reveal its secret without her having to open it.

    When the doctor suggested waiting—finding out at the gender reveal with Zosia—it had seemed like a good idea. Fun, even. Now it feels like an itch she can’t scratch.

    Carol turns the paper over and over again.

    Just a quick peek, she thinks.

    No one would even know—

    The doorbell rings.

    Carol jerks upright, the paper disappearing instinctively into her shirt pocket. “Saved by the bell,” she mutters.

    She pushes away from the table and heads down the hallway toward the front door. Halfway there she catches her reflection in the narrow mirror beside the coat rack.

    Carol pauses. Her hair is slightly flattened on one side from where she’s been leaning on her palm. She rakes her fingers through it quickly, fluffing it back into place, then smooths down the front of her shirt.

    Only then does she open the door.

    Zosia stands on the porch, framed by the warm evening light. She’s wearing a pale yellow sundress that falls loosely over the curve of her stomach, the fabric stirring slightly in the breeze. Her hair is pulled up into a loose knot, a few dark strands escaping around her face.

    Carol’s brain short-circuits for a second. “Uh—hi,” she says.

    Zosia smiles. “Hi.”

    Carol clears her throat. “Come in.”

    Zosia steps forward and wraps her arms around Carol in a warm, easy hug before Carol can properly get out of the way. The hugging has become something of a habit between them lately; not that Carol minds.

    She hugs her back automatically, catching the soft scent of citrus and vanilla from Zosia’s skin. The moment lingers just a second longer than necessary before they pull apart.

    “Something smells amazing,” Zosia says as she steps inside.

    Carol’s chest puffs slightly with pride. “It’s chilli.”

    Zosia’s eyebrows lift.

    “Homemade?”

    “Of course it’s homemade,” Carol says, shutting the door behind her. “I thought it was time I showed off my culinary skills.”

    Zosia laughs softly at that.

    As she walks further into the house, her gaze drifts around the living room, taking everything in. It’s noticeably different from the last time she was here.

    The surfaces are clear. The coffee table wiped clean. The faint stale smell of alcohol that once lingered in the air has been replaced by the rich, smoky aroma of simmering chilli drifting from the kitchen.

    Most importantly, there are no empty bottles anywhere.

    Carol notices the way Zosia’s eyes move around the room. That’s part of the reason she invited her over tonight. So she could see. See that Carol has gotten her shit back together. See that she doesn’t have to worry. Carol doesn’t say any of that out loud, of course.

    Instead she jerks her head toward the kitchen. “Want some tea?”

    “That would be lovely, thank you.”

    Zosia follows her down the short hallway, drawn immediately toward the stove the moment they step into the kitchen. A large pot sits on the front burner, steam curling lazily from beneath the lid.

    Carol fills the kettle at the sink while Zosia hovers beside the stove, peering curiously into the pot like a suspicious scientist examining a child’s experiment.

    Carol glances over her shoulder and chuckles. “You can taste it if you want.”

    Zosia straightens quickly, feeling called out. “I’m sure it’s fine.”

    Carol raises an eyebrow.

    “I’m not a control freak,” Zosia adds defensively, without Carol even having to say anything.

    “Mhm.”

    Carol turns back to the kettle, setting it on the burner. Behind her there’s a long pause. Thirty seconds, maybe. Then she hears the soft clink of the spoon against the pot.

    Carol smiles to herself until—

    “—Oh!”

    Carol spins around. Zosia has a spoon halfway to her mouth, eyes wide, one hand flying up to cover it as she starts coughing.

    Carol laughs. “Very funny, Zos.”

    Zosia’s face turns bright red.

    Carol rolls her eyes, still amused. “You’re not actually—”

    Zosia coughs more violently, bending slightly at the waist as another sputter escapes her.

    Carol’s smile falters. “Wait—you’re serious?”

    Zosia nods weakly, still coughing.

    Carol grabs a glass from the counter and shoves it under the tap. “Here—water—”

    Zosia shakes her head urgently. “Milk,” she chokes out, a hand at her throat.

    Carol abandons the glass and yanks the fridge open, grabbing the milk carton. It’s oat milk, but she assumes it will have the same effect as cow’s milk. Zosia takes it from her immediately and drinks straight from the carton. For several long seconds she just stands there gulping.

    Finally she lowers the carton, breathing hard, cheeks flushed deep pink.

    Carol stares at her. “…Was it that bad?”

    Zosia presses a hand to her chest, still catching her breath.

    “I think,” she says hoarsely, “you may have used a little too much chilli powder.”

    Carol blinks. “Chilli powder? Not… not whole chilli peppers?”

    Zosia stares at her, cheeks still red and the carton of milk clutched in her hand. She shakes her head. “Ja jebię! Amerykanie…”

    Carol breaks out in a grin. “Did you just cuss me out?” She doesn’t think she’s ever heard Zosia swear, even if it is in another language.

    Zosia narrows her eyes. “When did you learn Polish?”

    Carol laughs, the corners of her eyes crinkling. “Hey, ‘fuck you’ sounds the same in any language.”

    “I actually said fuck me,” Zosia corrects her, and it’s Carol’s turn to splutter as she chokes on air.

    She feels her cheeks flush. Those aren’t two words she ever expected to come from Zosia’s mouth. Her body reacts in other ways, but Carol does her best to push that from her mind.

    “Potty mouth,” Carol teases, despite the fact she regularly turns the air blue with her own cussing.

    Zosia just rolls her eyes. Turning her attention back to the chilli, she shakes her head and clucks her tongue.

    “I don’t think even I can salvage this. Want me to make something else?”

    “No!” Carol throws her hands up and crosses the kitchen in two short steps, ushering Zosia away from the stove. “No cooking for you tonight. You’re my guest.”

    Zosia pulls a face, her hand instinctively going to her bump. “In the interest of the safety of my unborn child, can I at least supervise?”

    “Oh, I’m not cooking either,” Carol insists. “Takeout?”

    “God, yes.” Zosia sighs in relief, earning a playful scowl from the other woman.

    “Hey, it wasn’t that bad!”

    “You try it then—”

    Zosia reaches behind Carol for the spoon, but Carol grabs her wrist and pulls her away.

    “Nope. I’m good.”

    Zosia laughs, turning to face Carol again—her free hand finding the other woman’s chest as she steadies herself—and suddenly all of the air leaves the room as they stand inches apart.

    Zosia towers over Carol, looking down at her with an unreadable expression as Carol forgets how to breathe.

    She swallows hard, lets out an awkward chuckle as she drops Zosia’s wrist and takes a step back—her hip knocking into the counter behind her.

    Carol’s mouth feels dry. She’s lightheaded as she turns and starts rummaging through the drawer full of takeout menus.

    “Uh, I’ll order in. What—what do you feel like eating?”

    Behind her, Zosia blinks slowly, as if coming out of her own daze. “Uh, anything is fine… but nothing too spicy.”

    Carol nods quickly, grateful for something practical to focus on. She flips through the stack of takeout menus in the drawer with slightly more enthusiasm than necessary.

    “Right. Non-spicy,” she mutters, scanning the options. “So that rules out Thai. And Indian. And probably Mexican.”

    Zosia leans against the counter behind her, arms loosely folded over the gentle swell of her stomach.

    “Probably,” she agrees.

    Carol pulls out a menu triumphantly. “Chinese?”

    “That works.”

    Carol grabs her phone and starts dialing before she can overthink it. The familiar rhythm of placing the order steadies her a little—fried rice, sesame chicken, vegetable dumplings, extra spring rolls because Zosia always demolishes those.

    She hangs up and exhales. “Should be about thirty minutes.” She announces.

    Zosia nods approvingly.

    While they wait, Carol busies herself tidying the kitchen—wiping the counter, putting away the milk carton Zosia abandoned, turning the burner off beneath the doomed pot of chilli.

    The smell of it still hangs thick in the air.

    Zosia wrinkles her nose.

    Carol notices. “Hey.”

    “I didn’t say anything.”

    “You didn’t have to.”

    Zosia laughs softly and drifts toward the kitchen table. She lowers herself into one of the chairs with a small sigh, one hand resting automatically on her belly.

    Carol watches her from over by the sink. She pats her shirt pocket and the little folded slip of paper there.

    She has an ulterior motive in inviting Zosia over to her place for a change. Carol knows there’s stuff they need to talk about, and she feels more comfortable with the home advantage.

    That doesn’t mean she isn’t scared shitless, though.

    “Do you, uh, want a Diet Pepsi?” she offers, crossing to the fridge before Zosia can even answer.

    “Sure. Thanks.”

    “No problem.”

    Carol takes two cans out of the fridge. She doesn’t usually drink the stuff, but she knows Zosia likes it.

    “Thank you,” Zosia says as Carol hands her one of the cans.

    Carol keeps the other and cracks it open with a hiss, condensation running down the side. Zosia eyes her curiously.

    “You don’t have to drink that for my sake, you know. You can have something alcoholic if you want.”

    Carol shakes her head. “This is fine… I actually haven’t had a drink in about two weeks.”

    Carol keeps her gaze fixed on the can instead of Zosia, running her finger up it to catch a falling bead of water.

    “That’s… that’s really great.”

    They haven’t really discussed Carol’s drinking. It’s one of the many things that sits quietly between them.

    Carol clears her throat. “It’s not a big deal. Not like I’m joining AA or anything. Just… cutting back.”

    Zosia nods in understanding, and Carol is grateful she doesn’t make a big deal about it. She’s seen Carol at her absolute worst with the alcohol, but she doesn’t bring it up. Doesn’t use it against her.

    Carol rubs at the back of her neck, not sure where to start. “I actually invited you over tonight because… because I wanted to talk.”

    “Oh.” Zosia sits up a little straighter. “Okay. What did you want to talk about? Is it the gender reveal? Because if it’s too much to organise, I’m sure Koumba will be happy to—”

    “No. No, it’s not that.” In truth, Carol hasn’t even begun to think about the gender reveal yet—other than wondering what the slip of paper says. “It’s, uh… why Koumba? For your birthing partner? Why him?”

    Zosia looks momentarily taken aback, unsure of how to answer. She tilts her head, clasps and unclasps her hands in front of her.

    “Well… there isn’t anyone else.”

    Zosia shrugs. Carol doesn’t miss the pain in her voice. The regret.

    “Did you… did you even think about asking me?”

    Carol hates how small her voice sounds as she asks, like it’s middle school all over again and she didn’t get picked for dodgeball.

    “Of course I did…”

    Zosia makes to reach out across the table for Carol’s hand, but stops herself. She places one hand on top of the other, rubbing absently at her wrist.

    “I wanted to ask you. I did. I just…” Zosia shakes her head, trapping her bottom lip between her teeth.

    “I’m the asshole who told you I wanted nothing to do with the kid?” Carol supplies helpfully.

    Zosia nods again, wearing a sheepish smile as she ducks her head.

    She tucks her hair back behind her ear with a soft sigh. “It’s okay. Really. I get it. You never wanted—”

    “I want to be your birthing partner!” The words spill out of Carol’s mouth before she can second-guess them.

    Across from her, Zosia’s eyes widen at the admission.

    “That is… if you want me to be?”

    Zosia looks torn. Her eyes soften as she finally reaches across the table, taking Carol’s hand in her own.

    “Carol, I am so grateful to have you in my life—”

    She stops short as Carol abruptly pulls her hand back. She stands, crossing her arms over her chest as she anxiously shifts her weight from side to side.

    “It’s fine.”

    “Carol—”

    “No, Zosia. Really it’s fine. You don’t have to worry about hurting my feelings. You can just say no… that’s probably the fucking sanest thing anyway. It was a stupid idea—”

    “Carol!” Zosia snaps, finally getting her attention.

    Carol stops moving, lets her arms drop to her sides, and stares at Zosia with the look of someone waiting to be let down easy.

    “Could you please sit?” Zosia gestures to the chair Carol just vacated.

    Reluctantly, she does. She places her hands on the edge of the table, like she’s getting ready to run.

    Zosia takes a deep, steadying breath. “Carol, I would love for you to be my birthing partner—”

    “But?”

    Zosia stares her down and Carol puts her hands up in apology.

    “Sorry,” she says, placing her hands down on her knees. They move up and down as Carol sits forward in the chair, heels raised, her legs bouncing on the balls of her feet as if the nervous energy humming through her body has nowhere else to go.

    Once she’s sure Carol isn’t going to interrupt again, Zosia continues. “You have been a godsend these last couple of months, and I am so grateful for everything you have done for me. For us.”

    Zosia rubs at her stomach.

    “…But I have to think of what’s best for my child. I can’t keep relying on someone who—one day soon—will up and walk out of our lives.”

    “Zosia—”

    “I don’t blame you, Carol. You don’t owe me anything, and you’ve already done so much—”

    “Zos—”

    “I just can’t keep depending on you—”

    “You can!” Carol blurts it out, hands slamming on the table in front of her. “You totally can! That’s what I’ve been trying to tell you! I want… I want to be in the kid’s life.”

    Zosia’s lips purse, forming a little “o.”

    “I’m not looking for joint custody or anything like that,” Carol rushes to explain. “I just… I want to be there. For both of you.”

    Zosia frowns, struggling to process what she’s hearing. “I don’t understand, you said—”

    “I know what I said.” Carol sighs. “But I said that to a stranger about a…about something the size of a kidney bean. I’ve watched this thing grow inside of you, and now it has… fingernails and fucking eyelashes and it’s real.”

    “And you’re not a stranger anymore. You’re… you’re my…” Carol bites her lip, swallowing what she really wants to say.

    “You’re my friend. And I want to be in your life. I want to be in your kid’s life.”

    For a tense moment Zosia is completely still, her expression blank. Carol feels a bubble of panic welling up in her chest. She fights the urge to get up and walk out of her own house.

    Then—Zosia breaks out in a wide smile, her whole face lighting up and her eyes shining with tears.

    She reaches across the table, taking both of Carol’s hands in her own. Carol doesn’t pull away this time.

    “In that case,” Zosia says softly, “I would love for you to be my birthing partner.”

    Chapter 12

    Chapter Notes

    Carol is halfway through rinsing a mug in the sink when her phone starts vibrating across the counter. She glances at the screen, brow furrowing at the unfamiliar number before wiping her hands on the front of her jeans and answering.

    “Hello?”

    “Carol? This is Davis Taffler.”

    Carol straightens slightly, the name taking a second to register.

    “Oh. Right. Taffler. Hey.”

    “I wanted to update you on the case against the fertility clinic.” His voice carries that smooth, clipped professionalism of someone who bills by the minute. He’s called and emailed a handful of times in the ten weeks since Carol retained him to go after the clinic.

    Carol hums distractedly, nudging the mug into the drying rack. She leans her hip against the counter, cradling the phone between shoulder and ear.

    “Okay.”

    “So, I’ve reviewed the waiver you signed when you had your eggs harvested for storage,” Taffler continues. “It does limit potential damages somewhat. However, the clinic appears eager to resolve the matter quietly. With as little public attention as possible.”

    “Uh-huh. Me too,” Carol says.

    The last thing she needs is any of this going public. The Wycaro fans can be a rabid bunch at the best of times, particularly in the lull between a new book being released and the next one being announced. They tend to sniff out any kind of public scandal and pounce on it.

    Carol still vividly remembers when the Wycarians found out she and Helen were married after the release of the third book. There had practically been a civil war in the fandom between those who’d suspected Carol’s true sexuality all along and the fans who refused to believe a lesbian could write such convincing heterosexual love scenes.

    “They’ve made an initial offer,” Taffler goes on, bringing Carol’s attention back to the phone call.

    “Mm.”

    “Five hundred thousand dollars.”

    Carol blinks, sure she’s misheard. “Sorry—what?”

    “Five hundred thousand,” he repeats calmly. “Up front. They’re hoping to avoid lengthy litigation.”

    Carol lets out a short, incredulous laugh. “Five hundred thou—are you serious?” She presses her palm against the counter, suddenly needing the support. “That seems… high.”

    “It’s actually quite conservative.”

    Carol squints at the fridge like it might help her process that sentence.

    “Conservative?”

    “Yes. I’m currently working toward an out-of-court settlement in the range of one point five to two million.”

    Carol’s brain stutters.

    “…What?”

    “Million,” Taffler repeats.

    The word lands in the kitchen with a strange, hollow weight. Carol rubs her forehead. She’s a bestselling author of multiple books, so it’s not like she’s short on money. The mortgage on the house is paid off, and there’s enough in her bank account for her to retire without worrying about paying the bills.

    Two million, though. That’s life-changing money.

    “Hold on, hold on.” She pushes off the counter and begins pacing the small stretch of floor between the sink and the table. “You’re telling me my suit is worth that much?”

    “Yes,” Taffler says, unruffled. “You have several viable avenues of damages.”

    Carol drags a hand through her hair. “Such as?”

    “Emotional distress. Reproductive harm. The cost of your original IVF cycles.” He pauses briefly. “And potential future custody litigation.”

    Carol stops pacing.

    Custody?

    She would never do that to Zosia.

    Her hand drifts absently to the breast pocket of her shirt, right over her heart.

    Her fingers find the familiar folded edge of paper there. She pulls it out slowly as Taffler continues on the other end of the line, discussing their potential options for the legal suit.

    The slip of paper is soft now, worn along the creases from days of being handled. The corners are rounded, the fold faintly frayed where she keeps worrying it open with her thumb before forcing herself to stop.

    She’s had it a week now.

    A week.

    Boy, or girl.

    One single word sitting inside that fold.

    Carol rubs the edge of the paper between her fingers while Taffler keeps talking.

    “…so from a legal standpoint, the emotional impact alone strengthens the case significantly—”

    She barely hears him. Her thumb slides beneath the corner of the fold again. Just a peek. It would take less than a second.

    Carol’s chest tightens.

    No.

    She presses the paper flat again, staring down at it like it’s personally testing her resolve.

    “…Carol?”

    “Yeah.” She clears her throat. “Yeah, I’m here.”

    “So my recommendation is that we continue pursuing settlement negotiations. Their first offer suggests they’re willing to move.”

    Carol sinks into the chair at the table. She turns the slip of paper over in her hands. In less than five months, she’s going to have a child out in the world.

    The thought still feels strange every time it crosses her mind, like trying on a coat that doesn’t quite fit.

    Taffler’s voice filters through the phone again. “Would you like me to proceed?”

    Carol looks down at the folded paper.

    She thinks about Zosia standing in her doorway in that yellow sundress. About the weight of her hand when she rests it absently over her stomach. Thinks about the tiny flutter she felt under her palm weeks ago that had made her whole brain go quiet.

    Babies cost a lot of money. There’s diapers and formula, and clothes and college tuition. That thought gives Carol pause. She’s going to watch the bump in Zosia’s stomach grown in to a whole ass baby, and that baby is going to grow into a child, and eventually an adult.

    The realisation lands heavier than the number Taffler just quoted. Because if she’s in the kid’s life… What does that make her?

    Carol exhales slowly. “Aunty Carol?” she mutters under her breath. The words leave a sour taste in her mouth. She imagines Zosia introducing her that way:

    This is Carol. She’s like a sister to me.

    Carol grimaces.

    She can’t think of anything worse.

    Not when every time Zosia smiles at her, something in Carol’s chest twists a little tighter.

    When standing too close to her makes Carol forget how to breathe.

    When she’s already so far gone it’s embarrassing.

    Carol presses the heel of her hand to her eyes. God, when did this all become so complicated?

    “…Carol?”

    Carol drops her hand and straightens slightly. “Yeah.” She sighs. “Go ahead. Keep working the settlement.”

    “Very good. I’ll be in touch.”

    The call clicks off a moment later, and the kitchen falls quiet again.

    Carol sits there for a long time with the folded piece of paper in her hands, staring at it while the afternoon light shifts slowly across the table.

    Her thumb drifts back to the edge of the fold.

    Boy.

    Or girl.

    She presses it shut again before she can look. Knowing the answer won’t make any of this any easier.

     

    -

     

    Carol stands in the middle of her study, hands on hips, staring at the whiteboard like it personally offends her.

    The room smells faintly of old paper and furniture polish, with a lingering saltiness that comes from the collection of decorative rope knots hanging along one wall.

    The whole space is unapologetically nautical—ship wheels, brass lanterns, a framed map of Wycaro that she had commissioned years ago when the books started selling well enough to justify such indulgences.

    Normally the room settles her. Today it’s just making her feel trapped. Carol folds her arms and studies the whiteboard again. It’s a hot mess.

    Multicoloured marker scrawls cover nearly every inch of the surface: arrows, bullet points, half-erased notes layered on top of each other until it looks like the planning board for a conspiracy theorist.

    At the very top, written in large block letters:

    GENDER REVEAL – 24 WEEKS

    Below that:

    DATE: ✔
    GUEST LIST: ✔

    And then… chaos. Cake ideas. Balloon ideas. Confetti cannons. Something involving a piñata that she crossed out after deciding it sounded too juvenile, as well as vaguely violent.

    Carol drags a hand down her face. Zosia had asked if it would be okay to have the reveal at twenty-four weeks, right before they start the prenatal classes.

    Carol had agreed immediately. Of course that was fine. Perfect, even. A whole month to organise something…

    Except that was a week ago, and she still hasn’t figured out what the hell she’s actually going to do for the party.

    Carol uncaps the marker in her hand and taps it absently against the board.

    The guest list is small—just a handful of people Zosia actually wants there. Koumba, unfortunately. Most of the people from the restaurant. Manousos, for Carol’s benefit, and maybe one or two others. The list was the easy part.

    Everything else…

    Carol squints at a scribbled line halfway down the board that reads Fireworks??? She circles it again, then immediately draws a frustrated line through the circle. Useless.

    Zosia had offered to help; or rather, she had offered Koumba’s help. Carol politely declined. There was absolutely no universe in which Carol Sturka would go to Zosia’s brother-in-law for help with anything, let alone help planning the gender reveal party for their child.

    Especially not after the disaster of that dinner last month. Carol still feels vaguely ill thinking about it.

    She definitely doesn’t want to ask Zosia for help either. Zosia who is so used to doing everything for herself—at home, at the restaurant. She handles everything. Carries every burden.

    Carol wants to do something nice for her. Something more than just driving her to a doctor’s appointment. She wants to surprise her. To make the party everything she’s ever wanted—and more.

    Preferably, she’d also like it to not look like it was planned by someone who’s never attended a gender reveal in her life.

    Carol sighs and leans her forehead lightly against the whiteboard. “This is stupid,” she mutters.

    She’s saved from her own personal hell when the doorbell goes. Carol straightens with a startled blink before tossing the marker onto her desk and heading downstairs.

    When she opens the door, Manousos is standing, holding a brown paper bag that smells suspiciously like garlic and grilled meat.

    Carol squints at him. “…Why is everyone feeding me lately?”

    Manousos grins and holds up the bag. “Because you would forget to eat otherwise.”

    Carol rolls her eyes but steps aside to let him in, the smell of warm pita and roasted lamb drifting into the house as he walks past her.

    He drops the bag onto the kitchen counter and starts unpacking containers and pulling out plates like he owns the place. It’s not unusual for Manousos to come over with dinner, but he usually calls ahead first—especially lately, since Carol has been eating dinner with Zosia most nights.

    Carol leans casually against the fridge, arms folded, watching him closely. “You didn’t answer my question.”

    Manousos glances up. “What question?”

    “Why does everyone in my life feel like they need to feed me?”

    He shrugs, amused. “I was messaging Zosia earlier.”

    Carol’s eyebrows shoot up. There it is.

    “You were what?”

    “She mentioned she’s working late tonight.” Manousos slides a full plate toward her. “So she said you’d probably be alone for dinner tonight.”

    Carol stares at him. “…Why was Zosia messaging you?”

    Manousos pauses with a stuffed pita halfway to his mouth. “What do you mean?”

    “I mean,” Carol says slowly, “since when do you two have each other’s numbers?”

    He blinks at her. “We swapped them when she came over to check on you during…” He trails off. There’s no need for him to finish. They both know when he’s talking about.

    Carol stiffens. Manousos continues without missing a beat. “You know. In case it happened again.”

    Carol folds her arms. “Well, that’s comforting.”

    Manousos glances at her and immediately recognises the tone. He shakes his head. “Don’t start, Carol.”

    “I’m not starting anything!” Carol throws her hands up in the air. “You’re the one having little chats about me with Zosia… behind my back—“

    “People care about you, Carol,” Manousos snaps at her, his tone stern but his words gentle. “Whether you like it or not.”

    “Whatever.” Carol snatches her fork off the counter and stabs it into the pile of rice on her plate with far more force than necessary. “I’m just surprised she didn’t mention having your number,” she says stiffly.

    Manousos watches her shovel a forkful of food into her mouth.

    “Mhm,” he says.

    They stand in silence for a moment, then he asks, “How’s the planning going for the party?”

    Carol groans and gestures over her shoulder to the stairs. “Come see for yourself.” They both take their plates as Carol leads him upstairs and into her study.

    Manousos steps inside and surveys the whiteboard. He takes a long moment before letting out a low whistle. “…Wow.”

    Carol gestures helplessly. “Right?”

    Manousos points to a section written in large capital letters.

    “Crop dusters?”

    Carol brightens slightly. “Yeah. Planes.”

    “Planes?” He frowns.

    “Yeah. Planes, like…” Carol swoops one hand through the air, making whooshing noises.

    Manousos stares at her like she’s stupid. “I know what planes are. What have they got to do with babies?”

    “Well,” Carol clarifies, “they can fly over the party and release coloured smoke. You know, pink for a girl, blue for a boy?”

    Manousos stares at her. “Carol.”

    “What?”

    “That is insane.”

    “It’s memorable!” Carol tries. She’s loath to admit it, but so far crop dusters are the best idea she’s got.

    Carol deflates as Manousos stares her down. She drops into the chair by her desk and rubs her temples, her plate of food abandoned. “Fuck it. I don’t know what I’m doing.”

    Manousos leans against the wall, arms folded. “Well.”

    Carol squints at him. She can already tell she’s not going to like what he has to suggest. “Well, what?”

    “You could ask Zosia’s brother-in-law.”

    Carol’s head snaps up. “No!”

    “He’d probably help you,” Manousos argues.

    Carol doubles down. “Absolutely not!”

    Manousos sighs. “Carol…”

    “No! No way am I ever giving Koumba the pleasure of me begging him for help!”

    Manousos throws his hands up. “Carol Sturka, you are impossible.”

    “Correct.” Carol replies gleefully. She’d rather fail on her own than impress Zosia with Koumba’s help. Except…

    She wants everything to be perfect for Zosia.

    Carol stares up at the whiteboard, trying to will the jumbled mess of ideas into some kind of coherent plan. She’s got three weeks left to make it work; otherwise, she might just have to fall on her sword and ask Koumba for help.

    The house grows quiet again once Manousos leaves. Carol takes their empty plates and dumps them in the sink before wandering back toward the living room, phone already in her hand.

    Her thumb hovers over Zosia’s name. They rarely go a day without talking now. Usually several times. Carol opens their message thread. It’s at the top of her conversations, right above Manousos.

    Carol: Bored.

    Three little dots appear almost immediately.

    Zosia: Sounds like a you problem. Some of us work for a living…

    Carol grins, imagining Zosia’s accent wrapping around the teasing words, the lilt of her brow, the little smile that would be playing on her lips…

    “Fuck.” Carol mutters to herself. Maybe she’s going stir crazy from spending all day staring at her whiteboard.

    Carol: Tragic. Do you need help with the deep cleaning tonight?

    There’s a pause this time. Long enough for Carol to grow anxious before she notices Zosia is texting back.

    Zosia: We’re okay, but thank you. I’m stuck in my office until service ends and we can start.

    Zosia: At least I have some interesting reading material to keep me entertained.

    Another message follows almost immediately—a photo this time. Zosia’s hand, long slender fingers curled around the spine of a very familiar book. The second Wycaro.

    Carol straightens a little, giving a shake of her head with a scoff.

    Carol: Your taste in literature is still questionable, I see…

    Zosia: I don’t know… I think the author shows promise.

    Carol huffs out a quiet laugh.

    Zosia: It’s good, though some parts are… very distracting.

    Carol’s thumb stills over the screen as she contemplates a reply.

    Carol: Distracting how?

    There’s just enough of a delay to make Carol’s pulse tick up.

    Zosia: The tension.

    Zosia: The way they drag things out.

    Zosia: It borders on cruel, actually.

    Carol exhales slowly, heat creeping up the back of her neck as she reads and re-reads Zosia’s last few messages.

    She ignores the urge to respond with something outright flirtatious. It feels too risky.

    She settles on:

    Carol: That’s called good writing. Gotta keep your readers on the hook…

    Zosia: Mhm. If you say so.

    Another pause.

    Zosia: There’s a scene near the beginning.

    Zosia: I had to read it twice.

    Carol: For clairty?

    Carol presses her lips together, already knowing exactly which drawn out scene Zosia is referring to.

    …Lucasia bound to the stately captain’s bed, Raban edging her mercilessly with a single purple phoenix feather.

    Carol clenches her eyes shut and sees the scene playing out as vividly as a blockbuster movie in her mind—only Raban is no longer a proud and haughty man, but a beautiful polish woman with eyes so deep you could happily drown in them.

    In her mind, Lucasia’s place is taken by—well, that’s between Carol and god.

    Her eyes snap open as she receives another message.

    Zosia: Obviously.

    Carol snorts a laugh.

    Carol: It’s heavy stuff. Got to make sure you’re interpreting it correctly.

    She tries for something light and a little self-deprecating, but when the next messages come through Carol can only stare blankly at them.

    Zosia: Oh?

    Zosia: And how would you do?

    Carol’s brain short circuits for a full ten seconds.

    Carol: I wouldn’t.

    A beat.

    Carol: Not over text.

    She immediately regrets the suggestive nature of the last message.

    There’s a longer pause now. Long enough that Carol’s stomach twists as she re-reads the earlier messages, wondering if she’s just crossed some invisible line.

    Then—

    Zosia: Probably for the best.

    Zosia: I have work to do.

    A final message follows.

    Zosia: But I’ll hold you to that.

    Carol stares at the screen, her pulse pounding in her own ears and her mouth dry. It’s the only place that is.

    “Fuck,” she mutters, dropping her phone onto her chest with a heavy sigh.

     

    -


    A week later, Carol is no further along. The whiteboard in her study still looks like a battlefield, every idea half-baked and rejected. She’s spent countless evenings staring at it like the perfect idea might spontaneously reveal itself.

    She doesn’t want to let Zosia down, not after finally admitting she wants to be in the baby’s life. Not when Zosia’s counting on her to make the reveal something special.

    So she swallows her pride and does the last thing she ever imagined doing—she goes to Koumba for help.

    Carol chooses a morning she knows Zosia is at a hair appointment to drop by the restaurant unannounced. Koumba is setting up the bar as she walks in, shoulders hunched and hands shoved in to the pockets of her jeans.

    “Carol?” He looks surprised to see her, but recovers quickly with a bright smile. “It’s lovely to see you—but Zosia isn’t here right now.”

    “Hey, uh, yeah I know.” Carol slides onto a stool at the bar. She drums her fingers against the bar top as Koumba watches her with curious eyes. “I’m actually here to see you.”

    “Oh.” Koumba can’t hide it this time. “To what do I owe the pleasure?”

    Carol wrings her hands together, looking anywhere but at the flamboyantly dressed man in front of her. He’s wearing a garish orange suit today, and—irrationally—it only serves to make Carol all the more angry at herself for having to ask him for help.

    She starts off on the defensive, “Look, I know you don’t like me—”

    “What are you talking about?” Koumba frown, genuinely puzzled. “Carol, I have no issue with you.”

    It’s Carol’s turn to frown. She scoffs at him. “Yeah, right.”

    “What have I done to give you this impression?” He presses, and Carol is stumped.

    “Well, I… Uh…” She’s at a loss for words as she scrambles through her memories, trying to pinpoint exactly when she came to the conclusion Zosia’s brother-in-law hates her.

    Even during their disastrous dinner, he’d had nothing but kind words for her. Carol purses her lips, reluctant to admit her own low opinion of Koumba might have coloured how she thinks he thinks about her.

    “That’s not important right now.” Carol waves her hands dismissively as she leans forward on her stool. “I need your help.”

    “Oh.” Koumba’s expression wavers. He tosses the towel he’s holding over his shoulder and leans back against one of the drinks fridges, crossing his arms over his chest. “Carol, I’m not really sure it’s my place to—”

    “Look, I wouldn’t ask if it wasn’t important!” Carol interjects, her voice rising with desperation. “I have no idea what I’m doing with this gender reveal thing.”

    “Gender reveal?” Koumba repeats, a sense of relief washing over him. He lets out an amused chuckle. “Ah. Of course I will help.”

    “Wait—What did you think I was going to ask?”

    Koumba brushes the question off. “Never mind. What do you need for the party?”

    “Uh… a fucking clue?” Carol admits with a heavy sigh, slumping forward on the bar. “I don’t know the first thing about these things, and right now the best idea I’ve got is a crop duster.”

    “A crop duster?” Koumba looks at her blankly.

    “Yeah, you know the planes…” Carol makes the same hand movement as she did for Manousos last week.

    “I know what they are, Carol.” He replies drily. “I must say, that is a terrible idea.”

    “That’s what Manny said.” Carol huffs. “I don’t know what Zosia wants, and I can’t ask her because then it wouldn’t be a surprise, and she’s so excited for this party and… and I don’t want to let her down.”

    Koumba doesn’t reply immediately. He watches her, those hawk like eyes taking in her utter despair at the thought of disappointing Zosia.

    “Okay. Give me your phone.” He holds his hand out expectantly.

    “Why? Carol asks with a frown, but unlocks and hands her phone over anyway. She expects him to put his number in so they can consult on the party.

    “Done.” He announces, handing the phone back over. Carol’s frown deepens as she finds it open on Zosia’s Instagram page. The big blue button that usually says follow—not that Carol stalks her page or anything—now says following.

    Carol’s spent weeks lurking on Zosia’s Instagram, absorbing photograph after photograph of the other woman. She’d gone right back on her profile, to when Malick was still alive and the pair would pose for silly photos. Zosia’s eyes had seemed brighter then, her smile a little less strained.

    “What the fuck? How does that help me with the gender reveal?” Carol feels her cheeks flush with a mix of anger and embarrassment. What if Zosia thinks it’s weird? What if she takes it the wrong way?

    Manousos dismisses her anger with a flourish of his hand. “There is a lady Zosia follows. LaylaBakes. She has been obsessed with her gender reveal cakes for years.”

    “A cake?” Carol scrolls through the list of people Zosia follows and finds the baker’s profile. The cakes are stunning, but she isn’t convinced. “Isn’t that a little… simple? And why the fuck did I need to follow her? You could have just showed me the LaylaBakes profile.”

    “I know.” Koumba beams at her, looking proud of himself. “But Zosia has been dancing around the idea of following you on there for a month now and, I for one, am quite frankly sick of hearing about it. Now she can follow you back and I can have some peace. Everybody wins.”

    “I…” Carol doesn’t follow. “Why didn’t she want to follow me?” Admittedly Carol’s page is mostly Wycaro related, pictures from book readings and fan meet and greets, but there are few personal photos sprinkled in there too.

    “Never mind.” Koumba dismisses her question again. “And a cake is simple, but so is Zosia.”

    Carol bites back the urge to disagree. Zosia is far from simple. To Carol, she gets more complicated every day.

    Sensing her doubts, he adds, “Zosia is not one for big, grand gestures. You could show up with a cake you made yourself and Zosia would look at you like you hung the moon and the stars.”

    Carol knows the exact look he’s talking about, and the thought of it makes her knees weak and her breath hitch. She swallows, trying not to read too much into it. “I dunno. A cake just doesn’t feel like it would cut it.”

    Koumba leans over the bar, bringing him down to Carol’s level. His eyes soften. “It wouldn’t just be a cake. Not to Zosia… Layla made Zosia and Malick’s wedding cake. To her it would be—”

    “A way of honouring Malick.” Carol finishes for him, the meaning behind the gesture slamming in to her like a ten tonne truck. “Of including him in the day.”

    Koumba nods slowly, a rueful smile finding his lips.

    Carol grows quiet, thinking it over. Eventually she nods. “Thanks, Koumba.”

    “My pleasure.” He says, and for once Carol believes him. He clears his throat as he stands, the melancholy moment passing. “It might be short notice, but if you reach out to Layla and mention Zosia’s name I’m sure she’ll be happy to help.”

    Carol nods again getting to her feet. “Will do.” She turns to walk out, but pauses halfway to the door. She turns back around, finds Koumba’s eyes still glued to her.

    Carol really can’t help herself.

    She’s like a moth to a flame as she asks, “Koumba, how long have you been in love with her?”

    She expects a visceral reaction from him—anger or furious denial. None comes. His eyes grow knowing and wistful as he offers her a sombre smile.

    “How long have you?” The question is sincere, but it lands like a physical blow.

    Carol purses her lips, looks down at the floor, hands clenching and unclenching at her sides. “I—”

    “Carol!” The bubbly voice behind her saves Carol from having to answer. She turns to find Zosia walking through the door, face lit up at the sight of her.

    The younger woman moves to greet her with a hug and Carol squeezes her back without a second thought—even as Koumba’s gaze burns in to her from the other side of the room.

    Zosia pulls away first, beaming at her. “What are you doing here? We didn’t have plans today… did we?”

    Zosia’s smile falters slightly as she doubts herself. At twenty-two weeks pregnant she’s already experiencing full baby-brain-fog thanks to the hormones surging through her body. Only yesterday she misplaced her car keys. After searching the house high and low for them she’d eventually found them in the freezer.

    “No. No we didn’t.” Carol rushes to assure her. She glances nervously back at Koumba as she tries to come up with a convincing lie. “I was just… uh, in the neighbourhood. Your hair looks amazing by the way.”

    “Aw, thank you, Carol.” Zosia reaches up and tugs at the ends of it self-consciously. She’s gone a little shorter this time, and seems pleased to have Carol acknowledge it.

    “Have you had lunch yet? The baby is seriously craving strawberry milkshake and fries right now.”

    “The baby, huh?” Carol teases, quirking a single eyebrow at her.

    “Yes. The baby.” Zosia laughs, her hand finding Carol’s arm, and the tension in Carol’s shoulders melts—thoughts of Instagram profiles, gender reveal parties and piercing personal questions vanishing along with it. “And it’s your fault anyway.”

    “Guilty as charged.” Carol grins, completely forgetting Koumba exists. “How does lunch on me at the diner sound?”

    “It sounds wonderful.” Zosia answers, slipping her arm in to Carol’s. Her eyes flick behind Carol, landing on Koumba like she only just realised he was there.

    “Oh, Koumba, would you like to join us?” She asks, and Carol feels the all too familiar stirring of the green eyed monster that lives in the pit of her stomach.

    Thankfully, he declines.

    “No, thank you.” He smiles at Zosia, then his gaze shifts to Carol as he adds, with a pointed look, “you know what they say. Two’s company. Three’s a crowd.”

    Chapter End Notes

    As ever, thanks for reading! I really appreciate all the kudos and comments. I read every one (even if I suck at replying) and knowing people are enjoying the fic definitely encourages me to write more.

    Feel free to follow me on Twitter (@Trufreak89) where I post about fic updates and have marginally better social skills.

    - Tan.

    Chapter 13

    Chapter Notes


    Carol stands in the middle of her study, one hand on her hip and the other holding a beer at her side. She hasn’t touched any of the hard stuff since Helen’s anniversary and her weekend bender

    She stares at the whiteboard like it’s about to betray her at any moment and wipe itself clean.

    It’s… done.

    For the first time in weeks, there’s nothing left to circle, nothing left to cross out, no frantic arrows connecting half-baked ideas into something resembling more mess than plan.

    The board is neat. Organised. Final.

    At the top, in decisive black marker:

    GENDER REVEAL — SUNDAY AFTERNOON

    Below it:

    LOCATION: RESTAURANT ✔
    GUEST LIST ✔
    DECOR ✔
    MUSIC ✔
    CAKE — LAYLA ✔ (PICK UP FRIDAY – VEGAS)

    Carol exhales slowly, rubbing the back of her neck as she scans over it again, searching for something she’s missed. There has to be something. There’s always something.

    But no.

    It’s all there.

    The only hitch—the only tiny, inconvenient, ridiculous thing—is that Layla won’t be done until Friday, which means she has to drive to Vegas to pick up a cake like some kind of deranged pastry courier.

    A cake that could make or break Zosia’s day.

    Carol huffs out a quiet breath. “Of course it’s in Vegas,” she mutters.

    Still. It will all be worth it to see the look on Zosia’s face when she sees it. The thought settles somewhere low in her chest, steady and sure.

    For Zosia.

    Carol reaches out and nudges the whiteboard to the side, wheels squeaking faintly on the tracks it’s mounted on as she shifts it to reveal the second board behind it—half-forgotten, pushed out of sight months ago when she’d decided, firmly, definitively, that she was done.

    Done with Wycaro. Done with writing. Done.

    Her gaze catches on the faded marker scrawl. Fragments of ideas. Names. Arrows leading nowhere. Half sentences that never became anything more.

    Carol lifts the eraser automatically, pressing it to the board. She stops as Zosia’s voice echoes faintly in her head.

    'The way you describe the ship… it feels alive.'
    'I like how you don’t rush things. You let it breathe.'
    'That twist in the second book? I didn’t see it coming at all.'

    Carol’s grip tightens slightly around the eraser.

    Zosia is making her way through the third book now. Slowly. Carefully. Like a meal she’s savouring. And every time she finishes a chapter, a scene, she tells Carol all about it—what she noticed, what she liked, what stayed with her.

    Helen used to read everything. First drafts, messy drafts, half-finished drafts. Pretentious ideas that never saw the light of day, like Raban as a woman.

    She would pay lip service. Skim the sex scenes. Reassure Carol that her audience would love it, but Zosia—

    Zosia reads like Wycaro is a living, breathing thing; exactly how Carol used to feel writing it.

    She exhales slowly, lowering the eraser as her eyes scan over the bare bones of her next book. There’s something there, a story waiting to be teased out of all the chaos.

    Carol’s had a new premise playing out in her mind these last few weeks, sparked by late-night conversations about her work with Zosia.

    She tries to focus on the board, and not dwell on the fact that Zosia mostly reads her Wycaro series before bed. That Carol’s words, her characters—that she—might be the last thing on Zosia’s mind before she falls asleep.

    Carol exhales slowly. The thought leaves something warm low in her stomach, something not entirely unwelcome.

    “…Fuck it.”

    She reaches for a marker. The first line comes hesitantly. Then another. And another.

    The squeak of the pen against the board fills the room, sharp and insistent, quickening as the ideas start to slot into place. Connections forming, threads weaving. The shape of something bigger taking hold before she can second-guess it.

    Her pulse picks up. She steps back, then forward again, adding more. Crossing things out. Rewriting them. Arrows, notes, fragments—

    Until the board is full.

    Carol stares at it, chest rising and falling a little faster than before. A slow smile tugs at the corner of her mouth. “Oh,” she murmurs.

    That’s… good.

    That’s really good.

    Before she can talk herself out of it, she opens her laptop and drops into the chair at her desk. The screen glows to life and, with a few clicks, a blank document stares back at her.

    She hesitates for half a second.

    Then starts typing.

     

    -

     

    Zosia curls her fingers around the warm ceramic mug, the smell of rich Assam tea strong and sugary as she takes a slow sip.

    The sunlight spilling through the kitchen windows is softer, warmer—mid-morning rather than the early dawn she’s grown accustomed to over the years. With less to do at the restaurant lately, she’s been allowing herself the luxury of sleeping in a little later.

    Koumba leans back in his chair across from her, watching with mild amusement.

    “You should enjoy this while you can,” he says. “In a few months, sleep will be but a distant memory.”

    Zosia smiles faintly, setting her mug down. “I’m trying.”

    And she is.

    It’s just—

    Everything feels… too close now.

    The steady, undeniable presence of the baby growing heavier, more real with every passing day. She’ll find out the sex in a few days. Prenatal classes next week.

    Zosia rests her hand absently over her bump, her thumb brushing lightly against the fabric of her shirt. The baby is active this morning, kicking faintly in response to the sugary tea.

    “I still can’t believe it’s happening,” she admits softly.

    Koumba’s expression softens. “You’re allowed to be excited, you know.”

    “I am excited,” she says quickly. Then, after a beat, “I just… worry that it might be too much too soon.”

    “For you?”

    Zosia hesitates, then softly shakes her head.

    Koumba arches a brow. “Carol?”

    “She’s doing all of this. The party, the planning, being my birthing partner… I don’t want her to feel overwhelmed. Not because of me.”

    Koumba huffs a quiet laugh. “Carol Sturka? Overwhelmed? I think she thrives on the chaos.”

    Zosia smiles, but there’s still a flicker of uncertainty there.

    “Has she told you anything about it?” she asks, “The party?”

    Koumba shakes his head easily. “Only that I’m invited. Sunday afternoon. That’s all I know.”

    Zosia worries at her lip. She hopes Carol hasn’t gone to too much trouble for the party. She glances at the clock on the wall.

    Carol should be here any minute. They’re supposed to meet with a potential midwife this morning. The thought sends a small, anxious flutter through her chest. She hasn’t told Koumba who she’s meeting today, or why.

    “I’ve been thinking about a home birth,” she says, tossing it out there almost casually to see how it lands.

    Koumba goes very still, his eyes widening. It would be comical if he didn’t look so worried. He exhales slowly, his gaze dropping to the table and the mug clasped in his hands. “Zosia, you know how I feel about that.”

    “I know,” she says gently. “But things are different now. It’s much safer—”

    Koumba’s jaw tightens as he finally looks up again. “Tell that to my mother.”

    The words land heavier than he probably intended. Zosia knows it’s a touchy subject. Rather than bristle, she softens, reaching across the table to squeeze his hand. “Nothing is set in stone yet. We’re meeting the midwife today. If she thinks it isn’t safe, then I won’t push it.”

    He doesn’t argue. But he doesn’t look convinced either.

    Zosia lets the silence sit for a moment before glancing at the clock again and reaching for her phone. Carol should be here by now.

    “I’ll just check where she is,” she says, already dialling.

    It rings.
    And rings.
    And rings—

    Zosia frowns slightly, shifting in her seat.

    “Hello?” Carol’s voice is rough. Thick with sleep.

    Zosia blinks. “Did I wake you?”

    “…No,” Carol says, which is immediately followed by, “Yes. Yes, you did.”

    Zosia bites back a smile. “We’re meeting the midwife in forty minutes.”

    There’s a pause, followed by a muffled “Fuck,” like Carol momentarily covered the speaker.

    Zosia laughs softly. “Get ready. I’ll come pick you up.”

    “I can still make it—”

    “I’m already grabbing my keys,” Zosia insists.

    There’s another pause as Carol weighs up her options. Then, quieter, “Okay. See you soon.”

    The line clicks dead and Zosia lowers the phone, still smiling.

    Koumba watches her knowingly. “Late again?” he hums. “Well. Let’s just hope she’s more punctual for the birth.”

    “She will be,” Zosia says firmly, pushing her chair back and holding her stomach as she rises. “Even if I have to handcuff her to me myself to make it happen.”

    Koumba’s grin turns wicked. “Careful,” he says, eyes alight with mischief. “She might enjoy that.”

    Zosia rolls her eyes, but her lips twitch as something from Carol’s books flickers through her mind.

    “Maybe.”


    -

    Carol is still dragging a brush through her hair when the doorbell rings.

    “Coming!” she calls, voice muffled by the toothbrush in her mouth. She pulls it out, spits, rinses, and runs a hand through her hair one last time as she scrutinises her reflection in the bathroom mirror. Good enough.

    When she gets downstairs and opens the door, Zosia is leaning casually against the frame, sunlight catching in her hair, the shorter cut framing her face in a way that makes something in Carol’s chest stutter.

    “Hi,” Zosia says, smiling.

    Carol forgets how to speak for a moment, until she finally squeezes out, “…Hi.”

    Zosia’s gaze flicks over her, amused. “You look like you just woke up.”

    “I kind of did,” Carol admits, stepping out and locking the door behind her. “I was up half the night. Writing.”

    Zosia’s brows lift as they head toward the car. “Writing what?”

    “Uh… Wycaro, actually.”

    Zosia pauses mid-step. “I thought you were done with that?”

    “I was,” Carol says. “I am. Sort of. I just—had an idea last night, and it was like a bug in my ear. Just wouldn’t leave me alone, you know?”

    Zosia studies her for a moment, something bright and curious in her eyes. “Will you let me read it when you’re done?”

    Carol hesitates.

    Helen’s face flashes, unbidden. Years of drafts passed back and forth. Margins filled with notes. She’s the only person Carol ever let peek behind the curtain, so to speak.

    “…Maybe,” Carol says finally. “Once you’ve finished books three and four.”

    Zosia pouts playfully as she opens the driver’s door. “I don’t mind spoilers.”

    “I do,” Carol says firmly, sliding into the passenger seat. “If you insist on reading my work, then you’re doing it properly and suffering through every last word.”

    “We have very different opinions of suffering.” Zosia laughs softly, starting the engine.

    Carol exhales slowly, watching the other woman out of the corner of her eye as Zosia pulls out of her drive. “So,” she says, clearing her throat, “this home birth thing. Are you sure about it?”

    Zosia glances at her with a mixture of amusement and wariness. “You sound like Koumba.”

    “He’s worried too?”

    “He has his reasons.”

    Carol shifts slightly in her seat. “What reasons?”

    Zosia’s fingers tighten almost imperceptibly on the steering wheel, her gaze fixed on the road ahead. “His mother died giving birth to him,” she says quietly. “But that was in Mauritania, and it was a long time ago. Home births are a lot safer now.”

    Carol stills. “I didn’t know that. About Koumba’s mom.” Admittedly, Carol doesn’t know a lot about Koumba. She doesn’t know a whole lot about Zosia, for that matter.

    Carol hesitates. “I thought he was the older brother. If his mom died, then what about Malick?”

    Zosia glances at her, then back to the road. “Different mothers. Same father.”

    “Half-brothers?” Carol asks.

    Zosia’s lips twitch faintly. “Don’t let Koumba hear you say that. He punched someone in high school for calling Malick his half-brother.”

    Carol lets out a soft huff of laughter. “Right. Noted.”

    She leans back in her seat, considering.

    “I might have had him wrong,” she admits after a moment. “We talked the other day. He doesn’t seem… terrible.”

    Zosia smiles, something warm and relieved in it. “He’s not.”

    “…and he’s important to you,” Carol says, in a quiet voice.

    “He is,” Zosia confirms.

    “I’m…uh, going to try harder. With Koumba.”

    Zosia glances at her briefly, something soft flickering in her eyes as she reaches across the centre console and squeezes Carol’s thigh. “Thank you. That means a lot.”

    The car settles into a comfortable quiet as they drive back towards the city. Carol watches the road ahead in between stealing glances Zosia’s way. Eventually, she breaks the silence again. “Can I ask you something?”

    “Always.”

    Carol hesitates, then pushes forward. “Do you ever think about… dating again?”

    Zosia’s grip on the wheel loosens slightly as they pull up at a light. “For a long time…” she says slowly, shifting to look at Carol. “No. Malick was… my person. My everything. I couldn’t imagine myself with anyone else.”

    Carol nods, throat a little tight. She gets it. She does. She never thought she’d be able to love again. Not after losing Helen.

    “But—things change,” Zosia says, and her eyes flick to Carol. They linger, just for a second, until the light turns and she looks back at the road.

    “I think, given the chance, I’d be open to dating again. After all, what are the chances,” Zosia continues softly, “of finding someone who just fits you, even once? Never mind… twice.”

    Carol nods, thinking about the chain of events that had to happen to bring Zosia into her life. The odds of them ever meeting feel impossibly small. And yet—

    “What about you?” Zosia asks.

    Carol huffs a quiet laugh, trying for casual as she waves a hand. “I’ve been on a few first dates. Not any second ones, though. And, for the record, dating sucks when you haven’t had to bother for nearly thirty years.”

    Zosia purses her lips. “You and Helen were together that long?”

    Carol nods stiffly. She’s still not great at talking about Helen, but she’s trying. “It took me a year before I even considered dating. Manousos tried to set me up with his cousin. It was a total train wreck.”

    “Blind date?”

    “Unfortunately.” Carol grimaces.
