    Zosia winces in sympathy. “I’ve been there. My college roommate tried to set me up once. It did not go well.”

    Carol’s phone buzzes in her hand. She tears her eyes away from Zosia long enough to glance down at it to find a message from Layla about picking up the cake.

    Saturday?

    Carol’s stomach drops.

    She and Zosia have their meet and greet for the prenatal classes on Saturday afternoon. There’s no way she’ll have time to drive to Vegas and back in time.

    Beside her, Zosia is oblivious to her internal crisis and still talking about her own disastrous blind date. “…and she was vegan—”

    Carol freezes.

    Her head snaps up.

    “Wait, what? What did you say?”

    Zosia blinks, turns again as they stop at another red light. “Vegan?”

    Carol’s pulse spikes. “You said—” She pauses, unsure she heard correctly, “she.”

    “Yes. I did... Is it really so surprising,” Zosia says evenly, eyes fixed on the road, “that I’ve dated women?”

    Carol stares at her, dumbfounded, as something sharp and electric unfurls low in her chest.

    Zosia shrugs lightly, taking Carol’s silence for something else. “It was a long time ago.”

    Something about the way she says it—casual, but not quite dismissive—makes Carol’s chest tighten and knocks her out of her stupor. She clears her throat, but the words still catch.

    “Uh, y-you just—never mentioned it,” Carol says weakly.

    “We’ve only known each other a few months,” Zosia points out gently. “There’s a lot we don’t know about one another.”

    Carol huffs out a breath, the car suddenly feeling smaller than it should. The air tighter.

    She does what she does best and deflects. “Yeah… probably should’ve thought about that before knocking you up.”

    Zosia laughs, the sound warm and soft—and full of relief. She finally looks Carol’s way again, a tentative smile on her lips. “Probably.”

    Silence settles over them again, but it’s different now.

    Thicker.

    Charged.

    Carol turns slightly in her seat, studying Zosia’s profile—the curve of her jaw, the way her fingers flex against the steering wheel, the faint crease between her brows.

    “You said… dated,” Carol says carefully, feeling like she’s tiptoeing through a minefield with a large magnet. “Would you… would you date a woman now?”

    Zosia doesn’t answer immediately. Her thumb taps once against the wheel.

    “Now,” she says slowly, “I’m having a baby. That has to be my priority.”

    That’s not an answer.

    Carol swallows. “Right.”

    There’s a long pause, then Zosia finally adds, softer, “but, if the right… person asked, I wouldn’t be averse to dating again. Eventually.”

    Carol nods, her pulse loud in her ears and a lump in her throat she just can’t shift. The rest of the drive passes in a strange, quiet tension. Carol stares out the window, mind racing in a dozen different directions at once.

    Zosia has dated women.
    Zosia could see herself moving on.
    Zosia—

    Carol exhales slowly, pressing her fingers against her thigh. She risks a glance sideways. Zosia is focused on the road, expression calm, composed. Unreadable.

    Everything Carol isn’t right now.

    She looks away quickly, afraid Zosia might see something on her face.

    The woman she’s spent weeks convincing herself she can’t have doesn’t feel quite so out of reach.

    Chapter End Notes

    Slightly shorter chapter this time, but the next one is probably going to be pretty long. As ever, thanks for reading. The response to this fic has been amazing.

    - Tan.

    Chapter 14

    Chapter Notes

    “…Carol?”

    Carol jumps as Zosia’s hand finds hers across the table. She pulls back as though burned, and a flicker of hurt crosses Zosia’s face. It’s gone almost as quickly as it appears. Carol gets the impression she’s been trying to get her attention for a while, while she’s been staring absently out the front window of the diner.

    “Sorry,” Carol mutters, ducking her head. She’s been on a different planet since the car ride to meet the midwife. Most of that conversation had gone straight over her head. She can’t even remember the woman’s name. “Uh—did you say something?”

    “I asked if you were okay.” Concern softens Zosia’s features, smoothing her brow, gentling her eyes. She starts to reach across the table again, then stops herself. Folds her arms instead, leaning on them like she needs the restraint.

    “Me?” Carol lets out a laugh that comes a little too sharp. “I’m—I’m fine. Peachy. Never better—”

    “Carol.”

    Zosia’s voice doesn’t rise, but it lands with weight. Carol stills, the single word knocking the air clean out of her lungs.

    “You haven’t seemed yourself all afternoon,” Zosia says quietly. “And I think… I think I know why.”

    The knot in Carol’s chest cinches tighter. “Y-you do?”

    “Yes.” Zosia’s gaze stays soft and steady. Full of something that takes Carol a second to place.

    Pity.

    Carol’s stomach twists.

    Zosia knows.

    She knows about Carol’s pathetic, hopeless crush and she’s about to let her down gently. Carol’s head swims.

    Please, not here.

    Not in her favourite diner.

    Not anywhere.

    “Uh, Zosia, it’s—you—we don’t have to—”

    “I know the home birth stuff is worrying you.”

    “Huh?”

    Carol stares at her, blindsided, like the floor’s just dropped out from under her. “I—”

    “Carol, you can talk about it. We can talk about it.” Zosia unfolds her arms, one hand drifting to her belly.

    “Mmm.” Carol presses her lips together and nods, mind completely at sea. Sure, she’s not thrilled about the idea of Zosia giving birth anywhere without a fully stocked hospital and a small army of medical professionals on hand—but that’s not what’s been tying her in knots all afternoon.

    Zosia reaches across the table again, unable to help herself this time, taking both of Carol’s hands in hers.

    Carol doesn’t pull away.

    She sinks into it.

    “This baby is as much yours as mine,” Zosia says gently, “and you should have a say too. If you think a home birth is too risky—”

    “No.” Carol shakes her head quickly. She caught enough of Zosia’s conversation with the midwife to understand how much this matters to her. “No, if the midwife—”

    “Jen.”

    Right. Jen.

    “—if Jen thinks it’s safe, then I’m on board.” Carol huffs out a breath. “Just… be prepared for me to panic and drag you to a hospital the second something feels off.”

    Zosia smiles at that, bright and warm, but it falters as she studies Carol again, head tilting slightly, like she’s working through a problem she can’t quite solve.

    “Then what is it?”

    “Uh…” Carol glances down at their joined hands, gives Zosia’s fingers a brief squeeze before slipping free. She reaches for what’s left of her burger, taking a deliberately large bite to buy herself time.

    Zosia frowns. “Is it the party on Sunday? Because if it’s too much—”

    “Mm-mm.” Carol shakes her head.

    Zosia’s frown deepens. “Is it—”

    “I’m writing,” Carol blurts out loudly, before Zosia can keep guessing and land on the truth.

    A couple of nearby diners glance over. Carol flushes and lowers her voice.

    “I’m writing,” she repeats, quieter. “I, uh… Helen always said it made me… distant. I zone out. Start thinking about the next chapter, the next scene.”

    She shrugs. It’s not a lie—even if it’s not the truth Zosia’s asking for.

    “Oh.” Relief washes over Zosia as she leans back, tension easing out of her shoulders. “Well, if you need anything. Space, or—”

    “I’m good,” Carol cuts in quickly, a little too fast. The idea of seeing less of Zosia lands somewhere low and unpleasant in her chest.

    She had considered it, once. Back when the first hints of this—whatever it is she feels for Zosia—started creeping in. Limiting contact. Keeping things strictly practical. Appointments. Updates. Nothing more.

    The thought alone had made her miserable.

    “I’m good,” she repeats, softer this time, offering a tentative smile. “Promise. I’ll… figure it out.”

    “Good.” Zosia’s smile returns, bright and open. She reaches across the table again, resting her hand lightly against Carol’s arm. “Because I don’t want you disappearing on me… on us.”

    “Never.”

    The word is out before Carol can stop it.

    She doesn’t try to take it back.

    She means it.

    She wants to be in Zosia’s life. In the baby’s life.

    Even if it means standing on the sidelines, like Koumba. Watching Zosia build something with someone else. Smiling like it doesn’t cost anything. Everything.

    Carol huffs quietly to herself, lifting her coffee. Better man than me, she thinks. Her gaze lingers over the rim of the mug, fixed on Zosia.

    Zosia’s smile widens, soft and unguarded, and Carol’s heart gives a traitorous stutter. She’s in too deep now to even think about walking away.


    -

     

    The late afternoon sun beats down on the front step, heat radiating up through the soles of Carol’s shoes as she paces once, twice, then a third time for good measure—too fast, too tight, like she’s trying to wear a groove into the concrete.

    She only stills when she hears movement from behind the door she just pounded on.

    Too late to leave now.

    She pastes on a smile just as the door swings open.

    “Manny! Mi amigo! Mi hermano!”

    Manousos doesn’t so much as blink. He leans one shoulder against the frame, arms crossing over his chest, dark eyes dragging slowly over her—taking in the too-bright smile, the restless energy humming under her skin, the way she can’t quite keep still even now, shifting her weight from foot to foot.

    Then he shakes his head.

    “No.”

    Carol’s smile falters. “I didn’t ask you anything.”

    “You don’t need to.” His tone is flat, immovable. “The answer is no.”

    “Manny,” Carol whines, already stepping into his space, hands lifting like she can physically push him toward agreement. “Seriously, when do I ever ask you for anything?”

    Manousos just looks at her.

    “Last week you asked to borrow my lawnmower. The month before, you needed me to fix your car. Last fall—”

    “Okay! Okay, jeez.” Carol throws her hands up, then immediately drags them through her hair, pacing past him as she steps inside without waiting for an invitation. “I get it.”

    He arches a brow, unimpressed, but doesn’t ask her to leave.

    “This is serious, though,” she presses, turning on her heel so fast she nearly collides with him again in the narrow hallway. “I need your help. You’re my only hope.”

    Manousos exhales slowly through his nose, closing the door behind him with a quiet click.

    “What do you need?”

    Carol doesn’t answer straight away. She paces the length of the entryway, then back again, then stops—only to start picking at the edge of her sleeve.

    “The cake—” she starts, then scrubs a hand down her face. “Okay. So. The baker, Layla, from Vegas, she messaged. She can’t have it ready by Friday anymore. It’s Saturday. Late morning at the earliest.”

    Manousos watches her, arms folded, tracking every step she takes like he’s watching a particularly erratic tennis match.

    “And I can’t go get it,” Carol continues, words tripping over each other as she resumes pacing, “because Zosia and I have the prenatal class meet-and-greet Saturday afternoon, and there’s no way I can make the drive to Vegas and back in time without her getting suspicious, and I need to keep her busy all day so she doesn’t start asking questions—”

    “So you need me to go to Vegas and pick it up,” Manousos cuts in.

    Carol stops mid-step. “No. No—Koumba’s driving out to get it.”

    Manousos frowns. “Then what do you need from me?”

    Carol hesitates. Her fingers drum against her thigh. “I need you to go with him.”

    Silence.

    Manousos’ gaze sharpens.

    “…Why?”

    Carol shifts, arms folding, then unfolding again almost immediately. “Because I—” She gestures vaguely, like she can pluck a reasonable explanation out of the air. “I just want to make sure everything goes smoothly.”

    “That’s not an answer.”

    She exhales hard, scrubbing her hands over her face again. “Fine. Because I’m worried he’s going to screw me over.”

    Manousos’ expression flattens. “Screw you over.”

    “Or forget the cake, or pick it up wrong, or—drop it,” Carol says, pacing again, faster now. “Deliberately or otherwise.”

    “Deliberately,” Manousos repeats, slower this time.

    Carol stops dead. “Okay, when you say it like that—”

    “You think Koumba is going to drive all the way to Vegas,” Manousos says, pushing off the wall now, stepping properly into the room, “pick up a cake for a party he knows matters to Zosia, and then what? Throw it out of the window on the eight-hour drive back?”

    Carol presses her lips together, arms wrapping tight around herself.

    “I don’t know.”

    “Yes, you do.”

    “I just—” She turns away from him, dragging both hands through her hair, gripping for a second before letting go. “I know we’re getting along better, okay? We are. He’s been… fine. Helpful, even.”

    She huffs out a laugh that doesn’t quite land. “But that doesn’t just magically make me trust him.”

    Manousos just watches her, quieter now. Carol keeps going, because stopping would mean sitting with what she just said out loud.

    “Do you really think he would do something that would hurt Zosia?”

    “I think I don’t know him,” Carol snaps, turning back to Manousos. “And I don’t like not knowing where I stand with people. Especially when it comes to—” She cuts herself off, jaw tightening.

    Carol has never felt more out of control in her whole life than she does right now.

    “If I wasn’t at least a little neurotic,” she adds, forcing a weak smile, “you wouldn’t love me.”

    “I do not love you, Carol Sturka,” Manousos says automatically.

    Carol snorts, the sound thin. “Liar.”

    Silence settles, thicker this time.

    Carol rocks back on her heels, then forward again, unable to settle. Her fingers tap against her arm, then her thigh, then curl into her palms.

    She’s been holding this in all day—since Layla’s message.

    Since the car.

    Since everything shifted.

    “Manny, please,” she says, quieter now. “I need to keep Zosia occupied all day Saturday. If Koumba disappears and she starts asking questions, I have to have answers. And I can’t do that if I’m also wondering whether the cake is even going to make it back here in one piece.”

    Manousos exhales, long and slow, gaze dropping briefly before lifting back to her.

    “You are insane,” he says.

    “I know.”

    He uncrosses his arms. “Fine.”

    Carol stills. “Fine?”

    “I’ll go with him.” He fixes her with a look. “Not because I think he’s going to sabotage the cake,” he adds dryly, “but because I like Zosia—and she does not need you losing it right now.”

    Relief hits hard enough that Carol actually sways.

    “Oh my god—thank you!” She closes the distance in two quick steps and throws her arms around him, gripping tighter than she means to. “Thank you, thank you!”

    Manousos stiffens, then sighs, patting her back once.

    “You owe me,” he mutters into her hair.

    Carol pulls back, hands still gripping his arms for a second before she lets go. She grins up at him, breathless, a little wild around the eyes.

    “Damn right I do, amigo.”


    -


    Carol pulls up outside Zosia’s duplex on Saturday morning, a few minutes earlier than they’d agreed on. The engine idles with a low hum as she grips the steering wheel a little tighter than necessary.

    It’s been days since she found out Zosia isn’t actually straight, and Carol’s brain still feels like pudding; her nervous system like it’s on fire.

    The late morning sun glints off the windshield, warm but not oppressive yet—the kind of heat that promises more later. Carol barely registers it. Her focus is somewhere else entirely. Looping. Spiralling.

    Prenatal class.
    Meet and greet.
    Normal people stuff.

    Hi, I’m Carol. I’m the biological mother but not the pregnant one. Also not the partner. Just—emotionally entangled and legally adjacent.

    She winces.

    “Yeah, that’ll go down great,” she mutters under her breath.

    Her gaze drifts toward Zosia’s house, then past it, unfocused. She runs through versions of the explanation again—shorter, simpler, less insane. None of them land. None of them make their situation sound like anything other than a case study in madness.

    She’s so caught up in her own thoughts she doesn’t notice the front door open. Doesn’t notice Zosia stepping out, walking down the drive—

    Until the passenger door opens.

    Carol blinks, dragged sharply back to the present.

    Zosia slides into the seat beside her, tugging the door closed with a soft thud, and for a moment Carol forgets how to breathe.

    She’s wearing an oversized men’s shirt—soft, worn cotton in a pale blue—hanging loose over black leggings that cling to her legs and curve around the swell of her bump. The sleeves are rolled messily to her elbows, collar open just enough to show the line of her throat.

    Effortless.

    Comfortable.

    Stunning.

    Carol can’t help but stare. Zosia catches it almost immediately, glancing down at herself with a small, self-conscious smile. “I know, it’s not exactly high fashion,” she says, smoothing a hand over the shirt. “I’m running out of things that actually fit.”

    Carol doesn’t respond right away, mouth too dry. Mind too blank.

    Zosia tilts her head. “It’s one of Malick’s,” she adds, like that explains it. “I haven’t gotten around to—” She gestures vaguely. “You know. Sorting through everything.”

    Carol blinks, finally catching up. “What? No, I mean—” She clears her throat, dragging her gaze back up with effort. “You look— It looks good. Great, even.”

    Zosia’s smile shifts, amusement creeping in. “Yeah?”

    “Yeah,” Carol says quickly, then forces herself onto safer ground. “But if you need clothes, we could—uh—go shopping? After this, I mean.”

    Zosia raises a brow. “You’d go shopping with me?”

    “Of course I would,” Carol says, like it’s obvious.

    Zosia studies her for a second, lips twitching. “You’re not sick of me already?”

    Carol snorts softly, shaking her head as she reaches for the gear shift. “No, ma’am.”

    The corner of Zosia’s mouth lifts, something warm and quietly pleased flickering across her face. “Good,” she says, settling back into her seat.

    Carol starts the car properly this time, pulling away from the curb. The engine hums steadier now, the road stretching out ahead of them.

    For a while, it’s quiet.

    Zosia rests one hand on her bump, her thumb tracing absent patterns through the fabric of the shirt. Carol notices. Of course she does.

    She can’t tell if Zosia is thinking about the baby—

    —or about her husband.

    “So,” Zosia says after a moment, glancing over. “Do you think it’s weird?”

    Carol flicks her a look. “What?”

    “That I haven’t gotten rid of Malick’s things yet.”

    Carol exhales slowly, eyes returning to the road. “No,” she says. “No, I don’t think that’s weird.”

    Zosia watches her, waiting.

    Carol shifts in her seat. “It took me a long time to let go of Helen’s things.”

    The name settles between them, familiar now, but no less heavy for it.

    “I mean, I still haven’t. Not completely,” Carol admits, a small, self-conscious huff escaping her lips. “There’s this green cardigan of hers I kept. I still wear it. Probably more than I should.”

    Zosia’s expression softens.

    She’s seen it.

    Hadn’t realised it was armour.

    “And there’s a book,” Carol adds, quieter now. “On her bedside table. Face down. Still open to the page she was on.”

    She swallows thickly.

    “It’s been two years,” she says, voice steadier than she feels. “I still can’t bring myself to move it. Let alone throw it out.”

    The car hums around them, the world outside carrying on like nothing’s shifted at all. Zosia doesn’t say anything right away.

    She just reaches across the centre console, her hand finding Carol’s where it rests on the gear stick.

    Warm.

    Steady.

    Carol glances down at their hands, then over at her. Zosia’s looking at her with that same quiet softness—no pity this time. Just understanding.

    And grief.

    The unwelcome thing they share.

    Carol’s chest tightens. She turns her hand slightly, fingers curling, threading through Zosia’s without thinking too hard about it—

    —until she does.

    Her grip falters for half a second, instinct tugging in the opposite direction. Boundaries. Lines she’s already blurred just by being here, by wanting more than she has any right to.

    She should pull away.

    But she doesn’t.

    Zosia’s fingers shift against hers, settling more firmly, like the contact is the most natural thing in the world.

    Carol lets out a quiet breath she hadn’t realised she was holding and holds on. She smiles, small and real, her thumb brushing once against the back of Zosia’s hand before she forces her attention back to the road.

    The Women’s and Children’s Health Centre comes into view ahead, clean lines and glass catching the sunlight.

    Normal.

    Ordinary.

    Nothing about this feels like either of those things.

    And yet—

    Carol tightens her grip on Zosia’s hand just slightly, grounding herself. “Ready?” she asks, though she’s not entirely sure who she’s asking for.

    Zosia squeezes back. “Ready.”


    Their hands part before they step through the clinic doors. Carol feels the absence immediately, like stepping out of direct sunlight and into shade. She flexes her fingers once at her side, like she might still find the warmth there if she tries hard enough.

    The reception area smells faintly of antiseptic and something floral underneath it, an attempt at comfort that doesn’t quite mask the clinical edge. A woman behind the desk looks up with a practiced smile.

    “Hi there,” she says brightly. “Prenatal meet and greet? Room three, just down the hall and to your left.”

    “Thank you,” Zosia says, already turning. Carol follows a step behind, her mind catching up slower than her feet.

    They reach the room, the door already open. Voices drift out; low, conversational, the soft hum of strangers getting acquainted. Carol steps in—and stops in her tracks.

    Three couples sit in a loose semicircle of chairs. All older. Late thirties, forties maybe. There’s a table at the side with pamphlets, a kettle, mismatched mugs. And standing at the centre of it all—

    Carol’s stomach drops.

    “Oh no,” she mutters under her breath, just loud enough for Zosia to hear.

    Zosia looks to her immediately. “What’s wrong?”

    Carol doesn’t answer. She can’t, because the woman leading the group has just turned around, confirming her worst fears.

    Plump, mid-forties, brown hair scraped into a slightly frizzy bun, cardigan stretched comfortably over her frame—and eyes that light up with unmistakable recognition the second they land on Carol.

    “Oh my—”

    Carol considers, briefly, turning around and walking back out of the room.

    Too late.

    “Carol Sturka?” the woman says, already moving toward her, hands clasping together in delight. “Oh my goodness, it is you!”

    Carol forces a smile that feels more like a grimace. “Hi, Maureen.”

    “So nice to see you again!” Maureen gushes, practically vibrating with excitement. “What are you doing here? This is for older mothers and—”

    Her eyes drop to Carol’s stomach. They widen. “Oh! Are you—?”

    Carol’s soul leaves her body. “No—”

    “How far along are you?” Maureen barrels on, words tripping over themselves. “I didn’t even know you were trying! Oh, this is so exciting—”

    “She’s not pregnant.” Zosia’s voice cuts in, calm but firm. There’s an edge to it that Carol isn’t used to.

    Maureen blinks, recalibrating. She finally takes in the very obviously pregnant woman beside Carol. “Oh! Of course, I’m sorry.”

    Zosia steps forward slightly, offering a polite but stiff smile. “I’m Zosia Diabate. We spoke on the phone?”

    Recognition clicks into place instantly. “Zosia! Yes, of course! Lovely to finally meet you.” Maureen pivots seamlessly, all warmth and professionalism now as she offers her hand. “I’m Maureen Reynolds, I run the class. Welcome.”

    Zosia shakes her hand robotically. “Thank you.”

    Maureen’s gaze flicks back to Carol, curiosity rekindling. “And you’re Zosia’s—?”

    “Her friend,” Carol cuts in quickly. Too quickly. “Just… here for support.”

    Just friends. The words hang in the air between them. Carol doesn’t register it, but beside her, Zosia shifts, her jaw tightening.

    Maureen nods, though her eyes linger a fraction longer than necessary on Carol. “That’s wonderful. Support systems are so important during pregnancy, particularly for older mothers.”

    Carol laughs, thin and awkward. “Yeah. Totally.”

    Beside her, Zosia’s strained smile wavers, just for a second, before smoothing back into place.

    Maureen moves back to the centre of the room, clapping her hands once to get everyone’s attention. “Okay! Let’s get started.”

    Carol and Zosia take two of the empty seats. Carol sits a fraction too stiffly, aware of everything all at once; Maureen, the other couples, Zosia beside her— and yet not quite with her.

    Introductions go around the room.

    Laxmi and her husband, Aurush—warm, easy smiles, already seasoned parents expecting their second.

    Xiu Mei and Neu Xiansheng—quiet, composed, hand in hand in a way that speaks of long familiarity.

    Narangerel and Otgonbayar—soft-spoken, observant, their words measured but kind.

    Carol barely absorbs any of it.

    Her attention keeps snagging—on Maureen, on Zosia, on the dull, persistent weight of her phone in her pocket.

    She’s had no word from Manousos yet. They should be in Vegas by now. They should have the cake. Unless something’s gone wrong. Unless Koumba—

    Carol drags in a breath, cutting the thought off before it spirals too far. Her knee starts bouncing; she stills it with a concentrated effort.

    When it’s their turn, Zosia speaks. “I’m Zosia,” she says, one hand resting lightly on her bump. “This is my first.” A beat. “And this is Carol… my friend.”

    The word is precise this time. Placed and razor-sharp. Carol feels the edge of it. She glances sideways, but Zosia’s already looking forward, expression smooth, unreadable.

    Something tightens low in Carol’s chest. She doesn’t know what she’s done—but she knows she’s done something to upset Zosia.

    The class moves on. Maureen talks through class structure, expectations, partner roles. Her voice is warm, practiced, engaging. Carol hears maybe half of it. The rest is drowned out by the restless hum under her skin.

    Her phone remains stubbornly silent in her pocket.

    Beside her, Zosia sits straight-backed, attentive. And distant. Not cold exactly, but Carol feels the distance between them acutely.

    Zosia is a very tactile person, and Carol’s gotten used to the small things she does. The casual touches. The quiet reassurances. A hand on her arm, fingers brushing hers. Now there’s nothing. Zosia hasn’t reached for her once since they walked through the door.

    Carol folds her arms, holding herself in place, resisting the urge to close the space between them when Maureen is watching so closely.

    After the session, the room loosens into smaller conversations. The men drift toward each other, easy small talk filling the space. The women gather closer, comparing due dates, symptoms, stories. Zosia slips into it effortlessly, one hand at her back, the other resting on her bump, smiling, laughing—glowing.

    Carol watches her a second too long, caught again by how effortless she looks in a room full of strangers. Then she forces herself to look away.

    Carol spots the group leader by herself and walks over to her with purpose. “Maureen, can I grab you for a second?”

    Maureen turns, delighted. “Carol. Of course!”

    Carol leads her a few steps aside, lowering her voice. “Hey, so, just to clarify, I’m not… like part of the class. Officially. I’m just here with Zosia. As a friend.”

    Maureen nods, though there’s a knowing glint in her eye that makes Carol uneasy. “Aren’t you her birthing partner?”

    “Uh… yes. We’re… We’re really good friends. I’d just appreciate it if this didn’t end up online.”

    Maureen blinks. “Online?”

    “The Wycaro fan group,” Carol says, already bracing. “You run the New Mexico one, right?”

    Maureen’s face lights up. “Oh! You remembered that?”

    Carol forces a tight smile. “Yeah. I remembered.”

    “Don’t worry,” Maureen says immediately, her hand finding Carol’s arm. It holds none of the comfort that Zosia’s touch does. “I would never share something like this without your permission.”

    Relief loosens Carol’s shoulders slightly. “Thank you.”

    “Besides,” Maureen adds gently, glancing toward Zosia, “I know how crazy some of the Wycarians can be, and Zosia deserves her privacy too.”

    Carol follows her gaze. Zosia is mid-laugh, radiant, fully at ease in a way Carol never could be in a room full of people.

    She indulges a few questions about her work from Maureen whilst Zosia chats with the other mothers. When the other woman asks if Carol is really done with writing Wycaro, her reply is coy.

    She has four chapters of a potential new book written up, but the only person she’s told is Zosia. Not even her agent, Val, knows she’s been working on Wycaro again.

    “Ready to go?” Zosia’s voice comes from just behind her.

    Carol turns. Zosia’s expression is polite. Neutral. Closed off. It catches her off guard.

    “I—yeah,” Carol says. “Ready.”

    They say their goodbyes to Maureen and walk out together into the late afternoon heat. It presses down heavier now, the air thick and slow.

    Carol falls into step beside Zosia, struggling to keep up with her longer strides. Their hands brush and Zosia pulls hers back immediately. Carol sees her flinch.

    “Hey,” Carol says carefully, testing the waters between them. “Did you still want to go shopping?”

    Zosia keeps her gaze straight ahead as she answers. “Not really. I think I just want to go home.”

    Carol frowns. Biting the bullet, she asks, “Did I do something?”

    “No.” Zosia’s answer comes too quick.

    Carol stops walking. “Zosia.”

    Zosia exhales, shoulder dipping, stopping a step ahead of her. For a moment, she doesn’t turn around. Then she does.

    “It just…” She hesitates, searching for the words. “It felt like you were trying to make a point in there.”

    Carol blinks. “What?”

    “When you said we were just friends.”

    Oh.

    “That’s—I mean, we are—”

    “I know we are,” Zosia cuts in, sharper now. “But the way you said it…” She shakes her head. “It sounded like you were making sure no one got the wrong idea. Like it would be a problem if they did.”

    Carol’s stomach drops.

    “That’s not—”

    “Like you’d be embarrassed,” Zosia finishes, quieter now. The hurt on her face is clear to see, and Carol kicks herself for missing it earlier.

    Carol steps closer without thinking. “No. No, that’s not it.”

    Zosia crosses her arms, but she doesn’t step back. “Was Maureen one of the women you dated? After Helen?”

    The question comes right out of left field. Carol barks out a laugh, then realises Zosia is being serious.

    “No,” she says firmly, dragging a hand over the back of her neck. “Maureen runs the New Mexico Wycaro fan group. My fans are… a lot. If she thought there was something going on, it would not stay in that room. It would be everywhere. People digging. Speculating.” Her jaw tightens. “And they wouldn’t leave you alone.”

    Zosia’s expression shifts, just slightly.

    “I didn’t want that for you,” Carol adds, softer now. “That’s all.”

    There’s a pause before Zosia exhales, some of the tension easing from her shoulders. “Okay.”

    Carol searches her face. “Okay?”

    “Okay,” Zosia repeats, a small smile tugging at her mouth now.

    Relief hits fast, almost dizzying. Carol lets out a breath, a weight lifting from her chest. “Good. So… shopping?”

    Zosia looks at her properly now. This time, when she reaches out, it’s deliberate. Her fingers brush Carol’s arm, light but certain.

    “Yeah,”

    As they get back into Carol’s truck, her phone goes off. She pulls it out to find a message from Manousos. It’s simple, but takes the weight of the world from her shoulders.

    Got the cake. Heading back.

    “What do you think?”

    Zosia twirls in front of Carol, the navy dress catching the light as it moves, clinging in all the wrong—right—places. It hugs her hips, skims over the pronounced curve of her stomach, pushes her breasts up in a way that feels deliberate.

    Carol thinks she might actually be in hell.

    She’s been perched on the little upholstered bench in the dressing room for the better part of an hour, watching Zosia cycle through outfit after outfit. Fabric, zippers, soft swishes of material. The occasional muffled curse in Polish when something doesn’t sit right.

    It’s not the waiting that’s getting to her.

    It’s Zosia.

    The woman glows in that unfair, luminous way that pregnancy seems to grant her. Softer, fuller—her body changed, yes, but not diminished. If anything, it’s made everything more pronounced.

    Carol drags her gaze up, forcibly, from the way the dress molds to her.

    “Looks nice,” she says, her voice a touch strained.

    Zosia turns back to the mirror, angling herself this way and that, hands sliding over her hips. “I already have something for the party,” she says, almost to herself. “But I don’t know. I’m not sure about it anymore. And this is divine.”

    She studies her reflection, lips pressing together slightly. There’s a flicker of doubt there.

    Carol leans back against the wall, arms folded, trying very hard to look at literally anything other than Zosia’s ass in that dress. “No, it’s great,” she says. “You look—”

    She stops herself, clears her throat. “It looks great.”

    Zosia hums, still staring at herself. “It’s a bit much, though.”

    Carol glances up again, frowning slightly. “No, really, it looks fantastic on you—”

    “Thank you, Carol. Truly. But I meant the price.” Zosia laughs and nods toward the price tag hanging at her side. “Nearly two hundred dollars for a dress I don’t technically need.”

    Carol exhales, some of the tension easing. “Oh… Right. I could get it for you?” she offers.

    Zosia turns her head, eyebrows lifting. “Carol.”

    “What?” she says, shrugging. “It’s fine. Smut sells.”

    “It’s not fine,” Zosia says, shaking her head. “You’re already paying for the whole party. I can’t let you buy me a dress too.”

    Carol opens her mouth to argue, then closes it again when Zosia gives her a look that says this conversation is already over.

    Zosia gives herself one last look in the mirror, smoothing her hands over her hips, then sighs. “I’ll just stick with the one I have at home. It’s nice, too.”

    Before Carol can respond, she reaches for the curtain and pulls it closed. The small space suddenly feels empty without her in it. Carol exhales slowly, dragging a hand over her face.

    There’s the soft rustle of fabric behind the curtain, the quiet slide of a zipper. “I’ve got a few more things to try on,” Zosia calls out. “More casual stuff.”

    “Yeah,” Carol says, pushing herself to her feet. “I can put that one back for you, if you want?”

    “That would be lovely.”

    A hand appears through the small gap in the curtain, holding out the navy dress. Carol steps forward to take it—and catches a glimpse she absolutely should not.

    Zosia, half-turned, in nothing but her underwear. Soft skin. The full, round curve of her stomach. The long line of her legs. Carol’s breath stutters.

    She snatches the dress quickly and looks anywhere else, heat rising fast up her neck. “Got it,” she mutters.

    “Thank you,” Zosia says, oblivious.

    Carol escapes the dressing room with the dress clutched in her hands, the cooler air of the shop floor doing absolutely nothing to settle her. She makes it halfway to the rack before she stops, looking down at the dress and then back toward the dressing rooms.

    Then, with a quiet exhale, she turns on her heel and heads for the register. The woman behind the counter smiles politely as Carol sets the dress down. “Find everything okay?”

    “Yeah,” Carol says, already reaching for her wallet. “Just this. It’s a surprise, though. Can you not say anything to my…friend?”

    “Of course.”

    It’s rung up quickly. Bagged even quicker. Carol takes it, thanks her, and heads straight for the exit before she can overthink it.

    The heat outside hits her immediately, dry and heavy. Her truck sits baking in the sun, metal hot enough to sting when she grabs the handle of the door. She places the bag on the floor in front of the rear passenger seat, just out of sight, then shuts the door again.

    Carol takes a second to collect herself. She doesn’t care if Zosia gets mad at her for buying it. The thought of that dress hanging up on a rack somewhere—rather than hugging Zosia’s figure—sits wrong in her mind.

    As she heads back inside, Zosia is just stepping out of the dressing room when Carol rounds the corner. She’s wearing a polka dot sundress that makes her look like a schoolteacher. A far cry from the sultry navy dress, but beautiful on her all the same.

    She looks up, puzzled. “Where did you go?”

    Carol doesn’t miss a beat. “Meter,” she says, jerking a thumb vaguely toward the door. “Had to top it up.”

    Zosia nods easily. “What do you think?” She does a twirl, arms down by her sides.

    All Carol can think of is the glimpse of the other woman in her underwear. “It’s nice,” she says, voice choked.

    “I think it’s a keeper,” Zosia announces.

    Carol nods. “Me too.”

    “But wait until you see this cute sweater! You’re going to love it.”

    Carol stays where she is for a second, watching as Zosia disappears back behind the curtain to change. The fabric swishes shut, cutting her off again.

    It’s ridiculous how immediately Carol feels the loss of her presence.

    She drags a hand over the back of her neck, shifting her weight from one foot to the other, trying to settle the restless energy buzzing under her skin. It doesn’t take.

    She busies herself instead, straightening one of the discarded hangers on the hook, then another, anything to keep her hands, and mind, occupied.

    Behind the curtain, there’s the soft rustle of clothes again. The quiet, familiar rhythm of Zosia moving—unhurried, comfortable in her own space in a way that Carol envies more than she wants to admit.

    “You okay out there?” Zosia calls, her voice slightly muffled.

    “Yeah,” Carol says quickly. “Fine.”

    “You’ve gone quiet on me.”

    Carol huffs a small breath through her nose. “I’ve been sitting in a dressing room for an hour. I think I’m allowed a moment of quiet contemplation.”

    Zosia laughs softly. “You volunteered.”

    “Did I?”

    “Yes,” Zosia says. “Very enthusiastically, if I recall.”

    Carol leans back against the wall again, arms folding loosely across her chest. “Doesn’t sound like me.”

    “Selective memory,” Zosia hums.

    There’s a pause, then the curtain shifts.

    Zosia steps out again, this time in a loose, soft-looking cream sweater and a pair of maternity jeans that sit comfortably under her bump. It’s a simpler look, casual, but it does nothing to dull the effect she has on Carol.

    Carol’s gaze flicks down—lingers a beat too long on Zosia’s ass—then snaps up again.

    Zosia watches her for a second, something curious flickering in her expression. “Well?”

    Carol clears her throat. “Yeah. Those—uh. Those look good.”

    Zosia smiles faintly, but there’s something knowing in it now. “High praise.”

    Carol shrugs, pushing off the wall. “I’m consistent.”

    “Mm.”

    Zosia turns back toward the mirror, adjusting the hem of the sweater, smoothing it down over her stomach. Her movements are slower now, more thoughtful, like she’s feeling rather than just looking.

    Carol’s eyes track the motion before she can stop herself, lingering on Zosia’s deft fingers before looking away. She focuses instead on the scuffed tile floor, the faint hum of the overhead lights, the distant murmur of other shoppers outside.

    Anything else, but Zosia.

    “I think I’ll take these,” Zosia says after a moment, more decisively now.

    “Good call,” Carol replies, a little too quickly.

    Zosia glances at her again, that same almost-smile tugging at her mouth, like she’s clocking something Carol hasn’t quite managed to hide.

    But she doesn’t comment on it. Instead, she disappears behind the curtain one last time to change back into her own clothes. Carol exhales, slow and controlled, like she’s been holding her breath without realising it.

    When Zosia comes out again, dressed as before, she gathers the items neatly over her arm.

    “Okay,” she says. “Now I’m actually done.”

    “Impressive,” Carol mutters. “I was starting to think we’d be here until the baby comes.”

    Zosia bumps her lightly with her shoulder as she passes. “You’re welcome to wait outside next time.”

    Carol huffs a quiet laugh, falling into step beside her. “And miss the show? Not a chance.” The words are out before she can stop them.

    Zosia pauses mid-step, turns her head slightly. “Show?” she repeats.

    Carol feels the heat rise again, immediate and unhelpful. “Y-you know what I mean,” she says quickly. “The fashion show. Outfit after outfit. Riveting stuff.”

    Zosia watches her for a long moment. Then her mouth curves, slow and deliberate. “Right,” she says, and keeps walking—a deliberate sway to her hips as she heads for the register.

    “Would you mind dropping me at the restaurant instead of home?” Zosia asks as they get in to Carol’s truck, the rest of the shopping bags safely stored in the trunk.

    Carol’s hands still halfway to putting her seat belt on. “Uh—why?” she asks, aiming for casual and missing by a mile.

    Zosia doesn’t seem to notice. She’s looking out the window, one hand resting absently on her bump, thumb tracing slow, distracted circles. “I just want to check in on things,” she says. “Koumba took the day off too, and I just want to make sure everything is running smoothly.”

    Carol’s stomach drops as she finishes putting on her seat belt and stars the truck. “I’m sure it’s fine,” she says, a little too quickly. “I mean—that’s what you pay your assistant manager for, right? To step up.”

    Zosia huffs out a small breath, not quite convinced. “Yes, but there’s usually at least one of us at the restaurant. I don’t know why he had to insist on taking today off, of all days.”

    Carol nods like she understands, even as her brain starts sprinting ahead, scrambling for solutions. The decorations are in Zosia’s office. The banner. The stupid balloon arch she’d spent two hours wrestling with this morning with Kusimayu’s help. If Zosia walks in—

    “Honestly,” Carol cuts in, glancing over at her, “you should just relax. Enjoy your day off. You work too hard.”

    Zosia turns her head slightly, studying her now. “I have paperwork,” she says with a sigh. “A lot of it.”

    Carol makes a face. “Exactly. That sounds like a terrible way to spend a Saturday. If I were you, I’d much rather spend it with me.”

    Zosia smiles faintly at that, but there’s still a thread of hesitation underneath it. “I’d really like to make sure my restaurant hasn’t burned down in my absence.”

    “It hasn’t,” Carol says, too fast again. Then, softer, “It won’t.”

    Zosia’s eyes linger on her. “You seem very confident about that.”

    Carol shrugs, “I… have faith.”

    “In my twenty three year old assistant manager?”nZosia pulls a face.

    “In… basic fire safety regulations,” Carol says. That gets her a laugh, at least.

    Zosia doesn’t back down, though. She shifts in her seat, adjusting slightly. “Anyway,” she says, “I won’t be long. I just want to stop by. See for myself.”

    Carol exhales slowly through her nose, trying to think of something to distract her with. “Or,” she says, a little too brightly, “you could not do that.”

    Zosia turns back to her, eyebrow lifting with amusement as she realises Carol isn’t about to let this go. “Oh? And what should I do instead?”

    Carol nods, fully committed now. “Take the night off… spend it with me.”

    She doesn’t think about the connotations of those words until they’re out there, hanging in the small space between them.

    Carol thinks she might just curl up and die.

    Zosia’s mouth curves, just slightly. “Aren’t you sick of me yet?”

    “What? No. Of course not… Forget about the restaurant, come over for dinner at my place.”

    Zosia tilts her head, considering the request. Amusement flickers in her expression. “Dinner?” she repeats. “That would depend...”

    Carol narrows her eyes. “On what?”

    Zosia’s smile widens, just a fraction. “Are you cooking?”

    Carol lets out a short, incredulous laugh. “Wow. Okay. Rude.”

    Zosia laughs softly, unrepentant.

    “I can cook,” Carol insists, glancing back at the road.

    “Helen used to do most of it, though,” she admits, after a beat. “But that doesn’t mean I’m incapable.”

    Zosia hums, like she’s weighing it up. Then, “We could make something together, if you’d like?”

    “Uh, I’m not sure if that’s a good idea.” Carol admits sheepishly. “I’d probably get in your way.”

    Zosia smiles, slow and easy. Open. “I’m a professional chef, Carol. I’m used to sharing a kitchen.”

    “Yeah, well,” Carol says with a shrug, “Helen used to say I got under her feet.”

    “I’ll manage,” Zosia replies lightly. Then, a little more pointed, she adds, “Who knows. Maybe I’ll teach you a thing or two.”

    Something in the teasing lilt of her voice does something to Carol. She feels it, low and immediate, heat creeping up the back of her neck before she can stop it.

    “Is that so?” she says, trying to sound calm. Casual. It’s difficult when her voice breaks mid-sentence.

    Carol forces her attention back on the road, tapping her fingers impatiently against the steering wheel as she waits for another car to drive past before pulling out of the parking space. “We’ll need to stop somewhere on the way,” she adds. “I don’t have much in the fridge.”

    Zosia nods easily. “That’s fine.”

    “Sprouts?” Carol suggests.

    “Perfect.”

    Beside her, Zosia settles into her seat, one hand resting again on her stomach, the other tapping idly against her thigh.

    Carol very deliberately keeps her eyes on the road.

    The box with the cake inside is heavier than it looks.

    Manousos adjusts his grip, careful, precise, keeping it level as he steps toward Koumba’s car. The cardboard is reinforced, taped tight at the seams, a faint sweetness leaking through—sugar and vanilla, rich and dense in the warm Nevada air.

    “Careful,” Koumba says, hovering at his shoulder like that’s in anyway helpful. “That’s precious cargo.”

    “I know,” Manousos replies stiffly, already lowering it into the trunk.

    He settles it in place with deliberate care, wedging it between two stabilizing boxes the bakery insisted on adding. Nothing shifts. Nothing tilts. He presses a hand flat against the top for a second, just to be sure.

    Behind him, Koumba clicks his tongue. “All of this fuss for a cake.”

    There’s a rustle of movement as Koumba reaches for the lid.

    “No.” Manousos snaps, the single word coming out flat and final.

    Koumba freezes, fingers just brushing the edge. Then he looks up, a slow, wolfish grin spreading across his face. “I was just going to take a little peek.”

    “No.” Manousos repeats.

    “Just prod a little hole to see if I’m having a niece or nephew,” Koumba says, undeterred. “A tiny one. No one would even notice.”

    Manousos straightens, meets his gaze. “It is a surprise.”

    Koumba waves a hand. “We can keep a secret. You and I, yes?”

    “No.”

    Koumba tilts his head, eyes glinting with amusement as Manousos stares him down. He breaks out in a laugh, low and delighted. “You are quite strict. I can see why Carol assigned you as my chaperone.”

    “You don’t get to peak.” Manousos says, voice calm and low. “Zosia chose not to know. If Carol can respect that, so can you.”

    Manousos had watched Carol wrestle with her conscience for weeks, pulling out the folded piece of paper over and over and staring at it like it held the meaning of life. She hadn’t peaked—as far as Manousos knew—not once.

    Koumba studies him for a second, then leans back, hands lifting in surrender. “Fine,” he says, still all smiles. “But, you should know you’re asking a lot of me. I’m not known for my self-control.”

    “I can see that.”

    Koumba laughs again, louder this time, and steps back as Manousos closes the trunk fully, the latch clicking shut with a final, satisfying clunk.

    “Come on,” Koumba says, already heading for the driver’s side. He pulls down a pair of sunglasses from where they rest on the top of his head. “We should get moving. It’s a long drive back.”

    “Eight hours,” Manousos says, circling to the passenger side.

    “Eight hours,” Koumba echoes, like it’s a minor inconvenience and not most of their day. It will be close to midnight before they get back to Albuquerque.

    The car is warm from sitting in the sun, the air thick until Koumba starts the engine and the vents kick in, pushing out a steady stream of cool air that swirls around both men as they settle in their seats.

    Koumba pulls out onto the road with easy confidence, one hand on the wheel, the other drumming lightly against the outside of the car door. He starts talking before they’ve even cleared the parking lot.

    About the bakery.

    About the city.

    About a woman he met in Vegas once who could do the most extraordinary things with vegetables. He talks too fast for Manousos to keep up, but isn’t entirely sure he’s talking about cooking.

    It doesn’t stop. The conversation shifts without rhyme or reason, looping from story to story, from opinion to memory, none of it requiring much response.

    Manousos doesn’t offer much anyway. A word here. A nod there. It seems to be enough for Koumba though, who barely stops talking long enough to draw breath.

    While Koumba is halfway through an animated description of a nightclub in Nouakchott, Manousos pulls out his phone. Types quickly.

    Got the cake. Heading back.

    He sends it before Koumba glances over.

    “Carol?” Koumba asks.

    “Yes.”

    Koumba hums, thoughtful. “She insisted you come with me,” he says. “Why is that? Does she not trust me?”

    Manousos doesn’t answer right away. He pockets his phone and looks out at the road ahead, the long stretch of highway already opening up in front of them. “Carol does not trust many people.”

    Koumba lets out a soft sound, somewhere between agreement and amusement. “I can imagine.”

    There’s a brief lull, the hum of the engine filling the space, the steady rhythm of tires against asphalt.

    “I didn’t trust her either,” Koumba says after a moment.

    Manousos glances at him.

    “Not at first,” Koumba continues, one hand lifting off the wheel to gesture loosely. “Zosia tells me about this woman—this… writer. She has her eggs wrongly implanted, and suddenly she’s around all the time.” He shakes his head, smiling faintly. “I did not like it.”

    Manousos says nothing.

    Koumba shrugs. “But I have been observing.”

    The road stretches out ahead of them, long and flat, the horizon shimmering slightly in the afternoon heat.

    “The way she looks at Zosia,” Koumba says, quieter now. “The way she is with her.”

    He taps the steering wheel once, thoughtfully. “She cares for her.” he says simply. It’s not an accusation, merely a statement of fact. Like he’s making a passing comment on the weather outside.

    Manousos doesn’t offer an opinion.

    Beside him, Koumba chuckles at the other man’s silence—all but confirming his suspicions about Carol’s feelings for his sister-in-law.

    The car hums steadily as they drive on, the cake sealed safely in the trunk behind them, untouched.

     

    -

     

    “Remind me again what we’re cooking?” Carol asks, already digging into one of the Sprouts bags. She pulls out a family-sized bag of Chex Mix, tearing it open before the question’s even finished.

    “Spaghetti,” Zosia says, and promptly takes the bag from her.

    Carol frowns. “Hey—”

    “They’re for later.” Zosia sets it out of reach, then leans back against the counter, her hip knocking lightly into Carol’s. “I’ve been craving salty things lately. T’ika says it means I’m having a boy.”

    There’s a small, nervous edge to her smile. “I guess we’ll find out tomorrow.”

    “Yeah. I guess we will.”

    Carol watches her for a moment as she turns back to the groceries, unpacking with quiet efficiency. A large bag of M&Ms appears next, set carefully aside for later.

    Carol huffs a soft laugh. “Chocolate too? Maybe there’s a girl in there. Are we sure you’re not having twins?”

    Zosia rolls her eyes. “Not unless one of them is very good at hide-and-seek.”

    She pulls the hair tie from her wrist, gathering her hair up into a loose, messy bun as she prepares to start cooking.

    Carol’s attention drops to the packet of spaghetti in her hands, studying it with exaggerated focus while, in her periphery, Zosia’s long, slender neck is suddenly—distractingly—bare.

    “You’ve never said what you’re actually hoping for,” Carol says, setting the pasta down before she gives herself away. She turns fully this time. “And don’t dodge it. Boy or girl?”

    Zosia leans back against the counter again, one hand settling over her stomach.

    “A girl,” she says eventually. “I’ll love them either way. But… I think I’d like a girl.”

    Carol smiles, easy and instinctive. “Yeah. I can see you as a girl mom.”

    Zosia glances at her. “You can?”

    “Mm.”

    Zosia studies her for a second, something softer settling in her expression. “What about you?” she asks. “What are you hoping for?”

    Carol hesitates. Just a fraction.

    “Honestly?” She presses her lips together, thinking. “I haven’t really thought that far ahead. I just know I don’t want to screw it up.”

    Zosia tilts her head. “That sounds like a non-answer.”

    Carol scoffs, pushing off the counter. “You wouldn’t say that if you knew my mother.”

    She crosses to the fridge, pulls it open, and grabs a beer, the cold bite of the glass grounding in her hand.

    Zosia doesn’t follow right away. “You said she was… difficult,” she says carefully. “You never said why.”

    It’s not directly a question, which gives Carol some room to wriggle out of answering if she wants to.

    Carol exhales, rubbing the back of her neck as she shuts the fridge with her hip. “Yeah. Well…”

    Her first instinct is to change the conversation—to crack a joke or deflect, like she does with everyone else—but Zosia isn’t everyone else.

    “When I was sixteen,” she says, quieter now, “she caught me kissing a girl.”

    Zosia’s expression shifts immediately. Softens. “I’m guessing she didn’t take it well.”

    Carol lets out a short, humourless laugh. “That’s one way of putting it.”

    She takes a long, slow drink, buying herself a moment. The kitchen feels smaller now. The silence, deafening.

    “She sent me away,” Carol says. “To this place—Camp Freedom Falls. It’s…”

    The words stall. She stares past Zosia, up toward the ceiling, like something might be written there that makes this easier. Nothing is, and the silence stretches.

    Then Zosia moves, closing the space between them. Her hand settles at the small of Carol’s back, warm and steady.

    Carol’s breath catches.

    She exhales slowly, the tension in her shoulders shifting under the gentle pressure of Zosia’s hand as it traces small, comforting circles.

    “It’s a conversion therapy camp,” Carol says finally.

    Zosia doesn’t ask anything else. Doesn’t press for details.

    Her hand slides around Carol’s waist, the other joining it as she steps in closer, drawing her in without demand. Her chin comes to rest lightly on Carol’s shoulder, her presence warm and solid at her back.

    Carol closes her eyes. Lets herself lean into it. Just a little.

    She doesn’t talk about Camp Freedom Falls. Not to anyone.

    “I’m really sorry,” Zosia murmurs, her voice low, her lips close enough that Carol feels it more than hears it.

    Carol swallows, her grip tightening slightly around the bottle in her hand. “Yeah,” she says, softer now. “Me too.”

    Zosia doesn’t let go straight away.

    Her arms stay wrapped around Carol for a moment longer, her breath slow and even where it brushes the side of Carol’s neck. Then, gently, she loosens her hold. Not all at once—just enough to give Carol space if she wants it.

    “Why don’t you go sit down,” Zosia says softly. “I can take it from here.”

    Carol exhales, opening her eyes. She shakes her head, her cheek brushing against Zosia’s. “No.”

    Zosia tilts her head slightly. “No?”

    “I said I’d help,” Carol replies, turning just enough to face her. “I’m helping.”

    There’s something in her tone—quiet, but firm enough that Zosia doesn’t argue.

    Instead, her mouth curves faintly. “All right,” she says. “But you’re on prep duty.”

    Carol snorts. “Demoted already?”

    “Promoted,” Zosia corrects, reaching for a pot. “You’re trusted with sharp objects.”

    “Yay for me,” Carol mutters, but she moves anyway, grabbing a knife from the block and setting a cutting board on the counter.

    The rhythm settles quickly after that.

    The rush of water filling the pot before Zosia sets it on the stove. The click-click of the burner, followed by the soft bloom of heat. Carol lines up vegetables—garlic, onions, a couple of tomatoes—and starts chopping, the knife hitting the board in a steady, grounding cadence.

    Zosia moves around her with practiced ease, close but never quite in the way. Reaching past her for the olive oil. Brushing her arm once, twice—light, incidental touches that linger just a fraction longer than necessary.

    Neither of them comments on it—or tries particularly hard to avoid it happening.

    “Not bad,” Zosia says after a moment, glancing down at Carol’s work.

    Carol scoffs. “Low bar.”

    “The onions are even,” Zosia replies. “That’s more than most people manage.”

    “Wow,” Carol says dryly. “High praise, Chef.”

    Zosia smiles to herself, turning back to the stove. The oil warms in a pan, a soft shimmer before the garlic hits it with a quiet sizzle. The smell blooms almost instantly—rich, sharp, familiar—and something in Carol’s chest loosens at it.

    She slides the chopped onions across, watching as Zosia tips them into the pan, stirring with easy, practiced movements.

    “You do that like it’s second nature,” Carol says.

    “It is,” Zosia replies, not looking up.

    Carol leans her hip against the counter, watching her for a second longer than she means to. The way her wrist turns, controlled and precise. The way she tastes the sauce with the tip of the spoon, frowns slightly, and adds a dash of salt something without measuring.

    “You’re staring,” Zosia says, without turning.

    Carol straightens immediately. “I’m observing.”

    “Mm.”

    “Learning,” Carol adds.

    Zosia glances at her then, one eyebrow lifting. “Of course you are.”

    Carol grabs the wooden spoon from where it rests against the stove. “Give me that,” she says, nudging Zosia lightly aside.

    Zosia lets herself be moved, just enough, watching as Carol takes over stirring with exaggerated focus.

    “Like this?” Carol asks.

    “Less aggression,” Zosia says.

    “I’m stirring it,” Carol protests.

    “You’re attacking it.”

    Carol huffs, but adjusts, slower now, more deliberate. The spoon drags through the softening onions, the sound low and rhythmic.

    “Better,” Zosia hums.

    “Don’t sound so surprised. I am capable of following basic instruction.”

    “Good to know,” Zosia replies, holding her gaze with an amused smile.

    Carol looks away first.

    The pasta goes in next, the water rolling into a boil as Zosia tips it into the pot. Steam curls up, warm and thick, fogging the space between them for a moment before dissipating.

    They fall into an easy sync, passing things without asking, working around each other like they’ve done this before. At one point, they reach for the same jar at the same time. Their hands brush and neither pulls away immediately.

    Carol feels it again—that low, flickering heat, sharper now, harder to ignore. She clears her throat, stepping back just enough to break the contact. “You—uh. You go ahead.”

    Zosia’s gaze lingers on her for a second, unreadable. Then she takes the jar with a small nod. “Thank you.”

    The moment passes.

    The sauce simmers, thickening slowly, the kitchen filling with the smell of tomato and garlic and spices. Carol leans back against the counter again, watching Zosia taste the sauce once more and nod to herself.

    “Okay?” Carol asks.

    Zosia glances at her, a small, satisfied smile forming. “Good enough.”

    Carol huffs. “Wow, harsh crowd.”

    “It’s edible,” Zosia says lightly.

    “High praise,” Carol echoes. “I had a good teacher.”

    Zosia laughs softly, and this time it lingers in the space between them. “How about you get us some plates?”

    “Yes, Chef.”

     

    -


    The desert shifts around them by the time the car gives up.

    It happens without warning—no dramatic sputter, no slow decline. Just a sudden spike on the temperature gauge, a sharp, insistent light on the dashboard, and then Koumba swearing under his breath as he guides the car off the shoulder of the I-40.

    The engine ticks loudly once it’s off, heat rising from beneath the hood in wavering distortions. The air outside is dry and cooling fast, the last of the sun stretched thin across the horizon, staining everything in rust and gold.

    They’ve just passed Winona, Arizona. Halfway back home and four long hours still to go.

    Koumba leans back in his seat, exhales through his teeth. “Well. That’s not ideal.”

    Manousos is already out of the car before Koumba can even contemplate calling roadside assistance.

    The gravel at the side of the road crunches under his boots as he circles the front, pops the hood. A wave of heat hits him immediately, thick and metallic, carrying the sharp, sour tang of overheated coolant. He squints slightly, leaning in, careful and methodical.

    Behind him, Koumba’s voice drifts out through the open door. “I’m calling AAA.”

    Manousos doesn’t answer. He studies the engine in silence, his eyes moving over it with practiced precision. No leaks. No obvious damage. Just heat—too much of it.

    He straightens after a moment, wiping his hands on the back of his shirt, and closes the hood with a firm, controlled motion.

    Koumba is still on the phone when he gets back in the car, one hand gesturing as he talks, even though no one can see him. “…yes, I’m on I-40, just past Winona—no, not Winslow, Winona—yes, I know the song, thank you…”

    Manousos shuts the door, the sound solid in the quiet stretch of road.

    Koumba glances at him, covering the phone briefly. “Well?”

    “Radiator overheated,” Manousos says. “We wait. One hour, maybe less. It cools, we go.”

    Koumba considers this, then nods once before returning to the call, wrapping things up quickly as he explains they no longer need assistance. When he hangs up, the silence settles in thick and fast. It’s the first time Koumba has been quiet since leaving Albuquerque that morning.

    The desert stretches out around them, vast and indifferent. The sky is dimming by the second, the heat of the day bleeding away into something cooler, sharper. A truck roars past them, the sound fading almost as quickly as it comes.

    Koumba shifts in his seat, glancing sideways. “Are you going to inform Carol of our delay?”

    Manousos leans his head back against the rest, eyes closing briefly. “No.”

    Koumba huffs a small laugh. “No? You are a terrible spy, Manousos.”

    “She will worry,” Manousos says. “She will call. Many times.”

    Koumba smiles to himself. “Yes, I imagine she would.”

    Manousos doesn’t respond, but there’s the faintest suggestion of agreement in the set of his mouth.

    The quiet stretches between them again. Koumba isn’t built for silence. “So,” he says, turning slightly in his seat, “how long have you known Carol?”

    Manousos opens his eyes. “Eighteen months.”

    Koumba waits, but the other man doesn’t offer anything else. A grin spreads across his face. “He speaks.”

    There’s a flicker of something in Manousos’s expression—dry, almost amused. “Sometimes.”

    “When you get the chance?” Koumba teases. He knows he can talk the hind legs off a donkey, given half a chance.

    “Yes.”

    Koumba laughs, full and easy, the sound filling the car. “I believe that. I have been doing enough talking for the both of us. Yes?”

    Manousos glances at him briefly. “Yes,” he states flatly.

    That only makes Koumba laugh harder. He shifts again, settling deeper into the seat as they wait for the engine to cool. “So, Carol tells me you are from Paraguay?”

    Manousos nods once. “Yes.”

    “Did you come here with family?”

    There’s a small pause before Manousos answers, his jaw tightening. “No.”

    Koumba’s smile softens slightly, but his curiosity doesn’t. “Just you?”

    “Yes.”

    “What brought you to Albuquerque?”

    “Work,” Manousos says. “I am an engineer.”

    Koumba lets out a low whistle. “Look at you, managing a whole sentence.”

    Manousos doesn’t react to that. He doesn’t react to much.

    Koumba studies him for a second, head tilting, then his grin returns, a little sharper this time. “Is there anyone special waiting for you back home? A nice chica, perhaps?”

    Manousos’s gaze shifts to the windshield, to the long stretch of empty road ahead, fading into dusk. “No,” he replies, falling back into one-word answers.

    “That is a shame,” Koumba says easily. “You are a good-looking man, Manousos. Strong and silent. American women love that kind of thing.”

    “I am not interested in women.”

    Koumba opens his mouth, ready with another joke—then hesitates. “You mean—”

    “I am gay,” Manousos says, cutting cleanly across it. The words land and settle uncomfortably between them. For a moment, the car is silent.

    Manousos feels it immediately—the shift, however slight. He’s used to men taking the admission the wrong way, as if they think he’ll be attracted to them on the sole basis of possessing a Y chromosome.

    His shoulders tighten, just a fraction, his gaze fixed straight ahead on the horizon. “Do not worry,” he adds, his tone even and controlled. “I am not going to hit on you.”

    There’s a heavy pause.

    Then—

    Koumba lets out a soft, amused sound.

    “Well,” he says, glancing over with a spark of something playful in his eyes, “now that is a shame.”

    Manousos looks at him then, properly this time. Koumba’s grin is easy, unbothered, a little flirtatious in a way that feels more like mischief than anything else.

    The tension eases, not all at once, but enough. Manousos exhales quietly, something in his posture loosening as he looks away again, his cheeks colouring.

    Outside, the last light slips below the horizon, the desert settling into night as they face a long wait in the small car.

    Manousos rubs a hand over his mouth, fingers catching on the faint rasp of stubble along his chin. He lets the silence sit for a moment, turning something over in his head, weighing it.

    He doesn’t usually speak unless he has something worth saying. “And Zosia?” he asks.

    Koumba glances at him, expression shifting—subtle, but still noticeable. A slight narrowing of his eyes, a tilt of his head. “What about Zosia?”

    Manousos’s gaze stays forward, fixed on the dark, barren stretch of highway ahead. “Is she also bisexual?”

    For a second, Koumba just looks at him. Then a slow smile spreads across his face, edged with amusement. “Ah,” he says, settling back into his seat. “Now we are asking the interesting questions.”

    Manousos doesn’t react, but there’s the faintest tightening at the corner of his mouth.

    Koumba studies him, something a little more knowing settling into his expression. “That,” he says lightly, “is Zosia’s business.”

    Manousos exhales softly through his nose.

    “If Carol wants to know,” Koumba adds, glancing sideways at him, “she should ask her.”

    That earns him a look. Brief and measured, but also amused.

    “Yes,” Manousos says in agreement. “She should.”

    Koumba waits.

    Manousos’s mouth twitches, just slightly. “But she will not.”

    “No?” Koumba asks, though he already sounds like he agrees.

    “She is too stubborn,” Manousos says. His tone is even, but not unkind. “Sometimes, I think… she prefers it this way.”

    Koumba’s brow furrows. “This way?”

    “Unhappy,” Manousos says simply. Then, after a moment, “She does not help herself.”

    Koumba huffs a quiet laugh, shaking his head. “I know the type.”

    Manousos looks at him curiously.

    “My brother,” Koumba says, his voice softening a fraction. “Malick. He was the same way. Always moping. Always wanting something, but never going after it.”

    Manousos shifts slightly in his seat. “What changed?”

    Koumba’s smile returns, warmer this time, threaded with something close to reverence. “Zosia,” he says.

    Of course.

    “We worked together,” Koumba continues. “The three of us at another restaurant. Long hours, too many people in a small kitchen, tempers always on edge.” He huffs a quiet laugh. “Zosia and I—we got along immediately. Like that.” He snaps his fingers once. “She is easy to like. Pretty, yes, but also… sharp. Funny. Talented. You know this.”

    Manousos says nothing, but his silence isn’t disagreement.

    “Malick noticed her straight away, of course,” Koumba goes on. “I fell for her first, but he… he fell very hard. Very hard indeed. And then—he did absolutely nothing about it.”

    Manousos’s brow dips slightly. “Nothing?”

    “Nothing,” Koumba repeats, with a shake of his head, amused. “He would just… watch her. Follow her around like a little lost lamb. It was painful to witness.”

    A faint sound escapes Manousos—something that might be a quiet laugh.

    “One morning,” Koumba says, “I was bringing Zosia coffee. I saw him standing there, behind the bar, staring at her like his heart was breaking.” He shakes his head at the memory. “So… I gave him the coffee in my hand. Told him to stop being ridiculous and go talk to her.”

    “And he did?”

    Koumba lets out a guffaw of laughter. “He had no choice! I practically pushed him across the room.” A small shrug. “They talked. They laughed. And that was it. The rest is… how do you say—? History.”

    Manousos manages a genuine smile.

    The car settles into a quieter kind of silence after that, the weight of the story lingering between the two men.

    Manousos frowns slightly. “You gave up your own happiness,” he says. “For your brother’s.”

    Koumba glances at him, expression open, unguarded in a way it hasn’t been before. “It was never something to give up,” he answers honestly. “Malick’s happiness—” he shrugs lightly “—that was my happiness.”

    Manousos studies him for a moment, something thoughtful in his gaze. “That is… admirable.”

    Koumba waves the praise off almost immediately. “No, no. Do not make me sound so noble.” He leans back, stretching his legs out a little. “Malick was better for her. He was a good man. A serious man. He loved her completely.”

    There’s no hesitation in it. No doubt.

    “I was…” Koumba’s smile is whimsical, a little crooked. “In my mid-thirties. A bit of a rascal. Not the kind of man you build a life with.”

    Manousos tilts his head slightly. “And now?”

    Koumba considers that, his gaze drifting out toward the darkened desert beyond the window. “Now,” he says slowly, “I have grown. A little.” A faint smile tugs at his mouth. “Maybe I could settle down. With the right woman.”

    A pause.

    “Or man,” he adds lightly, glancing sideways.

    Manousos doesn’t react, but the corner of his mouth shifts, almost imperceptibly.

    Koumba’s expression softens again as he looks ahead. “But Zosia…” He exhales quietly. “She is family now, and her happiness is more important to me than anything else in this world.”

    The words settle between them, steady and certain. Koumba grows uncharacteristically quiet for a moment. Then, softer, he adds, “ Carol makes her happy, I think.”

    His smile returns, but this time there’s something else in it. Something quieter. Sadder, maybe. Not regret—something gentler than that.

    “Yes,” Manousos agrees, holding his gaze. “She does.”

    Chapter 16

    Chapter Notes

    Carol sits cross-legged on one end of the sofa, remote in hand, flipping lazily through titles while Zosia settles beside her, tucking her legs underneath herself, bare feet disappearing into the cushions.

    “Pick something,” Zosia murmurs, leaning into the backrest, her shoulder brushing Carol’s. “You’ve been scrolling for ten minutes.”

    “I’m exploring our options,” Carol replies offhandedly. “There’s a difference.”

    Zosia hums, unconvinced. “Mm.”

    Carol brightens slightly as she lands on something interesting-looking. “How about this?”

    “A horror?” Zosia wrinkles her nose as she turns her head, eyeing her. “I don’t want to watch something where people make terrible decisions for two hours.”

    Carol makes a face. “That’s kinda every movie,” she points out, earning a playful nudge of her shoulder from the woman beside her.

    “How about a romantic comedy?” Zosia counters, reaching over to nudge the remote down a row.

    Carol huffs softly, but her attention drifts—not to the screen, but to Zosia, close enough that she can feel the warmth of her through the thin fabric of her shirt. Her hand rests over Carol’s on the remote.

    A romantic comedy.

    God.

    She feels like she’s already in one.

    The hopeless older woman, pining quietly, making bad decisions in slow motion—falling for the woman who is, through a series of deeply questionable events, accidentally pregnant with her baby.

    Hilarious.

    “Romantic thriller,” Carol says finally, pressing select before she can think too hard about it. “Compromise?”

    Zosia considers, then nods. “Fine. But if it’s terrible, I’m blaming you.”

    Carol rolls her eyes as she settles back into the sofa, and the opening credits of the movie flash up on screen.

    For a while, they sit in relative silence, the low hum of the television filling the space. Zosia shifts slightly, adjusting her position, her foot brushing against the cushion—then again, a little more insistently.

    Carol glances over. “What’s wrong?”

    “Nothing,” Zosia says, though she’s absently rubbing the arch of her left foot. “My feet just hurt a little.”

    Carol raises an eyebrow. “Probably those ten hours we spent shopping,” she teases.

    Zosia gives her a look. “I seem to remember it was your idea.”

    Carol snorts. “Yeah, but I didn’t expect it to take half the day! Next time I’ll pack provisions. Water, snacks… maybe a flare gun.”

    Zosia chuckles lightly at the other woman’s exaggeration before returning to rubbing at her foot in search of relief.

    Carol shifts, grabbing one of the throw pillows and dropping it onto her lap. She pats it with her hand. “Put ’em up.” She nods toward Zosia’s feet.

    Zosia blinks at her. “What?”

    “Your feet,” Carol says, gesturing again. “Put ‘em here.”

    Zosia hesitates, clearly unsure. “Carol—”

    “Massaging them will help,” Carol says, like it’s obvious.

    Zosia’s cheeks redden. “I can’t ask you to do that.”

    “You’re not asking,” Carol replies. “I’m offering.”

    Zosia still looks uncertain.

    A hint of a grin tugs at Carol’s mouth. “Relax. I don’t have a foot fetish. I actually hate feet, so if anything, you should feel honoured I’m even offering.”

    That earns her a small laugh.

    “…Okay,” Zosia says slowly.

    She shifts, carefully lifting her feet and resting them on the pillow in Carol’s lap, tentative at first, like she expects the other woman to start laughing and tell her she’s joking.

    Carol doesn’t do that, though. She takes one of Zosia’s feet gently, her hands warm as they settle around it, thumbs pressing into the sole with slow, deliberate pressure.

    Zosia exhales—sharp, surprised—then lets out a soft, involuntary moan.

    Carol feels it like a current under her skin.

    “Good?” she asks, her voice far steadier than she actually feels.

    “Very,” Zosia murmurs, her head tipping back slightly against the sofa. “That feels… really good.”

    Carol swallows, tearing her gaze away from the long column of Zosia’s neck to focus on the movement of her hands, the steady rhythm of them. Press and release. Slow circles along the arch. Her thumbs working into the tension there.

    Zosia makes another quiet sound, softer this time, almost a sigh. Carol feels it low in her chest. In her stomach. Lower. It spreads, sharp and unwelcome and impossible to ignore.

    She keeps her gaze down, her hands moving.

    The room settles again. The movie continues in the background, something tense unfolding on screen, but neither of them is really watching.

    “Are you sure you don’t have a foot fetish?” Zosia asks after a moment, her voice threaded with amusement.

    Carol freezes.

    Her grip tightens slightly before she forces herself to relax it, her mind tripping over itself.

    Did she—?

    Carol clears her throat. “What—what do you mean?”

    Zosia shifts, lifting her head enough to glance down at her. “Your second book,” she says. “There’s a scene with Raban and Lucasia—”

    Carol groans immediately, her face tightening in visible discomfort. “Nope!”

    Zosia laughs. “You know the one. He’s rubbing her feet and then his mouth—”

    “I know exactly which scene you’re referring to,” Carol cuts in, tone sharp but eyes filled with amusement. “And I hated every second of writing it.”

    Zosia’s smile widens. “Really?”

    “My editor made me include it,” Carol says, now working her thumbs into Zosia’s other foot, though her expression is pinched. “Said it added ‘intimacy.’ I had to shower straight after I finished writing it, and I couldn’t look at my own feet for a week.”

    Zosia laughs properly at that, the sound warm and bright. Her hand drifts down, resting lightly against Carol’s arm.

    “Thank you,” she says, softer now.

    Carol huffs, glancing up briefly. “It’s fine. I’ll just block out the memory of this later. Pretend it never happened—that you don’t even have feet.”

    Zosia’s smile lingers, but her gaze shifts, something more serious settling in. “I wasn’t just talking about the foot rub.”

    Carol stills slightly.
