    Zosia’s hand stays where it is, warm against bare skin. “Thank you for everything,” she says. “For coming to the appointments with me. For… all of this.” She gestures vaguely, meaning more than just the room, the evening, the moment. “For being there.”

    Carol looks back down at her hands. At Zosia’s foot in her lap. At the way her fingers still rest there, steady, grounding.

    “Don’t thank me for the party yet,” she says lightly, deflecting. “You might hate it.”

    Zosia watches her, her expression soft—open in a way that makes something tighten in Carol’s chest.

    “I’m sure I won’t,” she says quietly. “Not if it came from you.”

    Carol’s breath catches.

    It lands too close. Too heavy.

    She can feel it building again—that pressure under her ribs, that restless, unwieldy thing she’s been trying not to name.

    Carol pulls her hands back gently, setting Zosia’s foot down on the sofa. “I’m going to grab us another drink,” she says, a little too quickly as she pushes herself to her own feet, Zosia’s hand slipping away from her arm.

    “Okay,” Zosia’s expression falls as she watches her go.

    Carol doesn’t look back as she heads for the kitchen, the glow of the television fading behind her, her pulse still just a little too loud in her own ears.

    She lingers in the kitchen; one hand braced against the counter, the other wrapped loosely around the neck of a beer she hasn’t opened yet. She exhales slowly, steadying herself, letting the quiet settle back into her bones before she attempts to move.

    When she returns, she’s composed again.

    Or as close as she can get.

    Zosia is still on the sofa, but she’s no longer watching the screen. The glow of her phone lights her face instead, her brow faintly furrowed, her mouth set in a small, distracted line.

    Carol pauses for half a second, drinking her in, then steps in properly, holding out the soda. “Everything okay?”

    Zosia looks up, blinking like she’s just remembered where she is. “Hm? Oh—yes. Fine.” She takes the offered drink, her fingers brushing Carol’s briefly. “Just Koumba.”

    Carol lowers herself back onto the sofa, keeping a careful distance between them this time. “What about him?” she asks, trying to keep her tone light. Casual.

    Zosia huffs a quiet laugh, shaking her head as she sets her phone down. “He’s sending me updates about the wonderful time he’s having with his friend.”

    Carol snorts softly. “Of course he is.”

    “They’re thankfully rated PG.” Zosia laughs, and there’s fondness threaded through it. Then, softer, she adds, “I just hope he’s not late for the party tomorrow.”

    Carol lifts her beer, turning it idly in her hands. “I’m sure he’ll be on time,” she says, confident. Manousos text her earlier, so she knows they’re already more than halfway back to New Mexico.

    Zosia’s shoulders loosen a fraction at that. “I’m sure you’re right.”

    The movie still plays, forgotten in the background, voices rising and falling without either of them paying much attention.

    Zosia draws her legs up again, tucking them beneath herself, her fingers curling loosely around the soda can in her hands. “I hate when he’s out of town,” she admits after a moment.

    Carol glances at her, curious. “Why?”

    Zosia shrugs slightly, searching for the words. “I don’t know. It’s just… comforting, knowing he’s there. Next door.” A small pause. “Feels safer.”

    The quiet part is left unsaid.

    Without Malick.

    Something in Carol’s chest shifts. She leans back into the sofa, considering for a second. “You could stay here tonight,” she suggests.

    Zosia looks over, surprised by the offer.

    “I can drive you back in the morning,” Carol continues, like it’s nothing. “You can get ready for the party from there.”

    Zosia hesitates. “I don’t want to put you out.”

    “You’re not,” Carol says, a little quicker than she means to. Then, more deliberate, “It’s no trouble… You’re not the worst company.”

    Zosia studies her for a moment, something warm and thoughtful in her gaze. Then she breaks out in a smile. “In that case, I’d love to.”

    Carol feels something ease in her chest at the thought of Zosia staying longer.

    “Could I trouble you for something to sleep in?” She asks.

    “Of course.” Carol pushes herself up from the sofa, snatching the remote from the coffee table to switch the TV off. “Come on. We’ll find you something.”

    The house see s quieter upstairs as Carol leads the way into her bedroom, flicking on the light. She’s aware of Zosia stepping in behind her; of the way her presence settles into the space like she belongs there.

    Carol pads over to her chest of drawers, pulling the top one open a little too quickly. She rummages for a moment, deliberately focused—doing her best not to think about the fact Zosia is in her bedroom—as she digs out a loose T-shirt and a pair of soft shorts.

    She turns, holding them out. “These should work.”

    Zosia takes them, her fingers brushing Carol’s again. “Thank you.”

    “There’s a, uh, guest room,” Carol says, nodding toward the hall. “Second door on the left.”

    Zosia nods, offering her a small smile before slipping back out of the room. Carol exhales, slow and measured, once she’s alone. There hasn’t been another woman in her bedroom in years. Her gaze drifts to Helen’s side of the bed, to her nightstand and the unfinished book still resting there.

    Carol shakes her head, pulling herself out of her thoughts as she grabs herself some bed clothes to wear. She changes quickly, pushes her hair back behind her ears and glances at herself once in the mirror—satisfied with what she sees.

    A few minutes later, she walks down the hallway and knocks lightly on the guest room door.

    The door opens partway. Zosia stands there, already changed, Carol’s T-shirt loose on her frame, the neckline just low enough to make Carol’s brain stall for a second before she forcibly drags her gaze back up.

    “Hey, uh, you got everything you need?”

    “Yes,” Zosia says, smiling. “Thank you.”

    There’s a small pause. Zosia hesitates, her fingers curling slightly around the edge of the door. “Are you particularly tired?”

    Carol leans lightly against the frame. “Not really.”

    Zosia’s expression shifts—something a little shy creeping in. “Do you… want to watch another movie?”

    Not that they saw much of the first one.

    Carol doesn’t even pretend to consider it. “Sure.” She says.

    Zosia’s smile grows, but there’s still that hint of uncertainty in her voice. “Could we watch it in here?” she asks. “It would just be more comfortable if I can prop myself up with some pillows.”

    Carol’s brain blanks.

    In here.

    In bed.

    With Zosia.

    “Uh, y-yeah. Yes.” she replies, a fraction too slowly, her voice catching just slightly on the words. “Yeah, that’s—fine.”

    Zosia beams, doesn’t seem to notice her discomfort. She steps back, opening the door wider, already moving toward the bed as Carol takes a timid step inside. “I promise I won’t fall asleep halfway through.”

    “Me too,” Carol mutters, stepping inside. Every cell in her body feels like it’s on fire at the thought of getting into bed with the other woman.

    Zosia is already arranging pillows, stacking them behind her, shifting and adjusting until she’s comfortable. There’s something almost instinctive in the way she does it—careful, deliberate, like she’s building a nest.

    Carol tries not to stare.

    And fails miserably.

    She drags her attention back to the TV, grabbing the remote from the nightstand and flicking it on. The screen hums to life, casting a soft glow across the room.

    “What are we watching?” she asks, already scrolling through the streaming app.

    Zosia tilts her head, watching her. Then, with just a hint of mischief, “Romantic comedy?” She suggests.

    Carol turns her head and finds Zosia looking at her with wide, hopeful eyes. She exhales through her nose, already aware she’s lost. “You’re not even going to pretend to compromise this time, are you?”

    Zosia smiles, just a little brighter. “No.”

    Carol shakes her head, but there’s no real resistance left. “Fine,” she gives off a dramatic sigh, selecting something without even really looking. “Romantic comedy.”

    Zosia settles deeper into the pillows, clearly pleased.

    Carol sits on the edge of the bed for a moment—then, after a brief hesitation, shifts properly onto it, keeping a careful, measured distance between them as the movie begins.

    The space feels smaller, more intimate than the sofa—and far more dangerous.

    The movie is terrible. Carol knows it within minutes of it starting. The dialogue is stilted, the chemistry nonexistent, the plot hinging on a misunderstanding so flimsy it personally offends her on behalf of her entire profession. Two conventionally attractive people circling each other, saying all the wrong things, refusing to just be honest.

    It’s painfully predictable, and should be easy to focus on

    It isn’t.

    Not with Zosia beside her.

    Not with the quiet warmth of her presence pressing into the space between them, not with the soft rustle of fabric every time she shifts against the pillows, not with the slow, absent way her fingers trace along the edge of the blanket. The borrowed shorts ride up slightly when she moves, exposing long stretches of bare skin that catch the low glow of the television in the darkened room.

    Carol’s gaze catches on Zosia’s thighs once.

    Twice.

    Lingers.

    She drags her eyes back to the screen, jaw tightening as she considers getting up, grabbing another beer—something to take the edge off, something to dull the sharp, restless energy humming under her skin.

    But she doesn’t.

    Helen’s voice comes back to her, clear as anything.

    You get handsy when you’re drunk, Baby.

    Carol exhales quietly through her nose, shifting slightly where she sits. The last thing she needs is lower inhibitions with the thoughts running through her mind right now.

    The last thing she needs is to lean in without thinking, to close the space between them, to—

    She cuts that particular thought off.

    No good can come of it.

    Her mouth presses into a thin line, gaze fixed stubbornly on the screen as the couple on it dance around one another and their true feelings.

    God, she imagines what Helen would make of her current situation. Carol, in bed with another woman. A woman she can’t stop thinking about—and can’t have.

    A woman who isn’t Helen.

    The thought is so absurd, so wildly out of step with the life Carol thought she’d be living, that a quiet huff of laughter escapes her before she can stop it.

    Zosia turns her head, the movement soft but immediate, her expression puzzled.

    On screen, the couple are breaking up—voices raised, music swelling dramatically. There is absolutely nothing funny about it.

    Carol shakes her head slightly. “Sorry. I was just—” She hesitates, then exhales. “Thinking about Helen.”

    The air in the room shifts. Zosia stills beside her, her expression softening, something careful and uncertain settling in its place.

    “Oh,” she says quietly.

    For a moment, neither of them speaks. The movie continues in the background, but it feels distant now, irrelevant.

    Zosia’s fingers curl slightly against the blanket. She glances at Carol, then away, then back again, like she’s weighing something in her mind.

    “What was she like?” she eventually asks.

    Carol stares at the screen for a second, though she’s not seeing the movie anymore.

    Where would she even begin?

    “She was…” Her voice catches slightly, and she clears her throat. “Incredible.”

    The word feels insufficient.

    “She knew it, too,” Carol adds, a faint, fond edge slipping in. “Not in an arrogant way. Just—” she huffs a small breath, “—completely sure of herself… of me.”

    Zosia listens, quiet, attentive.

    “She was stubborn,” Carol continues. “God, so stubborn. Once she decided something, that was it. No changing her mind.” A small pause. “Smart too. Smarter than me, most of the time. And loyal. Fierce, when she needed to be.”

    Her throat tightens.

    “She loved… completely,” she says, voice wavering now. “There wasn’t anything halfway about it with her. If she loved you, then she made sure you knew it.”

    She stops there, the words catching somewhere behind her ribs.

    Then, almost without meaning to—

    “You remind me so much of her.”

    The silence that follows is heavier, and Carol instantly regrets saying the last part out loud.

    Zosia’s breath hitches, just slightly. Her brow furrowing. “Me?”

    Carol looks at her, then away again, down at her hands as she wrings them together. “Yeah.”

    She exhales slowly, shaking her head faintly. “She would have liked you,” she says. “You two would have gotten along. Probably would have ganged up on me, actually.”

    That earns the smallest hint of a smile from Zosia.

    “She would have been excited about the baby, too,” Carol adds, her voice thinning at the edges.

    Her vision blurs slightly. Tears threatening to fall. She blinks hard, but it doesn’t quite help. Beside her, Zosia shifts.

    Gently, her hand reaches across the space between them, finding Carol’s. Carol stills at the contact.

    Steady.

    Certain.

    She could pull away—should pull away—but she doesn’t. Instead, she holds on, laces their fingers together. Once she starts talking again, she doesn’t stop, afraid she’ll never be brave enough to say the words out loud if she doesn’t get them out now.

    “We were coming back from a tour,” she says, voice low now. Fragile. “For my third book. We’d been travelling for weeks, and we just wanted to get home. Get back to our own bed.”

    Zosia’s thumb rubs faintly against the back of Carol’s hand. The simple action feels grounding. Gives the courage to keep going.

    “We stopped near the airport. There was this… shitty little bar. Sticky floors, bad lighting.” A faint, humourless huff. “I wanted a drink before we drove back here… Just couldn’t fucking wait.”

    Her chest tightens.

    “We went outside so Helen could smoke.” The words start to come faster now, less controlled. Less calculated.

    “She just—” Carol’s breath stutters. “She just dropped. No warning. Nothing. One second she was there, and the next—”

    Her fingers tighten unconsciously around Zosia’s, like she’s afraid she too might vanish.

    “There was nothing I could do,” she says, voice breaking, tears finally falling. “The ambulance came so quickly. Minutes… but it didn’t matter.”

    The room feels smaller. Too small. Carol struggles to take a breath as her chest tightens, her throat closing.

    She chokes out the next words with great difficulty, “They said it was an aneurysm. Her body was there, but she was…” Carol squeezes her eyes shut.

    She swallows hard and says the next part in a low whisper, like saying it quietly makes it somehow feel less real. Less devastating. “Helen wasn’t there anymore.”

    Three days.

    Three days of machines and waiting and hoping for something that never came. “I had to—” Her voice falters completely. She sucks in a breath and forces it out anyway. “I had to decide—”

    The words hang there, heavy and suffocating.

    Carol shakes her head slightly, eyes fixed somewhere far beyond the room as she clings to Zosia’s hand like it’s the only thing anchoring her as she wades through the past.

    “She would have hated that,” she murmurs. “Being kept alive like that. I should have—” Her breath catches. “I should have let her go sooner.”

    “Carol—”

    Zosia moves before she can finish.

    Closer.

    Her arms come around her, careful but firm, pulling her into an enveloping hug. Carol doesn’t resist.

    She folds into it, into the warmth of the other woman, into her steadying presence, her hands gripping at the fabric of Zosia’s shirt like she needs something solid to hold onto.

    “I’m sure she would have understood,” Zosia murmurs into her hair, voice soft, certain. She squeezes Carol tighter. “It’s hard to let go. To say goodbye… to move on with your life without feeling like you’re dishonouring their memory.”

    Zosia speaks from experience. Her own husband’s passing is still a raw nerve, not yet fully scabbed over.

    Carol presses her face into Zosia’s shoulder, breathing her in, the faint flowery scent of soap and perfume, and something else; something that is just Zosia.

    “I want to,” Carol sighs, the words breaking apart as they leave her. “I really want to.”

    And for the first time in a long time—

    She cries.

    Not just for Helen.

    But for herself, too.


    -

    Carol wakes slowly, the world coming back to her in pieces.

    Warmth, first.

    Soft. Close.

    Too close.

    Her brow furrows faintly as she blinks her eyes open, the dim grey light of early morning filtering in through the blinds. It takes her a second to understand what she’s feeling. Where she is. Who she’s with.

    And then it hits her.

    Zosia.

    Carol fell asleep in her arms.

    They’re tangled together, limbs loosely entwined beneath the covers. Zosia is pressed against her, one arm draped across her waist, her face tucked in close, breath warm and even against Carol’s collarbone.

    Still asleep.

    Peaceful.

    Carol goes very, very still, afraid to even breathe too loudly for fear of waking the other woman.

    The night before comes flooding back. The movie. The talking. Discussing Helen’s passing. The way Carol had broken open and spilled out everything she’s kept locked down for years.

    And now—this.

    Her chest tightens, something sharp and uncomfortable curling low in her stomach.

    Shame.

    Embarrassment.

    The desire for the earth to open and swallow her whole.

    She swallows hard, staring up at the ceiling for a second before her gaze shifts, hesitantly, back down to Zosia. It feels wrong for Carol to see her like this. Unguarded.

    There’s no tension in Zosia’s face, no carefulness in the set of her mouth. Just quiet, steady sleep. One of her legs is thrown over Carol’s, anchoring her there without even realizing it.

    Carol exhales slowly through her nose.

    Carefully—so carefully—she lifts a hand, hesitates, then brushes a loose strand of hair back from Zosia’s face. Her fingers linger for half a second longer than they should.

    Gently, she starts to disentangle herself, easing Zosia’s arm away from her waist, shifting her leg free inch by inch, trying not to wake her.

    It almost works.

    Zosia stirs, a soft, sleepy sound slipping from her as the warmth beneath her disappears. Her hand moves instinctively, reaching out into the empty space Carol leaves behind, searching for her.

    Carol freezes, holds her breath for an excruciating moment as she waits to see if Zosia will wake. When she doesn’t, she quietly slips out of the room.

    The hallway is cool, the air sharper than in the bedroom. It helps, a little. Clears her head just enough to keep her moving.

    She passes her own room without stopping. Instead, she keeps going, down the hall to her study.

    The familiar space greets her like it always does—orderly, contained, safe. She closes the door behind her with a soft click and crosses to her desk, sitting down heavily in the chair.

    Work. That’s what she needs. Something structured—something she has control over.

    Her laptop hums to life when she opens it, the glow of the screen filling the dim room. Carol exhales sharply and pulls up her manuscript.

    The words are exactly where she left them—and so are Raban and Lucasia.

    In bed.

    Carol stares at the screen, expression flattening slightly as she takes in the last few lines she’d written—morning light, tangled sheets, bodies pressed close in the quiet intimacy of it all.

    Her mouth pulls to one side.

    “Fucking of course,” she mutters under her breath.

    For a moment, the image in her mind shifts from her characters—bleeds into something else entirely.

    A different bed.

    A different woman.

    Carol makes a face. “Nope,” she says quietly.

    Absolutely not.

    She leans back in her chair, dragging her hands over her face. The document stays open, untouched. After a second, she reaches forward and presses the return key until the previous words move up and disappear off the screen.

    She’ll come back to it, she decides.

    Later.

    Much later.

     

    -

    Zosia wakes slowly, the kind of soft, gradual awareness that comes only after a deep sleep. She feels the baby growing inside her stirring too.

    It swishes, changing position before a little hand or foot jabs out, pressing uncomfortably against her bladder. The movement, which had felt alien and surreal at first, is a comfort now—a reassurance that all is well.

    The room is quiet, pale morning light stretching gently across the walls, the sheets still warm where she’s been lying. She blinks up at the ceiling, her mind slow to catch up with her body.

    The memory of last night settles in, piece by piece.

    The way Carol’s voice had changed when she spoke about Helen—quieter, thinner, like the words were cutting their way out of her. The way she’d folded in on herself, like she didn’t quite know what to do with the weight of it anymore.

    And then—

    Zosia’s chest tightens slightly.

    Holding her.

    Carol pressed against her, trembling, breath uneven against her shoulder as she sobbed.

    Zosia hadn’t pulled away. Hadn’t tried to fix it. Had simply held Carol while she’d let it all out. At some point, somewhere between one breath and the next, they’d fallen asleep like that. Tangled together, close in a way that had felt… natural. Easy.

    Zosia turns her head. The other side of the bed is empty now, and the absence she feels is immediate.

    She frowns slightly, pushing herself up onto her elbows, the blanket slipping down around her waist. “Carol?” she calls out.

    No answer.

    The house is quiet.

    Zosia sits there for a moment, weighing up her options, then exhales softly and swings her legs over the side of the bed. The thick carpet is soft under her bare feet as she stands, tugging the oversized T-shirt down slightly before stepping out into the hallway.

    She moves slowly, instinct keeping her from calling out for Carol again. The door at the end of the hallway is ajar, and the soft tap-tap-tap of keys draws Zosia closer.

    She pauses at the door, her hand resting lightly against the frame as she peers inside. Carol is at her desk, still dressed in what she wore to bed.

    Working.

    Focused.

    Completely absorbed.

    Her head is bent over the laptop, shoulders hunched, fingers moving quickly across the keyboard, the rhythm steady, almost relentless. There’s an intensity to it—something consuming, like she’s trying to outrun a thought by putting it into words faster than it can catch her.

    Zosia watches her for a moment, a small smile tugging at her mouth. There’s something reassuring about it. Carol in her element, building something out of nothing. She doesn’t interrupt.

    Instead, she turns quietly and heads downstairs.

    The kitchen is bright with morning light, the air still cool. Zosia moves through it easily, pulling things together without much thought—coffee first, because Carol looks like she’s been awake for hours already. Then something simple: toast, butter melting into the surface while it’s still hot, and a sprinkling of cinnamon on top.

    She arranges it on a tray she finds, balancing the mug carefully beside the plate, and heads back upstairs.

    Carol hasn’t moved from where she left her. She’s still hunched over the laptop, shoulders slightly tense, completely lost in whatever she’s writing.

    Zosia steps into the room quietly, closing the door behind her with a soft push of her foot. She sets the tray down carefully on the edge of the desk, then reaches out, her fingers brushing lightly against Carol’s shoulder.

    Carol jumps—a sharp, startled movement, her hand jerking slightly on the keyboard.

    “Jesus—” she breathes, turning quickly.

    The tension drains out of her almost immediately when she spots Zosia. “Shit, Zosia, you nearly gave me a heart attack!”

    Zosia smiles, a little apologetic. “Sorry.”

    Carol exhales, dragging a hand through her hair. “No, it’s—fine. I just didn’t hear you.”

    Her gaze drops to the tray. A flicker of something crosses her expression—surprise, then something softer, quickly masked.

    “You didn’t have to do that,” Carol says, voice strained. “You’re—” She gestures vaguely. “You’re the guest. I should be—”

    “It’s just breakfast,” Zosia cuts in gently.

    Carol’s mouth presses into a thin line, like she wants to argue, then thinks better of it. “Still.”

    There’s something tight under the words. Something careful. Zosia doesn’t push it.

    Carol shifts instead, clearing a space on her desk with brisk, efficient movements—papers stacked, notebook shoved aside just enough to make room as Zosia slides the tray further onto the desk so it doesn’t fall.

    Carol reaches for the coffee almost immediately, wrapping her hands around it like a lifeline, the heat grounding.

    Zosia lingers.

    Close.

    Closer than she probably should.

    She leans in slightly, resting her weight against Carol’s back as she peers at the screen. “What are you working on?”

    Carol reacts instantly. The laptop snaps shut with a soft but decisive click. “No spoilers,” she chides.

    Zosia pulls back just enough to look at her, lips curving into a small pout.

    Carol huffs a quiet breath, shaking her head—even as she fights the urge to give in and let Zosia read it. “It’s not ready.”

    “It looked ready,” Zosia says lightly.

    “It’s not,” Carol repeats, a little firmer.

    Zosia studies her for a second, then relents, lifting her hands in surrender. “Okay. Fine. Keep me waiting.” She shifts, pulling back, but doesn’t move away completely.

    Zosia leans her hip lightly against the desk instead, watching Carol as she takes a sip of her coffee. There’s something soft and unguarded in the way they fall into place without thinking.

    Carol feels it. Of course she does. It hits her all at once, sharp and disorienting—the familiarity of it, the quiet, domestic ease of it all.

    Like an echo of what she once had.

    Her grip tightens slightly around the mug. Guilt follows quickly after this thought, swift and unwelcome. She looks down at her mug, breaking the moment before it can settle too deeply.

    “You know,” she says, a little too briskly as she gestures at the breakfast tray, “you didn’t have to go to all this effort.”

    Zosia tilts her head. “It’s toast, it was hardly an effort.”

    Carol nods, but she doesn’t look up. “Still.”

    There’s a pause.

    Something unspoken sitting between them now.

    Zosia shifts slightly, her hand finding Carol’s shoulder, her voice softer when she speaks again. “Are you okay?”

    Carol’s shoulder tenses under her grip, just for a second.

    Then Carol smooths a simple smile onto her face, something guarded sliding back into place as she lifts her gaze again. “I’m fine.”

    Zosia watches her closely. Knows, instinctively, that Carol is far from fine. She doesn’t push, though. Not yet.

    “Okay.”

    Zosia lets the quiet sit for a moment longer, then exhales softly, like she’s coming to a decision.

    “I should probably head home,” she says, glancing toward the door. “Get ready for the party.”

    Carol’s head lifts a fraction too quickly. “I can drive you,” she offers.

    Zosia smiles, small but firm. “You don’t have to do that. I’ll just get an Uber.”

    “It’s not a problem,” Carol says, already half-turning in her chair, like she might stand. “I’m not doing anything that can’t wait.”

    Zosia’s gaze flicks briefly to the closed laptop. Then back to Carol. “Yes, you are,” she says gently. “You’re in the zone. I can see it. Stay. Write.”

    Carol hesitates.

    Zosia’s smile turns a little teasing, a little softer around the edges. “Besides,” she adds, “you haven’t had a break from me in, what, twenty-four hours now? You must be sick of me.”

    Carol frowns immediately. “I’m not.” Her answer is quick. Unfiltered. It lands between them before she can pull it back.

    Zosia’s expression shifts—just slightly. Something warmer, a little more certain.

    “Still,” she says, “I’ll take an Uber.”

    Carol opens her mouth, like she might try and argue again, then stops herself. “Fine.”

    Zosia nods once, her hand dropping from Carol’s shoulder. “I’ll just change, then I’ll go.”

    She pushes off the desk and slips out of the room, leaving Carol sitting there with her untouched breakfast and the faint scent of Zosia’s perfume lingering in the air.

    Down the hall, there’s the soft sound of movement—the guest room door closing, the quiet rustle of fabric. Carol doesn’t mean to listen for it, but she does anyway, her attention drifting despite herself.

    After a beat, she shakes her head at herself and gets to her feet, intending to wait for Zosia downstairs.

    She’s sitting sideways on the bottom step, knees pulled up to her chest, when Zosia comes into view at the top. One hand trails lightly along the banister as she descends. She’s changed into the maternity jeans and the soft sweater she bought yesterday, the fabric skimming gently over her frame.

    Carol’s chest tightens as she remembers the dress.

    “Wait,” she says, already scrambling to her feet.

    Zosia pauses mid-step, blinking at her. “What is it?”

    “Just—one second.”

    Carol’s already moving, grabbing the keys to her truck from the bowl on the side table beside the front door. She doesn’t explain, just pulls it open and disappears outside before Zosia can ask anything else.

    Zosia lingers at the bottom of the stairs, bemused.

    A moment later, she hears the truck door open and then slam shut. Then Carol is back, a little out of breath, a fancy paper bag in hand.

    She holds it out. “For you.”

    Zosia’s brows lift as she takes it, peering inside curiously. Then—realisation hits.

    “Oh.”

    Her face lights up. She pulls the navy dress free, the fabric catching the morning light as it unfolds, soft and elegant in her hands. For a second, she just looks at it, delight and disbelief written all over her face.

    “Carol—”

    “You said you weren’t sure about your other dress,” Carol says quickly, already half-defensive. “It’s not a big deal—”

    “Yes, it is,” Zosia cuts in gently.

    Carol falters.

    Zosia steps a little closer, still holding the dress, her voice softer now as her hand finds Carol’s arm. “You’re already doing so much, and you paid for the party… you didn’t have to do this too.”

    Carol shrugs, trying for nonchalance and not quite landing it. “It’s nothing.”

    Zosia shakes her head, smiling in a way that says she doesn’t believe that for a second.

    “It’s not nothing.”

    Carol looks away slightly, her cheeks flushed as the intensity of the way Zosia is looking at her grows too much. There’s a brief pause where neither woman says anything.

    Then Zosia leans in close, quick enough that Carol doesn’t have time to react, and presses a soft kiss to her cheek.

    It’s quick.

    Light.

    But it might as well be a sledgehammer to the face the way it hits Carol. She goes completely still, fighting the urge to turn and meet Zosia’s lips.

    When Zosia pulls back, she’s beaming, already stepping away toward the door. “Thank you.”

    Carol blinks, a little dazed, her hand lifting halfway like she might touch the spot, then dropping again. “Yeah. Sure. Of course.”

    Outside, a car pulls up to the curb.

    “My Uber,” Zosia says, slipping her shoes on quickly.

    She turns back at the door, pausing to take in Carol’s bewildered expression. She bites down on her lip to keep from grinning. “Are you still picking me up at twelve-thirty? Please try not to be late today.”

    Carol finds her voice, something like her usual dry tone slipping back into place in an attempt to mask the inner turmoil she feels. “I’m never late,” she says, grateful her voice doesn’t crack.

    Zosia grins at that. “We’ll see.”

    And then she’s gone, the door closing behind her, leaving Carol standing there in the quiet house, her cheek still warm from Zosia’s lips.

    Chapter End Notes

    As ever, thank you for reading and for the amazing response to this fic. I'm hoping to have the next chapter up by Saturday.

    - Tan.

    Chapter 17

    Chapter Notes

    Carol stands in front of the mirror longer than she means to.

    She tilts her head slightly, studying herself from one angle, then the other, her fingers lifting to adjust a piece of hair that’s already exactly where it should be. It springs back into place anyway, soft and deliberate.

    Her makeup is subtle. Intentionally so.

    A little more than she’d usually bother with, but not enough to look like she’s trying too hard. Or at least—that’s the idea. She leans in closer, eyes narrowing as she checks the blend along her cheekbones, the faint smoky shimmer at the corners of her eyes.

    Her gaze drops to her top. Pink chiffon. Sleeveless. The cowl neck drapes softly, lower than she’s used to. She tugs at it instinctively, pulling the fabric up a fraction, then letting it fall back into place.

    ‘Too much?’ She thinks.

    She shifts back, smoothing her hands over her black slacks, pressing out creases that don’t exist. Her low heels lift slightly off the floor as she adjusts her stance, testing her balance, her posture.

    Too much.

    Or—

    Not enough.

    The thought lands sharp and unwelcome as she remembers Zosia in that navy dress.

    The way it had fit her. The way it had moved when she unfolded it from the bag, the quiet delight on her face, the way her eyes had lit up—

    Carol exhales sharply.

    That’s enough of that.

    She grabs her purse from the dresser with more force than necessary as she does her best to block out those kind of thoughts about her new friend, and heads for the stairs.

    Her heels click-clack against the hardwood floor, quick and decisive as she makes her way down, out the front door, and straight across the street without slowing.

    She rings the doorbell and keeps her finger pressed firmly against it.

    Once.

    Twice.

    A third time, just for good measure.

    The door swings open moments later. Manousos stands there, looking flustered and halfway dressed—crisp white shirt buttoned up, collar open, and one cuff neatly fastened while the other hangs loose at his wrist.

    His eyes flick over her, slow and assessing. “You look nice.”

    Carol exhales, the tension in her shoulders loosening instantly. “Thanks. I needed a second opinion,” she says, gesturing vaguely at herself. “On the outfit.”

    Manousos gives her a long, hard stare and asks, “Why?”

    “Because—” She cuts herself off, narrows her eyes as she realises what he’s insinuating. “Not because you’re gay.”

    He lifts a brow.

    “I needed a second opinion, and knocking on the Johnsons’ door and asking them would be fucking weird, okay?” she adds, tone matter-of-fact.

    “This is true,” Manousos agrees in his usual deadpan tone, the hint of a smile on his lips.

    Carol ignores his obvious attempt to bait her. “Are you nearly ready? I can drop you at the restaurant on the way to get Zosia, but you’ll have to be quick. I can’t be late picking her up. The kid will be in college before she lets me hear the end of it.”

    Manousos bites back a smirk. “I am almost ready,” he says, fiddling with his last cuff. “Two minutes. I just need to get the gift.”

    Carol freezes.

    “The what?”

    Manousos looks at her. “The… gift,” he repeats slowly, like he’s unsure if he’s using the right word.

    “They’re—we’re supposed to bring gifts?” Her voice pitches slightly, the calm cracking. “Nobody told me that. Why didn’t anybody tell me that?”

    He watches her for a moment, eyes wide and panicked, then says, in that same even tone, “Well, you already gave her a baby. I do not think anyone will top that.”

    Carol blinks at him, then huffs out a short, disbelieving laugh despite herself. “That’s not funny, Manny.”

    “It was a little funny.” He grins.

    Carol shakes her head, already pacing a step to the side and back again, anxiety creeping in fast. “This is a big deal for Zosia. It has to be right. Everything has to—” She gestures helplessly. “It has to go smoothly.”

    “It will,” he says simply.

    She stops pacing, looking at him.

    “Koumba has been at the restaurant since early this morning,” he adds. “Setting up.”

    Carol frowns. “How do you know that?”

    Manousos shrugs, “He called me.”

    She blinks again. “Since when do you have Koumba’s number?”

    Manousos tilts his head slightly. “Since you decided I was your errand boy.”

    A smile tugs at her mouth despite the nerves. “I prefer the term valued friend.” She turns, already backing away. “Anyway, I just need to grab my purse—I mean, I have my purse, I need my—keys. I need my keys.”

    “They are in your hand,” Manousos points out.

    Carol looks down.

    They are.

    “Right.” She exhales, a little embarrassed. “Just—meet me at the car. Two minutes.”

    He nods once.

    “I’ll tell Koumba we’re on our way,” he says, already reaching for his phone.

    “You do that,” Carol replies, turning and heading back across the street, her heels clicking faster now, her pulse just a little too quick for something that is, in theory, supposed to be a celebration.

    It has to be perfect.

    It has to.

    For Zosia.

    -



    The drive to Per Se is quick.

    Too quick for Carol’s liking.

    The roads are quiet, the late Sunday morning lull settling over the city like a held breath. Traffic lights turn green as Carol approaches them, one after the other, as if the world is clearing a path for her whether she’s ready or not.

    She isn’t.

    Her fingers tap lightly against the steering wheel, restless, her gaze flicking more often than necessary to the clock on the dashboard.

    Twenty-eight minutes until she has to pick Zosia up.

    Plenty of time.

    Still—

    She exhales, pulling into the parking lot and cutting the engine. For a second, she just sits there, hands still on the wheel, her mind catching up with her body. The kiss from this morning lingers on her mind, just like it did on her cheek.

    She pushes the thought aside as she kills the engine and gets out of the truck, her purse slung over her shoulder. Manousos follows after her, his posture as composed as ever, though there’s a tightness to it Carol doesn’t miss as they approach the front door of the restaurant.

    “Ready?” he asks.

    Carol nods once. “Let’s see what we’re working with.”

    Inside, the transformation is immediate. Per Se barely resembles itself. There’s blue and pink everywhere—soft pastels woven into something almost theatrical.

    A balloon arch frames the entrance, swaying slightly as the door closes behind them. Several tables have been cleared, pushed back to make space for guests already beginning to gather, drinks in hand, voices low but animated. Those closest to the door greet Carol as she and Manousos walk in.

    At the far end, a buffet table stretches along the wall, laden with food—too much food, probably, for the two dozen or so guests. It all looks great, though. Carol would expect nothing less from Zosia’s cooks.

    There’s a sound system set up near the bar, wires neatly tucked away.

    It all looks… good.

    Better than good.

    Carol’s chest loosens slightly. It’s one weight off her mind at least. “Okay,” she murmurs. “This works.”

    “It does,” Manousos agrees quietly.

    Guests nod at her as she passes—staff from the restaurant, mostly, all familiar faces. There’s an easy warmth to it, casual and unforced. Carol has become a regular face there over the last few months.

    “Carol!” Koumba appears like he’s been waiting for his cue.

    He crosses the room quickly, already smiling, already opening his arms. He’s dressed sharply in pressed grey slacks and a fitted yellow shirt that sits just right across his shoulders. A heavy gold chain peaks out from under his open collar. When he pulls her into a hug, the scent of his cologne hits her immediately—expensive and deliberate.

    “Look at you,” he says, pulling back just enough to take her in. “You look stunning.”

    Carol huffs lightly, brushing it off. “This old thing? I had it hanging at the back of the closet,” she lies, like she didn’t just buy the top this week. Like she didn’t spend hours debating between it and a simpler, more modest, blouse.

    “It is lovely,” Koumba says, eyes glinting with the soft overhead lights, “Very flattering… and pink.”

    Carol blinks, not quite following.

    “Zosia is wearing blue,” he adds, like it’s obvious. “You two are very adorable.”

    Carol’s stomach drops as she realises they’re going to match the decorations. “I—what?”

    Koumba tilts his head, amused. “This wasn’t planned?”

    “No,” Carol says quickly. Too quickly. “No, it wasn’t—”

    Her mind starts racing.

    Pink.

    Blue.

    Of course that’s what it’s going to looks like.

    People are going to notice. They’re going to read into it. They’re going to assume—

    Zosia.

    Oh, God.

    What if she thinks—

    Koumba’s hand finds her shoulder, his touch grounding. “You worry too much,” He says lightly, like he can see her spiralling in real time. “It is charming, but completely unnecessary. Zosia will love it.”

    Carol narrows her eyes at him, not entirely reassured.

    His reassuring smile just grows wider.

    “I saw the dress, by the way,” he adds, eyes sparkling. “Very nice. You have excellent taste.”

    Carol presses her lips together, something close to satisfaction flickering beneath the hum of anxiety she feels.

    Koumba turns his attention to Manousos next, pulling him into a hug just as easily. Manousos accepts it, but there’s a stiffness to him—his arms coming up a fraction too late, his posture not quite settling into it. When Koumba pulls back, there’s a faint flush high on his cheeks.

    Carol notices. She doesn’t comment, but files it away for later. Maybe she’ll ask Manousos about it once he’s had a drink or two. For now, she gestures lightly around the room. “It looks great in here.”

    Koumba beams. “Thank you! All the staff helped.” He gestures toward the back. “Come. You have to see the cake!”

    “That’s what I’m most concerned about,” Carol mutters, following him toward the kitchen.

    “Have faith,” Koumba says over his shoulder. “It made it back in one piece. Manousos was very diligent.” There’s a thread of teasing in it as he catches Manousos’ eyes, and the man’s flush deepens.

    Carol glances at him, one brow lifting slightly, but he avoids her gaze, looking straight ahead like she doesn’t exist.

    The kitchen is quieter than the restaurant floor, and cooler. It’s the first time Carol’s been in there without it bustling with people and activity. The burners lie dormant, the pots and pans quiet and still. The warring scent of lemon cleaner and spices hangs heavy in the air.

    And there it is. The cake sits on a metal serving trolley, patient and pristine.

    The sight of it stops Carol in her tracks. “Oh, wow.”

    It’s… beautiful.

    The detailing is delicate, precise—far better in person than the picture Layla sent her. The colours are soft but rich, the finish immaculate.

    “Okay,” she says slowly. “That’s—”

    “Impressive?” Koumba supplies.

    “Yes.”

    Her gaze catches on a small folded card resting beside it. She picks it up, unfolding it carefully.

    ‘Zosia, congratulations and good luck in your next chapter. Love, Layla.’

    Carol smiles faintly. “She’s going to love this,” she says, more to herself than anyone else. The cake and the decorations are perfect, but Carol can’t help the flicker of doubt she feels. Is it enough?

    “Do you guys think I should’ve gone with the crop duster?” she asks, half-serious. “Or the fireworks?”

    “No,” Koumba and Manousos say at the same time.

    Carol looks between them.

    Slowly.

    Suspicious.

    “When did you two become a double act?”

    Manousos shifts, clearly uncomfortable, his gaze dropping again. Koumba, on the other hand, looks delighted.

    “Our little road trip was quite enlightening,” he says smoothly. “Manousos and I have much in common.”

    Carol makes a quiet noise of scepticism. Then she checks her watch. “Shit. I have to get going,” she says, already stepping back towards the double doors. “I need to pick Zosia up.”

    Koumba nods. “Of course.”

    “I’ll have her back here for one,” Carol adds, more to herself than anyone else.

    Manousos inclines his head. “Drive safe.”

    “Always,” Carol says, already moving, her mind shifting gears again—away from the cake and the decorations, and from Koumba’s knowing looks.

    Back to Zosia.

    Always, somehow, back to Zosia.

     

    -

     

     

    Carol pulls up outside Zosia’s duplex at exactly 12:28. She glances at the clock on the dash, a small, satisfied smile tugging at her mouth.

    Two minutes early.

    Perfect.

    She kills the engine, grabs her keys, and heads up to the door, already rehearsing how she’ll gloat about it in her head—something dry, something understated. Nothing too smug, just enough to make a point.

    She lifts her hand and presses the doorbell. It takes a moment for Zosia to answer. When she does, whatever Carol was going to say disappears from her mind completely.

    Zosia stands there in the navy dress.

    It fits her like it was made for her—soft fabric skimming over her body, the cut flattering without trying too hard. Her hair is done, lightly curled and falling effortlessly around her shoulders. Her makeup subtle but deliberate.

    Carol forgets how to think. For a second, she just stands there, gawping.

    Zosia’s mouth curves slowly, knowingly. She leans lightly against the door-frame, eyes bright with amusement as she takes in Carol’s reaction.

    “Hi, Carol.” she says, her lips wrapping around the other woman’s name with a sense of comforting familiarity.

    Carol blinks hard, dragging herself back into her body. “H-hey.” She manages to squeeze out.

    Zosia steps back just enough to give herself space, then does a small turn on the spot, the skirt of the dress moving gently with her. “Well?” she asks, glancing over her shoulder. “What do you think?”

    Carol exhales under her breath, something like a laugh caught in it. “Best two hundred dollars I’ve ever spent.”

    Zosia grins, looking pleased with herself as she winks at Carol. “Good answer.”

    Carol shakes her head slightly, still looking at the other woman with hunger in her eyes. “You look—” She stops, reconsiders whatever she was about to say. “Yeah. It works.”

    Zosia huffs a soft laugh at that, clearly aware of the effect she’s having on the other woman. She turns to glance at the clock hanging on the wall in the hallway behind her, then quickly back to Carol again. “You’re early,” she says, eyebrows lifting. “I’m impressed.”

    Carol recovers enough to smirk. “I can be on time. Occasionally.”

    “Let’s just hope you’re on time when it counts,” she teases, a hand at her growing stomach as she steps aside to let Carol in.

    Carol moves inside, hovering in the hallway. Her eyes betray her immediately as they flick back to Zosia before she can stop herself. The younger woman fusses at her hair in the mirror by the door. “Ready to go?” she asks, hoping Zosia didn’t notice her staring as she straightens again. “We need to pick Koumba up on the way. He’s at the restaurant.”

    Zosia’s brow furrows at that. “Why is Koumba at the restaurant?”

    Carol shrugs, trying for casual. “I dunno. Something about a delivery, I think.”

    Zosia sees right through her. “But we’re closed for the party. Everyone has the day off.”

    “Uh, I didn’t really ask,” Carol says lightly. “He just said to grab him on the way.”

    Zosia studies her for a second, suspicion flickering briefly across her face. “That’s… odd.”

    Carol doesn’t give her suspicion time to grow. “We should go,” she says, glancing at her watch. “If we’re late, it’s on you. And I have a reputation to uphold.”

    Zosia narrows her eyes slightly, like she knows she’s being steered away from something, but lets it go—for now. “All ready,” she says.

    Stepping closer, she brushes past Carol as she reaches for her keys on a hook on the wall behind her, her fingers grazing lightly over Carol’s arm in the process.

    It’s nothing.

    Casual.

    Unthinking.

    Carol feels it anyway—sharp and immediate. Her mind flashes, unhelpfully and vividly, to earlier that morning. The press of Zosia’s lips against her cheek.

    She goes still for half a second, her body freezing up at the slightest touch. Zosia doesn’t seem to notice—or maybe she does. Maybe she’s waiting for Carol to acknowledge it.

    “Ready?” she asks, turning back to Carol, all soft edges and quiet anticipation.

    Carol clears her throat, pushing the thought down, locking it away where it can’t do any damage. “Yeah,” she says, a little shakily, feeling like she’s standing on the edge of a cliff, one foot already hovering off the ground. “Let’s go.”


    -

     

    The restaurant looks nothing like it had the last time Zosia stepped foot in it.

    Soft pink and blue balloons arc over the entrance, twisting together in a careful braid that frames the doorway. There are fairy lights strung along the windows, flickering warmly against the glass, and the low hum of conversation spills out onto the street before the door even opens.

    Zosia slows on the threshold.

    “What…” she breathes, eyes widening as she takes it all in.

    Carol, beside her, tries very hard to look casual about it. Like this isn’t the culmination of weeks of planning. “Surprise?” she offers, lifting one shoulder.

    Zosia steps inside like she’s afraid the whole thing might vanish if she moves too quickly. The restaurant is already full—staff, friends, even a few regulars who must have been tipped off. The tables have been pushed back to make space, clusters of people gathered with drinks in hand, all turning toward her as the door opens.

    A ripple of applause starts—tentative at first, then growing as the guests realise she’s there.

    Zosia laughs, a hand flying to her mouth as if to contain it. “Oh my God.”

    Her other hand finds Carol’s arm without thinking—fingers curling on the inside of her wrist, grounding and warm.

    “How did you—” She shakes her head, still scanning the room, taking in every detail. “The decorations… how did you pull all of this off without me knowing?”

    Carol huffs out a quiet breath, rubbing the back of her neck. “Took a village,” she admits. “Koumba was a big help.”

    Zosia’s gaze flicks toward the bar instinctively, like she expects to find him already watching. “Is that so?”

    “And,” Carol adds, unable to resist, “I had to keep you away from here all day yesterday. Which, by the way, was not easy. You’re kind of a control freak. Did you know that?” She grins.

    Zosia lets out a soft, incredulous laugh, her hand still resting on Carol’s arm. “That explains it then.”

    “Explains what?”

    “You acting so weird yesterday,” Zosia says, turning her head slightly to look at her, eyes bright with amusement. “All the vague excuses for why you wanted to keep me around. I thought you were hiding something.”

    Carol forces a laugh.

    If only she knew.

    “Yeah, well,” she shrugs, aiming for flippant and landing somewhere just shy of it, “turns out I was.”

    Zosia hums, clearly entertained, but there’s something softer in her expression too—something that lingers just a beat too long before she looks away again, back to the room.

    Before Carol can overthink it, a familiar voice cuts through the noise.

    “Zosia!”

    Koumba appears like he’s been waiting for his cue, weaving through the crowd with Manousos trailing a step behind him. He reaches her in seconds, pulling her into a tight hug without hesitation.

    “You look wonderful,” he says warmly, pressing a quick kiss to her temple.

    Zosia melts into it for a moment, laughing softly. “Thank you,” she says. “And for helping Carol. This…” she gestures around them, still taking it all in, “this is incredible.”

    Koumba’s grin softens as he makes eye contact with Carol. “It’s all Carol’s doing. I simply did as I was told.”

    Manousos steps forward then, offering Zosia an easy smile. “It’s good to see you again.”

    “You too,” Zosia replies warmly. “Were you roped into this as well?”

    Manousos glances briefly at Carol, then back to Zosia. “I was… recruited.”

    “Well, thank you.” Zosia beams at him, her hand still on Carol’s arm.

    Koumba claps his hands together gleefully once the greetings are done with. “Right. Time for some drinks. What can I get everyone?”

    “Soda for me,” Zosia answers automatically.

    “Same,” Carol says quickly.

    Koumba looks at her like he’s deeply offended. “Just soda?”

    “Yeah, just soda,” Carol insists.

    “It is a celebration,” Koumba counters. “You cannot celebrate properly with soda! Have a glass of champagne with us, Carol.”

    “I can celebrate just fine with soda,” Carol shoots back. “I’m driving.”

    Zosia’s hand shifts—no longer gripping her arm, but sliding lightly to the small of Carol’s back. The touch is subtle, but it sends a spark straight up Carol’s spine.

    “One won’t hurt,” Zosia murmurs, leaning in just enough that her voice doesn’t carry. “You worked hard for today.”

    Carol’s breath catches, and for a second the noise of the room fades into nothing. “Okay,” she says, quieter than she means to. “Yeah. Okay.”

    Koumba’s face lights up. “Excellent! I knew you would see reason.” He winks, before gesturing theatrically to Manousos beside him. “Come, let’s get these beautiful ladies some drinks!”

    Manousos follows with a small shake of his head, but there’s a hint of a smile tugging at his mouth as they disappear toward the bar.

    Zosia watches them go, then glances sideways at Carol. “When did those two even meet?”

    Carol snorts softly. “Long story.”

    “I have time.”

    “I’ll tell you later,” Carol says, waving it off. She doesn’t want to spoil the surprise of the cake.

    Zosia huffs out a quiet laugh, but relents.

    Carol hesitates, then adds, unable to resist, “Although, apparently you and Manousos have your own thing going. Messaging each other behind my back...” She arches a brow. “Maybe you should just add Koumba to the group chat at this point?”

    Zosia turns to her fully now, one eyebrow lifting. There’s a glint of something in her eyes—amusement, yes, but something sharper underneath it.

    “We were messaging,” she says evenly, “because we both care about you.”

    Carol opens her mouth to argue, then closes it again.

    “Whether you like that or not,” Zosia finishes, a faint smirk tugging at her lips.

    Carol huffs, aware of the searing heat quickly spreading across her cheeks. “I never said I didn’t like it.”

    “No?” Zosia’s smile widens just slightly.

    “No,” Carol mutters, a little sheepish now.

    They’re standing closer than they were a second ago. Carol’s not sure when that happened. Zosia’s hand is still at her back, light but steady, and Carol finds herself leaning into it without a care who sees.

    Zosia doesn’t pull away.

    Across the room, Koumba pauses mid-step, a tray of drinks in his hands. His gaze flicks between the two women, and he smiles to himself before turning to Manousos, excitedly asking, “Should we get shots?”

    “No.” Manousos deadpans. “Absolutely not.”

     

    -



    An hour into the party, Koumba stands at the front of the room, already gathering attention like it’s second nature. He claps his hands once—sharp, commanding—and the low hum of conversation begins to taper off almost immediately.

    “If I could have everyone’s attention, please?” he calls, his voice carrying easily over the room.

    A few stragglers take an extra second to quiet, but the energy shifts. People turn. Glasses pause mid-air. Conversations trail off into expectant silence.

    Carol’s stomach drops. “Oh, here we go,” she mutters under her breath.

    Zosia glances at her, amused. “You say that like you’re not the one who orchestrated all of this.”

    “Not this part,” Carol says quickly. “This is all him.”

    “We are here today for a joyous occasion. Not only to celebrate Zosia’s pregnancy—” a ripple of applause breaks out again, warm and immediate “—but also to find out whether I will be getting a niece or a nephew to spoil rotten.”

    Zosia stills beside Carol. There’s a subtle change in her posture, the way her hand instinctively comes to rest over her stomach—protective and grounded as another round of applause goes out around them.

    It all feels so much more real now.

    “And,” Koumba adds, dragging the moment out just enough to be annoying, “I believe we have something prepared.”

    He turns, gesturing toward the kitchen with a flourish. There’s a small beat, and then Kusimayu and T’ika appear, pushing the trolley out with careful precision. The cake sits at the centre.

    The detailing is immaculate—soft ivory frosting, delicate piping, accents of baby blue and pastel pink that catch the light. It’s elegant without being overdone. Familiar, in a way that settles somewhere deep in Zosia’s chest before she can name why.

    Carol notices.

    “Layla says congratulations,” she says quietly, just for Zosia.

    Zosia’s breath catches.

    “Oh,” she murmurs, the significance of the cake—and its baker—hitting her hard. Blindly, she reaches out for Carol’s hand beside her, their fingers locking together like puzzle pieces.

    The trolley stops in front of them, and Kusimayu and T’ika step back once it’s in place. Koumba moves in beside it, practically vibrating with anticipation.

    “This,” he announces, “is how we will discover whether we are welcoming a future chef… or—”

    “Or still a future chef,” Carol cuts in dryly, and a ripple of laughter breaks out within the room.

    Koumba sighs dramatically. “Yes. Fine. Still a future chef.”

    Zosia huffs a soft laugh, shaking her head at her brother-in-law’s antics even as she runs the pad of her thumb across the inside of Carol’s wrist. The skin there is sensitive, and sens a little shudder through her.

    “Come,” Koumba says, gesturing Zosai forward. Suddenly all eyes are on them, and Carol drops the other woman’s hand like she’s been stung. “The guest of honour.”

    Zosia hesitates for half a second—just long enough for the weight of the moment to settle—then steps closer to the trolley. Carol hangs back. It’s instinct. Habit. That quiet, familiar step to the side when she doesn’t feel like she quite belongs.

    Zosia notices.

    Of course she does.

    “Thank you, Koumba,” she says, eyes scanning the crowd of familiar faces. “And thank you to all of you for being here to celebrate this little one with us today.” She rests both hands on her stomach, smiling as she feels the baby moving in response to her raised voice.

    “But, as many of you know, I would not be standing here today without one… very important woman.” She pauses, turns her head just enough. “Carol.”

    Carol stares back at her, cheeks burning, as another round of applause makes its way around the room. She suddenly feels like a bug under a magnifying glass.

    “Carol, would you please join me?” Zosia asks gently, her eyes soft and full of adoration. There’s no room to deflect it. No joke to hide behind. Carol exhales a shaky breath, then steps forward.

    She stops at Zosia’s side, close enough that she can feel the warmth of her, feel the faint brush of fabric against her bare arm when Zosia shifts. The room feels smaller now. Tighter.

    Too many eyes on them.

    Too much meaning in the way Zosia is looking at her.

    Koumba, blissfully unaware of Carol’s state of blind panic—or pretending to be—hands Zosia a large kitchen knife.

    “Whenever you are ready.”

    Zosia takes it from him, her fingers curling around the handle, steady and practiced. For a second, she just stands there, looking at the cake.

    Carol’s gaze drifts to Zosia’s other hand, resting on her stomach.

    Eight weeks: A heartbeat and a future she never planned for.

    Carol swallows hard. The room fades at the edges, and Helen’s face flickers in her memory—bright, warm, gone.

    The ultrasound: That impossible heartbeat, fast and insistent. Zosia in the hospital bed—Scared and alone.

    Carol’s chest tightens. Zosia had been little more than a stranger that first day—a stranger who was having her child. Carol could have walked away in the hospital, but Zosia had asked her not to. Had asked her to stay.

    ‘I don’t want to be alone.’

    “What if I don’t—” she starts without meaning to, too quiet for anyone else to hear.

    Zosia’s head turns slightly. “Hmm?”

    Carol shakes it off, suddenly aware of her surroundings again. Of the eyes of the whole room on the pair of them. “Nothing,” she says.

    Zosia isn’t convinced, though. Her free hand shifts from her stomach to Carol’s back. The touch is light, steady and soothing.

    “I’ve got you,” Zosia murmurs, barely audible—meant only for Carol’s ears. The older woman’s breath catches in her throat. All she can focus on is Zosia’s touch.

    Koumba claps his hands again, and the moment is broken. “All right! On three?”

    A few people join in. The energy spikes again, bright and anticipatory.

    “One—”

    Zosia adjusts her grip on the knife.

    “Two—”

    Carol finds herself leaning in, just slightly.

    “Three!”

    The knife slides down, cutting through the cake with ease.

    Clean.

    Precise.

    The blade cuts through the soft layers, frosting parting, sponge giving way—

    And then—

    A soft, unmistakable pink blooms through the centre.

    For a second, there’s only silence. Then the room erupts in cheers and applause. Someone near the back whistles. Zosia doesn’t move. Doesn’t react. She just stares at the newly revealed flash of colour.

    Pink.

    A girl.

    Her little girl.

    Zosia’s breath leaves her in a soft, shaky exhale, something fragile and full breaking open in her chest all at once. Her hand flies back to her stomach, pressing there instinctively.

    “A girl,” she whispers, the words barely making it past her lips—but Carol hears them anyway. She doesn’t look at the cake. She looks at Zosia. At the way her face has softened, eyes bright, something like awe written across it.

    And then something shifts inside of Carol.

    Quiet.

    Irreversible.

    Carol swallows, her throat tight. “A girl,” she echoes, softer.

    Zosia turns her head, and their eyes meet. For a second, the noise fades again. The room, the people, the celebration—all of it drops away, leaving just the two of them standing there, too close, breathing the same air.

    Koumba barrels in next, breaking the moment with zero subtlety as he wraps Zosia in a hug.

    “A niece!” he declares, delighted. “I’m going to have a niece!”

    Zosia laughs through it, breathless, her arms coming up around him automatically. “Koumba—”

    Manousos steps in after, quieter, offering a small, warm smile. “Congratulations,” he says.

    “Thank you,” Zosia replies, still a little dazed.

    The noise comes back all at once.

    It rushes in around them—laughter, voices overlapping, the scrape of chairs against the floor, Koumba already calling for more champagne. The moment fractures, dissolving into something louder, brighter, easier to look at than what just passed between them.

    Zosia is pulled into it almost immediately.

    Kusimayu is first, wrapping her in a tight hug that nearly knocks her off balance. T’ika follows, then Bob and his wife, all talking over one another. Someone presses a drink into her hand that she doesn’t remember asking for. Someone else kisses her cheek. There are congratulations in at least three different languages.

    Zosia laughs through it, a little breathless, a little overwhelmed, her hand never straying far from her stomach.

    Carol hangs back.

    She watches from the edge of the crowd, a half-finished glass of champagne in her hand, the bubbles long since settled. People brush past her without really seeing her, drawn toward Zosia like she’s the centre of gravity in the room.

    Which she is.

    Of course she is.

    Carol takes a slow sip of her drink, the taste sharp and dry on her tongue. She tells herself she’s fine here. That this is where she belongs—on the outskirts, observing, not quite part of it. That it’s Zosia’s day.

    “Hello, Carol.”

    Carol turns her head as Koumba appears at her side like he’s been tracking her movement through the room.

    “You are hiding,” he says, not unkindly.

    “I’m not hiding,” Carol replies, her tone defensive.

    Koumba hums, unconvinced. His gaze flicks toward Zosia, then back to Carol. “You should be over there. With her.”

    “I’m good here,” she says, lifting her glass slightly as if that proves anything.

    “Mm.” He takes a sip of his own drink, studying her over the rim. “You do not look good here.”

    Before Carol can come up with a retort, a voice cuts in from behind them.

    “Congratulations.”

    Carol turns.

    A woman she vaguely recognises—one of the waiting staff, mid-forties, warm smile, already a little flushed from the champagne—steps closer, her gaze flicking between Carol and Zosia on the other side of the room.

    “You must be so thrilled,” the woman continues, her tone bright and certain. “Both of you.”

    Carol freezes, feels the words land before she has time to process them.

    Both of you.

    Like the child is as much Carol’s as it is Zosia.

    Like it was something planned between a loving couple—and not a happy accident between two strangers.

    “I—” she starts, then stops. Not even sure where to begin.

    The woman smiles wider, oblivious. “And a girl! How wonderful. You two must be over the moon!”

    Carol’s grip tightens slightly around her glass. “It’s—yeah. It’s… great.” It comes out flatter than she intends.

    The woman nods, satisfied, already turning away to refill her drink, the moment passing for her as easily as it came.

    But it doesn’t pass for Carol.

    It sticks.

    Her gaze drifts back to Zosia. Zosia, who is still at the centre of it all, still glowing, still… happy.

    Carol exhales slowly. “I didn’t correct her,” she says, quieter now, aware Koumba is watching her with careful eyes.

    “No.” He says, but there’s no judgement to it.

    Carol bites the inside of her cheek. “I should have.”

    “Should you?” he asks mildly, head cocked to the side.

    Carol frowns at him, shakes her own head. “We’re not—”

    “No. You are not,” Koumba agrees easily, glancing toward Zosia again. “But you are also not nothing.”

    Carol doesn’t have a reply to that.

    Across the room, Zosia’s gaze lifts and finds Carol immediately. Like she already knew where to look. There’s a flicker of something in her expression—something searching, something that softens when she sees Carol standing there with Koumba.

    She disentangles herself from the group. “Excuse me,” Zosia says, though no one is really stopping her.

    She crosses the room with quiet purpose, weaving through people until she’s standing in front of Carol again, close enough that the noise dulls just slightly around them.

    Koumba gives them both a look—one Carol can’t quite figure out—before excusing himself and joining Manousos back at the bar.

    “You disappeared on me,” Zosia says once they’re alone, her tone light but her eyes sharp.

    “I was right here,” Carol replies, lifting her glass in a small, defensive gesture.

    Zosia glances at it, then back at her. “Mm.”

    Carol shifts her weight from one foot to the other. “You were busy,” she says.

    “You could have joined me,” Zosia replies.

    Carol huffs softly. “I didn’t want to intrude.”

    Zosia doesn’t argue. Instead, her hand comes up—hesitant for a fraction of a second before settling lightly at Carol’s wrist. Her fingers finding the same sensitive patch of skin as before.

    Familiar now.

    “You okay?” she asks, quieter.

    Carol opens her mouth to deflect—maybe crack a joke instead—but stops. Zosia is looking at her like she expects a real answer.

    “Yeah,” Carol says, softer than she means to. “Yeah, I’m good. This is just… a lot.”

    Zosia nods, like she understands exactly what she means. “You can leave,” she says gently. “If it’s too much. I won’t—”

    “I don’t want to leave,” Carol cuts in, quicker than she intends. She exhales, running a hand through her hair. “I mean—I’m fine. I just…” She trails off, searching for something that doesn’t sound like a confession.

    Zosia’s grip tightens slightly. “You don’t have to justify being here, Carol.”

    “I’m not—”

    “You are,” Zosia says, a small smile tugging at her mouth. “But you don’t have to. I want you here.”

    Carol looks at her. Really looks. There’s something steady in Zosia’s expression. Something patient.

    Carol swallows. “I want to be here.”

    Zosia’s smile softens. “Good,” she murmurs, nodding to herself like it’s what she’s been waiting to hear. The space between them narrows, almost imperceptibly.

    Then—

    “Zosia!”

    The moment fractures again as Kusimayu calls her from across the room, waving her over to where she stands with an older couple. Zosia looks between the younger woman and Carol.

    “I’ll be right back,” she says.

    Carol nods. “Yeah. Go.”

    Zosia hesitates—just for a second—then her hand slides from Carol’s wrist, trailing briefly over her fingers before she turns and disappears back into the crowd.

    Carol’s chest tightens at the sudden loss.

    Before she can think better of it, she pushes off from the wall.

    Follows.

    This time, she doesn’t linger on the outside. She steps in beside Zosia, close enough that their arms brush.

    Zosia glances at her, surprised—then pleased. A slow smile spreading across her lips as she introduces Carol to the couple she’s talking to.

    Carol pretends not to notice the way her heart skips when her hip brushes against Zosia’s.

    But she doesn’t step away.

    And Zosia doesn’t ask her to.

     

    -

     

     

    The party doesn’t end so much as it loosens.

    The sharp edge of it—the cheers, the clustering, the constant movement—softens into something slower, more diffuse. People drift instead of gathering. Conversations stretch out instead of overlapping. The music dips low enough to sit beneath everything rather than on top of it.

    Zosia is still the centre of it all.

    She’s seated now, finally, one of the dining chairs pulled out for her near the edge of the room, her posture relaxed in a way it hadn’t been earlier. There’s a glass of lemonade in her hand, mostly untouched, condensation gathering at the sides. People still come and go, pausing to speak to her, to touch her shoulder, to rest a hand briefly over hers as they offer congratulations again.

    Carol watches from across the room. She leans against the edge of the bar, her fingers loosely wrapped around another glass of champagne she doesn’t remember being given. The bubbles have long since faded. She hasn’t noticed. Hasn’t even touched it.

    “She is going to be exhausted,” Koumba says, speaking Carol’s own thought out loud as he appears beside her, propping his elbows against the bar.

    Carol doesn’t look at him. “Yeah,” she agrees.

    “You should take her home soon.”

    Carol’s gaze flicks back to Zosia automatically. She’s laughing at something Kusimayu says, her head tipping back slightly, one hand resting protectively over her stomach without thinking.

    “Yeah,” Carol repeats, quieter this time. Her mind is a million miles away, but her thoughts are still of Zosia.

    Koumba studies her for a moment, then nods once, satisfied with what he thinks he sees. “Good.”

    Carol frowns slightly, finally looking back at him. “Good what?”

    “You are thinking about it,” he says simply, offering no elaboration.

    “I’m thinking she looks tired.”

    “Yes,” Koumba replies, a faint smile tugging at his mouth. “That is what I said.”

    Carol narrows her eyes at him. He’s making even less sense than usual, but before she can push it further, he’s already stepping away, pulled into another conversation.

    She doesn’t linger long after that.

    By the time she crosses the room, Zosia is mid-conversation again, but she notices Carol immediately, her expression shifting in that quiet way it does—something softening, something settling.

    “You look like you’re planning something,” Zosia says with a warm smile as Carol comes to a stop beside her.

    “I’m planning to get you out of here,” Carol replies. “You look like you’re about ten minutes away from falling asleep in that chair.”

    Zosia huffs a small laugh. “I am not.”

    “You are,” Carol insists. “And if you’re not now, you will be.”

    Zosia glances down at her drink, then back up at Carol. “You’re particularly bossy today.”

    “Sorry, I know that’s usually your thing,” Carol smirks.

    Zosia smiles at that, faint but warm. Then she sighs, a soft exhale that seems to settle somewhere deep in her chest. “Okay,” she concedes. “Maybe I am a little tired.”

    Carol nods. “Good. Let’s get you home and out of those shoes.”

    Zosia pushes herself up slowly, steadying herself with one hand against the table. Carol’s hand moves without thinking, hovering at her elbow, then settling lightly there as Zosia straightens.

    It’s automatic.

    Zosia lets herself be guided just slightly as they make their way toward the door, pausing only long enough to say a few quick goodbyes, to accept one last round of congratulations, and to ask Koumba to bring the gifts home with him later.

    Outside, the air is cooler, quieter. The door swings shut behind them, muffling the last of the noise into something distant and indistinct.

    For a moment, they just stand there.

    Zosia exhales slowly, her shoulders dropping as the tension of the room leaves her. “That was…” She trails off, searching.

    “A lot,” Carol supplies helpfully.

    Zosia glances at her, amused. “Yes, but also better than I could have imagined. Thank you.”

    Carol blushes. She doesn’t do well with praise. “Well, you deserved nothing less,” she replies, shifting her weight, her hand still resting lightly at Zosia’s back.

    “Come on,” she says after a second. “Let’s get you home.”

    The drive is quiet at first.

    The city moves around them in that slow, early evening way, traffic light but steady, the hum of the engine filling the space between them. Carol keeps her eyes on the road, her hands steady on the wheel, her posture composed in a way that feels almost deliberate.

    Zosia leans back into the passenger seat, one hand resting over her stomach, the other curled loosely in her lap. She watches the street for a moment, then lets her gaze drift—across the dashboard, the windshield, and finally to Carol.

    “You really did all of that,” she says after a while. “For me.”

    Carol doesn’t look at her. “It wasn’t just me.”

    Zosia hums softly. “You keep saying that.”

    “It’s true,” Carol replies. “Koumba basically strong-armed half the guest list.”

    “And the decorations?”

    Carol shrugs. “Team effort.”

    Zosia tilts her head slightly, studying her. “And the cake?”

    Carol hesitates, just for a fraction of a second. “That was Layla,” she says. “Koumba… he told me how much it would mean to you.”

    Zosia’s expression softens. “Did he tell you she made my wedding cake?” There’s something in her face as she asks this—something quiet and steady that lingers longer than the moment calls for.

    “Yeah.” Carol exhales lightly. She doesn’t know why, but the whole conversation feels like she’s tiptoeing through a minefield. “Yeah, he did.”

    Zosia’s eyes widen, and Carol pretends not to notice them watering. “Thank you, Carol. Truly.”

    “Zosia, you don’t have to keep thanking me.” Carol tries for flippant, but it doesn’t stop her heart from hammering in her chest at the way Zosia looks at her.

    Like Carol hung the stars and the moon just for her.

    “I do,” Zosia cuts in, not sharply, just firmly enough that it lands. “You didn’t have to do any of that, Carol.”

    Carol glances at her then, briefly, before looking back at the road. It feels dangerous to let her eyes linger. “I wanted to.”

    The words settle between them.

    Simple.

    Uncomplicated.

    True.

    Zosia leans back slightly, absorbing Carol’s admission. Her hand shifts over her stomach, absent and protective all at once. “I’m glad you were there,” she says.

    Carol glances at her again, longer this time. Consequences be damned. “Yeah,” she says quietly. “Me too.”

    They drive in comfortable silence after that, Zosia’s eyes closing more than once as the day catches up with her. They’re still closed as Carol pulls up outside Zosia’s duplex and cuts the engine.

    Zosia shifts slightly in her seat, eyes opening slowly, her hand still resting over her stomach. Her gaze drops briefly to it before lifting again, settling on Carol.

    Her smile is warm, open, as she turns more fully toward her, one leg angled slightly in Carol’s direction. “Thank you,” she says again, quieter, more deliberate.

    Carol shakes her head lightly. “You don’t have to—”

    “I do,” Zosia repeats, her voice softer now, but no less certain. “I’m not just talking about the party. Thank you for…” She gestures vaguely between them. “Everything. For sticking with me through all of this.”

    She reaches across, her hand finding Carol’s thigh.

    Carol’s breath catches in her throat. The space between them feels smaller in the confined quiet of the car, the distance measured in inches.

    “Your support means more to me than you know,” Zosia says, the words gentle but firm, like she’s placing them carefully so they can’t be ignored. “You mean more to me.”

    Something in Carol’s chest shifts.

    Deep.

    Unsteady.

    Her hand loosens on the steering wheel, fingers flexing once before she lets them fall away completely, her breath catching somewhere halfway in her lungs. “Zosia—”

    Zosia’s hand moves first. It’s not sudden or impulsive. She moves much the same way a lion tamer might—slow and deliberate. It’s the same movement she’s made all day, familiar now, practiced in its softness.

    Her fingers find the inside of Carol’s wrist, light enough that it could still be nothing—if Carol wanted it to be. Warm. Steady. Waiting.

    Carol goes completely still.

    Every instinct she has tells her to pull back, to put space between them, to say something that makes this smaller, safer, easier to step away from.

    She doesn’t.
