    Zosia watches her closely, her gaze moving over Carol’s face, searching for hesitation, for doubt—for any sign she’s crossed a line that can’t be uncrossed.

    Carol doesn’t give her one.

    But she turns—just slightly—her body angling toward Zosia like it’s instinct, like it’s something she’s already decided without realizing it.

    It’s enough.

    Zosia’s thumb shifts against her wrist, a small, grounding motion, and then she leans in.

    Slow.

    Deliberate.

    Not closing the distance all at once—just enough to feel the change in air between them, the warmth, the pull of it. Close enough that Carol could stop it. Close enough that she would have to choose not to.

    Carol doesn’t move.

    Her breath stutters, her gaze dropping for half a second—to Zosia’s mouth, then back to her eyes—and that’s the moment everything tips.

    The rest of the distance disappears.

    Their lips meet, soft and uncertain at first, the contact so light it almost feels imagined.

    Carol exhales against her, a quiet, involuntary sound escaping her as something inside her gives way, her hand lifting without thought, hovering for the briefest second before it finds Zosia’s jaw. Her fingers curl there, tentative, like she’s not entirely sure she’s allowed to.

    Zosia leans into the touch.

    The kiss deepens—not with urgency, not with anything rushed or overwhelming—but with a slow, deliberate certainty, like they’re both discovering the shape of it at the same time. The angle shifts, the contact steadies, and the hesitation gives way to something warmer, something that lingers.

    When they part, it’s gradual.

    Zosia doesn’t pull away so much as she eases back just enough to breathe, her forehead nearly brushing Carol’s, her breath still warm against her cheek. Her eyes stay closed for a moment longer, like she’s holding onto the feeling of it before letting it go.

    Carol doesn’t move.

    She’s not sure she can.

    Her hand is still at Zosia’s jaw, her thumb just barely brushing against her skin, like she’s afraid if she lets go too quickly the other woman might disappear in a puff of smoke.

    The world outside the car continues on without them—distant, irrelevant.

    Zosia exhales softly, something like a laugh caught in it, quiet and disbelieving. “Okay,” she murmurs, like she’s acknowledging something that’s just shifted beyond undoing.

    Carol lets out a shaky breath she hadn’t realised she was holding, her chest tight in a way that has nothing to do with panic and everything to do with the absence of it. “Yeah,” she says quietly, her voice softer than she means it to be.

    Zosia dips her head, her hand slipping away from Carol’s wrist as she tucks a loose strand of hair behind her ear, the movement suddenly smaller, more uncertain. Her gaze drops—briefly—to Carol’s mouth again before flicking back up, something hesitant threading through the warmth now.

    They’re still too close.

    Neither of them moves to fix it.

    “Do you—” Zosia starts, then stops, her breath catching slightly as she recalibrates. “Would you like to come inside?”

    Carol blinks, her mind scrambling to catch up—to the kiss, to the shift, to the fact that there’s no easy way to pretend like this didn’t just happen.

    She could say no.

    She should probably say no.

    Put some space between it. Between them. Give herself time to think, to untangle whatever this is before it becomes something she can’t step back from.

    But she doesn’t.

    Her gaze drops, just briefly, to Zosia’s mouth before lifting again. “Yes,” she says, quieter now, but steadier than before. “Yeah, I’d like that.”

    Chapter End Notes

    We finally got there folks!

    As a quick side note, I'm not a fan of gender reveals IRL (it just worked as a plot device to build up to) and gender does not equal sex.

    As always, thanks for reading and for hanging in there during the very slow-burn.

    - Tan

    Chapter 18

    Chapter Notes

    Zosia’s house feels different when they step inside, like the walls have closed in just enough to hold everything that followed them in from the car.

    Zosia lingers by the door for a second after it shuts behind them, her hand still on the handle like she’s forgotten what she meant to do next. The light in the hallway is softer than the restaurant, warmer, catching in the loose strands of her hair, in the slight flush still high on her cheeks.

    “Do you—” she starts, then stops, her voice catching as she turns back to Carol. “Do you want some tea?”

    Carol shakes her head immediately. Her hands are at her sides, restlessly clenching and unclenching, grasping at nothing. “No. Thank you.”

    Her heart is still racing, not quite settled back into anything resembling normal. So loud in her own ears that she worries Zosia can hear it too.

    Zosia nods, like she expected her to refuse the tea. Then she moves, a little too quickly, stepping further into the house, already reaching for something that isn’t there. “Are you—are you hungry? I can—there’s food, I can get you something, or—”

    “Zosia—”

    She doesn’t stop. “There are some of your beers in the fridge—”

    Carol crosses the space between them before she can finish. Cuts her off the only way she knows how.

    The kiss lands harder this time.

    Less careful.

    All the restraint from earlier in the car gone in an instant.

    Zosia makes a soft, surprised sound against her mouth, but it dissolves just as quickly as she leans into it, her hands finding Carol without hesitation, gripping at her arms, her shoulders, pulling her closer like she’s afraid she might disappear if she doesn’t hold on tight enough.

    It’s messy. Frantic. Neither of them quite in control.

    Not until Zosia starts guiding Carol backwards, directing her with her body until the backs of Carol’s legs hit the edge of the sofa a second later, the contact jolting through her.

    Zosia doesn’t let up there. She presses into her, guiding her down without breaking the kiss, following her as she climbs into Carol’s lap.

    The shift is sudden, but feels inevitable—like they’ve been hurtling toward this moment for weeks.

    Her knees bracket Carol’s hips, her hands sliding up into her hair, holding her there as their mouths meet again, deeper now, more certain. Carol’s hands come up instinctively, finding her waist, then her back, pulling her in closer like she’s trying to erase the space between them entirely.

    For a moment, there’s nothing else—just the simmering heat between them. Hot, ragged breaths and the soft, uneven rhythm of something that’s been building for far too long.

    They break apart only because they have to. Their foreheads rest together, noses brushing, breath mingling in the narrow space between them.

    Carol’s hands grow still at Zosia’s waist, the other woman’s fingers still curled into Carol’s hair. Neither of them moves, eyes searching and chests heaving.

    “Are you—” Carol starts, her voice rougher than she expects. She swallows, tries again, fingers digging into the soft fabric of Zosia’s dress. “Are you sure this is okay?”

    Zosia lets out a quiet, breathless laugh, her lips brushing against Carol’s as she speaks. “Okay?” she murmurs.

    She pulls back just enough to look at Carol properly, her eyes bright and playful, and a little wild around the edges. “This is much better than okay.”

    There’s something softer underneath it.

    Something painfully honest.

    “I’ve wanted this,” she admits, her voice dropping, losing some of its lightness. “For the longest time.”

    Carol feels the admission land. Feels it settle somewhere deep behind her ribs.

    “Wanted you…” Zosia murmurs, her voice husky and thick. Her accent wraps around the words in a way that sends shivers up and down Carol’s spine.

    Carol’s breath stutters, her grip tightening slightly at Zosia’s hips as something in her chest pulls tight, unfamiliar and overwhelming all at once.

    She doesn’t know what to do with that. Doesn’t know how to say it back without saying too much. So she doesn’t.

    She kisses her again instead.

    Zosia responds immediately, like she’s been waiting for permission, her hands slipping from Carol’s hair, trailing down over her shoulders, her arms. One of them disappears under the hem of Carol’s shirt, her touch warm against her skin.

    Carol inhales sharply, her back arching into it without thinking, her hands at Zosia’s hips, tugging her impossibly closer.

    Zosia’s mouth drifts from hers, her lips brushing along her jaw, then lower, finding the curve of her neck. The contact is softer, slower, but no less deliberate; each touch sending a sharp, electric response through Carol that she can’t quite contain.

    “Zosia—” she breathes, though she’s not entirely sure what she means to say.

    Zosia hums softly against her skin, like she’s listening anyway. When her mouth finds Carol’s again, the kiss is different.

    Deeper.

    Hungrier.

    There’s a pull to it now, something more insistent, more certain. Zosia shifts in her lap, the movement subtle but enough to make Carol’s thoughts scatter completely, her hands tightening at her thighs, her grip grounding and unsteady all at once.

    And then Zosia’s teeth sink into Carol’s bare shoulder, and—for a second—Carol forgets everything else.

    Where they are.

    Why they should slow down.

    Anything beyond the feeling of Zosia’s mouth.

    Zosia pulls back first this time, just enough to breathe, her forehead resting lightly against Carol’s, her chest rising and falling a little faster than before.

    “Stay,” she says, the word slipping out before she can seem to stop it. “Stay the night.”

    Carol blinks, trying to catch up. “Like—on the couch?”

    Zosia huffs a soft laugh, her hand still under Carol’s shirt, her fingers tracing a slow, absent line that makes Carol’s breath hitch again. “No. Not on the couch.”

    The implication is clear.

    Carol’s brain stutters, caught somewhere between panic and something else entirely. “Wait—” she says, pulling back slightly, breathless, trying to steady herself. “Can we—can we just—talk for a second?”

    Zosia freezes.

    The shift is immediate.

    Her expression changes, something uncertain flickering across it as she pulls back, climbing carefully off Carol’s lap to sit beside her instead, smoothing her dress down over her thighs like she’s suddenly aware of every inch of herself again.

    “I’m sorry,” she says quickly. “I didn’t—I thought—if that was too much—”

    Zosia smiles faintly. “No.” She traces a light line along Carol’s jaw. “I’ve just been thinking the same thing.”

    “We’re going to have to talk about it,” Zosia says. “Sooner or later.”

    Carol’s throat tightens. She looks back up at the ceiling, not quite able to meet Zosia’s gaze. The baby is the very thing that brought them into each other’s orbits, but right now it’s also the elephant in the room.

    “Do you—do you want to be a parent?” Zosia asks. There’s no accusation in it. No demand. Just a quiet request for honesty. Despite this, Carol finds she doesn’t have the words to answer.

    The words settle between them like a land mine. Carol feels it deep in her bones. One wrong step and it’s lights out.

    Carol hesitates. “I can print you a copy. You can take it home.”

    Zosia pauses, then nods. “Sure.” She softens slightly. “I’d like that. Thank you.”

    Carol nods, watching her as she finishes getting dressed, pulling her shirt on over the swell of her belly.

    Fourteen weeks suddenly seems like no time at all.

    The truck idles at the curb outside the restaurant, engine humming low. Through the front windows, Carol can see movement—staff weaving between tables, someone polishing glasses behind the bar, the early rhythm of dinner service settling into place.

    Zosia turns then, finally meeting her eyes. There’s something guarded there now, something reserved. She leans across the centre console and presses a quick kiss to Carol’s cheek. It’s soft. Familiar—and somehow entirely different.

    Should have done.

    I want this.

    I want you.

    The house is too quiet.

    The drinks cabinet sits across the room. Carol crosses to it, opens the door, and reaches for the whiskey bottle. Her fingers curl around the neck of it, but she pauses.

    It would be easy to take the bottle back with her.

    Too easy.

    To stop feeling.

    Just… sits in it.

    It’s not Manousos.

    Zosia stands on the doorstep, hands clasped in front of her and her lips pressed together. For a second, Carol just stares at her.

    Zosia’s lips curve faintly, her eyes still a little too bright. “I was reading your manuscript.”

    Carol stares blankly at her, still not understanding.

    “I told you. Your writing makes me horny,” Zosia adds, entirely unapologetic, her hands still cupping Carol’s face.

    Carol stares at her for a second longer, then lets out a short, disbelieving breath. “You came all the way over here because my story made you horny—”

    “Can I come in?” Zosia cuts in, letting her hands drop to her sides.

    Carol steps back automatically, still trying to process what’s going on. “Yeah—yeah, of course.”

    Zosia brushes past her, and the second the door shuts behind them she’s kissing her again. This time her hands are already moving, sliding down to Carol’s waist, fingers hooking lightly at her belt.

    Carol exhales sharply, her hands coming up instinctively to Zosia’s hips.

    “Zosia—” she starts.

    Zosia hums against her mouth, showing no sign of slowing as deft fingers work at her belt buckle. Carol pulls back just as it opens with a clink, her hands moving to cover Zosia’s. “Hey—hey.”

    Zosia pauses, just barely, her eyes soft and unfocused.

    Carol’s hands stay where they are, grounding them both. “I’m really glad to see you,” she says, her voice low, a little rough. “But we didn’t exactly leave things in a great place earlier.”

    Zosia’s expression shifts, something sharper flickering through it. Carol almost regrets bringing it up.

    “About earlier,” she continues, forcing herself to push through it. “When you asked if I was ready—”

    Zosia cuts her off with a small shake of her head. “I spent six months after Malick died alone,” she says, her voice quieter now, but steady. “Sad. Stuck.”

    Carol stills.

    Zosia holds her gaze. “We have a lot to talk about,” she continues. “Before the baby comes. And… things are going to change when she does.”

    One hand lets go of Carol’s, sliding up to rest lightly against Carol’s chest. “But until then,” she says, softer now, “I don’t want to waste the time we have. I’m happy. For the first time in a long time.”

    Carol’s throat tightens.

    Zosia tilts her head slightly, that faint, familiar spark returning to her eyes. “So,” she adds, just a little teasing now, “can you stop overthinking for five minutes and just take me to bed?”

    Something in Carol’s chest loosens all at once.

    Relief.

    Sharp and immediate.

    She lets out a quiet breath, her grip on Zosia’s remaining hand tightening just slightly as she pulls her closer.

    “Yeah,” she murmurs, her lips finding Zosia’s again, before shifting them in one smooth motion, guiding the other woman back until her shoulders meet the door. Careful—always careful—but there’s no hesitation in it.

    One hand comes up to Zosia’s face, her thumb brushing lightly along her jaw as she leans in again. “But I don’t think I can wait that long…” Carol adds, her voice low, and a little rough around the edges as her other hand slips between Zosia’s legs.

    Zosia laughs.

    It’s light.

    Easy.

    “I think I’m okay with that.”

    Chapter 20

    Chapter Notes


    The late afternoon sun spills across the pavement outside Zosia’s OBGYN clinic, bright enough to make Carol squint as the glass door swings shut behind them. The air is warm, dry, carrying that faint sterile scent that seems to cling to places like this—disinfectant and something vaguely floral that never quite masks it.

    Carol exhales as they step out onto the sidewalk. “Feels like we were just here.”

    Zosia adjusts the strap of her purse on her shoulder, one hand resting instinctively against the curve of her stomach. “We were. Two weeks ago.”

    “Didn’t we used to get at least a month between these things?” Carol mutters.

    Zosia glances at her, amused. “I’m twenty-eight weeks, Carol. The appointments are every two weeks now.”

    Carol pulls a face. “That seems excessive.”

    Zosia gives a soft chuckle. “You’re just grumpy because your favourite poster doesn’t go past the second trimester.”

    Carol stops short, mock-offended. “That poster is a public service, Zosia.”

    “It’s educational,” Carol insists, falling back into step beside her. “And it just… ends. No third trimester. It’s like going to a movie and they don’t show you the third act!”

    Zosia laughs properly at that, the sound bright and easy. She leans in, brushing a quick kiss against Carol’s cheek. “I’m sure the doctor said she’d order the second poster just for you.”

    Carol doesn’t laugh. Her gaze flicks immediately around the parking lot, scanning—people coming and going, a couple heading toward the entrance, someone unlocking their car two rows over.

    No one seems to be paying them any attention. Still, her shoulders tighten and her brow furrows almost imperceptibly. Zosia doesn’t miss the shift.

    The way Carol’s body closes in on itself just a fraction. The way her eyes move rapidly, her breathing faster. Zosia straightens, the smile fading just slightly as she pulls back.

    “Anyway,” she says lightly, like nothing happened. “Let’s get out of this heat.”

    They walk the rest of the way in a quieter rhythm, the earlier ease settling into something more restrained.

    Zosia’s little blue car sits where they left it, tucked between a battered sedan and a spotless SUV. Carol reaches into her pocket, pulling out the keys and tossing them lightly in her palm.

    “I’m in the third trimester now,” Zosia says, catching Carol’s eye and breaking the silence as they approach. “Final countdown.”

    Carol snorts. “It’s hardly an Apollo mission.”

    Zosia rolls her eyes, unlocking the car. “I feel like I’ve swallowed the moon,” she says, patting her baby bump. It sits higher up now, her belly more pronounced.

    “I could have told you how big the baby is in fruit now if they had a poster that goes past the second trimester,” Carol huffs, only partially teasing.

    “She’s the size of an aubergine now.”

    Carol frowns. “That’s not a fruit.”

    Zosia ignores her. “I still have so much to do before my due date. I haven’t even started on the nursery. It needs painting, and I still need to get the furniture sorted, not to mention picking a name…”

    Carol circles around to the passenger side, opening the door for her out of habit. “I can help with the furniture.”

    Zosia pauses, one hand on the roof of the car. She looks at Carol for a moment, something soft flickering in her expression. Then she climbs in, settling carefully into the seat.

    Carol closes the door behind her and moves around to the driver’s side, sliding into the little car. It’s a far cry from the spacious cab of her truck.

    The air conditioning hums to life as she starts the engine, pushing cool air into the warm interior so it feels a little less like Carol is sitting in an Easy-Bake Oven.

    Zosia reaches across the centre console, taking Carol’s hand once they’re alone, their fingers threading together easily this time. She squeezes gently.

    “You’ve already done more than enough for us,” she says.

    Carol glances at her. “I can afford a crib, Zosia.”

    Zosia smiles faintly. “I know you can.”

    “I might be retired,” Carol adds, a touch glib, “but I’m not short on cash.”

    Zosia arches a brow. “Can you really call yourself retired if you’re writing again?”

    Carol huffs and gives a wave of her hand. “Semantics.”

    “Have you spoken to your agent yet?” Zosia asks. “About publishing the new book?”

    Carol hesitates for half a second. “Val? Yeah. Yeah, I have.”

    “And?”

    “She loved it,” Carol admits after a pause. She’s still not sure if throwing herself back into the shark-infested waters—or more specifically, sands— of Waycaro is the right move. Especially with the baby coming. Neither Carol, nor Zosia and the baby, need that kind of exposure. “Val thinks the fans will eat it up.”

    Zosia’s smile widens. “They absolutely will. A pregnant Raban?” She shakes her head, amused. “They’re going to go feral. How ever did you even come up with that?” Zosia ribs.

    Carol shrugs, playing into Zosia’s teasing with a glint in her eye. “Just came to me.” She shrugs. “What can I say? I’m very good at writing mindless crap.”

    Zosia’s hand tightens slightly around hers. “Hey.” She nudges her with her shoulder. “You’re a great writer, Carol. And your fans love you.”

    The word hangs there between them for a beat too long.

    Zosia’s expression shifts, something sheepish creeping in. She withdraws her hand, resting it instead against her stomach, her gaze dropping.

    Carol clears her throat lightly, feels her chest constricting. “Yeah, well,” she says, forcing a small smile, “my fans are the type who’d go crazy for MPREG. I’m not sure if that says more about them, or me.”

    Zosia huffs a quiet laugh, the earlier tension slipping away.

    Carol glances at her before she shifts the car into gear. “I’m serious about the furniture,” she says, circling back. “At least let me go fifty-fifty.”

    Zosia shakes her head. “Carol—”

    “I can afford it,” Carol insists. “Taffler’s still negotiating with the clinic, but he’s pretty sure he can push the settlement into seven figures.”

    Carol notices. “Did you—uh…” She keeps her tone casual, but there’s a careful edge to it now. “Did you get in touch with a lawyer yet? If not, I can ask Taffler for a referral.”

    Zosia looks out the window. “I don’t need one.”

    Carol frowns. “You don’t—?”

    “I already agreed to settle,” Zosia says.

    Carol blinks. “You did?”

    Zosia nods, still reluctant to turn and look at the other woman.

    Zosia hesitates, clearly uncomfortable. As if she already knows what Carol is going to say. Eventually she breathes out, “Five hundred thousand.”

    Zosia’s jaw tightens. “It’s not nothing.”

    “Taffler said they offered me the same lowball number,” Carol continues. “You should hold out for more—”

    “My priority is my baby,” Zosia says firmly. “Not being dragged through a long legal battle.”

    Carol’s grip tightens on the steering wheel. “Let me talk to Taffler. He can find you someone who’ll handle everything—”

    The word lands sharp in the small space of the car.

    Carol goes still.

    Zosia freezes at the same time, shocked by the force behind her own words.

    “I’ve been late on my last two mortgage payments,” Zosia continues, quieter now. “The medical bills are already stacking up. Malick’s not here anymore—and I have to make this work on one income.”

    “That money might not mean much to you,” Zosia adds, her voice steady but tired, “but it means I can breathe. It means I can make sure my daughter is taken care of.”

    The words settle heavily between them, and Carol exhales slowly. “I didn’t know.”

    Zosia shrugs lightly. “Why would you?”

    “I’m sorry,” Carol says. “I shouldn’t have pushed.”

    Zosia nods, accepting the offered apology. “It’s okay.”

    Carol hesitates, then adds, softer, “If you need anything—”

    “I’m good,” Zosia says, her tone still sharp with pride, but her expression settles into something softer. “But I appreciate the offer.”

    The silence settles between them again. Not as sharp as before, but not easy either.

    After a moment, Carol reaches across the console, taking Zosia’s hand again. This time she threads their fingers together deliberately.

    “You’re not alone,” she says, her voice firm.

    Zosia looks at their joined hands, then up at Carol. She smiles.

    It’s warm.

    But it doesn’t quite reach her eyes.

    Carol’s presence in her life is still far from a guarantee right now.

    “—And I’m paying my half for the nursery. The furniture, the paint, the whole nine yards,” Carol announces, leaving no further room for argument.

    Zosia relents, something warm blossoming in her chest at Carol’s words.

    My half.


    -


    The store they find themselves in a short time later smells faintly of wood polish and baby powder, the kind of overly clean, curated scent that tries to convince you everything inside is soft and safe and uncomplicated. Light filters down from high fixtures, bright and warm, catching on rows of carefully arranged displays.

    Carol trails a step behind Zosia as they move down the aisle, her hands tucked into the pockets of her jacket, gaze flicking over the rows of cribs lined up like they’re in a miniature showroom.

    Each one looks… identical. More or less.

    Wood. Slats. Mattress.

    She doesn’t see what all the fuss is about. Zosia, on the other hand, slows in front of one near the middle of the aisle, her attention caught immediately.

    “Look at this one, Carol,” she says, stepping closer.

    Carol follows her gaze.

    It’s white, wooden, clean lines but softened at the edges. Slightly curved rails, something particularly traditional about it. Like it belongs on a movie set, with soft ambient lighting and impossibly calm parents.

    Zosia runs her hand lightly along the top rail, her expression softening in a way that makes Carol’s chest tighten a little. Makes her wish she was built to get excited about this kind of stuff.

    Instead, Carol leans in, glancing at the tag hanging discreetly from the side and lets out a low whistle. “Are these things made of solid gold? That can’t be good for the baby to sleep on.”

    Zosia laughs, the sound light and easy as her hand catches Carol’s arm. “I told you, babies are expensive.”

    “Zosia, I went to college in the nineties on less than this!” Carol straightens, still eyeing the number like it might change if she stares at it long enough. “That’s not expensive, that’s extortion.”

    Zosia smiles faintly. “I have some money set aside for furniture,” she says. “And the settlement should be finalised soon, so—”

    She stops herself. Her gaze flicks to Carol, a brief, almost reflexive check. Like she’s bracing for the argument they’d had in the car to resurface.

    Carol sees it.

    Feels the same anxious sense of dread.

    And lets it go.

    She shrugs lightly, already stepping away from the crib. “Alright, well, let’s see what else they’ve got before we remortgage your house for a glorified wooden box.”

    Zosia watches her for a second longer, then exhales quietly and follows.

    They move further down the aisle, past rows of near-identical designs until Carol pauses in front of one with a small display screen mounted beside it.

    “This one rocks itself,” she says, tapping the button. The crib gives a gentle, mechanical sway, almost hypnotic. “And plays soothing sounds…” She pushes another button and the gentle sound of waves on a far-off beach starts up from the speakers built into the side.

    Zosia leans in slightly, watching it move. “That’s… kind of cool. I’m not so sure on a ‘smart’ crib, though. I was thinking something more traditional.”

    Carol folds her arms, considering it. “Our little aubergine might like this one.”

    Zosia turns to her, one eyebrow lifting. She doesn’t comment on the ‘our’ part. That feels far too loaded. Instead, she asks, “Aubergine?”

    Carol shrugs. “You haven’t picked a name yet.”

    “I have been thinking about it,” Zosia says, defensive but amused. There are twelve weeks until her due date and so much still to be done.

    Admittedly, settling on a name for the tiny person who’s going to be living in her house for at least the next eighteen years should probably be high up on her list of priorities.

    “I’ve got it down to a shortlist.”

    Carol smirks, crosses her arms over her chest as she gives Zosia a knowing look. This is a woman who turns deciding what toppings to put on their pancakes in the morning into the equivalent of NATO brokering a deal for world peace.

    “Oh yeah? How many?”

    Zosia hesitates, only serving to confirm Carol’s suspicions. “Fifty,” she finally admits in a small voice, looking away as she scratches at her ear.

    Carol barks out a laugh. “Zos, that’s not a shortlist.”

    Zosia nudges her lightly with her shoulder, an amused smile breaking out over her lips. “It is in progress.”

    Carol shakes her head. “Jesus. At this rate she’ll be old enough to name herself before you decide.”

    “That would probably take the pressure off.” Zosia grins, then adds, just a little too casually, “You know, Raban is actually in the top spot right now.”

    Carol freezes, obviously trying to decide whether she’s being serious or not, then bursts out laughing. “Absolutely not.”

    Zosia’s lips twitch. Of course she’s not being serious, but that doesn’t mean she’s about to let Carol off the hook. “Why not?”

    “Why not? Because I will take you to court, why not,” Carol says, still grinning.

    Zosia gasps softly, placing a hand over her chest in mock offence. “You’d try to get custody just to stop me naming her after my favourite fictional pirate?”

    Carol blinks. “Custody?” She laughs again, shaking her head in earnest. “No—no, I meant for trademark infringement.”

    Zosia swats her arm, the gesture light and familiar. “You’re terrible.”

    Carol grins, rubbing her arm like it actually hurt.

    And for a moment—

    It feels easy again. The tension from earlier loosened, not gone entirely, but softened enough to be able to breathe around.

    They drift further along the aisle and deeper into the store, passing more cribs until Carol slows again.

    “Hello,” she says, tilting her head slightly. “This one I recognise.”

    Zosia follows her gaze.

    And stops.

    It’s the one she’d shown Carol months ago. Back when everything had felt… simpler. Before the gender scan. Before the weight of everything had really settled in.

    The fairy-tale crib.

    Solid wood, darker than the others, with delicate vines carved into the rails. The detail is intricate, almost too pretty for something so practical. It looks like it belongs in a storybook rather than on a shop floor.

    Zosia’s face softens instantly. Her fingers brush over the carved wood, tracing the pattern lightly. Her eyes light up in a way that makes Carol’s insides squirm.

    And then—

    She spots the price tag, and the light dims. Zosia exhales quietly, her hand dropping back to her side. “It’s too much,” she says, more to herself than to Carol. “For something she’ll only grow out of in six months.”

    Carol watches her. The way she pulls back from it. The way she makes the decision before letting herself want it too much. Carol’s heart hurts at the thought of Zosia not getting something she so clearly wants.

    “Maybe we should look at the ones that convert,” Zosia adds, gesturing vaguely down the aisle. “Into toddler beds. We’d get much more use out of them.”

    Carol nods easily. “Yeah. Sure. That makes sense.”

    Zosia offers her a small smile, like she was looking for Carol’s reassurance to let the dream crib go. She starts walking, already scanning ahead.

    Carol lingers for a second, her mind already made up. “Go on ahead,” she says. “I’ll catch up. Just need to—” She glances down at her shoe. “Lace.”

    Zosia doesn’t question it, doesn’t even look down at Carol’s still-tied lace. She nods absently and keeps moving.

    Carol crouches, making a vague show of adjusting her shoe, waiting until Zosia’s a few steps further down the aisle before straightening slightly and pulling her phone from her pocket.

    She snaps a quick picture of the tag of the fairy-tale crib. Her thumb hovers for a second over the screen before she slips the phone back into her pocket.

    Better to ask forgiveness than permission.

    Carol stands, glancing once more at the crib before turning to follow Zosia down the aisle.


    -


    The late afternoon sun has dipped low enough to take the edge off the heat, but the asphalt still radiates warmth beneath their feet as they step out of the baby store.

    They haven’t purchased anything yet, but Zosia has a mental list of the pieces she’d like, and Carol promises to bring her back with her truck next week so they can make a start on turning Zosia’s spare room into the nursery.

    That should give Carol enough time to order the crib and have it delivered.

    The car park stretches out in front of them, rows of vehicles baking under the fading light, the air carrying that familiar mix of dust, gasoline, and sun-warmed metal.

    Carol digs Zosia’s car keys out of her jacket pocket, her attention half on the car, half on Zosia beside her. They fall into step easily, shoulders brushing now and then as they walk.

    It happens without either of them really thinking about it.

    Their hands brush—just the lightest contact at first. The back of Zosia’s fingers against Carol’s. Warm. Intentional. Carol’s breath catches almost imperceptibly as Zosia’s hand turns, fingers threading gently through hers.

    She lets it happen.

    For a few steps, it feels… easy. Natural in a way that still surprises her. Zosia’s hand is soft, her grip steady but unassuming, like she’s offering something rather than taking it.

    Carol’s shoulders loosen by a fraction.

    And then she sees her.

    Maureen.

    Halfway across the car park, weaving between cars with that unmistakable purposeful energy. Her Wycaro tote bag slung over her shoulder. Her head already turning, scanning—

    Carol’s stomach drops.

    She pulls her hand away so fast it’s almost violent, like she’s been burned. Her fingers slip free of Zosia’s, and she folds her arms tightly across her chest, shoulders drawing in, posture closing off in an instant.

    Zosia’s hand lingers in the air for a second, empty.

    Carol doesn’t look at her. She can’t. But she sees it anyway—the flicker of hurt in her peripheral vision. The way Zosia stills beside her, not quite sure what happened to cause the abrupt change.

    “Carol! Zosia!” Maureen’s voice carries across the lot, bright and delighted.

    Carol forces a smile that feels like it’s been carved out of stone as she turns toward her, offering a stiff wave. “Hey, Maureen.”

    Maureen hurries over, practically vibrating with excitement. “I thought that was you two! Been baby shopping?” She nods at the store they just left. “That was the best part of pregnancy for me. Well, that and my husband actually getting off his ass and—”

    Carol nods along, murmuring polite acknowledgements, her arms still locked tightly across her chest. She’s acutely aware of Zosia standing beside her, just out of reach now. Aware that the space she just created between them can be measured in more than distance.

    Of the absence where her hand had been just moments ago.

    Maureen chatters for another minute, oblivious, before finally letting them go with a wave and a promise to tag Carol in a funny meme she saw about Raban later. Carol’s stomach sinks further at that.

    “Drive safe!” Maureen calls, already pulling out her phone.

    Carol manages a tight nod, then turns quickly toward Zosia’s car, unlocking it with a sharp click.

    The silence between her and Zosia settles thick and heavy as they climb inside.

    The interior is still warm; the lingering heat trapped under the glass feels like a metaphor for Carol’s own simmering feelings. She starts the engine, cranks the air conditioning up higher than necessary. The vents hum to life, pushing cool air into the space between them.

    She grips the steering wheel for a second, knuckles turning white, then exhales. “I—” she starts, then stops. Doesn’t know what to say about her behaviour—what she even can say to excuse it. “I’m sorry.”

    Zosia doesn’t respond right away. Carol risks a glance across the console. Zosia is watching her closely, but there’s no anger in her face. Nothing about her body language is closed off. She’s turned to face Carol, arms down by her side. Open, but… quiet.

    Carol swallows. “I panicked. When I saw Maureen. If she were to see something and post about it online, it might get traction.” She huffs out a breath, shaking her head slightly. “If it gets out that I’m—” She gestures vaguely between them. “That I’m seeing someone, let alone someone who’s pregnant, it’ll turn into a whole thing.”

    Her fingers tighten against the wheel again as Zosia stays quiet. Carol babbles, nervously trying to fill the silence. “I don’t want that. Not for me, but—” She glances at Zosia, her voice softening. “Especially not for you. Or the baby.”

    Zosia studies her for a moment longer. Then, quietly, she reaches across the centre console and takes Carol’s hand again. Carol startles slightly at the contact, her fingers instinctively tensing before they relax into Zosia’s grip.

    Zosia squeezes gently. “It’s okay.”

    Carol lets out a short, disbelieving breath. “No. It’s really not.”

    Zosia’s thumb brushes lightly over the back of her hand, slow and steady. “Carol—”

    “I’m almost fifty years old,” Carol cuts in, her voice tightening. “I shouldn’t be—” She gestures again, frustrated. “I shouldn’t be afraid to hold my girlfriend’s hand in a parking lot in case someone sees me.”

    The word clangs like a church bell in the suddenly silent car.

    Girlfriend.

    Carol freezes.

    Her heart thunders in her chest as Zosia remains impassive. Then her brow lifts slightly, amusement flickering into her expression before she breaks out in a grin. “Oh?” she says, her tone light, teasing. “So I’m your girlfriend now?”

    Carol’s face goes hot immediately. “I—what? No, I didn’t—I mean, I did, but—” She drags a hand through her hair, flustered. “Slip of the tongue?” she poses it like a question, like she expects Zosia to have an answer for her.

    Zosia’s smile deepens, soft and knowing. She glances around the car park, quick and discreet, making sure no one is paying them any attention.

    Then she leans in.

    The kiss is brief. Gentle. A soft press of lips that lingers just long enough to make Carol’s breath hitch and her pulse race.

    Zosia’s hand slides up, her fingers brushing along Carol’s jaw before settling at the back of her neck. Her thumb traces a slow, soothing line against the flushed skin there.

    Her hand lingers when she pulls back—not by much. Zosia stays close enough that Carol can still feel the warmth of her breath against her cheek, can still smell the sweet floral scent of Zosia’s perfume when she takes a deep, steadying breath.

    “It really is okay,” Zosia says quietly. “I understand.”

    Carol searches her face, still uncertain.

    Zosia’s expression is steady. “You’re a private person,” she continues. “I get that, and I get why—and this is… still new. For both of us.”

    Her hand moves from Carol’s neck to cup her cheek, her palm warm against her skin. “I’m okay with keeping things private. For now.”

    There’s something unspoken beneath her words. A quiet acknowledgement that for now won’t last forever. That it comes with an expiration date that is barrelling towards them. An unstoppable force that will either sweep Carol up, or crush her.

    Zosia doesn’t say it out loud, though.

    Not here. Not now.

    She understands Carol needs her patience right now.

    Carol’s throat tightens. She turns her head slightly, pressing a soft kiss into the centre of Zosia’s palm. The gesture is instinctive. Intimate in a way she doesn’t quite have words for.

    “Thank you,” she murmurs against her skin.

    Zosia’s fingers curl slightly against her cheek, and Carol lets out a quiet breath, her lips brushing Zosia’s palm once more before she leans back.

    “Jeez, how the hell did I get lucky enough to find someone like you?” The question slips out before she can stop it. She doesn’t try to take it back, though. Because it’s true.

    Because she hadn’t expected this. Hadn’t expected anyone after Helen—and certainly not someone like Zosia. Someone who just gets her. 

    For the longest time, she’d been certain that part of her life was over. That whatever capacity she had for this—for connection, for mutual understanding—had been buried along with her wife.

    But now—

    Now there’s Zosia.

    And the baby.

    The thought lands heavier than the rest. It always does. A weight she hasn’t quite figured out how to carry yet. She doesn’t know if she can be a mother. Doesn’t know what that would even look like for her.

    But she knows she wants this.

    Wants Zosia.

    Which means she’s going to have to figure the rest out.

    Carol clears her throat lightly, pulling away to shift the car into drive. “Do you want to come over?” she asks, aiming for casual and missing by a mile. “We could… have dinner. I can cook? Or—not. You’d probably prefer not.” She laughs, feeling like a giddy sixteen-year-old again, one who just got her first kiss.

    She tries not to think about how that turned out. About the direction her life took after her mom walked in just in time to witness her second kiss. About how different her life might have been had she never heard of Camp Freedom Falls.

    Zosia huffs a quiet laugh. “Probably. Dinner sounds nice, though. Maybe curl up on the sofa after? See how the night goes?”

    Carol smiles faintly at that, pulling on her seatbelt before she pulls out of the parking space.

    Zosia settles back into her seat, one hand resting lightly over the curve of her stomach. Then she glances at Carol. “Speaking of dinner… what are you doing next Friday?”

    Carol’s grip tightens almost imperceptibly on the wheel. It’s her birthday next Friday, and she’s turning fifty. The number sits uncomfortably in her chest, heavier than it should—especially with Zosia sitting beside her, ten years younger and alive and glowing in a way Carol hasn’t felt in a long time.

    She keeps her tone neutral. “Nothing exciting… why?”

    Zosia watches her for a second, long enough that Carol starts to worry she knows exactly what next Friday is. Then she says, “Koumba’s invited us to dinner. With Manousos.”

    Carol snorts softly. “What? Like a double date?”

    Zosia’s eyes widen in disbelief. “They’re dating?”

    Carol pulls a face. “Well, I can’t say for certain, but Koumba’s car sure has been blocking my damn driveway a lot lately.”

    Zosia rolls her eyes, a small smile tugging at her lips. “I think he really likes him. It’s cute. Maybe that’s why he wants us at dinner? To test the waters with Manousos?”

    Carol hums, pretending to consider it longer than she actually does. “Alright,” she says finally. “I guess I can tolerate Koumba for an evening in the name of young love.”

    “Such a bleeding heart,” Zosia teases, her hand moving to settle on Carol’s leg. She doesn’t reach for the inside of her thigh this time; traffic is much too busy for that. She just lets her hand rest there, a comforting weight that reminds Carol she’s not alone.

    Carol glances at her briefly, smiles, then turns her attention back to the road—completely unaware of what she’s let herself in for Friday night.

    Chapter End Notes

    As always, thanks for reading and for the love this fic is receiving! My twitter notifications are full of people tagging me in stuff that reminds them of Stursia pregnancy and I love it 😊

    Chapter 21


    Morning light filters softly through the curtains, pale and warm, settling across the bed in quiet patches. The room smells faintly of clean linen and Zosia’s shampoo, something floral and grounding that Carol has come to associate with waking up here.

    Zosia lies on her back, one arm tucked beneath her head, the other resting loosely over her stomach. The gentle rise of it is unmistakable now, the curve more pronounced with each passing week.

    Carol lies beside her, propped slightly on one elbow, her attention fixed entirely on Zosia. Specifically, on her stomach.

    “Mia,” Carol murmurs, pressing a light kiss just above Zosia’s navel.

    Zosia smiles faintly, eyes half-lidded as she watches her. Beneath Carol’s lips, something shifts. Small and subtle. Zosia’s breath catches just slightly as the movement rolls low in her belly, like a slow turn.

    “Nora,” Carol continues, another kiss, a little lower this time.

    The baby moves again. Stronger now. A gentle press outward, as if responding to the other woman’s touch. Her voice.

    Zosia’s fingers drift instinctively into Carol’s hair, brushing through it. Carol glances up briefly, curious, but Zosia just smiles and shakes her head faintly, urging her on.

    “Ophelia.”

    Another kiss, and this time the movement is immediate. A small, insistent push beneath Carol’s mouth, followed by a softer stretch that eases back again.

    Carol stills. Her expression shifts—surprise first, then something gentler, something almost reverent. “Look who’s awake.”

    Zosia nods, her voice quiet, warm. “And kicking.”

    Carol lets out a shaky breath, her hand coming up instinctively to rest over the spot, feeling for it again.

    “Paisley,” she continues, pressing another kiss there. The response is slower this time, but it comes—a gentle roll beneath her palm, like a quiet acknowledgement she’s there.

    Zosia watches her, something tender settling in her chest at the way Carol’s focus has shifted entirely, the playful edge gone, replaced with something far deeper.

    Carol doesn’t look away. Her thumb brushes lightly over the curve of Zosia’s stomach, her touch careful, almost curious. “Okay,” she murmurs, half to herself. “Now I feel like I should be taking this more seriously. Maybe we should be taking note of which names she reacts to?”

    Her expression is so serious as she says this, so earnest, that Zosia can’t help but chuckle. “Maybe.” She agrees, fingers still running lightly through the other woman’s hair, and Carol doesn’t notice the teasing lilt to her voice.

    She pauses, frowning slightly as she lifts her head. “Hmm, I can’t think of any Q names.”

    Zosia snorts, laughing as Carol’s game of listing girls’ names in alphabetical order comes to an abrupt halt.

    “Hang on,” Carol says, pushing herself up slightly. “Let me check—” She reaches blindly toward the nightstand, fingers searching for her phone. Zosia catches her wrist before she can grab it, her grip gentle but firm as she pulls Carol’s hand back down, pressing it against her stomach again.

    “Outsourcing to Google is cheating.” She laughs.

    Carol exhales a small, amused breath, letting herself be pulled back into place. “I was trying to be thorough.”

    “You are really not helping narrow down the shortlist,” Zosia points out.

    Carol hums in acknowledgement, but doesn’t argue. Instead, she leans down again, abandoning the alphabet entirely. Her lips brush against Zosia’s skin, slower now, more deliberate, tracing a path downward.

    Zosia’s breath shifts, just slightly.

    Carol’s voice drops, “We could always stay in bed all day,” she suggests, her lips grazing just below the curve of Zosia’s stomach. “Really commit to the naming process.”

    She presses a kiss to the inside of Zosia’s thigh, just beneath the hem of her shorts.

    Zosia giggles, the sound light and breathy as she reaches for Carol’s shoulder and tugs her back up. “Tempting,” she murmurs, pulling her into a kiss.

    Carol goes easily, settling beside her, one hand braced against the mattress as their lips meet. It’s warm and familiar, but easy in a way that still feels a little new to Carol.

    When Zosia pulls back, she’s smiling. “But I have work,” she says. “And you have your brunch with Val.”

    Carol groans softly, letting her forehead fall briefly against Zosia’s shoulder. “But it’s my—” She cuts herself off.

    Zosia’s smile deepens, but she doesn’t comment. Doesn’t call attention to it.

    “Mm?” she prompts lightly.

    Carol huffs, brushing it off. “Nothing.”

    Zosia hums, clearly unconvinced, but lets it go.

    For both of them.

    Zosia can’t help but wonder what happens when this stops being easy?

    What happens when the baby arrives? What does this—whatever it is between them—turn into when it has to exist in the real world, not just in quiet mornings and stolen kisses?

    They’ll have to talk about it.

    Soon.

    But not today.

    Not on Carol’s birthday.

    Then—

Like the sudden absence of Carol’s voice, along with her peppered kisses, has caused the baby to go looking for them. Zosia stills, her hand sliding instinctively to the spot, palm flattening gently against her skin as she waits.

    It comes again.

    The café sits on the corner of a cobbled street in Old Town, just a few streets from Zosia’s restaurant, all polished brass and tall windows. The kind of place that smells faintly of espresso and money. Sunlight pours in through the narrow glass windows, catching on crystal tumblers and crisp white tablecloths, turning everything just a shade too bright to be entirely comfortable.

    Carol pushes through the door a few minutes late for her brunch, brushing a hand through her hair as she scans the room. Val is impossible to miss.

    She’s already seated at a table near the window. Short cropped white curls, lips painted a shade of red that refuses to be ignored, and big wide glasses framing her wrinkled face. Even sitting still, she radiates energy, her presence filling the space around her.

She hopes her cheeks don’t flush at the memory of licking away those drops of water—and what that led to.

    Val waves a hand dismissively. “Please, I’m just impressed you called me at all! I was starting to think you were avoiding me, kid.”

    Carol winces slightly, a sheepish smile tugging at her mouth. “I kinda was.”

    Val barks out a laugh. She’s always loved Carol’s brutal honesty. “Oh, I know you were. My question is why?”

    Carol glances down at the table, tracing a finger lightly along the edge of her napkin. “I was done,” she says after a moment. “With Wycaro. With writing. All of it.”

    Val leans back in her chair, studying her over the rim of her glasses, the heavy wrinkles around her eyes deepening. “So what changed?”

    Carol hesitates, but not because she doesn’t know the answer.

    She knows it all too well.

    Zosia.

    The way her life has shifted, quietly but completely, around her. The way everything feels… possible again in a way it hasn’t in years. Not since Helen.

    But she’s not ready to say that out loud.

    Not here.

    Not to Val.

    She shrugs instead, aiming for casual. “Found some new inspiration, I guess.”

    Val stares at her for half a second, still scrutinising Carol closely, then bursts out laughing.

    “Well, whatever the hell you’re doing, keep doing it, kid!” She says, leaning forward again. “Because those chapters you sent me?” She bangs the table for emphasis, attracting the attention of a disapproving older couple at the table next to theirs. Carol ducks her head, her hand finding the back of her neck. She’d almost forgotten how loud Val could be. “Some of your best work, Carol. I mean that.”

    Carol shifts slightly, head still ducked, uncomfortable with the praise. “I don’t even know if there’s a book in it, to be honest.”

    Val snorts. “Bullshit.”

    Carol looks up, cheeks burning now as the woman at the other table tuts out loud at Val’s language.

    “You don’t send me something like that unless there’s a book in it,” Val continues. “You know that. I know that. Don’t start playing coy with me now, Sturka. I’m too old to play the blushing bride.”

    Carol huffs a quiet breath, conceding the point with a small nod and the hint of a smile. If she’s being honest, she’s missed Val. “Okay. Maybe there’s a book.”

    “That’s what I like to hear.” Val grins, flashing her veneers at Carol.

    A waiter appears at the table, notepad in hand, and they order brunch—something with eggs and toast that Carol doesn’t fully register, too hyper-aware of Val watching her.

    “And we’ll have two mimosas,” Val adds, handing back the menu.

    Carol shakes her head. “Just orange juice for me.”

    Val blinks. “Excuse me?”

    “I’m driving.”

    Val scoffs. “Since when has that ever stopped you?”

    Carol presses her lips together. “I said orange juice.”

    Val studies her for a beat, then smirks slightly. “Fine. Be boring. But at least let me toast to your birthday.” She waves the young waiter off with her hand, effectively dismissing him.

    Carol grimaces at her last remark. “I’d rather you didn’t.”

    “Oh, come on, Carol. Don’t be glum. Don’t you know, fifty is the new thirty?”

    “Fifty is still fifty,” Carol mutters. She’s never been one to celebrate her birthday, but this year she’s particularly keen to avoid thinking about it. Fifty is a big milestone, especially when you’re dating someone on the wrong side of forty.

    The waiter returns with their drinks, setting a flute in front of Val and a glass of orange juice in front of Carol. Val raises hers immediately.

    “To fifty,” she says.

    Carol lifts her glass reluctantly, tapping it lightly against Val’s.

    “To… making it another year,” she amends dryly, and they drink.

    Val tilts her head slightly, eyes narrowing just a fraction. “So,” she says, stretching out the single syllable. “Who is she?”

    Carol almost chokes.

    She coughs, reaching quickly for her glass. “What—what are you talking about?” she splutters.

    Val leans back, entirely too pleased with herself. “Oh, please. The last time you handed me a manuscript this good, you and Helen had just gotten married and were fucking like bunnies.”

    “Don’t ‘Jesus, Val’ me,” she continues, waving her fork in Carol’s direction. “I know you. You look good. You look happy. You’re writing like you’re on a deadline, and you’re not drinking.” She gestures to the orange juice. “So it’s got to be a woman.”

    Carol shakes her head too quickly. “You’re wrong.”

    Val raises a brow, and Carol feels a flicker of something uncomfortable in her chest. Guilt, maybe. Or something adjacent to it.

    She’d been at their wedding. Had seen the way Carol looked at Helen with all the love and adoration in the world as she’d said “I do.”

    Had heard them make their vows to one another.

    Until death do we part.

    Carol had never dreamt Helen could be the one to go first. Had never planned for it. Never wanted it.

    Val’s face lights up, delighted. “Zosia! Now that is a name, kid. She’s not a stripper, is she?”

    Val grins. “Touchy.”

    Carol huffs. “She’s a chef, not a stripper.”

    Carol pokes at her food. “I’ve known her about five months. We’ve been… dating for a month. It’s not—not serious.”

    Val smiles, slow and knowing. “Please. A woman my age knows when another woman is getting some from someone younger.”

    Because there are other things she’s not saying.

    Much bigger things.

    About the size of an aubergine.

    “No scandals,” she repeats, trying to sound aloof as she picks up her glass. “You got it.”

    Koumba pulls his car up at the bottom of Carol’s driveway, the engine idling as the early evening light stretches long across the quiet street. The air outside is warm, tinged with the faint scent of cut grass and sun-warmed asphalt.

    He glances over at Zosia as he shifts the car into park. “Dinner is at seven,” he says, tapping the steering wheel lightly. “Don’t be late.”

    Zosia laughs, pushing her long dark hair back behind her ear. “Tell that to Carol.”

    Koumba eyes her for a second, something amused flickering across his face. “Does she have any idea?”

    Zosia shakes her head. “She seemed a little suspicious this morning,” she admits.

    Koumba hums. “Do you think she will be mad?”

    Zosia considers that for a moment, then smiles faintly with a shake of her head. “No. Carol’s bark is much worse than her bite.”

    Koumba’s grin widens. “You would know, my dear.”

    Zosia flushes immediately, turning toward the door to hide it. “I’ll see you at seven,” she says quickly.

    She reaches for the gift bag at her feet, then pauses, glancing toward the house. After a beat, she sets it back down on the seat.

    “Hold onto that for me,” she adds. “I don’t want her to see it yet.”

    Koumba nods, already reaching for it. His own presents for Carol sit on the back seat. “Of course.”

    She closes the car door behind her and makes her way up the driveway, each step slowing just slightly as she approaches the front door.

    For a moment, she hesitates.

    Then she knocks.

    “Why are you knocking?” she asks, stepping aside to let Zosia in. “You could’ve just come in.”

    Zosia shifts her weight slightly, a small, sheepish smile tugging at her lips as she comes to a stop in Carol’s hallway. “It felt rude to just walk in.”

    Carol closes the door behind her and leans back against the frame, folding her arms loosely. “Zos, you’re here almost as often as I am these days.”

    Zosia huffs a quiet laugh. “That’s true, but still.” She tilts her head, a teasing note slipping into her voice. “What if you were naked when I walked in?”

Not rush back to work. Keep Bob running the kitchen until you’re ready. Spend some real time with the baby.”

    Zosia shakes her head. “Then you know I can’t afford to wait months for a settlement, or pay lawyer’s fees.”

    Really quiet.

    Possible.

    Carol blinks. “Okay?”

    “Okay,” Zosia repeats. “Tell Taffler to add me to the lawsuit.”

    Carol glances at her, a grin spreading slowly across her face. Zosia stares back in disbelief. “You know about it?”

    Carol raises a brow, amusement flickering across her face. “Well, now I really want to cancel dinner.”

    Zosia laughs, stepping back toward the door. “Not happening. You’re sitting through dessert and everything.”

    Carol sighs dramatically, but she’s still smiling as she follows her out.

    “Everyone decent?” Carol calls out as she closes the door behind them.

    From somewhere deeper in the house, Manousos’ voice carries back. “In the kitchen!”

    Zosia smiles faintly, glancing at Carol as they slip off their shoes and make their way down the hall. The sound of something simmering grows louder as they approach, the soft clink of utensils against metal, and the low hum of conversation.

    They step into the kitchen. Manousos stands at the stove, one hand stirring a pan with practiced ease. He’s dressed in slacks and a crisp dress shirt, an apron tied neatly over the top and a dish cloth slung over his shoulder.

    At the island, Koumba is finishing opening a bottle of wine, the cork easing free with a pronounced pop. He turns at the same moment, catching sight of them, and his face breaks into a wide grin.

    “Ah,” he says warmly, “the birthday girl has arrived!”

    Carol groans, dragging a hand down her face as she shoots a look at Manousos. “I take it you told them?”

    Manousos just smiles, entirely unapologetic. “Happy birthday, Carol Sturka.”

    Carol exhales through her nose, but there’s no real irritation behind it. “Traitor,” she mutters, though her lips twitch faintly.

    Koumba pauses mid-pour, then looks to Zosia, and the swell of her stomach, and laughs at himself. “Ah—of course. Forgive me. Old habits.”

    Manousos gestures toward the fridge. “There’s iced tea, or water if you prefer, Zosia.”

    “Iced tea would be lovely, thank you” Zosia says, her voice warm. Carol can see she’s itching to take over at the stove—or offer some advice about that way Manousos is making his sauce—but she keeps it to herself.

    He hands Zosia the glass of iced tea with a small nod. “For you, my dear.”

    “Thank you,” she says, fingers curling around the cool surface.

    To Carol, he hands a glass of the red wine. She mutters her thanks.

    Manousos wipes his hands on a towel, glancing between them. “Dinner is almost ready. If you want to move to the dining room.”

    Koumba doesn’t wait, already moving ahead, wine glass in hand, guiding the way like he’s done it a hundred times before.

    Carol watches him go, then smirks faintly. “You’re looking very comfortable here, Koumba.”

    He glances back over his shoulder, grin easy. “About as comfortable as you are at Zosia’s place.”

    Carol’s mouth opens, then closes again. She lets it go. She’s not entirely sure how much Koumba knows, or what Zosia has told him. She assumes he knows… something. Enough to make that comment land the way it does.

    But she doesn’t push it.

    Instead, she falls into step beside Zosia, brushing her fingers lightly against the back of her hand as they follow Koumba into the dining room, the warmth of the house wrapping around them as the evening begins.


    -






    Manousos appears in the dining room a short while later, carrying four plates with steady hands and practiced balance. The smell hits first—rich, savoury lamb, threaded with garlic and rosemary. Crisp roast potatoes and sautéed vegetables.

    Zosia’s eyes widen slightly as he sets the plates down one by one. “Impressive. I know who to call if we’re ever short-handed at the restaurant,” she says, glancing at the remaining plate still balanced in his hand. She has professional waiters who struggle with more than two plates.

    Manousos gives a quiet laugh as he places the last plate in front of Carol. “I waited tables in college,” he explains simply, taking the empty seat across from Carol. The dining table is small and intimate, with room to sit four. Carol has Zosia on one side of her and Koumba on the other.

    Zosia leans forward slightly, taking in the meal. “This looks amazing.”

    “I’m sure it tastes even better,” Koumba says easily, already cutting into his food. “Manousos is a most excellent chef. Especially for someone with no formal training.”

    Manousos ducks his head slightly at that, a faint flush creeping into his cheeks as he focuses a little too intently on his plate.

    Carol glances between them, then lifts her glass. “Well,” she says, her voice warm, “thank you. All of you. And, to good company.”

    The others raise their glasses. “To good company,” they all echo.

    Koumba lifts his glass again with a grin. “And to Carol.”

    Carol rolls her eyes, but she’s smiling as she raises her glass again. “Alright, alright.”

    They toast again, the mood light and easy as they settle into the meal.

    Conversation flows between bites—nothing heavy, nothing forced. Small stories, quiet laughter, the occasional teasing comment that lands without sting. The kind of evening that feels… safe.

    By the time the plates from the main course are cleared, Carol is leaning back slightly in her chair, more relaxed than she’d expected to be.

    Koumba finishes topping up everyone’s wine glasses—Zosia still nursing her iced tea—before he claps his hands together lightly. “Right,” he says brightly. “Now we get to the important part.”

    Carol narrows her eyes. “What part?”

    “The gifts, of course.” Koumba grins.

    Carol groans softly, already shaking her head. “You guys didn’t need to—”

    “Nonsense,” Koumba cuts in, already on his feet. “Gifts are the best part of birthdays!” He disappears from the room before Carol can argue further.

    She exhales, dragging a hand down her face. “I hate birthdays.”

    Zosia smiles faintly beside her, her shoulder brushing Carol’s. “You’ll survive.”

    Koumba returns a moment later, arms full of gift bags in various sizes and colours. He sets them carefully in front of Carol.

    “The blue one is mine,” he says. “Should you wish to start there.”

    Carol huffs a quiet laugh despite herself and reaches for it. She pulls out a bottle wrapped in tissue paper, peeling it back to reveal the label. Her expression shifts instantly.

    “Writer’s Tears,” she says, a grin spreading across her face. “Koumba, thank you! This is my favourite.”

    “I know,” he says, pleased.

    Carol glances sideways at Zosia, who is very deliberately not looking at her.

    “Right,” Carol says, amused. “Thank you. Both of you.”

    She sets the bottle down carefully and reaches for the next bag—a bright yellow one.

    “That one is from me,” Manousos says quietly.

    Carol opens it, pulling out a sleek, weighty pen. She turns it in her hand, testing the balance.

    “Fancy,” she says.

    Manousos shrugs lightly. “Zosia mentioned you’re writing again.”

    Carol snorts. “Do you all have a group chat about me or something?” But she’s smiling as she says it, her thumb brushing over the mother-of-pearl inlay of the pen. “Seriously, though. It’s great, thank you.”

    She sets it aside and reaches for the last bag—the tallest of the three. Zosia watches her closely now, something a little more nervous in her expression. Carol pulls out a cardboard tube, turning it over in her hands.

    “I hope you like it,” Zosia says.

    Carol slides the cap off and tips the contents into her hand, two posters rolled tightly together. She unrolls the first one carefully, and her eyebrows lift.

    It’s the poster from the doctor’s office. The one she’d complained about ending after the first two trimesters. She chuckles, already reaching for the second. When she unrolls it, her laughter breaks.

    “The third trimester,” she says, delighted as she turns to look at Zosia beside her. “You’re kidding me? You found this?”

    Zosia smiles, her face lighting up at Carol’s expression of disbelief. “I asked Doctor Beeken’s receptionist where I could order them.”

    Carol shakes her head, still smiling. “I love it! I actually love it.” She looks between the two posters like she’s already picturing them hanging up in her house. “I’m framing these beauties. They’re going in my office.”

    She turns toward Zosia without thinking, gratitude warm and immediate, and leans in to kiss her. Zosia blinks, surprised, but melts into it anyway, her hand lifting instinctively toward Carol’s arm.

    The moment lingers—then Carol pulls back, remembering where she is. Her expression shifts almost instantly, panic flickering across her face as she glances at Koumba, then Manousos.

    “Oh—uh—” she says, flustered, turning quickly. “Thank you, Koumba.” She leans over and presses a quick, chaste kiss to his bearded cheek.

    Koumba chuckles, clearly entertained by her attempt to distract from the fact she just openly kissed Zosia in front of them. Carol half rises, turning toward Manousos. “And—”

    “Please don’t,” he says quickly, holding up a hand to stop her, his expression stone cold.

    Koumba laughs outright at that, slapping the table with his hand as he shakes his head. Carol freezes, caught somewhere between embarrassment and the all-consuming compulsion to flee.

    Zosia reaches for her hand as Carol stands there—hovering with indecision—her fingers sliding between Carol’s, thumb brushing gently over the inside of her wrist.

    “Carol,” she says lightly, and it breaks her from her trance.

    Carol looks down at her, at their conjoined hands.

    “We’re among friends,” Zosia adds. “No judgement.”

    Carol hesitates. Zosia can see it written all over her face, the instinct to pull back, to close off, to laugh it away—but she doesn’t.

    Instead, Carol exhales slowly and sits back down properly, her fingers tightening around Zosia’s. “Right,” she says, a little more firmly than she feels. “Okay.”

    She glances at the others, then back at their joined hands. Clears her throat. “Zosia and I, we’re… dating,” she says. “Kind of, sort of…”

    There’s a beat, then Koumba laughs, warm and easy. “Carol, I could have told you this long ago,” he says, a soft kind of mirth in his eyes.

    Carol groans, her face flushing. Zosia just smiles, something proud and endearing in her expression as she squeezes Carol’s hand.

    Manousos offers a small, patient smile. “I’m glad,” he says quietly, “to see you happy, Carol.”

    Carol shifts in her seat, clearly uncomfortable with the attention, and reaches for her glass. She takes a long sip of wine, buying herself a second before she has to reply.

    “Right,” she says, setting it down. “Enough about me.”

    She gestures between the two men. “What’s going on with you two? Koumba seems to be over here an awful lot lately.”

    Manousos goes very still, his gaze dropping immediately to the table. Koumba, by contrast, looks entirely unbothered, practically gloating as he meets Carol’s eye and winks.

    “We’ve been spending time together,” he says, a small, knowing smile playing at his lips. “I very much enjoy Manousos’ company.”

    Manousos flushes deeper, refusing to look up. “Who wants dessert?” he says quickly, already half rising from his chair.

    Koumba chuckles, his eyes following the other man out of the room, but doesn’t push it.

    The evening continues, the warmth of it settling deeper now, threaded with something just a little more honest than before, as Carol sits with her hand in Zosia’s while Manousos returns with dessert—four plates of a chocolate tart that looks suspiciously like Zosia’s.
