    It’s late by the time they step back out into the quiet cul-de-sac. The night has settled fully now, the air cooler than before, carrying that faint hum of insects and the distant buzz of street lamps overhead. The world feels still as the city lights twinkle below them.

    Carol links her arm through Zosia’s as they walk across the road, her grip loose but warm, the faint buzz of wine sitting comfortably under her skin. She’s not drunk, just buzzed enough to blunt some of her harder edges.

    She carries the gift bags in her other hand, unlinks from Zosia, and fishes her keys out of her pocket.

    “Remind me,” she murmurs, glancing sideways at Zosia, “to thank them again tomorrow.”

    Zosia smiles. “You already thanked them about five times.”

    “Still…” Carol says, but doesn’t attempt to finish the thought—an absent smile playing on her lips.

    They reach the door, and Carol fumbles briefly with the keys before getting it open. The house greets them with quiet familiarity as the door clicks shut behind them.

    Carol doesn’t hesitate. She turns, setting the bags down carelessly by the door, and pulls Zosia in, kissing her like she’s been holding it back all evening. “I’ve been dying to do that all night,” she mutters against her mouth.

    Zosia chuckles, her hands settling at Carol’s waist. “Since you kissed me at the table?”

    Carol pulls back just enough to groan. “I cannot believe I did that.”

    “You forgot yourself,” Zosia says, amused.

    Carol huffs. “Clearly.”

    Zosia leans in again, kissing her slower this time, something softer threaded through it. “I’m glad you did,” she adds quietly. “And… I appreciated what you said. About us.”

    Carol’s expression shifts slightly, something more serious flickering through. “Yeah?”

    Zosia nods. “I know it’s not easy for you, even with people you trust.”

    Carol exhales, her hand sliding up to rest at the back of Zosia’s neck. “I’m trying,” she says. “To do better—to be better.” Her thumb brushes lightly along Zosia’s jaw. “You make that easier.”

    Zosia’s smile brightens, and she leans into her, kissing her again. It lingers this time, deeper, unhurried, Carol’s hands settling at her hips, grounding and familiar.

    When Carol pulls back, her gaze drops briefly, then lifts again, something playful returning. “The posters were perfect,” she says, her mouth curving. “But I seem to remember there was talk of another present…”

    Zosia’s expression shifts, a spark of mischief lighting in her eyes. “Oh, yes,” she says. “I almost forgot.”

    She takes a small step back. Slowly, deliberately. Carol expects her to turn and go back out to Koumba’s car for the gift. Instead she slips the straps of her dress down her shoulders, letting the fabric fall in a soft whisper to the floor at her feet.

    Carol’s breath catches.

    Zosia stands there in red lace lingerie, delicate and deliberate, the lines of it framing her body. For a second, she just watches Carol. Then, almost instinctively, her hand moves to her stomach, a flicker of self-consciousness breaking through. “Is this… okay?” she asks, quieter now.

    Carol doesn’t answer immediately.

    She looks like she’s forgotten how.

    Then she exhales, a small, disbelieving laugh escaping her as she steps forward. “Zosia, it’s—It’s perfect,” she says, her voice a low growl as she adds, “You are so fucking perfect.”

    Zosia lets out a small sigh of relief, some of the doubt slipping away as Carol’s hands find her. Her touch is gentle, careful but certain as she lowers herself slightly, her attention drawn instinctively to the curve of Zosia’s stomach.

    The moment shifts, the space between them charged with something heavier, something harder to ignore. Zosia exhales shakily, her hand sliding into Carol’s hair.

    Carol’s lips brush against the swell of Zosia’s stomach as she gets on her knees. Soft, familiar, reverent in a way that makes Zosia’s fingers tighten slightly in Carol’s hair.

    Carol doesn’t stop there; her lips move, trailing teasing, feather-light kisses across bare skin—and then lace as Carol’s mouth moves further down, finding Zosia’s centre. She mouths at her through the material, eliciting a sharp gasp from the younger woman.

    Carol glances up, her eyes dark and smouldering, her mouth curving faintly. “Bed?” she asks.

    Zosia nods immediately, pupils blown wide and breath catching as she reaches for Carol’s chin, urging her to her feet. “Bed,” she says, “Now.”

    Chapter 22

    Chapter Notes

    Hi folks,

    I've had a lot of requests to include smut, but I wanted to keep the fic non-graphic, so instead I wrote a companion piece that picks up at the end of the last chapter. It's called 'Happy Birthday, Baby' if you'd like to read it and you can find it on my profile.

    As always, thank you for reading and for all the love this fic is getting. I read every comment, even if I don't reply. I'm mostly used to lurking in fandoms, but I'm trying to get better at interacting, and everyone I've encountered in the Pluribus fandom has been super friendly and supportive 😊

    - Tan


    The classroom smells faintly of antiseptic wipes and powdery-sweet baby lotion. A semicircle of plastic chairs and tables faces a demonstration table at the front, where Maureen had, not ten minutes ago, expertly swaddled a doll with the kind of confidence that made it look deceptively simple.

    It hadn’t been.

    Carol had managed to ‘swaddle’ her own hand in with the doll.

    Twice.

    Now, the dolls are in their arms, soft-bodied and oddly weighted, dressed in nothing but small cotton vests as the class moves on.

    “Alright,” Maureen says, clapping her hands together lightly to get the class’s attention. “Everyone did terrific with the swaddling, now we’re going to move on to diapers.”

    This week’s prenatal class is focused on the basics of newborn care, and it’s making Carol painfully aware of just how little time she has left to learn all of this stuff. Zosia is thirty weeks now, already three-quarters of the way through her pregnancy, and Carol doesn’t feel anywhere close to being ready.

    She glances down at the doll in her lap, with its unnerving bald plastic head, then at the diaper in her hands like it’s a puzzle she never agreed to solve—except she did.

    Carol willingly agreed to all of this.

    If that doesn’t prove she’s a masochist—well, she isn’t sure what would.

    “Don’t look so worried. You’ve got this,” Zosia murmurs beside her as they both stand up, amusement already threading through her voice as she knocks her shoulder into Carol’s.

    Carol snorts, trying to hide her apprehension. “I wrote four books with a complex geopolitical system. I think I can handle a diaper.”

    “Mm,” Zosia hums. “We’ll see.”

    Carol shoots her a look, then sets about it. She places the baby doll on the table and unfolds the diaper with more determination than technique.

    “Okay,” she mutters, mostly to herself. “This goes under…”

    She lifts the doll’s legs, slightly too high, and the head tips back in a way that feels vaguely accusatory. “Fuck me—”

    Zosia bites back a laugh. “Careful, you’ll give her whiplash.”

    Carol pulls a face. “Zos, it’s a doll.”

    “It won’t be in ten weeks,” Zosia points out, a hand at her stomach as she rubs it, trying to soothe the squirming baby inside.

    Carol ignores the jibe, she’s under enough stress as it is with the damn doll, tongue pressing briefly against the inside of her cheek as she tries to line things up. The tabs don’t quite meet where she expects them to. She adjusts. Then adjusts again.

    Zosia leans in slightly, her hair tickling Carol’s cheek. “You’re overthinking it.”

    “I am not!”

    “You are.” Zosia laughs. It’s light and musical and eases some of the tension in Carol’s shoulders.

    Carol huffs, refastens one side with a decisive motion, then the other. She smooths the front with both hands like she’s squaring up a manuscript draft.

    “There,” she says, standing back to admire her work. “Perfect.”

    Zosia tilts her head, studying the doll and the diaper closely. There’s a pause, then she says, “I don’t think that’s right.”

    Carol turns to her, affronted. “It’s absolutely right.”

    Zosia raises a brow. “You’re sure about that?”

    Carol lifts her chin slightly. “I’d bet my truck on it.”

    Maureen makes her way over just then, her expression warm and encouraging as she glances between them. “How are we getting on over here ladies?”

    Carol gestures to the doll with a small flourish. “Easy-breezy.”

    Maureen leans in, inspecting it with a practiced eye. She nods. “Very nice, Carol.”

    Carol’s mouth curves as she crosses her arms over her chest, satisfaction landing immediately. She glances sideways at Zosia, smug. “See?”

    Maureen continues, “Just a couple of pointers… it’s on back to front. The little animals should be on the back.”

    Carol freezes.

    “And—usually—we put the diaper under the vest. It’s less messy that way.”

    Zosia bursts out laughing at that. Not a polite laugh—full, bright, unrestrained. It spills out of her, shoulders shaking as she tries and fails to contain it.

    Carol stares at the doll like it’s betrayed her. “You’re kidding me? How the fuck did I miss that?”

    Maureen smiles kindly, patting her shoulder. “You can’t imagine how many dads I’ve seen make the same mistake.”

    Carol exhales sharply through her nose, deflating as she peels the tabs back open. “Unbelievable,” she mutters as Maureen walks off to check on the next couple.

    Beside her, Zosia is laughing so hard she has tears in her eyes. A hand comes up to cover her mouth, though it does nothing to muffle the sound of her laughter.

    ‘She fucking knew,’ Carol thinks to herself.

    “Don’t think you’re getting my truck. I can’t believe you—” Carol starts, but whatever she’s about to say gets lost as Zosia’s laughter abruptly cuts off.

    Her hand drops from her mouth to her stomach.

    The shift is immediate.

    Carol sees it before she understands it—the way Zosia’s expression tightens, her posture going still, breath catching slightly as her palm presses more firmly over the curve of her belly.

    “Zos?” Carol’s voice sharpens, all trace of teasing gone.

    Zosia inhales slowly through her nose, using her other hand to brace herself against the table. “Just—” She starts, but doesn’t finish as the feeling of something pulling tight across her abdomen cuts her off again, her fingers splaying slightly as if she can map the disturbance from the outside.

    It lasts—

    Long enough for Carol’s pulse to spike.

    Then it eases.

    Zosia exhales, the tension leaving her shoulders as quickly as it came. She blinks, grounding herself again as the vice-like grip on her stomach disappears and the throbbing pain subsides to a dull ache.

    Carol is already leaning toward her. “What was that? Are you okay? Is the baby okay?”

    “We’re okay,” Zosia says, her voice quieter now, more measured. Strained. She shifts, stepping back and easing into the seat behind her. “It just—felt tight. Like… a cramp. Across here.” Her hand moves slightly to demonstrate.

    Maureen, her attention drawn back to them by the commotion, steps away from Laxmi and her husband and comes back over to them. “What did it feel like, Zosia? Exactly?”

    Zosia glances up at her. “Tight. Uncomfortable. Not sharp. It went away after… thirty seconds, maybe?”

    Maureen nods, calm and reassuring. “That sounds like a Braxton Hicks.”

    Carol frowns. “A what now?”

    “Braxton Hicks. They’re sometimes called ‘practice contractions,’” Maureen explains, her tone easy. “They’re perfectly normal, just your body preparing for labour, Zosia. They can feel like tightening or mild cramping, they’re usually irregular and not too painful.”

    Zosia listens, her hand still resting protectively over her stomach, while Carol pulls a face. Maureen might be a professional, but Carol has trouble taking a woman she’s seen in full pirate cosplay seriously. “Are you sure about that? That seemed like a lot of pain for a practice contraction.”

    “They’re completely normal,” Maureen continues. “We’ll cover them properly next week when we talk about late-stage pregnancy and preparation. The key thing is that they stay irregular.”

    Carol leans forward, her hand finding Zosia’s shoulder. “And if they don’t?”

    “If they become regular,” Maureen says, meeting her gaze, “especially if they’re coming every ten minutes or so, then you need to get Zosia checked out immediately. But one or two like that, spaced out? That’s nothing to worry about.”

    Carol’s face twists. “Easy for you to say.”

    Zosia nods, reaching up to squeeze the hand Carol has on her shoulder. “Thank you.”

    Maureen gives her a reassuring smile before moving on to the next couple.

    Carol doesn’t move.

    She’s still watching Zosia, her brow furrowed. “Are you sure you’re alright?”

    Zosia glances at her, softer now as she pulls her hand back from Carol’s. “I’m sure.”

    Carol exhales, but the tension doesn’t fully leave her. “Maybe we should call Manousos and Koumba, tell them not to come over later?”

    Zosia blinks. “What? Why? I’m fine.”

    “We can reschedule.” Carol insists, “It’s just painting the nursery, it’s not like it’s urgent—”

    “It it,” Zosia cuts in, her tone gentle but firm. “The baby is going to be here soon, Carol.”

    “I know, but—”

    “And the furniture is arriving next week,” Zosia continues. “The room needs to be ready.”

    Carol hesitates, her instinct to protect Zosia clashing with the innate need to make her happy.

    Zosia can see the internal battle waging on her face, and she reaches for her hand again, squeezing it lightly. “It was one little practice contraction. I’m fine.”

    Carol studies her for a moment, then sighs. “Okay. Fine, but you’re only allowed to supervise. Whilst sitting down.”

    Zosia’s mouth curves faintly. “Supervising?”

    “Yes,” Carol says, settling back slightly. “You sit. You point. You instruct. You don’t lift anything heavier than your hand.”

    Carol looks around to make sure nobody is paying them any attention, drops her voice, leans in close, and adds, “And you let me fuss over you for the rest of the day. Your every whim. Got it?”

    Zosia gives a quiet laugh, breaking into a full grin. “I think I can live with those terms.”

    Carol nods once, still not entirely convinced, but conceding anyway. Then she glances down at the doll on the table in front of them, still wearing the diaper over its vest and staring up at her with vacant, accusatory plastic eyes.

    “…stupid doll,” she mutters.

    Zosia laughs again, softer this time, her thumb brushing lightly over the back of Carol’s hand from where she sits.

    “Good thing you’ve got time to practise.”

    The carpet of Zosia’s spare bedroom lies completely hidden beneath sheets of crinkled plastic that shift underfoot with every step. The walls, like the rest of the room, are bare now. Stripped of anything personal. Reduced to a blank canvas, neutral and waiting.

    Carol kneels in the middle of it all, a flathead screwdriver wedged under the lip of the paint can on the floor in front of her. She pries it open with a firm twist, the lid giving way with a dull pop.

    Her phone buzzes, forgotten somewhere behind her on the floor.

    Carol ignores it.

    She peers inside the paint can as she places the lid aside and frowns. “This is yellow.”

    Zosia stands just behind her, one hand resting lightly on Carol’s shoulder, the other curved instinctively over her stomach. She leans slightly to look.

    “Yes, it is,” she says, amused. “I told you I was going with yellow.”

    Carol glances up at her, unimpressed. “Yeah, but it’s really yellow. I thought you meant, like, I dunno, pastel yellow?”

    Zosia laughs, the sound soft and easy. “I read that babies start seeing colours in the first few months. Yellow is one of the first ones they recognise.”

    Carol considers that, then shrugs as she dips the screwdriver back in to loosen the lid fully. At least she isn’t the one who has to wake up to it every morning. “I guess it’s better than pink.”

    “I like pink,” Zosia says, arching a brow.

    Carol looks up at her again, something teasing flickering across her face as she pushes herself up slightly on her knees. “Of course you do.”

    Zosia leans down just enough for Carol to meet her halfway, and Carol takes the opportunity, pressing a quick kiss to her lips.

    Zosia huffs a quiet laugh against her mouth.

    Behind them, Carol’s phone buzzes again.

    And then again.

    Persistent.

    She exhales through her nose and reaches back, blindly patting around on the floor behind her until she finds it. A quick glance at the screen shows her half a dozen Reddit notifications before she places it face down again at her side.

    “The fanss are really going at it in the Wycaro subreddit,” she mutters. “Probably another thrilling discussion about what exact shade of purple the slipsand is.”

    Before Zosia can comment, the front door opens downstairs and Koumba’s voice carries up through the house.

    “Hello?” he calls out. “We’re here!”

    Zosia straightens, turning toward the door. “Upstairs!” she calls back.

    Footsteps follow, heavier now, the sound of them climbing the stairs. A moment later, Koumba appears in the doorway first.

    Carol blinks.

    He’s dressed in dark jeans and a simple black T-shirt, a six-pack of beer hooked easily over the fingers of his right hand. It’s the most down-to-earth she’s ever seen him look, not a splash of extravagant colour anywhere. Carol has to fight the sudden urge to throw the yellow paint at him.

    “Well,” she says, openly appraising him, “this is new. I think this is the first time I’ve seen you not wearing colour.”

    Koumba grins as he steps further into the room. “First and last, my friend. All of this black is already making me depressed.”

    Manousos follows behind him, dressed in overalls that already have patches of paint all over them, and offers both women a serious nod. “Hello.”

    “Hey, Manny,” Carol says, pushing herself to her feet fully now. “Thanks for coming, guys. You know what they say, many hands make my life easier.” She grins, glad she’d mentioned Zosia asking her to help paint the nursery to Manousos, who had immediately offered to help. Koumba joining them had been a natural progression.

    Her phone buzzes again on the floor beside her, notification after notification filing in, one after the other.

    Koumba’s eyes flick down to it, then back up to her with a grin. “Someone is popular today.”

    Carol waves it off, taking the beer he offers her. “Google alerts. My readers lose their minds every time someone mentions my books online.”

    “Ah,” Koumba says, amused. “The burden of fame.”

    “Hardly,” Carol mutters, cracking the beer open. She picks up her phone again, holds the side button until the screen goes dark, then tosses it onto the windowsill. “There. Problem solved.”

    Koumba frowns, horrified at the idea of turning his own phone off. “What if someone needs to contact you?”

    Carol shrugs. “The two people who call me the most are both standing in this room. I think I’m good.”

    With that, she takes a long swig of the ice-cold beer. Beside her, Zosia sighs. She folds her arms loosely over her growing stomach. “Not too many of those,” she says. “I would like the paint on the walls, not all over the floor.”

    Zosia smiles at him before turning her attention to her brother-in-law. “That goes for you too, Koumba. I still remember the mess you and Malick made of my kitchen when we first got the house.”

    Carol snorts. “Right. No hard liquor until we’re done painting. Got it, boss.” She gives a mock salute, grinning as Zosia rolls her eyes.

    “No hard liquor at all,” she murmurs, her lips brushing against the shell of Carol’s ear, “I’d like you marginally sober and awake in bed tonight.”

    She presses a quick kiss to Carol’s cheek before stepping back, expression the picture of innocence while Carol’s brain melts inside her skull.

    Carol clears her throat, cheeks warming as she takes a very deliberate small sip of her beer. “Copy that,” she mutters.

    The moment passes, quickly forgotten as they fall into the work.

    Plastic shifts underfoot, the soft scrape of paint rollers against trays filling the room. Carol and Koumba take to the walls, long, steady strokes of bright yellow climbing over the neutral paint beneath. Manousos crouches near the edges, carefully taping along the baseboards with quiet focus before following up with crisp white paint.

    The smell of fresh paint settles into the air, sharp but clean, mixing with the faint scent of beer and the occasional burst of laughter when someone inevitably gets paint dripped on them.

    Zosia drifts in and out, eventually retreating downstairs to put together something for them to eat, her presence still felt even when she’s gone.

    The hours stretch and the light shifts. By the time they’re done, the room feels and looks entirely different.

    Brighter.

    Warmer.

    Ready for new life.

    Carol steps back, roller in hand, scanning the walls with a critical eye before nodding once. “Not bad.”

    Koumba glances around, equally satisfied. “Not bad at all.”

    Manousos peels off the last strip of tape with careful precision, straightening as he looks over their work. He gives a satisfied nod of his head.

    Zosia reappears in the doorway, pausing as she takes it in. Her face softens almost immediately. “Oh,” she breathes.

    Carol watches her reaction closely.

    Zosia steps inside slowly, turning in place, her eyes moving over the freshly painted walls. “I love it,” she says, turning back to them with a smile that lights up her face and makes Carol’s heart flutter. “Thank you so much.”

    She thanks all of them, but when she crosses the room it’s Carol’s cheek that she kisses, before pulling her in for a hug.

    Carol huffs softly, but there’s no real resistance as she melts into Zosia’s embrace.

    Still—

    Zosia pulls back, frowning faintly. “Missing? I’m sure it will look fine once the furniture is all in.”

    “Maybe.” Carol shakes her head. Shoots Koumba a conspiratorial look. “What do you think?”

    Koumba looks around, hand on his chin as if he’s considering the plight of the human condition, and not just some paint on the wall. He gives a resolute nod. “She’s right. There’s something missing.”

    Zosia looks between them, more confused than ever. “What are you talking about?”

    Manousos smiles faintly. “I think I might have what you’re looking for,” he says stiffly. Glancing at Koumba, he adds, “Will you help me?”

    “Of course,” Koumba jumps immediately. They head out without further explanation, footsteps fading down the stairs and out the front door.

    Zosia turns back to Carol, narrowing her eyes slightly. “What’s going on?”

    Carol just grins at her. “Be patient.”

    Zosia exhales a small laugh, shaking her head as she waits. A minute passes, then the sound of footsteps returns—heavier this time, more deliberate.

    Carol steps behind Zosia as they near the doorway. “Close your eyes.”

    Zosia laughs. “Carol—”

    “Trust me.”

    Zosia does, her eyes sliding shut as she shakes her head, still smiling. Carol rises slightly onto her toes, hands coming up to cover Zosia’s eyes just to be sure. “No peeking.”

    “What is this?” Zosia asks, laughter threading through her voice.

    “You’ll see.”

    Manousos and Koumba manoeuvre something into the centre of the room, careful and slow. The only clue Zosia gets as to what it is is the soft scrape of wood against plastic as they set it down.

    Carol waits until they’ve stepped back, then she lowers her hands. “Okay,” she says. “You can open ’em now.”

    Zosia’s eyes flutter open. They land on the centre of the room—and she freezes.

    A soft gasp escapes her, her hand flying to her mouth. She can’t quite believe her eyes.

    A cot sits there, solid wood, intricate carvings winding along the rails. It’s the same one she’d stood in front of weeks ago, the one she’d quietly dismissed as too much.

    Too impractical.

    Too expensive.

    Her eyes flick between it and Carol, disbelief written plainly across her face.

    “You—” She doesn’t finish the sentence. Instead, she turns and throws her arms around Carol, holding her tight and burying her face in Carol’s neck. “Thank you,” she breathes. “It’s perfect.”

    Carol laughs softly, arms coming up to wrap around her. “Yeah,” she murmurs. “Thought it might be.”

    The bedroom is dim, lit only by the soft glow of a single bedside lamp. The air still carries a trace of fresh paint from earlier, mixed now with the familiar scent of clean linen and Zosia’s shampoo.

    Carol stands at the edge of the bed, tugging the throw pillows free one by one and tossing them unceremoniously onto the floor.

    “I still don’t get these things,” she says, shaking her head, lobbing the last one aside with a soft thud. “What’s the point? You put them on in the morning just to throw ’em off again at night.”

    Zosia stands in the doorway of the ensuite, toothbrush in hand, watching her with quiet amusement. Foam gathers at the corner of her mouth as she tries—and fails—not to smile.

    Carol glances back at her. “It’s like the textbook definition of insanity.”

    Zosia raises a brow, entirely unconcerned, then disappears back into the bathroom. The sound of running water follows while she spits and rinses her mouth out, before the tap clicks off again.

    She flicks the bathroom light off as she steps back into the bedroom, closing the door to the ensuite behind her.

    “The point,” she says, her voice light and teasing, “is that they look pretty.”

    Carol watches her for a beat, eyes raking over her as she stands in nothing more than an oversized T-shirt, and shrugs. “Still not convinced.”

    Zosia smiles, crossing the room. She leans in to press a quick kiss to Carol’s cheek—

    —but Carol turns at the last second, deliberately catching her mouth instead. The kiss deepens easily. Familiar now. Wanted.

    Carol’s arms slip around her waist, drawing her closer, one hand settling instinctively at the small of her back, the other at her hip. Zosia leans into it, her body softening against Carol’s, a quiet hum escaping her as the kiss lingers.

    When she pulls back, she’s smiling. “Wide awake I see.”

    Carol huffs a quiet laugh, her thumb brushing lightly along Zosia’s side. “It would take a lot more than a couple of beers to have me falling asleep on you.”

    Zosia’s smile lingers, but it softens as Carol’s attention shifts. Her hand moves, pressing lightly against the curve of Zosia’s stomach.

    “You doing okay?” Carol asks, quieter now. “No more… contractions?”

    Zosia shakes her head gently. “I’m fine. I haven’t felt anything out of the ordinary all day. Just her,” she adds, her hand covering Carol’s for a moment. “She’s really kicking.”

    Carol exhales, some of the tension easing from her shoulders as she feels the baby moving under her palm. “Good. But you tell me right away if it happens again, okay? Don’t wait. Even if it’s the middle of the night.”

    “I will,” Zosia promises.

    Carol nods once, then adds, almost absently, “Oh, that reminds me, I downloaded an app. Tracks symptoms, contractions… it’s supposed to help spot early signs of labour.”

    Zosia’s mouth curves, amused. “Of course you did.”

    Carol ignores her teasing tone, reaches automatically toward the bedside table—then pauses.

    “Hold on—” She turns, scanning the room before spotting her jeans draped over the back of the chair near the dresser. “There it is.”

    She crosses the room, picking up the jeans—covered in splatters of dry yellow paint—and fishes her phone from out of one of the back pockets.

    The moment she turns it on, the screen lighting up, her expression shifts. A frown settles in as she stares incredulously at the lump of plastic in her hand.

    Zosia notices the change in her demeanour immediately. She steps closer, slipping her arms around the shorter woman from behind, her chin resting lightly against Carol’s shoulder. “What’s wrong?”

    Carol doesn’t answer straight away.

    Her thumb scrolls once.

    Twice.

    Endless notifications.

    Missed calls.

    A lot of them.

    Her jaw tightens.

    Zosia’s hold on her shifts slightly, less playful now, more grounding. “Carol?”

    Carol exhales slowly, her shoulders stiff beneath Zosia’s arms. “I don’t know,” she says quietly, her frown deepening as she takes it all in. Notifications from her social media accounts. Mentions. Tags. Messages.

    And beneath them—

    Missed calls.

    Val.

    Margaux.

    Over and over again all afternoon.

    Something cold settles low in her stomach.

    “Carol,” Zosia says again, softer now. “What is it? What’s going on?”

    Carol shakes her head, pulling gently free from Zosia’s arms, not unkindly but with a kind of distracted urgency.

    “I—” She swallows. “I don’t know. I need to call Val.”

    She starts pacing without thinking, already tapping the screen, bringing the phone to her ear as it rings.

    Zosia watches her, concern settling in her chest as she lowers herself onto the edge of the bed, hands resting loosely over her stomach. The last time she saw Carol looking this stressed was that first day— at the hospital.

    The room feels different now, like all the oxygen has been sucked out

    The line rings.

    Once.

    Twice.

    Three times—

    Then it clicks, Val’s voice greeting her immediately as she picks up. “Just when were you going to tell me that girl of yours is fucking pregnant?”

    Carol stops pacing.

    Goes Completely still as the words land heavy and sharp, enough to knock the breath from her lungs.

    Her gaze lifts slowly—

    —and meets Zosia’s.

    “Fuck.”

    Chapter 23

    Chapter Notes

    Apologies for the cliffhanger at the end of last chapter and the distress caused, hopefully more Carol tummy kisses will make up for it😅Also, I've changed the chapter count again so we're going to be closer to 30.

    As always, thank you for reading and supporting this fic.

    - Tan


    Carol doesn’t speak straight away.

    For a second, she just stands there in the middle of the room, phone pressed to her ear, eyes locked on Zosia’s. The weight of Val’s outburst hangs heavy in the air, thick and suffocating.

    Then she exhales, sharp and controlled. “Yeah,” she says into the phone in response of something Val says. Something Zosia can’t make out. Carol’s voice is quieter now, stripped of anything casual. “Yeah, I figured.”

    Zosia can only watch, unable to hear Val’s side of the conversation as Carol turns slightly, pacing once across the room, a hand dragging through her hair as she listens.

    “No—I didn’t tell you because it wasn’t any of your business,” she says, a little sharper this time, then pauses, jaw tightening as Val talks over her. “I know—I know, Val. I get it.”

    She glances at Zosia again, something apologetic flickering across her face. “Fine,” she says after a beat. “Fine, we can meet.”

    “Tomorrow morning,” Carol repeats, then lets out a small shaky breath. “Do you want to meet for brunch or—” She cuts off, listening again, her expression flattening slightly. “Your office,” she says, nodding once. “Right. Nine a.m.”

    Zosia hears the crackle of Val’s raised voice on the other end of the line but still can’t make out the words. Carol hesitates this time, her eyes flicking toward Zosia again.

    “And bring—” she starts, then stops, swallowing slightly before finishing, “bring Zosia.” Carol looks at Zosia fully now, something like a question in her eyes.

    Zosia holds her gaze for a moment, then nods. Carol exhales, a fraction of the tension leaving her shoulders. “Yeah,” she says into the phone. “We’ll be there. See you then.”

    She hangs up before Val can get another word in, and the silence that settles in the room feels heavy and oppressive.

    Carol lowers the phone slowly, lets herself slump onto the bed, the mattress dipping beneath her weight. Her shoulders slump, all the energy draining out of her in one go as she sits with her back to Zosia.

    For a moment, she just sits there, taking everything in. Zosia hovers behind her, giving her space. Then Carol’s thumb moves and the screen of her phone lights up again.

    She scrolls, and scrolls.

    Thread after thread.

    Notifications stacked on top of each other. Mentions. Tags. Reddit posts. Speculation. The words blur together at first—then sharpen into something far more concrete.

    Fertility clinic.
    Wrong embryos.
    Carol Sturka.
    Zosia—

    Her stomach twists. Zosia’s full name is there. Her photo too. An old one, pulled from some article about the success of the restaurant last summer. Smiling. Unaware.

    Exposed.

    Carol’s grip tightens around the phone, her jaw clenching hard enough to ache. Anger flares first—hot and immediate.

    Someone leaked absolutely everything.

    The fear follows next, colder in the pit of her stomach. Sharper and more intense. Carol knows what her fanbase can be like at its worst. She’s seen it before.

    When news of her and Helen came out, there had been theories. Entire threads dedicated to tearing Helen apart. Calling her a gold digger. A parasite. Someone using Carol for her fame and money.

    It had been absurd.

    Offensive even.

    Helen had been the one holding them together in those early years, long before Wycaro made Carol Sturka a name anyone cared about.

    Carol swallows hard, her gaze flicking back to Zosia. She can’t imagine that kind of scrutiny turning itself on her.

    On the baby.

    Zosia shifts quietly behind her, closing the distance without drawing attention to it. Her chin settles lightly on Carol’s shoulder, her arms sliding loosely around her waist.

    “What’s going on?” she asks, her voice soft but steady.

    Carol doesn’t soften the blow. She has to be honest. Zosia has to know what to expect. “Someone posted about the clinic,” she says, her voice low, tight. “About them implanting my eggs in you.”

    She swallows again, her thumb still dragging across the screen. “Your name’s out there, Zos. Your full name. And your picture. Where you work—”

    Zosia’s arms tighten slightly around her, but her voice stays even. “Okay,” she says gently. “Okay. We’ll deal with it.”

    Carol shakes her head, her eyes still fixed on the screen. “No, you don’t get it,” she says, more sharply than she means to. “It’s bad, Zosia. It’s really fucking bad.”

    Her scrolling speeds up, hands shaking, each new post feeding the knot tightening in her chest.

    Zosia watches her for a moment, then reaches around, plucking the phone cleanly from Carol’s hand.

    “Hey—”

    Zosia doesn’t argue. She just turns, sets the phone down on the bedside table, and looks back at Carol with a level expression. “No more internet privileges for the rest of the night,” she says, calm but firm.

    Carol blinks at her, the edge of her panic catching on something softer. A reluctant smile tugs at her mouth. “You’re going to make a great mom, you know that?”

    Zosia’s expression warms, though there’s a flicker of worry beneath it. “I know,” she says lightly. “Now come here.” She moves first, climbing onto the bed and settling back against the pillows, then reaching for Carol.

    Carol doesn’t resist.

    She lets herself be pulled down, shifting until her head rests against Zosia’s stomach, the curve of it familiar now, grounding in a way nothing else in her life is.

    Zosia pulls the covers over them both, one hand settling in Carol’s hair, fingers threading through it in slow, soothing strokes.

    Carol exhales, her body finally starting to unclench. Beneath her cheek, there’s movement. A slow, rolling shift. She presses her palm there instinctively, her breathing evening out just a little.

    “Val wants to meet in the morning,” she murmurs. “Nine o’clock. At her office.”

    Zosia hums softly in agreement, fingers still playing in Carol’s hair.

    “She’s pissed,” Carol adds, quieter now. “That I didn’t tell her about you. About the pregnancy.”

    Zosia’s hand pauses for half a second in her hair, then resumes its steady rhythm. “Don’t worry about all of that tonight,” she says gently. “We’ll deal with it tomorrow.”

    Carol lets out a small, disbelieving huff. “God, Zosia, I love you, but—”

    She freezes.

    The words hang in the air between them.

    Carol’s head lifts slightly, her eyes snapping up to Zosia’s, panic flashing across her face as she realises what she’s just said.

    Zosia meets her gaze. She sees it—the fear, the immediate instinct to retreat, to take it back.

    “We’ll deal with it tomorrow,” Zosia repeats, just as softly as before, her fingers continuing to move through Carol’s hair like nothing’s changed.

    Like Carol didn’t just drop an atom bomb between them with those three little words.

    Carol stares at her for a moment longer. Then something in her loosens. Relief, visible and immediate. She exhales, sinking back down, her cheek pressing against Zosia’s stomach again.

    Her lips follow, pressing a series of quiet kisses through the fabric of her T-shirt. Zosia’s hand rests lightly over her hair, her thumb brushing small, absent circles against the back of Carol’s neck.

    She feels content, lying there with Carol beside her, but underneath it, there’s a thread of worry she can’t quite shake. She knows her all too well now. Knows Carol’s nature and how quickly she can shut down—pull away when things become too much.

    They’ve come so far.

    Zosia isn’t sure how fragile that progress still is.

    After a moment, she speaks again. “Can you make me a promise?”

    Carol stills slightly, her lips lingering where they are before she tilts her head just enough to look up. “Depends,” she murmurs. “What am I promising?”

    Zosia’s expression softens, something open and earnest in her gaze. “That you won’t shut me out,” she says softly. “That you won’t try to deal with all of this on your own.”

    Her hand shifts, brushing lightly along Carol’s temple. “We’re in this together. The two of us.”

    Carol watches her for a second, really watches her. Then she smiles. Her left hand lifts, finding Zosia’s, their fingers lacing together like pieces of a puzzle.

    “I promise,” she says, giving Zosia’s hand a small squeeze. Then she adds, in a quieter voice, “And it’s the three of us.” She presses another kiss to Zosia’s stomach, as if to emphasise her point.

    Zosia’s breath catches, just slightly, at the words. It takes her a second to compose herself. “Okay,” she says, lips curling into a smile.

    They lie there like that for a while, the room quiet around them, hands still locked together as the fingers of Zosia’s free hand continue their slow passes through Carol’s hair.

    Carol’s breathing eventually evens out. Her adrenaline fades and her body grows heavier. Zosia feels it—the subtle shift as she starts to drift. Just as her eyes begin to close, Zosia speaks again.

    “Carol?”

    Carol hums faintly. “Mmm?”

    There’s a pause.

    Then—

    “I love you too.”

    Carol goes very still. Then she smiles, soft and unguarded, her lips brushing once more against Zosia’s stomach. For a moment, wrapped up like this, she feels something she hasn’t in a long time.

    Safe.

    Happy.

    Then she thinks about their meeting with Val in the morning, and her stomach turns.

    She should have known it couldn’t last.

    The building housing Val’s office is all glass and polished stone, the kind of place that reflects too much light—too clean and sharp for comfort. Morning sun pours in through the tall windows, bouncing off chrome fixtures and glossy surfaces until everything feels just a little overexposed.

    Carol slows slightly as they step inside, her hand hovering at the small of Zosia’s back without quite touching. The space smells faintly of coffee and expensive perfume, the quiet hum of phones and keyboards threading through the reception area as they venture deeper inside.

    Zosia takes it in with a quiet kind of curiosity, her gaze drifting along the walls lined with framed black-and-white portraits. Authors. Familiar faces. Some she recognises, most she doesn’t.

    She stops, her attention settling on one in particular.

    Carol.

    The photograph is striking. Stark lighting, sharp contrast, Carol’s expression serious, almost severe. There’s something distant in it. Untouchable.

    Zosia studies it for a moment, something soft flickering in her expression. “I like that photo.”

    Carol glances at it, then immediately looks away, a faint flush creeping up her neck. “It’s only there because I make Val money,” she mutters. “Cash cow gets pride of place.”

    The receptionist—young, barely out of her twenties—glances up from behind the desk, caught between politeness and awkwardness. “Hi, can I help you—”

    Carol cuts her off, not unkindly. “What happened to Genevieve?” It’s been months since Carol was last here. Most of her meetings with Val happen over brunch or drinks. Val only ever calls her into her office when she’s in trouble.

    The girl blinks, big brown eyes full of confusion. “I—well, I just started last month, so I’m not—”

    A voice cuts across the room before she can finish. “Genevieve, tell me when Carol Sturka—” Val appears in the doorway of her office, mid-sentence, and stops short when she sees them.

    “Oh. There you are.”

    Her focus moves immediately—past Carol, landing squarely on Zosia.

    And staying there.

    It’s not subtle. Her eyes rakes over Zosia’s body, lingering on the unmistakable curve of her stomach. Her lips press into something that might be a thin smile, or silent disapproval. It’s hard for Zosia to tell.

    Val clicks her tongue once, sharp. “Get in here.”

    Carol exhales through her nose, then glances back at the receptionist. “Your name’s not Genevieve, is it?”

    The girl shakes her head, lowering her voice conspiratorially. “I don’t even think the last Genevieve was a Genevieve.”

    Carol pulls a face. “Yeah, that tracks. Get out while you can.”

    The receptionist smiles despite herself.

    Zosia watches the exchange, something warm flickering in her chest at the ease with which Carol offers the advice.

    Carol turns, and they walk across the wide open reception area to Val’s office, Carol holding the door open for Zosia.

    She steps inside first.

    Val’s office is larger than expected. Bright. Floor-to-ceiling windows behind her desk, more framed covers and awards lining the walls. It feels curated. Controlled.

    Carol closes the door behind them and gestures toward the chairs opposite the desk. Zosia lowers herself carefully into one, smoothing her dress out. Carol takes the seat beside her, her posture already tight.

    Val doesn’t sit immediately.

    She stands behind her desk, arms folded loosely, studying Zosia again with a withering look.

    Zosia shifts under it without meaning to.

    Val frowns. “You look a lot bigger than I did at two weeks’ pregnancy.”

    Zosia blinks. “I—sorry?”

    Val waves a hand. “Well, you can’t possibly be any further along than that, since I just saw Carol two weeks ago and she failed to mention a fucking baby.”

    Zosia’s shoulders draw in slightly, her confidence dipping under the scrutiny.

    Carol sees it.

    And something in her snaps.

    “Hey!” she says sharply, leaning forward. “Take it out on me, Val. Zosia and the baby are off limits.”

    Val doesn’t hesitate.

    “Fine,” she says briskly, dropping into her chair. “You’re a fucking idiot, Sturka.”

    Carol stiffens.

    “How could you not tell me about this?” Val continues, gesturing vaguely toward Zosia like she’s an afterthought in the equation—and not the centre of Carol’s world.

    Carol’s jaw tightens. “She has a name!” she grinds out through clenched teeth.

    Val pauses.

    Carol leans forward further, her voice dropping, dangerous now. “And you’re going to start using it. Or we walk. Both of us. And you can kiss any future Wycaro books goodbye!”

    The room goes still, like all of the air has suddenly been sucked out of it.

    Zosia sits beside her, heart beating a little faster. Not from fear, but from something else entirely.

    Pride.

    —And maybe a tiny bit of arousal.

    She’s never seen Carol like this before.

    Val studies Carol for a second, her wrinkled face stern and serious.

    Then she breaks. A laugh bursts out of her, loud and unrestrained as she sits down and leans back in her chair. “Alright, alright,” she says, holding up her hands. “Jesus, kid, what crawled up your ass this morning?”

    Her focus shifts back to Zosia, but it’s different now. Less sharp. More measured.

    “Zosia,” she says, extending a hand across the desk. “Apologies for my rudeness. I’m Val. It’s nice to finally meet you. Though, I would’ve preferred better circumstances.”

    Zosia takes her hand, her grip firm. “Likewise,” she says, her tone cool as she stares the much older woman down.

    Val’s grin widens. She glances sideways at Carol and winks. “Another dark-haired firecracker. You do have a type, don’t ya?”

    Carol’s expression hardens immediately. “Don’t,” she says flatly.

    Val raises a brow, amused but letting it drop as she raises her hands in front of her, palms out like she’s taming a wild animal. “Fine.”

    Carol leans back slightly, folding her arms. “You wanted us here to talk about the rumours online.”

    Val barks out a laugh. “‘Rumours’?” She points toward Zosia, to the swell of her stomach. “Because that looks like a lot more than a fucking rumour. How far along is she anyway?”

    Zosia answers, “Thirty weeks.”

    Beside her, Carol glares at Val, still unhappy with the way her agent is addressing Zosia. “You’re on thin ice, Val.”

    Val sighs, pushing her glasses up her nose before pinching the bridge of it. “Okay, okay. Fine. What’s done is done.”

    She leans forward, her tone shifting—more businesslike now. “Margaux’s been up online all night trying to do damage control.”

    Carol snorts. “Great.” She doesn’t bother hiding her disdain for her young publicist. If Margaux’s the best they’ve got, then they’re screwed.

    Zosia glances between them, and finally speaks more than two words for the first time since sitting down. “Why do you even need damage control?”

    Val looks at her, considering. “Because this shit could’ve been spun into gold.” She gestures toward Carol. “Widowed author denied last chance at motherhood? That’s a headline. That’s sympathy. That’s book sales.”

    Carol’s mouth tightens, but she says nothing.

    Zosia frowns slightly.

    Val continues, relentless. “Instead, what we’ve got is ‘aging author shacks up to play house with pregnant younger lover.’”

    The words land hard.

    Carol is on her feet before she’s even aware she’s moved. “Fuck this!” she says sharply. “Zosia, we’re done here.”

    She turns, reaching for Zosia’s hand. “Come on.”

    But Zosia doesn’t make any attempt to stand.

    Carol pauses, thrown.

    Zosia is still sitting, her back straight now, eyes locked on Val with a steadiness that doesn’t waver.

    Val watches her, something like interest flickering behind her glasses.

    Zosia speaks, her voice calm but firm. “Carol has done nothing wrong,” she starts, as Carol takes her seat again beside her.

    “We’re both consenting adults,” she continues, her hand resting lightly over her stomach. “And what she’s doing—supporting the woman carrying her child—is admirable.”

    Her attention doesn’t waver from Val, though she can feel Carol’s gaze burning in to her. “I would bet a lot of her readers see it that way too.”

    Val leans back in her chair, studying Zosia for a long moment, then lets out a slow breath through her nose. “They would,” she concedes. “If she was just supporting you.” She looks pointedly between them. “Not fucking you.”

    The words hit harder than anything she’s said so far. They feel like a slap to the face, and Carol’s jaw tightens instantly.

    “That’s it,” she growls, pushing to her feet again, this time with real intent. “Shove your book deal up your ass, Val! We’re done.”

    She turns toward the door—

    —but Zosia’s hand catches hers before she can take a step. Her thumb presses lightly against the inside of Carol’s wrist, right over her pulse, moving in a slow, grounding rhythm.

    Carol stills like Zosia’s found her reset button.

    The anger doesn’t vanish, but it shifts—loses some of its sharp edges, steadied by the contact. She exhales through her nose, body still tense but no longer coiled to snap.

    Zosia doesn’t let go.

    Val watches the exchange closely, eyes sharp behind her glasses, but for once, she keeps her commentary to herself.

    Zosia lifts her chin slightly, her voice calm. “Why is that a problem?”

    Val raises a brow. “Excuse me?”

    “Carol’s fans,” Zosia clarifies. “Why would her being with me be an issue? They already know she was married to a woman.”

    The agent considers this, shrugs. “You’re not wrong. The whole gay thing?” She waves a hand. “Didn’t hurt her sales. If anything, it helped. Carol’s charming when she wants to be, and let’s be honest—half her readership is made up of bored housewives who’d get off to a grilled cheese if it flirted hard enough with them.”

    Carol rolls her eyes but doesn’t interrupt.

    Val continues, more matter-of-fact now. “But this is different. Carol dating a younger woman?” She tilts her head. “That’ll get their backs up. And the pregnancy?” She snorts softly. “People like their romantasy authors hot and mysterious. Not elbow-deep in diapers.”

    Zosia doesn’t flinch. “If that’s the case,” she says evenly, “then Carol’s next book is a problem too.”

    Val pauses, caught off guard.

    Zosia holds her own. “It’s about a pregnant Raban, isn’t it?”

    Carol stiffens slightly beside her.

    “So me being pregnant isn’t a liability,” Zosia continues. “It’s marketing gold.”

    For a moment, Val just stares at her. Then a slow, amused smile spreads across her face.

    “Well, well,” she says, leaning forward slightly, staring at Carol this time. “You let her read the manuscript?”

    Zosia glances at Carol, confused. “Is that—an issue?”

    Carol shifts beside her, uncomfortable now in a new way—not with anger, but ensnarement. She drags her hand free from Zosia’s, crossing her arms over her chest, chin dropping briefly before she finally mutters, “Yeah. I did.”

    Val’s grin widens. “There are exactly two people on God’s green earth Carol Sturka lets read her unpublished work,” she says. “And one of them isn’t with us anymore. God rest her soul.”

    The air shifts.

    Carol goes still, doesn’t dare look over at Zosia. Her shoulders squared, staring somewhere just past her agent’s desk.

    Val’s expression softens a fraction. “Alright,” she says, quieter now. “Sorry, I didn’t realise it was that serious.”

    Zosia turns toward Carol fully.

    For a second, Carol still doesn’t look at her. Then she does, and everything in her expression changes. The tension doesn’t disappear, but something else breaks through it—something open, unguarded, and unmistakably real.

    Zosia swallows at the sight of it.

    Carol steps closer, her hand lifting to rest against Zosia’s shoulder. “It is,” she says, her voice steady despite everything. “And if you’ve got a problem with that—” her eyes flick back to Val, sharpening again—“you can find some other poor schmuck’s picture to hang up in your lobby.”

    There’s a beat, the air in the room growing thick and palpable. Zosia can practically taste the hostility on her tongue.

    Val exhales, leaning back again, her hands lifting in surrender. “Alright,” she says. “Alright. Message received, loud and clear, kid.”

    She picks up a pen that probably cost more than Zosia’s fanciest chef’s knife and taps it lightly against the desk. “We’ll figure something out. We always do.”

    Her tone shifts back into business mode, though there’s still a trace of something more measured beneath it. “I would’ve preferred to control the narrative,” she admits. “Spin it properly. Instead of it blowing up online like this. But—we work with what we’ve got.”

    Carol’s shoulders ease slightly, though the tension hasn’t fully left her. “Do we know who leaked it?”

    Val shakes her head. “Margaux’s digging. The Reddit account that posted it was created yesterday. One post. That’s it.” She shrugs. “Looks deliberate.”

    Carol frowns, her mind already racing. “Could be someone from the fertility clinic, or the prenatal classes. Hell—maybe it’s Maureen.”

    Val’s head snaps up. “Maureen fucking Reynolds?” she demands. “She knew before me?”

    Carol shakes her head. “She runs the class,” she says. “But I don’t think it’s her. We’ve been going for weeks and nothing’s leaked. She doesn’t even know Zosia and I are together.”

    “I guess she does now,” Carol adds with a heavy sigh. “Fucking great.”

    Val mutters something under her breath, then waves it off. “We’ll find out,” she says. “And when we do, I will personally make the fucker wish they were never born.”

    She leans forward again. “In the meantime, we need to get ahead of this. I want you two to sit down for an interview with a friendly journalist who owes me one. We frame it properly. Control the narrative, tease the next book.”

    Carol hesitates. “Do we—is that really necessary?”

    “It’s necessary,” Val counters.

    Carol shakes her head slightly. “I’m not—” She exhales. “Val, I’m not great with being open about my shit. You know that.”

    Zosia glances at her, reading the squaring of her jaw immediately. She knows what this is for Carol. Knows how much she hates her private life being exposed. Scrutinised.

    “Maybe we could take some time?” Zosia says gently, playing devil’s advocate. “Think it through before we decide.”

    Val watches them both, sighs, conceding the point with a small nod. “Fine. But don’t take too long,” she says. “We need to get in front of this thing, and fast.”


    -


    The air outside feels stifling after leaving Val’s carefully conditioned office, the sharp edge of it cutting through the tension that follows them out the door. The glass façade reflects the late morning sun, much too bright and painful to look at.

    She walks beside Zosia in silence, her jaw still tight, her mind clearly somewhere else. The parking lot stretches ahead of them, rows of cars baking gently under the New Mexico sun, heat rising faintly from the asphalt.

    Chapter 24

    Chapter Notes


    The glass doors of Val’s office building slide shut behind them with a soft hiss, sealing them back out into the late afternoon sun.

    Carol doesn’t say anything at first. Her jaw is tight, shoulders set as she and Zosia walk down the street towards Zosia’s distinctive little blue car, her pace just a fraction quicker than usual.

    She unlocks the car and moves automatically, circling to Zosia’s side to open the passenger door. It’s a small, ingrained habit now—one she doesn’t think twice about.

    “Careful,” she mutters, one hand hovering near Zosia’s back as she helps her in.

    Zosia lowers herself into the seat with a soft exhale, instinctively bracing a hand against her stomach. “I’ve got it,” she says gently.

    Carol hums, squeezing her shoulder lightly before closing the door and heading around to the driver’s side. She gets in, shuts it a little harder than necessary, and sits there for a beat, hands gripping the wheel.

    Zosia knows she’s ready to burst. She’d watched her grow tighter and tighter throughout the interview Val arranged for them.

    It’s been a couple of weeks since their story leaked online, and Carol’s agent is still trying to do damage control—though Zosia isn’t sure it’s entirely necessary.

    Carol’s fans have been overwhelmingly supportive. Zosia’s had a few hundred friend requests on her socials and some questionable Google reviews left about the restaurant, but—for the most part—things are settling again.

    “He was rude,” Carol snaps finally, the words coming out sharp. “Actually, no—not rude. He was a total asshole! I don’t know why Val even picked him!”

    Zosia glances at her, expression calm, steady. “He’s a journalist,” she says softly. “It’s his job to ask difficult questions.”

    “That wasn’t difficult,” Carol shoots back. “That was invasive. There’s a difference.”

    Zosia doesn’t argue. She studies her instead—the tension sitting heavy in her shoulders, the way her fingers flex against the steering wheel.

    “It went fine,” she says after a moment. “You did really well.”

    Carol exhales through her nose, head tipping back against the seat. “Yeah,” she mutters. “Loved the part where he decided to ask if I thought my dead wife would approve of you.”

    The words land heavier than she intends.

    They always do when it’s about Helen.

    Zosia’s hand finds her wrist without hesitation, fingers warm as they curl gently around it. Her thumb brushes slow, deliberate strokes over the inside.

    Carol stills.

    Her eyes slide shut as she leans back, letting the contact anchor her. Her breathing deepens, steadier now, as Zosia maintains that quiet rhythm against her skin.

    For a moment, neither of them speaks.

    Then Carol inhales, long and measured, and opens her eyes again. The sharp edge in her expression has softened. She looks tired—or maybe just stretched thin.

    “I’m sorry,” she says quietly. “You’re only dealing with all of this because of me. You don’t need this kind of stress… I mean, fuck, you’re two months away from giving birth and having to put up with this kind of shit—”

    “The one good thing about today,” she murmurs, “is that you look… amazing.”

    Zosia glances down at the simple white fabric of her dress stretched over her bump, then back at Carol with a small smile. “I feel like an elephant,” she chuckles.

    “You look beautiful,” Carol says, leaving no room for argument. “It’s very distracting. All I could think about during that stupid interview was kissing you.”

    Zosia’s hand drifts to Carol’s chest, smoothing over her white button-down—Val had insisted they both wear white—before settling over her heart. “You don’t look too bad yourself,” she says. “Very handsome.”

    Carol snorts. “I don’t know about that. I look like a fucking Mormon in this getup.” She gestures at her tan slacks and loafers with a wry smile.

    Zosia laughs—warm, unrestrained—and Carol’s expression softens instantly at the sound. “I’m being fucking serious, Zos,” she mutters, though there’s a hint of a smile.

    Drawn in by that laugh—by her—Carol leans forward again, kissing Zosia once more. The tightness in her chest eases a little as she exhales into it.

    When they separate this time, she stays close, resting her forehead lightly against Zosia’s, eyes closing for a brief second.

    “I don’t know if that article’s going to help or make things worse,” she admits quietly. “The fans are already moving on, and it won’t even be out for at least a week, maybe two.” She exhales. “Feels like all it’s going to do is stir things up again.”

    Zosia is quiet for a moment, considering. Then, “Why wait?” she asks.

    Carol opens her eyes, frowning slightly as she pulls back. “What do you mean?”

    “Why wait for the article?” Zosia says. “You could talk to them yourself. Your fans. Tell them your side.”

    Carol leans back slightly, the idea clearly catching her off guard. “I don’t—” She hesitates, running a hand through her hair. “Helen used to handle all of that. The fan stuff. I’ve… never really been great at it.”

    Zosia watches her, expression softening. “I could help,” she offers.

    Carol glances at her, something like relief flickering through before she reins it in. “Yeah?”

    Zosia nods. “Of course.”

    Carol exhales, thinking it through. “I don’t even know what that would look like,” she admits. “What, I just… post something? Or—” She pauses. “Maybe an AMA? Like a Reddit thing?”

    Zosia’s mouth curves, something playful creeping in. “Well,” she says lightly, “if only we knew a Wycaro superfan with her own podcast.”

    Carol frowns. She’s starting to get the feeling Zosia didn’t just come up with this idea on the spot. “Who?”

    Zosia arches a brow. “Maureen.”

    Carol blinks. “Maureen has a podcast?”

    “She does,” Zosia says. “It’s actually quite good… I may be a subscriber.”

    Carol leans back fully, considering, her fingers still loosely laced with Zosia’s. “You think that would help?”

    Zosia shrugs lightly. “I don’t think it would hurt.”

    Carol sits with it a moment longer, turning the idea over.

    Then she nods once.

    “Okay. I’ll reach out—see if we can set something up. Thank you, Zosia.”

    Zosia smiles in a way that makes Carol’s chest tighten—in a good way—then lifts their joined hands to her mouth, pressing a kiss to Carol’s knuckles. “You’re very welcome.”


    -


    The kitchen is already alive by the time Zosia steps into it. It’s early afternoon, and the restaurant is in full swing, prepping for that night’s dinner service.

    Heat clings to the air, thick with the scent of garlic, butter, and slow-simmering meat. Pans clatter, knives hit chopping boards in quick, rhythmic bursts, voices overlap—orders, timings, questions.

    It’s controlled chaos. Familiar and comforting to Zosia, like listening to a favoured song on repeat.

    Zosia stands at the stove, one hand braced lightly against the counter as she leans over a saucepan, tasting from the edge of a spoon. The sauce coats her tongue—rich and vibrant, and almost there.

    “Needs salt,” she mutters to herself, her voice lost in the chorus of the noise.

    She reaches for the small dish beside her, pinching a little between her fingers and sprinkling it in, stirring as she goes. The motion is instinctive—muscle memory, her body moving before her brain even catches up.

    It feels good to be working, to shut off her thoughts and let her body operate on autopilot for a little while.

    “Zosia.” A voice comes from behind her, cutting through the noise.

    She ignores it.

    “Zosia.” It comes again, louder this time. More insistent.

    She stirs again, lifts the spoon, and tastes.

    It’s better, but still not perfect.

    “Zosia!” Koumba repeats her name for a third time.

    “What?” she snaps, without bothering to turn or look up at him. “I’m working, Koumba.”

    There’s a pause behind her.

    Then Koumba steps fully into her line of sight, arms folding loosely across his chest, expression calm but unimpressed. “Yes,” he says mildly. “I can see that.”

    Zosia exhales sharply through her nose. “Then why are you asking?”

    “Because,” Koumba continues, unbothered by her tone, “you are thirty-four weeks pregnant and supposed to be not working.”

    Zosia sets the spoon down with a little more force than necessary. “I was bored,” she says, like it’s an explanation and not an excuse. “Carol’s out, meeting her agent and her publicist, doing damage control for all of… this.” She gestures vaguely, like the situation exists somewhere in the air around them. “I didn’t want to sit in the house all afternoon on my own, so I came here. To work.”

    Koumba studies her for a moment, his head tilting slightly. “Mm.”

    Zosia shoots him a look. “What does that mean?”

    “It means,” he says evenly, “Carol is going to kill me if I let you stay.”

    Zosia straightens, her spine going rigid. “Let me stay?” she repeats, her voice growing as sharp as the knives on the rack on the wall nearby. “This is my restaurant. I can be here if I want to be, with or without Carol’s permission.”

    A few heads turn at the sound of her raised voice. They’re used to Zosia shouting in the kitchen, calling out orders and demands. What they’re not used to is Zosia shouting at her brother-in-law over something not work related.

    Koumba notices the curious looks, hears the furtive whispers. He doesn’t rise to it, doesn’t match her volume. Instead, he reaches out, gentle but firm, and nudges her away from the stove. “Come,” he says quietly. “Walk with me.”

    Zosia opens her mouth to argue, then shuts it again, because part of her know’s she’s making a scene—and also because, despite her insistence on being there, she’s dead on her feet.

    She lets him guide her out of the kitchen, the noise fading behind them as they step into the dining room. It’s empty at this hour, chairs still tucked neatly under tables, sunlight spilling through the windows in long, quiet stretches across the floor.

    The sudden silence feels almost jarring.

    Zosia lowers herself into one of the dining chairs with a heavy exhale, one hand coming instinctively to her stomach as she adjusts, shifting to get comfortable under the weight of it.

    Her back aches and her ankles are puffy and swollen. She can’t remember the last time she slept through the night, even with Carol curled up at her side, and she’s spent the last couple of weeks restless and anxious without the luxury of work to distract her.

    Koumba pulls out the chair opposite her and sits, leaning forward slightly, his forearms resting on his thighs.

    “What is going on?” he asks, his tone gentle and probing.

    Zosia shakes her head immediately. “Nothing. I told you, I was bored—”

    “No,” Koumba says firmly, shaking his head.

    Zosia exhales, her shoulders dropping a fraction. Her gaze falls to the table, fingers tracing an invisible line along the wood grain.

    “I’m not… dealing very well. With not working,” she admits after a moment.

    Koumba doesn’t interrupt, doesn’t push for more. He lets her come to it in her own time.

    “I’ve worked since I was sixteen,” she continues when the silence grows too much. “I’m not used to just… sitting around. Waiting. It makes me feel—” She hesitates, searching for the word. “Useless.”

    Koumba’s expression softens.

    “And Carol—” Zosia exhales, a small, frustrated sound catching in it. “I appreciate everything she does, I do—but, right now, it’s too much. The hovering and the fussing. Watching me like I’m going to break if I breathe too hard.” She shakes her head. “It’s starting to drive me crazy.”

    Koumba’s mouth twitches faintly. “Have you told her this?”

    Zosia shakes her head immediately. She couldn’t do that to Carol. “No.”

    “And why not?”

    “Because she has enough on her plate,” Zosia says, her tone a little sharper again. She catches herself, reins in it. “The story about the pregnancy leaking, Carol’s agent, the interview, the podcast—she’s already running herself ragged on top of trying to take care of me, and I don’t want to throw that back in her face.”

    Koumba watches her for a moment, eyes narrowing, then leans back slightly in his chair, considering her.

    “Zosia,” he says carefully, “Carol means well. You know that.”

    “I know,” she says quickly. “I do.”

    “But,” he continues, “you also have another six weeks before this baby arrives.” He gestures lightly toward her stomach. “And right now, you are like a boiling pot.”

    Zosia chuckles despite herself. Koumba’s always had a way with words.

    “If you do not turn down the heat, everything inside will soon overflow,” he lifts his hands up and then brings them crashing down, emphasising his point, “And Carol will be the one to be burned.”

    Zosia’s lips press together, but there’s the faintest hint of a smile tugging at the corner of her mouth at his clunky choice of metaphor.

    “Talk to her,” he says. “Tell her how you are feeling. She will understand.”

    Zosia is quiet for a moment, considering his advice, then she nods. “Okay. I will.”

    Koumba inclines his head, satisfied. “You can stay,” he says, almost as an afterthought.

    Zosia looks up, caught off guard—and mildly annoyed at being given permission to stay in her own restaurant.

    “I will not tell Carol,” he adds, a hint of amusement creeping into his voice, “But—” He lifts a finger. “You are not allowed in the kitchen. It is too dangerous in your condition.”

    Zosia opens her mouth to argue, then closes it again. “Fine,” she says stiffly. “Office only.”

    “Office only,” Koumba echoes, grinning with a flourish, “And I will grace you with the pleasure of my company.”

    Zosia smiles at that, something warmer settling into her expression now. She reaches across the table, her hand finding his. “Thank you, Koumba… You’re going to make an amazing uncle.”

    Koumba laughs, the sound low and easy as he leans forward, his other hand coming to rest gently against the curve of her stomach.

    “I am,” he says, glancing down fondly, “and this little one and I are going to get into all kinds of trouble.”

    Zosia snorts softly at that, knowing he’s not wrong.

    “Of course, she will need a name for you to shout when we do.”

    Zosia sighs, pulling her hand back and slumping in her chair. “I still can’t decide. What if I pick wrong and I hate it? What if she hates it?”

    Koumba considers this for exactly half a second. “Might I suggest Koumba?” he says. “It is a very fine name. Very strong.”

    Zosia lets out a laugh, the tension in her shoulders easing properly for the first time since she walked in. “I think one Koumba is more than enough for this family; but I’ll keep it in mind.”



    -


    Zosia hears Carol’s truck before she sees it. The low rumble of the engine as it turns into the driveway, gravel crunching under the tyres, the familiar sound settling somewhere uneasy in her chest—instead of comforting her the way it usually does.

    She’s been pacing for the better part of ten minutes, part restless and part necessity thanks to the dull ache in her back that refuses to budge.

    Back and forth across the living room, the length of the rug worn into a path by her uneasy steps. Her hands keep drifting to her stomach, then falling away again as she tries to focus.

    Just say how you feel, she thinks to herself.

    She runs through her script again in her head—slower this time. The words careful and measured.

    I love you. I know you mean well, but I need—

    Her chest tightens.

    Autonomy.

    The word feels too sharp. Too clinical.

    I need to feel useful.

    Better.

    Not just—

    Her throat closes slightly around the thought.

    Not just like an incubator.

    The sound of the front door unlocking drags her from her thoughts. Carol doesn’t knock, hasn’t had to since Zosia gave her the spare key a month ago. Walking into Zosia’s home feels as natural as walking into her own now.

    “Zos?” she calls as she steps inside, her voice carrying easily down the hall.

    “In here,” Zosia answers from the living room, trying to keep her tone steady.

    She stops pacing.

    Plants her feet.

    Mentally prepares herself.

    Carol appears in the doorway a second later, and whatever Zosia rehearsed in her mind evaporates in an instant—because Carol is holding flowers.

    A whole, bright, unapologetically vibrant bouquet—wild and full, bursting with colour. Soft pinks and deep reds, yellows threaded through like sunlight, greenery spilling out around them.

    The sight of them hits Zosia hard. She feels her eyes start to sting. “You… bought me flowers?” she asks, her voice catching halfway through.

    Carol’s mouth curves, pleased with herself. “Yes,” she says, holding the bouquet out to her. “Excellent observation skills.”

    Zosia lets out a shaky breath, blinking rapidly as she steps closer. “I—why?”

    Carol shrugs, like it’s nothing. “Do I need a reason?”

    Zosia huffs a small, wet laugh, shaking her head. “Malick only ever bought me flowers when he’d done something wrong.”

    Carol laughs outright at that, the sound warm and unfiltered. “God, the bar is set so low in straight relationships, huh?”

    Zosia smiles despite herself, though it wavers.

    Carol glances down at the bouquet—nerves setting in as Zosia still doesn’t reach to take them from her—then back at her. “There’s this little florists right across the street from where I had lunch with Val. These were in the window, and it’s all I could focus on.” She shrugs again, softer this time. “They reminded me of you. So I bought ‘em.”

    Zosia’s breath catches.

    That’s it.

    That’s all it takes.

    The tears spill over before she can stop them, her grip tightening slightly as she finally takes the flowers from Carol.

    Carol’s expression shifts immediately. Concern flickers in her eyes as she steps closer. “Hey—what? Zosia, they’re just flowers—”

    Zosia shakes her head quickly, hot tears slipping down her cheeks. “I know,” she says, her voice breaking. “It’s not—it’s not the flowers.”

    Carol doesn’t hesitate.

    She gently takes the bouquet back from her, carefully setting it down on the coffee table before stepping forward and pulling Zosia into her arms.

    “You can talk to me,” she murmurs, her voice softer now, comforting as one hand rubs absent circles into the small of Zosia’s back.

    Zosia leans into her immediately, bending slightly to rest her head against Carol’s shoulder. Her arms wrap around the smaller woman, holding on tighter than she means to.

    “I don’t want to hurt you,” she says, the words muffled against Carol’s shirt.

    Carol stills.

    Just for a second.

    Her body goes rigid beneath Zosia’s hands. “Okay,” she says slowly. “That—That doesn’t sound good.”

    Zosia feels it—the shift in her whole demeanour as Carol prepares herself for the worst.

    She pulls back quickly, shaking her head. “No, it’s not—it’s not like that,” she says, reaching for her hands. “Nothing like that.”

    Carol searches her face, still wary, but lets herself be guided over to the couch.

    They sit, and Zosia keeps hold of her hands, fingers threading through Carol’s like she’s trying to anchor herself.

    “I’ve just been…” She exhales, trying to find the right words. The last thing she wants is for this all to come out like an accusation. “Restless. Since I stopped working. I don’t—I’m not handling it well.”

    Carol’s thumbs brush lightly over the backs of her hands, but she doesn’t interrupt.

    “And you,” Zosia continues carefully, “you’ve been amazing. You really have. I love you, and I appreciate everything you’re doing for me, for the baby. I’m just feeling—”

    She hesitates, struggling to find the right word.

    Carol tilts her head slightly, reading her all too well. “Smothered?” she offers.

    Zosia lets out a small, nervous laugh. “Yes… a little.”

    Carol winces. She looks down briefly, her jaw tightening. “Right.”

    “I didn’t want to upset you,” Zosia adds quickly. “I know why you’re doing it, and I know you’re worried.”

    Carol exhales slowly, nodding once. “I might have gone a little overboard,” she admits. “The last couple of weeks. I just—” She breaks off, shaking her head. “When you had that scare with the contractions, it freaked me out.”

    Zosia’s expression softens. “I know.”

    “I know they’re not regular,” Carol continues, quieter now. “And you keep telling me that, but—”

    “They’re still happening,” Zosia says gently. “And it scares you.”

    Carol nods stiffly, enjoying this conversation about as much as Zosia is.

    “I get why you’re worried,” Zosia says. “I really do. But I need a little breathing room. It’s a lot right now—the hormones, not working, relying on you financially—” She lets out a small breath. “I just feel like I’m totally useless right now…”

    Carol’s gaze flicks up to hers. “You’re not—” She stops herself, recalibrates. “Okay. I hear you. Less… smothering.”

    Zosia squeezes her hands lightly, the ghost of a smile reaching her lips.

    Carol exhales, her shoulders dropping as she lets herself relax a little once she realises Zosia isn’t trying to break up with her. “You shouldn’t have to be relying on me for too much longer, at least financially,” she adds after a moment. “Taffler called me. The magazine article seems to have lit a fire under the clinic—they’re willing to settle.”

    Zosia’s eyes widen slightly. “Really?”

    Carol nods. “Yeah. Taffler thinks the deal should be through in a week or so.”

    Relief flickers across Zosia’s face. “That’s… that’s good.”

    “It is,” Carol agrees. Then, softer, “and I’ll try to do better. Not smother so much.”

    Zosia smiles, leaning in to kiss her—gentle, grateful. Her hand comes up to cup Carol’s cheek. “Thank you… and, for the record, I like being able to rely on you. I don’t—I was on my own for a long time. Depending on people doesn’t come naturally to me. But with you… everything feels natural.”

    Carol smiles at that, melts into her touch, and leans in for another kiss. When they pull back, Zosia glances toward the coffee table. A huge grin spreads across her face.

    “And thank you for the flowers. It was very sweet of you, truly.”

    Carol follows her gaze, giving a small laugh. “Actually, they got me thinking.”

    Zosia tilts her head. “About what?”

    Carol leans back slightly into the couch, one arm draping along the back behind Zosia. “Us,” she says. “This… whole thing.”

    Zosia hums softly. “It is a bit unusual.”

    Carol snorts. “That’s one way of putting it.”

    Zosia shifts, curling into her side, fitting comfortably against her. “I wouldn’t change any of it,” she says, her voice warm against Carol’s shoulder.

    Carol’s arm tightens around her automatically. “Me either,” she says. Then, after a beat, adds, “But… we kind of skipped all the normal dating stuff.”

    Zosia glances up at her. “We did,” she acknowledges, unsure where Carol is going with this.

    Carol’s mouth curves faintly. “So I was thinking… maybe we fix that.”

    Zosia raises a brow. “Fix it how?”

    “A date night,” Carol says. “I mean a proper one. Not just dinner at your place or mine. A real date, before this little one comes and we don’t have any time to ourselves.” Carol’s hand finds Zosia’s stomach, resting there like it’s the most natural thing in the world.

    Zosia’s expression softens, pleasantly surprised by the suggestion. “I’d like that.”

    Carol smiles, a little more sure of herself now. “Yeah?”

    “Yes,” Zosia says, her hand coming to cover Carol’s on her stomach as the baby inside her kicks away. “What did you have in mind?”

    Chapter End Notes

    As always, thanks for reading. I've got a lot on this week, so probably won't have another chapter up until the weekend.

    - Tan

    Chapter 26



    The evening of their date, Carol knocks on Zosia’s front door instead of letting herself in.

    It feels faintly ridiculous given the key in her pocket, but she waits on the porch all the same, one hand tucked behind her back, the other smoothing over the front of her blazer.

    The evening air is still warm, the last of the sun bleeding out of the sky and leaving everything washed in a soft, dusky light. It’s seven o’clock on the dot, the exact time she told Zosia she’d come pick her up.
