    She hears movement inside. Distant at first, the sound of footsteps coming down the stairs, then closer as Zosia approaches. The door opens a moment later.

    Zosia stands there framed in the hallway light, and for a second Carol forgets the line she’d prepared in her head.

    She looks stunning.

    Her hair is down, spilling in dark waves over her shoulders, and she’s wearing a maternity dress—the same vibrant yellow of the nursery walls upstairs— that skims over the curve of her stomach before falling loose and stopping just below her knees. It’s nothing overly elaborate, but the sight of her still makes Carol’s chest tighten.

    It feels a lot like coming home.

    Zosia’s eyes move over her in turn, taking in the slacks, the blazer, the touch of eyeliner Carol almost scrubbed off twice before leaving the house, and the way she has her hair swept back.

    A smile pulls at Zosia’s mouth. “Why are you knocking?” she asks, amused. “You have a key.”

    Carol straightens a little, her hands out of sight behind her back, “Because…” she says, drawing the word out, “I am doing this properly.”

    Zosia leans lightly against the doorframe, her brow lifting. “Properly?”

    “Yes,” Carol nods, “I’m here to pick you up for our date, and we’re pretending I don’t have a key to your place, and you don’t have a bun in the oven.”

    That earns her a real smile from Zosia. “So we’re role playing now?” She teases, and Carol’s stomach flips. It’s ridiculous, given everything they’ve already done together, but there it is.

    Carol grins. “We can definitely add a little role play to tonight’s agenda. Maybe in bed, later?”

    Zosia bites her bottom lip, suddenly acting coy, “Sorry, Carol. I don’t put out on a first date. I’m not that kind of girl.”

    “Date night finishes at ten.” Carol replies without missing a beat. Then she brings her right hand out from behind her back with a flourish. Between her fingers she holds a single red rose.

    “For you,” she says, holding it out. “And, ideally, it won’t make you cry this time.”

    Zosia laughs softly, the sound warm and delighted as she takes it from her. “No promises.”

    She leans in and kisses Carol, quick at first, then slower when Carol’s hands rise instinctively to her waist. The rose brushes lightly against Carol’s shoulder as Zosia pulls back.

    “Thank you,” she says.

    Carol tries not to look too pleased with herself, but fails spectacularly as she can’t keep the grin off her face

    Zosia glances down at the flower, brings it up to her nose to smell it, and then looks back up at Carol. “So,” she says, tone light and curious, “what are our plans?”

    Carol’s shakes her head, giving nothing away. “It’s a surprise.”

    Zosia narrows her eyes playfully. “I hate surprises.”

    “No, you don’t.”

    “Okay, I don’t… but that doesn’t mean I’m not curious.” Zosia concedes, smiling. Carol responds by brings a hand up to her mouth, miming zipping her lips shut.

    Zosia rolls her eyes, “Alright, fine. Give me one second to grab my keys.” She says, stepping back from the door.

    “There’s no need,” Carol says, and Zosia pauses halfway through turning.

    “I didn’t bring the truck.” Carol slips her hands into her pockets, trying to sound casual about it. “I borrowed Manousos’s car.”

    Zosia blinks slowly. “You borrowed Manousos’s car?”

    Carol nods. “Yup. Sporty little thing. Much easier for you to get in and out of.”

    For a second, Zosia just looks at her. Then her expression melts, “That was very thoughtful of you,” she says quietly. “Thank you.”

    Carol shrugs, suddenly feeling a little self-conscious. “I had to leave the title deed to my house with him as collateral.”

    Zosia laughs outright at that, shaking her head. “Well, in that case, we better be careful.”

    “Tell me about it. One dent to his pride and joy and you’re going to find yourself with a permanent house guest.” Carol jokes.

    “That doesn’t sound like the worst thing.” Zosia ducks her head and smiles at her, a little bashful, and Carol momentarily forgets how to breath.

    She clears her throat, feels a blush creeping its way over her cheeks. That’s a conversation for another day. “Uh, are you ready to go?”

    “Sure.” Zosia turns to place the rose on the side table by the door, but the movement is interrupted halfway through by a sharp wince. Her hand goes to her lower back immediately, fingers pressing in there as her face tightens.

    Carol is at her side, stepping over the threshold, before she can think better of it. “Zos?”

    Zosia exhales slowly through her nose, waiting for the discomfort to ease. “I’m okay.”

    Carol’s brow furrows. “Are you sure?”

    “Yes.”

    “We can do this another night,” Carol says quickly. “Seriously. It’s not a big deal. We can stay in, order food, watch something terrible—”

    Zosia turns to look at her, patient but firm. “Carol.”

    Carol stops.

    Zosia’s hand is still at her back, but the tension in her face is already easing. “I’m fine,” she says. “Just a little twinge. It’s nothing to worry about.”

    Carol doesn’t answer straight away. She wants to argue. Wants to insist they call the night off. Wants to wrap her up in bubble wrap and keep her safe.

    Instead, she catches herself.

    Remembers Zosia’s request.

    “Okay,” she says at last, though it comes out more reluctant than she intends. “Okay. If you’re sure.”

    Zosia’s expression gentles. “I am. I’ve been looking forward to tonight. I can’t wait to see what you planned for us.”

    Carol nods once, still not entirely convinced, but choosing not to push.

    Then she offers her arm, a little crooked and formal, the ghost of a grin returning to her mouth. “In that case,” she says, “shall we?”

    Zosia smiles as she slips her arm through Carol’s. “Lead the way.”


    -


    “So,” Carol says, her voice carrying just enough to be heard over the low hum of chatter and the soft scratch of brushes on canvas, “what do you think?”

    There’s something painfully earnest in the way she asks, shoulders a little tight, paintbrush in hand and hovering mid-air like she’s forgotten what she was doing with it.

    Zosia glances from her own canvas to Carol, and the grin that spreads across her face is immediate. Reassuring.

    “I think,” she says slowly, “you are full of surprises.”

    Carol huffs, but there’s relief in it. She lifts her brush again, adding a careful stroke of pale blue to the edge of her lake. “I’m a woman of many multitudes.”

    The studio is warm and bright, filled with the scent of acrylic paint and cheap wine. At the front of the room, the instructor gestures enthusiastically at her own canvas, walking everyone through the next step of the lakeside scene—something about reflections and light.

    Carol is at least two steps behind, finding it difficult to focus on the art when the most beautiful thing in the room is the woman sitting beside her.

    Around them, a handful of other couples lean close over their paintings, some laughing, others taking it much too seriously for Carol’s liking. Like their competing for the Turner Prize and not at a paint and sip party.

    There’s a mother and daughter a few tables over, their heads bent together in concentration, and a pair of friends arguing quietly about whether the sky should be more purple or more pink.

    Carol’s attention drifts back to Zosia.

    To the way her brow furrows slightly when she’s focused. To the careful way she moves her hands, her strokes slow and deliberate

    “Are you having fun?” Carol asks, glancing sideways at her as the instructor—a young blonde woman in paint splattered overalls and a tie-dye t-shirt—moves on another step, leaving Carol to trail further behind.

    Zosia nods, her smile softening. “I am. I haven’t painted since college.” She dips her brush into a wash of green, dragging it lightly across her canvas. “I used to really enjoy it.”

    Carol’s expression brightens at that. “Yeah?”

    “Yeah.” Zosia looks back at her. “Thank you. This was a really good idea. Definitely up there in my top three most original dates.”

    “Top three? Wow.” Carol laughs. Tries to play it cool, but is clearly pleased with herself.

    “Well,” she says, gesturing vaguely with her brush, “I used to paint. A bit. Figured I should maybe dust off one of my many talents to impress you.”

    Zosia glances at Carol’s canvas again, taking it in properly this time. The lines are clean. Confident. The perspective just slightly better than everyone else’s.

    “It’s really good,” she says, genuinely impressed. “Though I’m not surprised by that.”

    Carol narrows her eyes slightly, like she’s waiting for a punchline. “Oh?”

    Zosia’s mouth curves, something playful flickering there as she leans in closer, dropping her voice, “Mm, you are very good with your hands.”

    Carol snickers at that, blushes like a school girl and bumps her shoulder into Zosia’s. “Behave,” she mutters, though there’s a smile tugging at her lips.

    Zosia laughs quietly, steadying her brush.

    “You did surprise me, though,” she adds after a moment, glancing around the room. “This is… more public than I expected.”

    Carol follows her gaze. The space is open, shared, filled with other people—but the tables are small, the lighting soft and intimate. It’s easy to forget they’re not alone as their knees brush under the table.

    Carol shrugs lightly. “Felt like a good middle ground,” she says. “Out, but not… too out.”

    Zosia nods, understanding what she means without her having to fully say it.

    “And,” Zosia adds, her tone teasing now, “I’m also very impressed with your choice of drink.”

    Carol glances at the glass of soda beside her, the ice half melted under the studio lights and condensation beading down the side. Almost everyone else in the room is sipping on wine.

    She shrugs again, “It doesn’t really bother me.”

    Zosia’s smile warms at that, her hand drifting briefly to rest against the curve of her stomach before she returns to her painting.

    Carol watches the movement, her expression shifting almost imperceptibly.

    “Are you comfortable enough?” she asks, voice quieter now, hesitant again.

    Zosia nods, adjusting slightly in her chair, nudging the cushion behind her into a better position. “I’m fine.” She glances over her shoulder at the cushion, then back at Carol. “Thank you for thinking to bring this for me, by the way.”

    Carol beams, openly pleased. “I’m glad you’re comfortable.”

    Zosia’s chest tightens a little at that. At the care threaded through her words. The open adoration in her expression.

    Carol turns her attention back to her painting, adding to the texture of the tress at the edge of her lake. She doesn’t focus for very long, though. Her attention quickly finding its way back to Zosia.

    “I didn’t make any dinner plans. I wasn’t sure how long you’d want to be out for. We can go somewhere after, or—”

    Zosia cuts her off, her hand settling lightly on Carol’s thigh under the table.

    Carol stills, but doesn’t pull away.

    “How about our diner?” Zosia suggests.

    Carol lifts a single brow, amusement playing on her face. “Our diner?”

    Zosia bumps her shoulder lightly, copying Carol’s earlier move. “Yes, our diner.”

    Carol’s mouth curves. “I wasn’t aware we were sharing my diner.”

    Zosia shrugs, unapologetic, paintbrush in hand and eyes fixed forward on her own canvas. “I’m claiming joint custody. If anything happens I still get access on weekends.”

    Her tone is light, like she’s making a joke, but there’s something underneath it. An edge to it that cuts deep. Carol doesn’t laugh.

    Her hand shifts, covering Zosia’s where it rests on her thigh, fingers curling around hers. “I’m not going anywhere, Zos.” she says, steady and certain. “I’m in this for the long haul.”

    Zosia’s breath catches, just slightly. Her eyes tear up, Carol’s admission—and her hormones—getting the better of her.

    Carol glances around the room, quick and habitual, checking who’s looking their way. No one is paying them any real attention, though. Everyone’s too absorbed in their own paintings; their own conversations.

    Still, she hesitates—but not for too long. She leans in and presses a small, careful kiss to Zosia’s cheek.

    It’s quick.

    Almost nothing.

    But it means the world to Zosia in a room full of people.

    Zosia smiles, tilts her head toward Carol, voice dropping just a fraction and her eyes growing impossibly dark, “In that case,” she murmurs, “maybe we should skip the diner?”

    “Oh?”

    Zosia’s fingers shift suggestively further up Carol’s thigh. “We could order takeout,” she suggests. “Eat it back home… In bed.”

    Carol doesn’t even pretend to think about it. “Yeah,” she says, her mouth curving. “Sounds good.”


    -



    Carol eases the car to a stop at the bottom of her driveway, the engine ticking softly as it cools when she switches it off.

    She glances up automatically, looks across the street—and finds Manousos standing at his living room window. Being very obvious about it as he peers out at them, looking for any sign of damage on his car. Carol snorts under her breath, lifts a hand in a lazy wave.

    Zosia follows her gaze and laughs softly. “Well,” she says, shifting the takeout bag on her lap, “at least the deed to your house is safe.”

    Manousos nods at them once, solemn like he’s overseeing a high-stakes hostage exchange, then disappears from the window.

    Carol shakes her head, amused, and pushes her door open. The night air is cooler now, the full moon hanging low and heavy in the sky as she circles around the car. She opens Zosia’s door, one hand already reaching for the bag.

    “I’ve got it,” Zosia says, but she lets Carol take it anyway.

    Carol tucks the paper bag against her hip and wordlessly offers out her free hand. Zosia takes it, steadying herself as she steps carefully out of the car. Her movements are slower these days, more deliberate.

    She’s thirty-four weeks pregnant, and the baby rapidly growing inside of her is about the size of a cantaloupe—as Carol so eagerly informed her that morning.

    To Zosia, it feels like she’s carrying around a bowling bowl under her dress.

    They make their way slowly up the drive together, Carol’s hand at the small of Zosia’s back the whole time. The house is quiet and dimly lit as they step inside.

    Carol nudges the door shut behind them with her foot. “Plates?” she asks, glancing toward the kitchen.

    Zosia wrinkles her nose immediately. “Let’s just eat out of the containers tonight.”

    Carol huffs a laugh, “Jesus, I’ve been such a bad influence on you.”

    Zosia grins, stepping into her space. “Not all bad.”

    She leans in and kisses her—slow and unhurried. Carol melts into it without hesitation, her free hand coming up to Zosia’s waist.

    Zosia pulls back first, her palm resting lightly against Carol’s chest. “Thank you,” she says quietly. “For tonight. It was… perfect.”

    “It’s not over yet,” Carol murmurs, something soft and warm in her gaze as she regards the other woman. She nods gently toward the stairs. “Go on and head up. I’ll grab some forks.”

    Zosia reaches for the bag tucked against Carol’s hip. She starts to protest, but Zosia gives her a withering look. “I can handle it, Carol.”

    Carol exhales, relenting as she hands it over. “Of course.”

    Zosia leans in for another quick kiss as she takes it. “Don’t be long.”

    Carol watches her go—watches her slow, steady climb of the stairs, the sway of her dress, the familiar curve of her body— and there’s an adoration in her expression she doesn’t bother to hide when she’s alone.

    She shakes herself out of her reverie after a moment and turns for the kitchen. She makes a beeline for the cutlery draw, grabbing two forks, then stops by the fridge and pulls out two bottles of water. Zosia always gets thirsty during the night.

    She closes the fridge with her hip, turns to head back in to the hall, then stops. Tucking the water bottles into the crook of her arm, she yanks open the freezer compartment. The tub of mango ice cream sits right where she left it.

    Zosia’s favourite.

    Carol smiles to herself, grabbing it and two spoons, juggling everything in her arms before heading upstairs to join Zosia.

    She pushes the bedroom door open with her shoulder—

    —and stops in her tracks.

    Zosia is halfway through changing, stepping out of her dress, the fabric pooling at her feet. She turns at the sound of the door, catching Carol standing there, arms full and momentarily frozen.

    Zosia smiles. “Ice cream? Really?”

    Carol blinks once, then grins, recovering. “Figured you should make the most of eating for two while you can.”

    She sets everything down on her nightstand and climbs onto the bed, already unpacking the takeout containers as Zosia finishes changing.

    A loose shirt that hangs down over her thighs replaces the dress. The material looks soft, the colour faded. Carol tilts her head, curious. “Is that your shirt?”

    Zosia glances down, then back up. “It is. Found it in your drawer. I must’ve left it here last time.”

    “Huh.” Carol hums, her eyes raking over the other woman—taking in her bare legs, long and lean. “I’m gonna have to start charging you for storage.”

    Zosia rolls her eyes at that, though not without affection. She pulls the covers back and climbs into bed beside Carol. “In that case, I’ll start charging you for laundry. Half the last load I did was your tank tops.”

    Carol pauses, looking genuinely affronted. “So that’s where they’ve all gone.”

    Zosia chuckles softly. She leans over and kisses Carol again, quick and easy this time, before snatching the container of beef lo mein out of her hands.

    “Hey! That one’s mine.” Carol protests, reaching to take it back.

    Zosia pulls a face, pouting at her with those full lips and big brown eyes. “But the baby wants it…”

    “Fine…” Carol sighs dramatically, sits back and picks up the container of Egg Foo Young with a huff instead.



    Later, they’re propped up against the headboard, the room dim except for the soft glow of the television set. The air smells faintly of soy sauce and mango ice cream—the tub sitting open between them, melting at the edges.

    “Don’t forget we’re meeting the midwife next week,” Zosia says absently, spooning up a bite. “She’s coming by to talk through the home birth—And the birthing pool gets delivered Tuesday.”

    Beside her, Carol makes a noncommittal sound. Zosia glances at her. She’s on her phone, her eyes glued to the screen.

    “Carol, are you listening?” Zosia asks, nudging her lightly with her knee.

    “Huh?” She looks up, blinking. “Oh—yeah. Yeah. Sorry.”

    Zosia narrows her eyes, amused. “Are you on the Wycaro reddit again? Do I need to confiscate your phone?”

    Carol laughs, shaking her head. “No.” She lifts the phone slightly, flashing the screen at Zosia. “Actually—message from Maureen.”

    Zosia straightens a little. “Maureen?”

    “Yeah.” Carol’s expression shifts, attention sharpening. “Someone in the New Mexico fan group figured out who leaked the story.”

    Zosia sits up properly now. “Really?”

    Carol nods, eyes still scanning the message. “Yeah.”

    “What are you going to do?”

    Carol’s brow furrows slightly. “We’re meeting Taffler next week about the settlement. I’ll talk to him about it then.”

    Carol exhales, setting her phone aside on the nightstand. The tension eases out of her shoulders as she turns back, refocusing on Zosia. 

    “Hey, don’t worry about all that stuff right now,” she says softly, picking up her spoon and digging it into the ice cream between them. When she lifts it again she offers it to Zosia with a grin. “It’s still date night.”

    Chapter 27

    Chapter Notes


    The drive to Taffler’s law firm is a quiet one.

    Carol keeps one hand on the wheel, the other resting loosely over the manilla envelope sitting in her lap while they wait at a red light. It’s thick—papers, printouts, everything they need tucked neatly inside.

    Beside her, Zosia adjusting again in her seat. Her brow furrows just slightly as she tries to hide her discomfort. It’s subtle, would be easy to miss if Carol didn’t know to look for it; Didn’t know Zosia.

    She’s been unsettled all day, holding her back and shifting her weight from foot too foot as she wanders aimlessly around the house, muttering in Polish under her breath so Carol can’t understand. Except she does.

    Pain is pain in any language.

    “You okay?” Carol asks, her voice carrying easily over the low hum of the Corolla’s engine as she turns her head to take Zosia in fully—one eye still on the light for it changing.

    Zosia exhales slowly, one hand braced against her lower back as she adjusts in the seat. “I’m fine,” she says, though there’s a tightness to it, like she’s talking with a clenched jaw. “Just… uncomfortable.”

    Carol turns her attention back to driving as the light changes and the cars in front of her at the intersection start rolling forward. She sneaks another glance the other woman’s way, her eyes flicking briefly down to where Zosia’s other hand grabs at her stomach as another wave of discomfort hits her. “That looks more than just being uncomfortable.”

    Zosia huffs a quiet, dismissive breath. “It’s Braxton Hicks,” she says. “They’ve been coming and going all morning. Nothing regular.”

    “We could go see Dr. Beeken—just to be safe.”

    Zosia shakes her head. “No. They’re not close together. Not getting stronger. Really, it’s fine, Carol. I’ll lay down when we get back.”

    Carol doesn’t look convinced. She purses her lips, torn between respecting Zosia’s wishes and doing a U-turn in the middle of traffic and heading straight for her doctor’s office.

    Her fingers tap once against the envelope before she exhales through her nose and nods. “Okay,” she says finally. “But if they get closer—”

    “I’ll tell you,” Zosia cuts in gently, glancing over at her with a tired smile. “I promise.”

    Carol nods once, though the tension doesn’t quite leave her shoulders as she carries on driving towards Taffler’s office.

    She pulls Zosia’s little car into the parking lot a few minutes later and kills the engine. Grabbing the envelope, she slides out of the car before circling to Zosia’s side to help her out.

    Inside, the office is cool and bright—all glass and chrome, clean lines and quiet professionalism.

    A young blonde woman with glasses waits expectantly for them in front of the reception desk. Her expression sharpens with recognition as they approach.

    “Hi,” she says, voice bright and eager. “Ms Sturka, Ms Diabaté?”

    Carol’s mouth curves into something knowing. She clutches the envelope to her chest even tighter. “That’s us.”

    “I’m Tabbitha,” she continues, smoothing her hands nervously over the front of her blouse. “I’ve been assisting on your case.” Taffler’s intern looks all of twenty, probably barely out of college.

    Carol’s grin widens, just a fraction too sharp to be polite. “Oh, I’m very aware of your work, Tabbitha.”

    Zosia’s hand finds Carol’s wrist in that moment— her touch light, but grounding.

    Tabbitha hesitates, uncertainty flickering across her face, but she recovers quickly, offering a small, professional smile. “If you’ll both follow me? Mr Taffler is waiting for you.”

    She leads them down a quiet hallway, heels clicking softly against the floor, and pushes open the door to a small conference room.

    Inside, Davis Taffler is already seated at the glass table—dressed in a suit that probably cost more than Zosia’s car—a neat stack of documents sat in front of him for them to sign.

    He looks up as they enter, all easy confidence and polished calm. “Carol. Zosia. Good to see you both. Please, take a seat.”

    Carol doesn’t bother with pleasantries as she sits, the envelope landing on the table in front of her with a soft thud. Zosia lowers herself carefully into the chair beside her, one hand instinctively resting on her stomach.

    Taffler folds his hands in front of him, an easy smile on his face, like he’s a politician standing in front of a television camera. “The fertility clinic has made a generous offer,” he begins. “Three million. After my fees, you’d be looking at approximately two point four million between you.”

    Carol leans back slightly, expression unreadable for a beat.

    “Six hundred thousand for you, huh?” She says, after doing the math, her eyes narrowing on Taffler.

    He doesn’t crack under her scrutiny. If anything, his smile brightens. “Yes, for me     and my firm. For the work we put in to make this settlement happen, Carol. Thirty three percent is pretty standard in the industry.”

    “No, no I get it.” Carol presses. Beside her, Zosia shifts in her seat, visibly in pain. She wants this over and done with so she can get Zosia home. Or, better yet, to her doctor’s office.

    “It’s just I was expecting a discount,” Carol says lightly, drumming her finger on the envelope in front of her.

    “A discount?” Taffler repeats, laughter lacing his voice. “Carol, I like you, you’re a great client, but I’ve got mouths to feed. Kids to put through college. Not to mention all the hours my intern has put into this—“

    “That’s funny.” Carol cuts him off, her expression anything but amused. “Given your intern is the one that sold us out online.”

    The room stills, all of the air seeming to be sucked out at once.

    Taffler’s brows lift, the picture of polite surprise. “I’m sorry?” he says. “That’s quite a serious accusation, Carol. What proof do you have to support that?”

    Carol doesn’t answer. She just pushes the hefty envelope across the table to him. It slides smoothly along the glass

    “Take a look.”

    Taffler hesitates for half a second, then pulls it toward himself, ripping it open. Papers shift as he pulls them out, the quiet rustle loud in the heavy silence of the room as he scans through the contents.

    “What is this?” he asks, his smile fading. He already knows exactly what he’s looking at.

    “Evidence,” Carol says simply. “Confirmation of the ISP used to post the story about Zosia’s pregnancy online.”

    She tilts her head slightly, letting Taffler stew before she lands the killing blow. “It traces back here. To this office.”

    Zosia leans forward then, picking up where Carol leaves off, her voice calm and precise despite the faint tension in her posture.

    “The Reddit account was created specifically to leak the story,” she says. “It only posted in Wycaro subreddits, and stayed active for a few weeks after.”

    Taffler’s eyes move faster now across the page in his hand.

    “We had help,” Zosia continues. “From a fan—Jarmell Gurkey. He wasn’t comfortable sharing exactly what he does for a living, but he was able to access the user’s laptop remotely while she was still posting.”

    Zosia lets that sit with Taffler for a moment.

    “He took a screenshot.”

    Taffler goes still. Slowly, he leafs through the documents in his hands and pulls a photograph from the stack. It’s candid. Slightly grainy. A young blonde woman with glasses, unaware she’s being photographed.

    The colour drains from his face.

    Across the table, Carol watches him, her mouth curving into something sharp. Satisfied.

    “Tabbitha’s a real cutie,” she says, almost conversationally. “I’m sure she works very hard for you, Davis.”

    The change in the lawyer is immediate.

    He leans back in his chair unbuttons his suit jacket before tugging at his collar, as if it’s suddenly too tight. There’s a sheen of sweat forming at his hairline now, catching the light from above. His composure doesn’t shatter—but it cracks.

    He clears his throat.

    “Well,” he says, voice smoothing itself out with effort, “in light of this… troubling development, I’m prepared to adjust my fees accordingly.”

    Carol says nothing. Just watches him, eyes sharp and unforgiving.

    Under the table, Zosia’s hand finds hers.

    They’ve been sitting on this information for a week, ever since Maureen reached out. Carol had wanted to storm into Taffler’s office the very next morning and sock him straight in his slimy face.

    Zosia had convinced her to play it smarter.

    “I can reduce it from the standard thirty-three percent to… twenty,” he continues, folding his hands in front of him. “And, of course, you can rest assured the intern will be appropriately disciplined.”

    His gaze flicks briefly to the photograph still on the table before he pushes it aside.

    “Though,” he adds, attempting something like a reassuring smile, “it’s worth noting the leak ultimately strengthened your case. It created public pressure. Increased the settlement value.”

    Carol’s expression hardens, Zosia’s hand in hers the only thing stopping her from leaping to her feet.

    Taffler doesn’t seem to notice. “Let’s,” he finishes with a flourish of his hands, “chalk it up to youthful indiscretion.”

    Zosia shifts beside her.

    This time it’s not subtle. Her breath catches, shoulders tightening as her hand presses more firmly into the curve of her stomach. The discomfort sharpens across her face before she can mask it.

    Carol is already turning toward her. “Zos—?”

    “I’m fine,” Zosia says quickly, though her voice is thinner now, strained at the edges.

    Taffler is on his feet in an instant, eager for the distraction. “Water,” he says. “I’ll—get you some water.” He doesn’t wait for a response before slipping out of the room. The door clicking shut behind him.

    Carol leans closer immediately, her hand finding Zosia’s knee. “You don’t look fine. I really think we need to—”

    Zosia exhales slowly through the pain, eyes closing briefly as she rides it out. “It’s just a stronger one,” she says. “It’s nothing to worry about.”

    Carol watches her carefully, jaw tight as she shakes her head. “We should go get you checked out. We can come back, sign later—”

    “No,” Zosia says, opening her eyes and shaking her head. “Let’s just get this done.”

    Carol hesitates. She chews the inside of her cheek, then nods, though the worry on her face lingers.

    “What do you think?” she asks quietly, switching gears. “About the settlement.”

    Zosia takes a steadying breath, settling back into the chair, one hand still resting protectively over her stomach. “I think we should take it,” she says. “It’s more than fair.”

    She glances at Carol. “But it’s your decision.”

    Carol shakes her head immediately. “No. It’s ours.”

    Zosia’s expression softens slightly at that.

    Carol exhales, leaning back in her own chair, running a hand through her hair. “It’s enough,” she says. “More than enough. You can pay off your mortgage, your medical bills, and we’d still have more than enough to give this little one a comfortable life.” Her eyes settle on Zosia’s stomach, her lips curling into a genuine smile.

    “Maybe we can even take a little vacation before she gets here. A weekend away somewhere?”

    Zosia lets out a quiet, breathy laugh, adjusting again as another flicker of discomfort passes through her. “The only place I want to spend a weekend right now is bed,” she mutters. “And not in the fun way.”

    Carol huffs a soft laugh despite herself.

    The door opens again behind them and Taffler re-enters with a glass of water, setting it down carefully in front of Zosia like a peace offering.

    “Thank you,” Zosia says, before taking it. She sips at the water, trying not to wince as another wave of cramps hit her.

    Carol straightens. “We’ll sign.”

    Taffler brightens instantly, his relief evident. “Excellent—”

    “—at fifteen percent.”

    Taffler blinks. “I’m sorry?”

    Carol doesn’t miss a beat. “Your fees. You can have fifteen percent, nothing more.”

    A flicker of amusement crosses his face, like he thinks she’s joking. “Carol,” he says lightly, “you’re a valued client, and I’m very sorry Tabbitha overstepped trying to be helpful, but at that rate I’d be—”

    “You told her to do it.” Carol blurts out, and the room stills again.

    Carol’s voice is calmer now. Certain. “I know you did,” she continues. “Your intern’s mom is a huge Wycaro fan. Did you know that? She’s a member of the New Mexico fan club.” She tilts her head slightly. “The president of that fan club—Maureen Reynolds—had a very interesting little chat with her the other day.”

    Taffler’s expression tightens, his lips pressed thin and the lines of his brow deepening as he frowns.

    “She told her everything,” Carol finishes. “That her daughter felt pressured into leaking the story for her boss. You.”

    The silence between them seems to stretch on for an age. Then, Taffler exhales sharply through his nose. “…Fifteen percent,” he says.

    Carol nods once.

    “That brings the final payment to the two of you to two point five million,” he adds, already shifting into damage control, pen in hand as he makes notes on the contract in front of him.

    Carol leans forward slightly, businesslike now. “We’re splitting it evenly. Right down the middle. A million and a quarter each.”

    “And,” Carol adds, with a glance sideways at Zosia, “I want two hundred and fifty thousand put into a trust for the baby. Accessible when she turns eighteen.”

    It’s not something they’ve discussed, but Carol wants something put aside for the baby.

    Taffler looks up, quick to agree. He wants Carol onside. She has enough on him to have his license revoked with one phone call. “Of course. That’s easily arranged once we have a birth certificate.”

    Zosia shifts. “I’d like the same,” she says quietly. “Could it be added to one trust?”

    Taffler nods again, already writing. “Consider it done.”

    Carol leans back, satisfied. For the first time since they walked in, the tension in her shoulders eases. “Good,” she says. “Let’s get this shit signed.”


    -


    By the time night settles in, the house feels impossibly small. Everything narrowed down to Zosia’s bedroom and the steady, restless shift of her laboured breathing.

    Carol has tried everything to make her comfortable.

    Pillows stacked behind her, adjusted and readjusted until Zosia is half-reclined, half-sitting, searching for some position that doesn’t make her wince.

    Her hands have worked endlessly over Zosia’s back, slow circles, firmer pressure when she asks for it, lighter when she flinches.

    She’s read aloud—voice low, steady, something familiar from one of her own books, though she can’t for the life of her remember a single word she’s read.

    It’s hard to focus on anything other than Zosia.

    There’s a tray on the bedside table, barely touched. Soup gone cold. Crackers left broken where Zosia lost interest halfway through.

    Nothing is helping.

    The last time Carol felt this useless she was sitting by her wife’s bedside, holding her hand as the doctor’s turned off the machines keeping her body alive.

    Carol does her best to push those kind of thoughts away.

    They won’t do either of them any good.

    Zosia shifts again, a frustrated sound slipping out of her as one hand presses into the side of her stomach. It feels wrong—tight in a way that doesn’t ease no matter what she tries. Her other hand balls into a fist at her back.

    “My back—” she murmurs, breath catching. “It hurts so bad.”

    Carol is already moving, her hands returning to the other woman, pressing gently along her lower back. “I’ve got you,” she says softly, though there’s a thread of tension in her tone. This has gone on too long. “Tell me if this helps.”

    It doesn’t.

    Zosia’s shoulders tense under her touch, her breath uneven as another wave builds—slow and creeping. Unavoidable.

    She sits forward slightly, clutching at her stomach as a fresh contraction hits. The pain cuts through her composure, steals the breath from her lungs and brings tears to her eyes.

    Carol’s eyes narrow on her watch. She’s been timing the contractions for the last forty minutes—and the numbers don’t lie.

    Zosia bends forward again, both hands braced on the bed now, her head dipping as she tries to breathe through it—but this one is worse. Stronger. It grips and doesn’t let go, ripping a soft, helpless sound from her lips.

    And then—

    The tears come.

    Silent at first, then spilling over as she squeezes her eyes shut, overwhelmed by it. “Make it stop… Please, make it stop.”

    Carol’s chest tightens painfully at the sight of her. “Hey—hey, it’s okay,” she murmurs, moving closer, one hand coming up to cradle the side of Zosia’s face, thumb brushing at the tears she can’t quite keep up with. “I’ve got you. I’ve got you. It’s going to be okay.”

    Zosia shakes her head weakly, breath hitching. “It—hurts,” she cries, voice breaking.

    “Zosia,” she says carefully, keeping her voice soft but firm. “Those last two contractions, they were nine minutes apart.”

    Zosia doesn’t respond immediately, still caught in the tail end of it, her breathing uneven as the pain slowly begins to ease.

    Carol watches her, waiting. “That’s not Braxton Hicks,” she adds, quieter now. “That’s—” She exhales, tries to keep the panic she feels from leaking into her voice. “Zosia, I think you’re in labour.”

    Zosia shakes her head immediately, even before the words fully land. “No,” she says, too quick. “No, I can’t be. Its’s too soon!” Her hand tightens over her stomach, like she can make everything stop through sheer force of will alone.

    “I’m only thirty-five weeks,” she insists, her voice thin but stubborn. “There’s no way—I can’t—”

    Carol moves closer, her hands finding Zosia’s. “Hey,” she says gently. “Look at me.”

    Zosia doesn’t want to.

    But she does.

    Her eyes are glassy, wide with something that’s starting to edge toward panic.

    “We need to go to the hospital,” Carol says, her tone calm, deliberate. Leaving no room for argument.

    Zosia shakes her head again, more frantic this time. “No. No, it’s not time,” she says. “We still have five weeks. I’m—I’m supposed to have a home birth, I haven’t even packed a bag—” Her breath stutters. “We don’t have a name yet, Carol! She can’t—she can’t be coming! Not yet…”

    Carol feels it then—that flicker of fear trying to take hold. She pushes it down. She has to be strong. For Zosia and the baby.

    “Hey,” she murmurs, softer now, her forehead pressing lightly against Zosia’s for a second, grounding them both. “Listen to me.”

    Her hands come up, framing Zosia’s face, steadying her as she looks her square in the eyes and says, “We have to go to the hospital.”

    Zosia’s breath catches again—not from pain this time, but from the weight of it all.

    Carol’s thumb brushes away another tear. “I know this isn’t what we planned,” she adds, her voice gentler now, something reassuring threading through it. “But we’ll figure it out. We always do.”

    Another contraction starts to build. Carol feels it in the way Zosia’s body tenses beneath her hands.

    They’re coming faster now.

    “Zosia, please. We have to go to the hospital. Right now.”

    Zosia nods stiffly, finally facing up to the reality of the situation.

    The baby is coming—whether they’re ready or not.

    Chapter End Notes

    I'm posting chapter 28 immediately after this one, as I didn't think folks would appreciate waiting for it 😅

    Chapter 28

    Chapter Notes

    TW: Non-graphic descriptions of giving birth

    Chapters 27 & 28 posted at the same time, please make sure you've read 27 first.



    The world narrows to Zosia’s hand in hers.

    Everything else is just noise.

    “Hey—hey, I’ve got you,” Carol murmurs, her voice sure and steady in a way that doesn’t match the way her heart is hammering in her chest. Her fingers tighten instinctively around Zosia’s, grounding herself as much as Zosia. “It’s okay. It’s going to be okay.”

    Zosia cries out, the sound raw and torn from somewhere deep as another contraction rips through her. Her body arches against the hospital bed, the thin gown twisted around her legs, damp with sweat. Machines beep and whirr around them, wires trailing from her to monitors that flash numbers Carol doesn’t know enough about to make sense of.

    The room is organised chaos.

    Nurses move in quick, practiced motions, voices overlapping—calling out timings, readings, instructions. Someone adjusts the straps around Zosia’s stomach, another checks the IV line in her arm. The smell of antiseptic hangs sharp in the air.

    Carol feels like she’s in the way. Like she shouldn’t be here, in the midst of this bedlam, but she doesn’t dare let go of Zosia’s hand.

    She can’t.

    “Carol—” Zosia gasps, her grip tightening painfully around her hand, short nails digging in to Carol’s palm. Her eyes are wide, glassy, fixed on her. “Don’t—don’t go. Please don’t leave me—”

    “I’m not going anywhere,” Carol says immediately, leaning closer, her free hand coming up to cup the side of Zosia’s face. “I’m right here, baby. I’ve got you. I’m right here for my girls.”

    Zosia lets out another broken sound that might be a sob, her head tipping forward as she tries to breathe through the pain.

    “Contraction’s been going for forty seconds,” one of the nurses calls out, glancing at the monitor. “Five minutes since the last one.”

    “They’re getting closer,” Zosia manages, her voice thin and shaking. “And stronger—God, they’re so much stronger—”

    “I know,” Carol murmurs, even though she doesn’t—can’t—fully understand what she’s going through. Her thumb brushes over Zosia’s cheek, catching the tears that keep slipping free. “You’re doing so good, Zos. Just hang in there.”

    The doctor steps in then, snapping on a pair of gloves with a sharp tug, her movements efficient and focused. “Alright, Zosia, I’m just going to check your dilation, okay?”

    Zosia nods weakly, barely registering the words through the pain.

    Carol watches, helpless, as the doctor works, her jaw tightening.

    “Cervix is fully effaced,” the doctor says, and Carol has no idea what that means. If it’s a good thing or a bad thing. She wishes she’d read more of Zosia’s pregnancy books. Wishes she hadn’t put it off, hadn’t kept telling herself she still had time.

    “…And you’re at eight centimetres.” The doctor carries on, bringing Carol’s attention back to the present.

    She blinks, eyes wide and dazed. “Eight—what does that mean?”

    “It means she’s in active labour,” the doctor replies, already moving, already giving instructions to someone behind her.

    Carol’s stomach drops. “Active labour—does that mean the baby’s coming? Like—right now?”

    One of the nurses looks up from the monitor, her tone calm, reassuring despite the urgency of everything else. “It means your partner’s body is doing exactly what it’s supposed to do,” she explains. “We need her to reach ten centimetres before she can start to push.”

    Carol nods, trying to keep up, trying to anchor herself in the information. She’s a writer, she knows the details are important. It’s how she makes sense of the world.

    “Her waters haven’t broken yet,” the nurse continues. “And dilation can take about an hour per centimetre at this stage—but it can also move faster. Every labour is different.”

    Carol swallows. “So… two hours?”

    “Maybe,” the nurse says. “Maybe more, maybe less.”

    Zosia lets out another cry, louder this time, her whole body tensing as the next contraction hits. Her hand crushes Carol’s, her other gripping the bed sheets so tightly her knuckles blanch white.

    “I can’t—” she gasps. “I can’t do this—this isn’t—this isn’t what I wanted—”

    The same nurse steps forward, holding out a small mouthpiece attached to a long plastic pipe. “Gas and air, Zosia. It’ll take the edge off—”

    Zosia shakes her head immediately, frantic. “No—I didn’t—I wanted a natural birth, at home—it isn’t time yet, she’s not ready—”

    “Zosia.” Carol’s voice cuts through, her hand tightening around hers. She leans in, close enough that their foreheads almost touch. “Hey. Hey, look at me.”

    It takes effort, but Zosia’s eyes find hers.

    “The plan’s changed,” Carol says gently. “That’s all. It doesn’t mean anything’s wrong. We just—we go with it, yeah? You’ve still got this, Zosia. You’re bringing our daughter into this world, and if anyone can do it, it’s you. You are strong, and crazy stubborn and I love every last thing about you.”

    Carol doesn’t even flinch at saying all this in a room full of people. Zosia and the baby are all that matter right now.

    Zosia’s lip trembles as another wave of pain hits.

    Carol takes the mouthpiece from the nurse, her hand steady now, even if the rest of her isn’t. She holds it out for Zosia. “Come on,” she murmurs. “Just try it. For me.”

    For a second, Zosia hesitates. Then, with a shaky breath, she takes it from her.

    The nurse offers a small, encouraging smile. “That’s it. Nice and slow. Big deep breaths, Zosia.”

    “Thirty-five weeks is considered late preterm,” she adds, her tone reassuring. “Your baby’s early, but she’s not high risk. We see this all the time.”

    Carol clings to that.

    Late preterm.

    Not high risk.

    Another contraction builds, visible in the way Zosia’s body tenses, her shoulders drawing tight as she inhales sharply through the mouthpiece. Her eyes squeeze shut, a strangled cry escaping around it.

    “Here it comes,” the nurse says, glancing at the monitor with Zosia’s vitals on it. “Just ride it, Zosia. Nice big breaths, sweetie. You’re doing great.”

    Zosia’s free hand finds Carol’s again, gripping it tight. Desperate.

    Carol leans in, her voice low, steady, even as her pulse races. “Breathe with me,” she says. “Like we practiced, yeah? In through your nose—”

    She demonstrates it, exaggerating the inhale just like Maureen did during the prenatal class.

    “—and out through your mouth.”

    Zosia tries to follow, her breathing uneven at first, then catching the rhythm Carol sets.

    “In… and out,” Carol murmurs. “That’s it. You’ve got this.”

    Zosia shakes her head weakly, tears slipping from the corners of her eyes. “I can’t do this,” she cries. “I can’t—”

    “Yes, you can,” the nurse says firmly, stepping closer. “Your contractions are three minutes apart now. That’s a very good sign. It means your body’s doing exactly what it needs to do.”

    Zosia’s breath stutters.

    “It’ll be time to push soon,” the nurse continues, her voice softer now. “Just keep breathing. You’re doing brilliantly. Both of you are.”

    Carol lets out a breath she didn’t realise she was holding.

    She doesn’t feel brilliant.

    Her heart is racing, panic clawing at the edges of her composure, every instinct in her screaming that something is wrong, that she’s going to lose them—

    She swallows it down.

    Pushes it away.

    Because Zosia needs her steady.

    So Carol tightens her grip on her hand, leans in closer, and keeps her voice calm.

    “I’ve got you,” she says again, softer this time. “Every step of the way. You’re not doing this alone, Zosia.”


    -


    “Okay, Zosia—this is it.”

    The room tightens around the words.

    Carol barely hears anything else. Her entire world is narrowed to the woman in front of her—sweat-damp hair clinging to her temples, chest heaving, fingers still locked painfully around hers.

    “You’re fully dilated,” the doctor says, voice calm but firm. “Next contraction, I need you to push.”

    Zosia shakes her head weakly, tears slipping free again. “I can’t—I can’t—”

    Carol leans in, her forehead brushing Zosia’s, her voice low and steady despite the tremor in her chest. “You can. You already are,” she murmurs. “You’ve come this far, baby. Just a little more.”

    Zosia’s breath stutters, her grip tightening.

    Another contraction builds—Carol sees it before it hits, in the way Zosia’s body tenses, her shoulders drawing tight, her breath catching sharp in her chest.

    “Here it comes,” the nurse says.

    “Push!” the doctor instructs.

    Zosia cries out as the pain peaks, her whole body bearing down, muscles straining as she pushes with everything she has. Her face flushes deep red, jaw clenched, breath forced from her lungs in a broken sound.

    “That’s it,” Carol says, her voice right there, unwavering. “That’s it, Zos. You’re doing it. You’re doing so good—”

    Zosia collapses back slightly as the contraction eases, gasping for air, her chest rising and falling erratically.

    “I can’t—” she whispers again, weaker now.

    “Yes, you can,” Carol says, firmer this time. “One more. Just like that.”

    Another contraction comes, faster this time. Stronger.

    Zosia cries out again, pushing through it, her fingers crushing Carol’s hand as the room fills with voices—encouraging, instructing, counting.

    “Again, Zosia, push—push—”

    She does.

    Everything narrows. Tightens.

    And then—

    “I can see the head,” the doctor says.

    Carol’s breath catches.

    “Zosia, one more push—”

    Zosia gathers what little she has left and bears down again with a raw, broken cry—

    And then—

    A sound.

    Sharp. Immediate.

    A cry that cuts clean through the noise.

    Carol freezes.

    The world stops.

    “There she is,” someone says, and suddenly there’s movement—quick, practiced hands rallying around.

    Carol doesn’t even realise she’s crying until she tastes the salt of her tears on her lips.

    The baby’s cry fills the room—strong, insistent.

    Alive.

    “She’s healthy,” the nurse says, already moving, checking her pulse, her temperature, “Good tone, strong cry—”

    Zosia’s head tips back against the pillow, her body going slack with exhaustion, tears streaking down her temples as she gasps for breath.

    “Baby to mom,” the doctor says.

    And then—

    She’s there.

    Placed carefully on Zosia’s chest, still damp, still crying, tiny limbs moving instinctively as she settles against warm bare skin.

    Carol stares in awe.

    She can’t look away.

    She’s so small.

    Tiny strands of dark hair plastered to her head, her skin flushed and wrinkled, little fingers flexing, curling in the air grasping at nothing.

    Carol lets out a breathless laugh, something disbelieving breaking through her chest. “Jesus,” she whispers, shaking her head slightly. “She’s—she’s the size of a cantaloupe.”

    Zosia lets out a soft, exhausted chuckle, her face flushed, damp with tears and sweat as she looks down at their daughter.

    “She is not a fruit,” she murmurs, though there’s no real argument in it. Her hand trembles slightly as she touches the baby’s back, fingers brushing carefully over her.

    Her expression softens, something deep and overwhelming settling in. “She’s… perfect,” she says quietly.

    Carol swallows hard, her chest tight.

    She couldn’t agree more.

    “We still have to name her,” Zosia adds, her voice faint but teasing at the edges as she looks up at Carol. “Preferably something that isn’t fruit related.”

    Carol huffs a quiet laugh, wiping at her face with the back of her hand. “Yeah,” she murmurs, reaching out to put a hand on Zosia’s shoulder. “That’s probably a good call.”

    They sit in their shared joy for a moment, the noise of the busy hospital room fading, until it’s just them. Carol, Zosia and the baby.

    Carol watches both of them like she can’t quite believe they’re real.

    Zosia glances up at her again then, eyes soft despite the exhaustion pulling at them. “Do you want to hold her?”

    Carol blinks. “I—” She hesitates, something flickering across her face. “Can I?” She asks, almost in disbelief.

    Zosia smiles faintly. “Of course you can.”

    A nurse steps in, gentle and practiced, lifting the baby carefully, adjusting the blanket around her small body before turning to Carol.

    “Here,” she says softly.

    Carol’s hands come up instinctively—then pause, hovering for a fraction of a second before she takes her.

    The weight is almost nothing.

    And yet it’s everything.

    Carol’s breath catches as the baby settles in her arms, tiny and warm, her cries softening as she shifts against her.

    “Oh,” Carol breathes, her voice dropping instinctively, like anything louder might break the moment. She sways slightly without thinking, a natural rhythm settling into her body as she looks down at the precious bundle in her arms.

    “Hey,” she murmurs softly. “Hi, baby.”

    Her thumb brushes lightly over the tiny blanket wrapped around her, careful and reverent.

    “You’re beautiful,” she sighs, voice thick and close to breaking, “Just like your momma.” She grins and looks up, waiting for Zosia’s reaction.

    It doesn’t come.

    Carol’s smile falters.

    Zosia is still.

    Too still.

    Her gaze unfocused, distant in a way that sends something cold sliding down Carol’s spine.

    “Zosia?” she says, frowning slightly. Still, there’s no response. Then—

    The monitor behind her blares.

    Carol’s head snaps up, she cradles the baby in her hands tighter as it begins to cry at the piercing noise. Cold dread washes over her. “Zosia—?”

    Zosia’s eyes roll back slightly as her body slackens on the bed—

    “Zosia!”

    The room erupts.

    “Postpartum hemorrhage!” the doctor snaps, already moving. “She’s bleeding—uterine atony—”

    Carol’s stomach drops.

    Blood.

    There’s blood.

    Too much. Pooling beneath Zosia, bright red and just plain wrong against the stark white hospital gown she wears.

    “Start fundal massage—now!”

    “Seven point seven two of Oxytocin infusion—”

    “Somebody get the Methylergonovine—move—”

    Hands move fast, urgent, voices overlapping again but sharper now, tighter.

    Carol stands frozen for half a second, the baby still in her arms, her heart slamming against her ribs.

    “Is she okay?” she asks, her voice breaking. “Zosia—Zosia, can you hear me—?”

    A nurse appears at her side then, firm but gentle. “I need you to come with me.”

    “I’m not leaving her—”

    “We need the space to work,” the nurse says, voice steady and calm. Carol doesn’t know how anyone can be calm right now.

    She looks down at the tiny bundle in her arms. Red in the face from crying, full body wriggling in distress. Carol swallows hard and nods. Lets herself be guided backwards, her feet moving even though everything in her wants to stay.

    “Zosia,” she calls again, her voice cracking as she’s ushered out of the room. “Zosia—”

    The doors swing shut behind them.

    And suddenly it’s just her. Standing in the hallway, cradling a baby that doesn’t even have a name yet. Her hands shake as she looks down at her, tears blurring her vision.

    “Hey,” she whispers, her voice unsteady now. “Hey, it’s okay… I’ve got you…”

    The baby cries louder, unsettled by the chaos, by the shift. Carol presses her closer instinctively, swaying slightly, trying to soothe her even as panic claws at her own chest.

    Her eyes flick back to the closed doors.

    Please

    Please not again.

    Not Zosia.


    -



    Carol paces the length of the family room, back and forth in a line, footsteps muffled by the carpet. Her arms are folded tight across her chest, one hand gripping her opposite elbow like she’s physically holding herself together

    She’s not sure how long she’s been in here.

    Minutes.

    Hours.

    It all feels the same.

    The baby is in the NICU. Healthy, they said. Just small. They want to monitor her breathing, make sure she can regulate her temperature, make sure she can feed.

    All normal.

    All fine.

    Carol had nodded when the nurse told her all that. Said thank you. Smiled like she understood. In truth, she barely absorbed a word of it. Because, Zosia—

    Zosia is still in surgery.

    Carol presses her lips together, blinking hard as her eyes burn. Because she knows this hospital. Knows the smell of it. The hum of the lights. The way the air feels too clean and too heavy all at once.

    This is where they brought Helen.

    This is where Carol sat in a room not unlike this one, staring at a closed door and waiting for someone to come out and tell her—

    Her breath catches in her throat.

    She stops pacing.

    For a second, the walls feel like they’re closing in. She shakes her head sharply, dragging in a breath with difficulty. It feels like there’s an elephant sitting on her chest.

    “No,” she mutters under her breath. “No. Not happening.”

    She swipes at her eyes, rough, like she can wipe the thoughts away along with the tears.

    “She’s not—” Her voice breaks slightly. She steadies it. “She’s not going anywhere.”

    The door opens and Carol’s head snaps up, her entire body going still—

    But it’s not a doctor. It’s Koumba, with Manousos following closely behind him.

    Relief and something else—something heavier—crashes through her all at once.

    Koumba doesn’t hesitate. He crosses the room in a few quick strides and pulls her into a tight hug, arms wrapping around her solid and steady.

    Carol folds into it.

    Just for a second.

    “Hey,” he murmurs, one hand coming up to the back of her head, holding her there. “Everything is going to be okay. Zosia is a fighter.”

    Carol exhales shakily, gripping the back of his shirt like it’s the only thing keeping her on her feet. “I know,” she says, though it comes out thinner than she means it to.

    Manousos steps in closer, his expression more reserved but no less concerned. “We came as soon as we could.” he says. Carol just nods, she doesn’t even remember calling them.

    She pulls back from Koumba’s embrace, wiping at her eyes again, trying to pull herself together. She can’t afford to fall apart right now. Zosia needs her.

    “Thank you,” she says, her voice still rough. “Both of you.”

    Koumba keeps a hand on her shoulder. “How is she?” he asks gently. “And the baby?”

    Carol nods quickly, like she needs to get the words out before they catch somewhere in her throat. “The baby’s good,” she says. “She’s—she’s small, but they expected that. They’ve got her in the NICU just to keep an eye on her.”

    Her voice softens, just slightly. “She’s… she’s strong.”

    Koumba smiles faintly at that. “Just like her mothers.”

    “And Zosia?” Manousos asks.

    Carol’s jaw tightens.

    “She’s still in surgery,” she says, quieter now. “She started bleeding after they got the placenta out. Something about—” She gestures vaguely, frustrated. She still doesn’t understand what happened herself. “Her uterus not contracting properly. They said it’s… postpartum haemorrhage.”

    The words feel alien in her mouth, clinical and detached. “They’re operating now,” she finishes. “I’m just—waiting.”

    Her arms wrap around herself again, tighter this time.

    “God,” she mutters, her voice cracking as she looks around the room like the neutral decor personally offends her. “I hate this fucking hospital.”

    Koumba shifts slightly, his tone gentle. “Nobody likes hospitals—”

    Manousos cuts in sharply, a string of rapid Spanish that makes the other man stiffen. Carol doesn’t catch all of it—but she hears enough.

    ‘Su esposa murió aqui’.

    Koumba’s eyes widen immediately. “I didn’t—Carol, I’m so sorry, I didn’t know—”

    Carol lifts a hand, cutting him off before he can spiral. “Don’t,” she says quickly. “I just—”

    Her voice falters.

    She swallows hard.

    “I don’t want to think about Helen right now,” Carol shakes her head. “I—I can’t. Zosia—Zosia is going to be okay. She is—she has to be.” She says it like she needs it to be true.

    Like if she says it enough times, it will be.

    The door opens again and all three of them turn in unison. Carol’s breath catches—that same cold spike of fear cutting through her chest before she can stop it.

    The doctor steps in to the room and Carol doesn’t move. Doesn’t dare even take a breathe. She just stands there, waiting for her world to fall apart for the second time in as a many short years.

    ‘    I can’t lose her.’ She thinks to herself, fighting the urge to clamp her eyes shut.

    The doctor’s expression shifts.

    Softens.

    And then—

    “She’s out of surgery,” she says.

    Carol’s knees almost give out on the spot.

    “And she’s doing well,” the doctor continues. “She lost a significant amount of blood, but we were able to stabilise her. She’s had a transfusion and is responding well.”

    The room tilts. Relief hits Carol so hard it almost knocks the air from her lungs. Koumba’s hands are on her instantly, catching her as she sways, holding her upright.

    Carol lets out a broken sob, the sound tearing free before she can stop it. “Thank—” she tries, her voice collapsing under the weight of it. “Thank you—”

    She presses a hand to her face, shoulders shaking as everything she’s been holding in finally breaks loose.

    “She’s okay,” Koumba murmurs again, steadier this time. “She’s okay.”

    Carol nods against her hand, dragging in a breath, trying to get control back.

    “Can I see her?” she asks, her voice hoarse as she looks at the doctor.

    The doctor nods. “You can, but only one visitor at a time.”

    Carol’s eyes flick to Koumba, something apologetic in the look—but he shakes his head immediately.

    “You go,” he says firmly. “She’ll want to see you first.”

    Carol hesitates for half a second—then steps forward, pulling him into another hug. “Thank you,” she murmurs against his shoulder, squeezing him tight.

    He squeezes her back. “Go. Well be here.”

    Carol pulls away, wiping at her eyes again as she turns back to the doctor, forcing herself to focus.

    “Uh, the baby… Can I bring the baby?” she asks quickly. “She’s in the NICU, but—Zosia’s going to ask for her. I know she is.”

    The doctor considers it, then nods. “Yes. For a short visit. We don’t want to overexert her.”

    Carol nods immediately, feels her own exhaustion heavy in her bones as the adrenaline that’s been sustaining her for the last couple of hours finally drains away. “Of course.”

    “She’ll need to stay in the hospital for a couple of days,” the doctor continues. “And the baby will need monitoring as well. But if her stats remain stable and she feeds without assistance, she may be able to stay with Zosia overnight.”

    Carol doesn’t hesitate. “I can help,” she says. “Whatever she needs. I—I can take care of the baby until Zosia’s back on her feet.”

    “We can all help.” Koumba adds, Manousos nodding in agreement behind him.

    Carol exhales, steadying herself as she squeezes Koumba’s hand in thanks.

    The doctor gives them a small, approving nod of her own. “She’s lucky to have you all.”


    -


    Zosia’s hospital room is quiet when Carol steps inside, the lighting soft and low. The only sound comes from the steady hum of machines, and the air smells faintly of antiseptic.

    Carol pauses for a second at the door.

    The baby is warm and solid in her arms, swaddled tight in a pale blanket, her tiny face relaxed in sleep. Clean now. Peaceful. No trace left behind of the chaos she arrived in—just soft breaths and the occasional twitch of her little body.

    Zosia lies in the bed, skin pale even against the stark white sheets. Her hair is pushed back loosely from her face, her body half-sunk into the mattress like the weight of everything has finally caught up with her. One arm rests at her side on top of the sheets, the other across her stomach—empty now.

    Her eyes stay closed.

    Carol pulls the chair closer with one hand, the legs scraping softly against the floor, and sits.

    “Hey,” she says, her voice quiet. Gentle so as not to disturb the baby.

    Zosia stirs.

    Her eyes open slowly, heavy with sleep, with medication, with everything her body’s been through. It takes her a second to focus—but when she does, her eyes land on Carol.

    “Hi,” she murmurs, her voice rough, barely above a whisper.

    Carol’s mouth curves, the tension in her shoulders finally cracking. “Hi.”

    Zosia’s gaze drifts, slow and instinctive, drawn to the bundle in Carol’s arms.

    Her breath catches—just slightly, and Carol can see the effort it takes for her to even keep her eyes open. “She’s okay?” she asks, the words quiet, careful. Like she’s afraid of the answer.

    Carol nods immediately. “She’s doing great,” she says. “They’re keeping an eye on her, but everything seems okay.”

    Zosia exhales, some of the tension leaving her body as her head sinks back into the pillow.

    “Good,” she murmurs.

    Carol watches her for a moment, taking her in properly now that she’s awake. “How are you feeling?”

    Zosia huffs a faint, tired breath, her lips twitching. “I’ve been better.”

    Carol lets out a quiet laugh at that, relief threading through it. “Yeah,” she says softly. “I figured.”

    There’s a pause, then Carol glances down at the baby. “Do you want to hold her?” She asks.

    Zosia’s eyes lift again, sharper this time despite the exhaustion. “Yes,” she says, almost immediately. “Please.”

    Carol stands carefully, shifting the baby in her arms as she steps closer to the bed. She leans in, slow and deliberate, and gently lowers her onto Zosia’s chest.

    Zosia’s arms come up instinctively, even through the fatigue, cradling her with a tenderness that makes something in Carol’s chest tighten.

    The baby stirs faintly, a soft breath, but doesn’t wake. Zosia looks down at her, and the love in her gaze immediate. Absolute.

    “Hi,” Zosia whispers.

    Carol stays close, one hand hovering protectively before settling lightly at the back of the baby’s head. For a moment, neither of them says a word.

    Zosia’s fingers brush gently over the baby’s cheek, her touch careful and reverent.

    Then—

    “Ava,” she murmurs.

    Carol blinks. “What was that?”

    Zosia lifts her gaze slightly, meeting Carol’s eyes with a new found strength. There’s more clarity there now. More certainty.

    “Ava,” she repeats, a little stronger. “That’s her name.”

    “Ava.” Carol repeats it quietly, testing the shape of it. She smiles. “It suits her… and it’s much better than Jackfruit.”

    Zosia smiles faintly at that, her attention already drifting back down to their daughter.

    Carol leans in, pressing a gentle kiss to Zosia’s forehead, lingering there for just a second. Her hand remains at Ava’s head, thumb brushing lightly over the fine hairs there.

    “I’m really glad you’re both okay,” she says quietly, the words catching on their way out. She swallows hard.

    “I—I don’t…” She exhales, shaking her head slightly. “I don’t know what I would’ve done if—”

    She doesn’t finish.

    Can’t let herself even consider it.

    Zosia’s eyes flick back up to her, “Hey,” she murmurs, and shifts her hand slightly, finding Carol’s. Her fingers curl around it despite the weakness in them.

    “Thank you,” she says, her voice quiet but steady. “For taking care of us. For getting us here when you did.”

    Her thumb brushes lightly over the back of Carol’s knuckles.

    “We wouldn’t be here without you.”

    Carol shakes her head instinctively, like she wants to argue—but the words don’t come. Instead, she leans in, presses a soft kiss to Zosia’s lips and finally says the quiet part out loud.

    “I wouldn’t be here without you, Zosia. Without both of you.”

    Chapter End Notes

    Baby Stursia is finally here, and finally has a name! Naming the baby was honestly the hardest part of this fic 😅 There's one chapter left to go, which I'm hoping to have up in a couple of days.

    A lot of people have been asking for more chapters, but I only planned on covering up to the baby being born, and I'm a stickler for planning fics in great detail, so if I do come back to Carol, Zosia and Ava's story it will probably be a sequel or some loosely connected one shots.

    As ever, thanks for reading and for the wonderful support this fic has received.

    - Tan

    Chapter 29

    Chapter Notes


    Carol’s truck rolls to a slow, careful stop in front of Zosia’s house. She doesn’t cut the engine right away, though. Her hands stay on the wheel for a second longer than necessary, her eyes flicking instinctively to the rearview mirror.

    Ava is exactly where she’s been the entire drive—safe and secure, swaddled and strapped into her car seat. Her tiny face turned slightly to the side, her eyes closed.

    Carol watches for a moment, waiting to see the blanket rise and fall with her soft little breaths.

    Zosia doesn’t miss it. “You’ve checked on her about twenty times since we left the hospital,” she says lightly, her voice warm with quiet amusement as she shifts slightly in her seat.

    Carol huffs under her breath, finally tearing her gaze away from the mirror. “I’m just being careful. She’s like three days old and tiny. She’s not even meant to be out of your belly yet.”

    “The doctor gave her the all-clear. Gave it to both of us.”

    Carol hums, unconvinced. “I’m still allowed to worry. About both of you.”

    Zosia chuckles. “Is that why you drove twenty miles an hour the entire way home?”

    “I did not—” Carol starts, her voice rising, but cuts herself off as Ava stirs in the back. She gives a heavy sigh. “Okay, maybe I drove a little under the speed limit.”

    “A little?” Zosia laughs softly, the sound still a bit breathy, still carrying the faint exhaustion of not only having recently given birth but losing close to a litre of blood. “We were overtaken by a cyclist, Carol.”

    Carol glares at her, mock affronted as she turns off the engine. “First of all, he was clearly an athlete. Did you see that yellow Spanx getup he was wearing? And second, the dude was a giant! His legs were bigger than my whole body…”

    Zosia just shakes her head, smiling, her hand coming instinctively to her stomach before she remembers— it shifts instead to rest lightly over the blanket in her lap.

    Carol’s eyes flick back to the mirror.

    “She’s fine,” Zosia says gently.

    Carol exhales, a little sheepish now that she’s been caught out. “I know,” she mutters. “I just—she’s really quiet. Aren’t babies meant to cry? Like all the time?”

    Zosia’s smile deepens. “Give it time,” she says knowingly.

    Carol snorts softly. “Yeah, well. She could be eighteen, if she’s sleeping that soundly, I’m still be poking her in the middle of the night to make sure she’s breathing.”

    Zosia lets out a proper laugh at that, the sound lighter now, freer. But there’s something else beneath it too, something soft and loving.

    Because Carol says it so easily.

    Eighteen.

    The future.

    Like she’s already decided she’s going to be there for all of it. Every birthday. Every scrape of her knee. Every heartbreak. Carol will be there.

    Zosia glances at her, her expression softening as she takes her in—the tension still in her shoulders, the way her hands hover like she’s not entirely sure what to do with them now that the drive is over.

    Carol clears her throat, like she’s aware she’s said something a little too revealing. “Anyway,” she mutters, pushing the door open. “We’re here.”

    She gets out quickly, circling the front of the truck before Zosia even has time to reach for her own door.

    Carol opens it for her, one hand already offered out. “Careful,” she says, all teasing gone as she helps Zosia ease out of the seat.

    Zosia moves slowly, her body still tender, the memory of surgery lingering in every careful shift of her weight. She leans into Carol just slightly as she steadies herself, one hand braced briefly against her arm.

    “I’ve got you,” Carol murmurs.

    Zosia nods, exhaling as she straightens. “I know.”

    She looks up at her, expression loving, and leans in just enough to press a soft kiss to Carol’s cheek.

    “Thank you.”

    Carol huffs quietly, like she doesn’t quite know what to do with that. “Yeah, well,” she mutters. “Got to take care of my girls.”

    Zosia smiles at that.

    Carol pulls back, already turning toward the back of the truck. “I’ll get Ava.”

    She moves with purpose now, opening the rear door and carefully lifting the car seat out, her movements precise, deliberate.

    There’s a confidence there that wasn’t there a few days ago when she first held her. She’s still cautious, moving like the baby is made of glass and not flesh and blood, but her movements are surer.

    She checks the straps automatically, adjusts the blanket without thinking, her gaze softening as she looks down at the tiny face inside.

    “Hey, kiddo,” she murmurs under her breath.

    Ava doesn’t stir. She’s done little more than sleep and eat for the last few days. Carol smiles, supposing this is the easy part.

    When she turns back toward the house, carrying the car seat with both hands, something about her shifts—shoulders squared just a little, chin lifted.

    Proud.

    Like she’s exactly where she’s supposed to be.

    The front door closes softly behind them, the quiet of the house settling in around them almost immediately. It feels different now—full in a way it hadn’t before, even in the silence.

    Carol lingers just inside for a second, one hand still resting on the handle of the car seat, her eyes flicking between Zosia and the baby like she’s waiting for instructions she hasn’t been given yet.

    “So,” she says, a little unsure, “what do we—”

    Zosia shifts beside her, one arm braced carefully against the wall as she steadies herself. Her movements are measured, colour still a little too pale for Carol’s liking.

    “She needs to feed,” Zosia says gently.

    Carol nods immediately, like that’s something she can work with. “Okay. Yeah. Where—where do you want to do that?”

    “Upstairs,” Zosia replies, exhaustion colouring her voice. “In bed.”

    Carol glances at her, taking in the way she’s holding herself, the careful way she moves. Concern flickers across her face. “You alright?”

    “I’m fine,” Zosia says. “Just sore.”

    Carol doesn’t look entirely convinced, but she nods anyway. “Alright. Upstairs it is.”

    She lifts the car seat again, with the same care and caution, and they make their way up together—Carol a step ahead, Zosia slower behind her, one hand trailing along the banister.

    Once in Zosia’s bedroom, Carol crosses to the bed and sets the car seat down gently, like she’s afraid even that might be too much. She hesitates for a second, then reaches in and carefully lifts Ava out like she’s a hand grenade with a loose pin.

    The baby stirs immediately, a small, uncertain sound breaking from her as her face scrunches and she begins to cry.

    “Hey, hey—” Carol murmurs quickly, instinct kicking in as she brings her up against her shoulder. “It’s okay, little one. I’ve got you.”

    She sways without thinking, back and forth in a slow rhythm, one hand supporting Ava’s head as the other rests lightly along her back. A soft hum slips from her, low and steady, something half-remembered that settles into a lullaby before she even realises she’s doing it.

    Ava’s fussing eases.

    Across the room, Zosia watches with a quiet smile.

    There’s something soft and open in her expression, something that settles deep in her chest as she takes them in—Carol with Ava tucked against her, moving instinctively, like she’s always known how to do this.

    That’s her family.

    The thought lands gently, but it carries weight. New. Exciting. A little terrifying.

    She just gave birth to a whole person. A baby made from the two people she has loved most in her life. A tiny, fragile thing that depends entirely on her—on both of them.

    Zosia lowers herself carefully onto the bed, adjusting the pillows behind her with a quiet exhale as she settles in. Carol glances over once she’s sure Ava has calmed, then walks her over, slower now. She shifts her slightly in her arms, just enough to look at her properly.

    Ava’s skin is still flushed from birth, soft and delicate, but there’s a warm glow to it—richer than Carol’s and closer to Malik’s. Her hair, what little there is, sits dark against her head, thick in places with the faintest hint of a curl.

    Carol’s mouth curves, something openly fond breaking through. “Hey,” she murmurs. Ava blinks up at her, cloudy blue eyes wide and unfocused. They don’t seem to quite land on anything yet.

    To her, the world consists of blurry shapes and soft voices.

    Carol huffs a quiet laugh. “She’s got my eyes.”

    Zosia smiles faintly, settling the pillow more securely in her lap. “They can still change.”

    Carol’s expression drops immediately. “No way.”

    “It’s common,” Zosia says, amused. “They’re a blue-grey now, but they can still change. It take months before eye colour settles.”

    Carol frowns, glancing toward the stack of pregnancy books on the bedside table like they’re an assignment she’s been putting off. “Alright,” she mutters. “I’m going to have to do some homework… But she has my eyes until proven otherwise.”

    Zosia lets out a quiet laugh, adjusting her shirt as she gets comfortable. “There’s a lot we still need to get,” she adds, glancing around the room. “A proper feeding pillow. More clothes. Bottles…”She trails off slightly, her hand resting instinctively against her stomach before shifting to the pillow in her lap.

    “At least we already have the pump,” she says. “So I can express before these things burst.” She palms her breasts, which are at least two cup sizes bigger than when Carol met her. Full and swollen, she feels like a dairy cow.

    Carol nods, already halfway ahead of her. “We can order everything online this afternoon,” she says. “You’re not going anywhere until you’re feeling better. I’ll take care of it.”

    Zosia looks at her, something warm flickering across her face. “You don’t have to do everything.”

    “Yes, I do,” Carol says simply. Then, softer, “I want to.”

    She steps closer, lowering Ava carefully toward Zosia. “Here.”

    Zosia brings her arms up, cradling Ava against the pillow as she settles her in place. Her movements are slow, careful, but sure. Carol wishes she could feel that same confidence in handling her. Every time she touches the baby, she feels like she might break her by accident.

    Carol stays close, hovering just long enough to make sure Ava is secure before stepping back half a pace.

    Zosia unbuttons her shirt, pushing the fabric aside and adjusting her bra, her focus entirely on Ava as she guides her. Carol watches, expression wistful.

    Zosia glances up, catching her. “What is it?”

    Carol blinks, caught, then clears her throat. “Nothing.”

    Zosia raises a brow.

    Carol’s mouth quirks slightly, a faint blush creeping in. “I’m just… going to have to get used to sharing those now.”

    Zosia lets out a quiet laugh, the sound warm and tired all at once. Then it softens, something a little more bashful settling in. “I don’t think we’ll be sharing anything for a while,” she admits. “The doctor said at least six weeks.”

    Carol climbs onto the bed beside her, the mattress dipping under her weight. Her hand finds the back of Ava’s head, fingers gentle as they brush over the soft curls.

    “Don’t worry about any of that stuff yet,” she murmurs, pressing a kiss to Zosia’s cheek. Zosia leans into it slightly.
